# Master Index

## Uncategorized

- [[10-best-vllm-alternatives-for-llm-inference-in-production-2026]] — 10 Best vLLM Alternatives for LLM Inference in Production (2026) — Long vendor listicle (PremAI blog, same vendor/publisher as the companion engine-comparison article in this batch -- treat as one data source, not independent corroboration) surveying 15 vLLM alternatives for 2026. Confirms the TGI maintenance-mode date again verbatim: 'As of December 11, 2025, TGI entered maintenance mode... Hugging Face now only accepts PRs for bug fixes, documentation improvements, and lightweight maintenance. No new features will be added.' States SGLang is 'deployed on 400,000+ GPUs worldwide, becoming the de facto standard for agentic workloads' and gives SGLang 23.6K GitHub stars. Engine-level software limitations captured (hardware specifics excluded per scope): vLLM's PagedAttention suffers memory fragmentation under sustained load severe enough that 'the fix is usually restarting the server'; very long contexts (32K+) can 'destabilize a server handling shorter requests'; speculative decoding increases memory overhead enough to be impractical in some configs; vLLM does not support GGUF or EXL2 quantization formats; large-model startup/load time is 5-10 minutes, requiring load-balancing strategy during deploys. llama.cpp limitations: 'batching is less sophisticated than vLLM, throughput under concurrent load is lower'; no speculative decoding; limited vision support; server 'lacks enterprise features (metrics, health checks, graceful shutdown).' Ollama limitations (self-described by the same vendor piece): 'Not production-grade: Lacks enterprise features... Limited batching: Concurrent requests are handled sequentially. Not suitable for high-throughput serving'; Ollama is confirmed to run on top of llama.cpp with '2-5% overhead for the abstraction layer' per this source (note: a llama.cpp maintainer quoted elsewhere in this batch's HN thread disputes the current accuracy of 'Ollama = llama.cpp wrapper' framing post-GGML-direct integration). TGI vs vLLM throughput benchmark (H100, Llama-3.1-8B, 1000 ShareGPT prompts, software-only metric): SGLang 16,215 tok/s > LMDeploy 16,132 > vLLM 12,553 > TensorRT-LLM ~10,000+ > TGI ~9,500 (frozen) > llama.cpp ~6,000. Explicitly frames TGI as 'frozen' and advises migration for new projects while vLLM 'remains solid for teams valuing ecosystem and documentation.' This is marketing content for PremAI's own managed inference platform -- the 'Why Consider PremAI Instead of Self-Hosting' and ROI sections should be read as vendor claims, not independent evidence. `review` (6226 words)
- [[2025-mid-year-llm-market-update-foundation-model-landscape-economics-menlo-ventu]] — 2025 Mid-Year LLM Market Update: Foundation Model Landscape + Economics | Menlo Ventures — Menlo Ventures survey of 150 technical decision-makers (enterprises >=5,000 employees and VC-funded startups), fielded June 30-July 10 2025, measuring self-reported SHARE OF PRODUCTION AI WORKLOADS (not spend) by model/provider, weighted by application scale. Finds Anthropic leads enterprise usage at 32% (overtaking OpenAI's 25%, down from 50% in 2023), Google at 20%, Meta Llama at 9%, DeepSeek at 1%. Open-source/open-weight model usage FELL from 19% to 13% of workloads over six months, attributed to a persistent 9-12 month performance gap versus closed frontier models, deployment complexity, and enterprise reluctance to use APIs from Chinese labs. Enterprise LLM API spend more than doubled from $3.5B (Nov 2024) to $8.4B (mid-2025). Only 11% of teams switched vendors in the past year; 66% upgraded within their existing provider. Inference now exceeds training spend: 74% of startups and 49% of enterprises report inference as majority of compute, up from 48%/29% a year prior. `review` (2042 words)
- [[2025diff]] — 2025.diff — Full unified diff for GIE PR #2025. Key addition in pkg/epp/util/request/headers.go: OutputInjectionHeaders = {destination-endpoint, destination-endpoint-served} (lowercased) plus InputControlHeaders and ProtocolHeaders={content-length}, unioned via IsSystemOwnedHeader(key). pkg/epp/handlers/request.go's generateHeaders() and response.go's generateResponseHeaders() now skip any header key where IsSystemOwnedHeader(key) is true inside the 'include all headers' loop: '// Include any non-system-owned headers.\nfor key, value := range reqCtx.Request.Headers {\n  if request.IsSystemOwnedHeader(key) { continue }\n  ...}'. New unit test asserts the exact attack scenario is blocked: inbound header map sets metadata.DestinationEndpointKey (x-gateway-destination-endpoint) to a malicious '1.1.1.1:666', and after generateHeaders() runs, 'assert.Equal(t, "1.2.3.4:8080", gotHeaders[metadata.DestinationEndpointKey])' -- i.e. the client-supplied value is dropped and only EPP's own computed target endpoint is forwarded to Envoy. `evergreen` (841 words)
- [[2061]] — 2061 — GitHub API record for GIE PR #2061 ('fix prometheus auth', author sallyom, merged 2026-01-05, 1 file changed / 1 addition / 1 deletion). PR body states the intent directly: 'Update inferencepool charts so prometheus can be enabled with metrics-auth disabled. Fixes #2060.' This confirms the EPP Helm chart exposes a CLI flag --metrics-endpoint-auth on the EPP binary that is templated from monitoring.prometheus.auth.enabled (post-fix); the PR is purely a Helm values-wiring bug fix (single line in epp-deployment.yaml, see companion note [[2061diff]]) and does not itself describe the underlying bearer-token/mTLS mechanism -- it only establishes that the metrics endpoint has an auth flag that chart users must be able to independently disable from Prometheus-scrape-enablement, implying auth is otherwise expected to be required/on when Prometheus monitoring is enabled via the chart's default wiring. `review` (1226 words)
- [[2061diff]] — 2061.diff — One-line Helm-chart diff for GIE PR #2061 ('fix prometheus auth', sallyom, merged Jan 5 2026, fixes issue #2060) in config/charts/inferencepool/templates/epp-deployment.yaml: changes the guard controlling the EPP container's --metrics-endpoint-auth=false flag from '{{- if not .Values.inferenceExtension.monitoring.prometheus.enabled }}' to '{{- if not .Values.inferenceExtension.monitoring.prometheus.auth.enabled }}'. Before the fix, simply enabling Prometheus scraping (monitoring.prometheus.enabled=true) was sufficient to leave --metrics-endpoint-auth at its (implied) default of true/on regardless of whether the separate auth.enabled sub-value was actually set, i.e. auth state was wrongly tied to whether Prometheus monitoring was enabled at all rather than to a dedicated auth toggle. After the fix, metrics auth is disabled only when the chart's new nested monitoring.prometheus.auth.enabled value is explicitly false, decoupling 'is Prometheus scraping configured' from 'is bearer-token/mTLS auth on the metrics endpoint required' at the Helm-values level. `draft` (44 words)
- [[29-posts-tagged-with-engineering-litellm]] — 29 posts tagged with "engineering" | liteLLM — LiteLLM's own engineering blog index: carries the joint LiteLLM/Pfizer debugging account of a Redis configuration bug that cut Pfizer's gateway throughput ~48% with zero HTTP errors, plus named production users (Netflix, Lemonade, Rocket Money) and operating notes for running the proxy across 100+ pods. `review` (1740 words)
- [[a-cost-benefit-analysis-of-on-premise-large-language-model-deployment-breaking-e]] — A Cost-Benefit Analysis of On-Premise Large Language Model Deployment: Breaking Even with Commercial LLM Services — PRIMARY ACADEMIC SOURCE: arXiv:2509.18101 (Pan & Wang, Carnegie Mellon University, posted Aug 30 2025) -- 'A Cost-Benefit Analysis of On-Premise Large Language Model Deployment: Breaking Even with Commercial LLM Services.' This is almost certainly the exact source behind tianpan.co's claim of 'a peer-reviewed analysis of 54 LLM deployment scenarios' (the paper explicitly analyzes 54 model-API combinations: 9 open-source models x 6 commercial APIs) -- NOTE THE MISATTRIBUTION: this is an arXiv PREPRINT, not a peer-reviewed publication; tianpan.co's 'peer-reviewed' characterization appears to be inaccurate. Builds a formal TCO model: C_local(t) = C_hardware + C_electricity x t (hardware cost + monthly electricity x months) versus C_API(t) = linear API cost scaled to match the open model's token-generation capacity, solving for the break-even month t*. Covers 9 open models (Kimi-K2, GLM-4.5, Qwen3-235B as 'large'; gpt-oss-120B, GLM-4.5-Air, Llama-3.3-70B as 'medium'; EXAONE 4.0 32B, Qwen3-30B, Magistral Small as 'small') against 6 commercial APIs (GPT-5, Claude-4 Opus, Claude-4 Sonnet, Grok-4, Gemini 2.5 Pro). KEY FINDING, A FOURTH DISTINCT BREAK-EVEN FRAMEWORK DISAGREEING WITH THE REST OF THIS BATCH: break-even is expressed in MONTHS OF PAYBACK, not tokens/day -- small models break even in as little as 0.3 months (EXAONE 4.0 32B vs Claude-4 Opus) and at most 3.0 months even under unfavorable comparisons; medium models break even in 2.3-34.0 months depending on which commercial API is the comparator; large models break even in 3.5 months (Qwen3-235B vs Claude-4 Opus) up to a striking 69.3-108+ months (nearly 9 years) when compared against aggressively-priced providers like Gemini 2.5 Pro or GPT-5 -- explicitly because 'commercial provider pricing heterogeneity creates order-of-magnitude variations in deployment economics.' States on-premise deployment is 'economically viable...making it viable primarily for organizations with extreme high-volume processing requirements (>=50M tokens/month) or strict data residency mandates' -- this 50M tokens/month figure is a FIFTH distinct volume threshold in this corpus, and notably much lower than Azumo's 10B tokens/month claim, illustrating how far build-vs-buy break-even estimates diverge depending on methodology, model-size assumptions, and which commercial API is used as the cost baseline. Restricts its OpEx model to electricity only (explicitly excludes staffing, maintenance, networking, and failure costs 'to keep our cost model tractable,' and flags this omission as a limitation and direction for future research), meaning its break-even numbers are a FLOOR, not a complete picture, and are NOT directly comparable to the fully-loaded TCO figures (which include staff/on-call) that dominate the rest of this batch. Provides an online calculator at v0-ai-cost-calculator.vercel.app. LONG, DATA-DENSE SOURCE (~6,000 words, full methodology + 5 tables + 68 references) -- strong candidate for source-analyst follow-up given its load-bearing role as the apparent origin of the '54 scenarios' figure cited secondhand elsewhere in this batch. `review` (6076 words)
- [[a-system-for-microserving-of-llms]] — A SYSTEM FOR MICROSERVING OF LLMS — CMU/Tsinghua/Purdue/Amazon/NVIDIA paper (arXiv:2412.12488, Dec 2024, published later at MLSys) proposing 'LLM microserving,' a multi-level architecture that decomposes a coarse-grained request-level serving API into fine-grained, composable sub-request-level REST primitives (prep_recv, remote_send, start_generate) plus a unified KV-cache interface (kv.new_sequence, kv.fork_sequence, kv.mark_send, kv.attention, etc.) so that a single 'programmable router' — implemented as async Python code calling these primitives against a list of abstract 'engine' objects () — can reconfigure prefill/decode disaggregation, data parallelism, and novel hybrid strategies without restarting or reconfiguring the underlying serving system. This is architecturally close to what the user's query is asking for: the router's dispatch logic is written against an engine/backend abstraction (an object exposing prep_recv/remote_send/start_generate), completely decoupled from how those engine instances are deployed (the paper's own engines are bare processes on GPUs, not K8s pods) — swapping the backend substrate only requires the engine objects to expose the same three async methods, not a K8s-specific service-discovery layer. Evaluation: Llama 3.1 8B on single NVIDIA A100 SXM 40GB per engine, ShareGPT (mean input ~200/output ~260 tokens) and a synthetic long-context dataset (mean input 3000, output 100, std 5), comparing 1P1D, a novel 1P1D-balance (20% token boundary: last 20% of prefill tokens computed on the decode engine to redistribute load), and 1P2D disaggregation patterns implemented in ~ a few lines of router code, against vLLM v0.6.3.post1 (10-step scheduler, latency-optimized) as the baseline. Claims up to 47% reduction in job completion time (JCT) versus existing static disaggregation strategies via the new 1P1D-balance pattern, attributing the gain to fine-grained load rebalancing that a fixed-strategy router cannot express. This is the intellectual precursor cited/echoed by NVIDIA Dynamo's disaggregated-serving design (shared CMU/NVIDIA authorship: Zhihao Jia, Tianqi Chen affiliations include NVIDIA). `evergreen` (8016 words)
- [[a-year-in-llm-serving-workload-evolution-caching]] — A Year in LLM Serving: Workload Evolution, Caching — University of Chicago/Harvard researchers with engineers from Chutes (a decentralized multi-model LLM serving/compute marketplace, anonymized in the paper as 'CompanyX') release and analyze a full year (2025-04-11 to 2026-04-12) of unsampled, request-level production trace data: 6,122,413,756 requests, 35.8 billion input tokens, 2.52 billion output tokens, 314,970 users, 9,174 models (3,922 public, 5,252 private), 875,921 serving instances. Note: this is a multi-tenant cloud serving marketplace trace, not a single corporate internal platform like T-Tech's, but its findings generalize to internal multi-model fleets. Key measured findings: (1) output token lengths shrank over the year — monthly median output fell from a few hundred tokens early in the trace to below 100 tokens near the end, while input lengths stayed stable in the thousands of tokens, attributed to growing agentic/tool-call workloads replacing long free-text completions; (2) prefix-cache token hit ratios are bimodal (most requests see near-zero or near-complete reuse) and temporally concentrated — half of same-(user,model) repeat requests arrive within 0.1s, ~80% within 10s, and 99% of cache reuse occurs within 15 minutes of the prior request; (3) in cache-eviction simulation, simple FIFO/LRU policies match or beat more complex state-of-the-art eviction algorithms on LLM prefix-cache workloads, while ARC performs substantially worse than LRU at intermediate cache sizes; (4) the paper identifies and quantifies a fundamental load-balancing/cache-locality tension: routing to maximize prefix-cache hit ratio concentrates traffic on fewer instances (good for cache, bad for load balance), while spreading load across instances to balance utilization fragments the prefix cache and increases cache replication across the fleet — demonstrated with a DeepSeek-V3 case study comparing high-load (traffic spread across instances) vs low-load (traffic concentrated on one instance, higher cache hits) regimes over 60-minute windows. The production routing policy observed selects among least-loaded instances, then picks the one with highest expected prefix-cache hits. Cites vLLM, SGLang/RadixAttention, DistServe, Splitwise, Mooncake, Preble as related serving-optimization work. Trace released publicly at github.com/HarvardMadSys/chutes_workload. `review` (12368 words)
- [[ai-gateway-deep-dive-2026-architecture-product]] — AI Gateway Deep Dive (2026): Architecture, Product … — Long (9552-word, AI-assisted but curated) practitioner survey by Jimmy Song comparing the broader 'AI Gateway' product category -- a superset of the GIE/Envoy-AI-Gateway infrastructure-routing layer this batch focuses on, covering commercial and OSS products that add prompt/content governance on top of multi-LLM routing. Caveat: mid-2025 vintage per its own disclaimer, pre-dates Envoy AI Gateway v1.0 and current GIE v1 GA API names, so treat feature claims as dated against the other sources in this batch.

Core argument: traditional API gateways rate-limit by request count and are blind to token cost, non-deterministic/unsafe LLM outputs, multi-backend model routing, and stateful conversation context -- an AI Gateway extends gateway capability across four axes: security (authn/authz, PII masking, content review of both request and response), observability (token/cost usage tracking, logging/audit, OpenTelemetry integration), prompt engineering (RAG injection, prompt templates/decorators, semantic caching), and reliability (multi-model load balancing, automatic retry/failover, token-based rate limiting/quotas).

Comparison table names 9 products with license and technical foundation: Envoy AI Gateway (Apache v2, Envoy+Envoy Gateway, CNCF, Tetrate/Bloomberg-initiated), Apache APISIX AI Gateway (Apache License, Lua/OpenResty plugin 'ai-proxy'), Kong AI Gateway (Konnect, self-hostable, Lua+C, AI request/response transformer plugins), Solo.io Gloo AI Gateway (commercial closed-source enterprise product built on Envoy+Kubernetes Gateway API), F5 NGINX AI Gateway (commercial closed-source, security/compliance-focused with pluggable 'Processors'), Traefik Hub AI Gateway (commercial SaaS, Go, AIService CRDs), MLflow AI Gateway (Apache License, Databricks GenAI toolkit, Python, YAML-defined endpoints, simple internal unified LLM entry point), and Portkey AI Gateway (MIT License, JS/TS, 1600+ model/provider support, virtual-key hosting, semantic caching).

Explicitly names what generic/non-AI-aware gateways and even some AI gateways do NOT handle well, framed as open 'bottlenecks': immature ecosystems with advanced features often gated to paid enterprise tiers; non-unified LLM API specs causing vendor lock-in risk across gateway configs; added network-hop latency especially when content review/semantic matching calls external services synchronously; content-filtering circumvention (prompt injection, prompt leakage) that gateways cannot fully close, requiring model-side security in addition; growing operational/configuration-management complexity at multi-tenant, multi-environment scale; and an explicit statement that cost-benefit is NOT favorable for small-scale usage -- 'AI Gateway isn't a rigid need in all scenarios.'

Covers Solo.io's open-source split specifically relevant to this batch's governance question: Kagent (donated to CNCF, an agentic framework for autonomous K8s operations, MCP-compatible) versus KGateway (Solo's open-sourced Kubernetes-native Envoy-based gateway, in CNCF Sandbox, serving as the shared foundation under Solo's own commercial Gloo AI Gateway and Agent Gateway products) -- illustrating a recurring OSS-core/commercial-shell pattern among gateway vendors. Includes a worked internal-enterprise use case (financial company intelligent Q&A assistant) showing an AI Gateway doing complexity-based routing between a local fine-tuned model and GPT-4, PII anonymization before external calls, post-hoc content review, and per-department token-usage dashboards used to trigger approval workflows -- illustrating cost-attribution and audit-logging as capabilities bolted onto the gateway layer rather than the inference-serving layer. `review` (9552 words)
- [[ai-guardrails-implementing-safety-for-production-llm-apps]] — AI Guardrails: Implementing Safety for Production LLM Apps — Practitioner guide (BigDataBoutique, Itamar Syn-Hershko, June 2026, 15-min read) arguing LLM guardrails are a four-family layered defense architecture, not a single product, anchored on OWASP Top 10 for LLM Applications 2025 (LLM01 Prompt Injection, LLM02 Sensitive Info Disclosure, LLM06 Excessive Agency, LLM07 System Prompt Leakage), NIST AI 600-1 (July 2024), and the EU AI Act timeline (GPAI obligations 2 Aug 2025, high-risk obligations 2 Aug 2026). Taxonomy: input guardrails (prompt-injection classifiers like Prompt Guard 2 / Azure Prompt Shields, PII stripping via Microsoft Presidio, 5-50ms latency budget, dominated by small classifiers/regex/NER); output guardrails (toxicity/harm classifiers, PII redaction, JSON Schema/Pydantic validation, grounding checks, streaming requires a buffering-strategy decision); behavioral/dialog guardrails (NeMo Guardrails' Colang dialog policies, tool-use allowlists, the only layer that stops gradual multi-turn jailbreaks); retrieval/tool guardrails (document trust scoring against indirect/cross-domain prompt injection, tool parameter validators, sandboxed code execution) -- explicitly the 'agent-era addition' and non-negotiable for RAG/agent systems. Open-source stack comparison: Guardrails AI (composable Python validators + re-ask/fix-up loops + Guardrails Hub community registry, best for structured-output enforcement); NVIDIA NeMo Guardrails (Colang DSL orchestration, both Colang 1.0 and 2.0, parallel rails execution + OpenTelemetry tracing added recently); Meta Llama Guard 4 (12B multimodal safety classifier, pruned from Llama 4 Scout MoE, released Apr 30 2025, Apache 2.0, self-hostable) + Prompt Guard 2 (86M param injection/jailbreak classifier, 5-20ms, Apache 2.0); Microsoft Presidio (rule+ML PII/PHI detection across 30+ entity types, anonymize via replace/hash/mask/encrypt). States the common converged open-source stack explicitly: 'Presidio strips PII at ingress, Prompt Guard 2 detects injection inline, NeMo Guardrails orchestrates dialog policy, Llama Guard 4 classifies output safety, and a schema validator gates structured output' -- and explicitly frames this as composed layers, not one sufficient tool. Contrasts against cloud-native (Bedrock Guardrails incl. Automated Reasoning checks GA Aug 2025 claiming up to 99% validation accuracy; Azure Prompt Shields with 'Spotlighting' trusted/untrusted content separation; Google Model Armor GA in Security Command Center with GKE Service Extensions integration) and specialist vendors (Lakera Guard, 98%+ detection claimed, acquired by Check Point 2025; Cisco AI Defense, ex-Robust Intelligence; Protect AI). States most teams end up hybrid: cloud-native synchronous baseline + open-source model for custom policy/data residency + specialist vendor for red-teaming. Prescribes a four-layer reference architecture (pre-prompt / pre-inference / post-inference / post-action) enforced at an AI gateway (LiteLLM, Kong AI Gateway, Portkey), fail-closed for high-risk surfaces, and decision caching (30-60% cache hit rates reported in production, cutting latency and cost). Evaluation section: baseline eval set needs 200+ benign, 200+ adversarial (sourced from HarmBench/AdvBench plus own red-teaming), 50+ edge cases, version-controlled; automated red-teaming tools named: NVIDIA Garak (moved from leondz/garak), Microsoft PyRIT (multi-turn adversarial orchestration), promptfoo red-team mode (CI integration); target metrics: block recall >=95% for high-risk, block precision, false-refusal rate tracked weekly, latency overhead budget 100-300ms across all four layers; a four-layer stack on a 1000-token query commonly adds 2000-4000 tokens of overhead. Lists explicit anti-patterns: treating the system prompt as the guardrail (trivially bypassed), optimizing block recall without tracking false refusals, shipping without an eval set, set-and-forget policies, and logging raw user content (GDPR/exfiltration risk). `review` (2971 words)
- [[ai-inference-cost-economics-in-2026-gpu-finops-playbook-spheron-blog]] — AI Inference Cost Economics in 2026: GPU FinOps Playbook | Spheron Blog — PRIMARY SOURCE WITH A VENDOR INTEREST: Spheron is itself a GPU cloud rental platform, so this playbook has a direct commercial interest in presenting self-hosting-on-GPU-cloud (specifically Spheron) favorably against both managed inference APIs and against AWS -- a mirror-image vendor bias to BentoML/Azumo (those sell against self-hosting; Spheron sells self-hosting infrastructure). Confirms the core utilization claim Forte Group cites: a case study serving Llama 3.1 70B to an internal enterprise tool with 500 DAU, weekday 9am-6pm traffic, found average GPU utilization of 22% ('After' state); applying FP8 quantization (1.8x traffic on the same footprint) and continuous batching (utilization 22%->68%) plus moving batch jobs to spot and switching cloud providers cut monthly spend from ~9,100 to ~6,151 (59% reduction). Gives exact cost-per-million-token (CPM) formulas and a GPU pricing/throughput table (04 Apr 2026 Spheron rates): A100 80G 8x ~.67/M tokens, H100 SXM5 8x ~.90/M tokens (~2,800 tok/s for Llama 3.1 70B FP16 via vLLM), H200 8x ~.80/M, B200 8x ~.18/M; FP8 on H100 cuts CPM to ~/bin/bash.95-1.10/M. States continuous batching (PagedAttention) raises GPU utilization from a baseline of 15-30% to 60-80% at typical traffic patterns -- a 3-4x effective throughput improvement at the same GPU cost, corroborating the utilization-reality gap theme from an inference-infrastructure-vendor's perspective. Gives its own break-even framework: under 20M tokens/month managed APIs win on total cost including ops; above 100M tokens/month self-hosting almost always wins on unit economics; self-hosting breaks even at roughly 50-100M tokens/month for most 70B-class models once engineering overhead is included -- a THIRD distinct break-even band (50-100M tokens/month) that disagrees with tianpan.co's 100M+ tokens/month 'scale stage,' dev.to/rileykim's internally-contradictory 50M vs 500M tokens/day claims, and promptcost.org's 500K-2M tokens/day claims (which is a different unit: tokens/day vs tokens/month -- converting promptcost's range to monthly terms, ~15M-60M tokens/month, actually lands closer to Spheron's band than to the others, illustrating how unit confusion alone drives much of the apparent disagreement in this corpus). Compares Spheron (9.20/hr for an 8x H100 SXM5 pod) against AWS p5.48xlarge (~5.04/hr for the same GPU count) and Runpod (~1.52/hr), with AWS adding ~00/month egress fees per 10TB -- while flagging this is itself a vendor-favorable comparison. `review` (4130 words)
- [[aibrix-towards-scalable-cost-effective-large]] — AIBrix: Towards Scalable, Cost-Effective Large — AIBrix (ByteDance, arXiv 2504.03648, Feb 2025) is a cloud-native open-source LLM inference control/data-plane built on vLLM, offering a comparator system to llm-d. Its LLM-aware gateway extends Envoy Gateway with six selectable routing strategies: random, throughput, least-request, least-kv-cache (lowest average KV cache usage), least-latency, and prefix-cache-aware (prioritizes instances with reusable prefix cache above a hit-rate threshold); AIBrix reports this reduces mean latency by 19.2% and P99 latency by 79% versus baseline routing, and is co-developing with Google's gateway-api-inference-extension project (the same upstream llm-d's EPP builds on). Its distributed, disaggregated DRAM-based KV cache pool enables cross-engine/cross-node KV reuse via scan-resistant eviction, asynchronous metadata updates, and shared-memory cache-engine colocation; on the Bird-SQL benchmark (4xA10 GPUs) combining it with vLLM's native prefix caching improved throughput by ~50%, cut average/P99 TTFT by ~60%/~70%, and cut average/P99 ITL by ~30%/~70% versus vLLM prefix caching alone. Notes vLLM v0.7.1 constraint: MLA (used by DeepSeek-R1) requires prefix caching disabled. Also describes LoRA-aware routing, KV-cache-utilization-driven autoscaling, and hybrid Kubernetes+Ray orchestration. `evergreen` (4550 words)
- [[aibrix-towards-scalable-cost-effective-large-2]] — AIBrix: Towards Scalable, Cost-Effective Large — AIBrix's academic white paper (arXiv:2504.03648, 'AIBrix: Towards Scalable, Cost-Effective Large Language Model Inference Infrastructure', submitted 22 Feb 2025, 'The AIBrix Team', maintainers@aibrix.ai). 25 named authors, 24 affiliated with ByteDance (footnote 1); two explicitly marked as ByteDance interns from University of Michigan (Shuowei Jin) and University of Illinois at Urbana-Champaign (Gangmuk Lim); one author (Kante Yin) affiliated with DaoCloud -- this is the primary-source confirmation of AIBrix's founding/governance claim: overwhelmingly ByteDance, with academic internship and one external-company contributor, not a broad multi-stakeholder foundation.\n\nThe Related Works section is the paper's own explicit layer-boundary argument: it places AIBrix against two prior categories and states why each is insufficient for LLM serving. Microservice frameworks (Knative, Istio) are 'not designed for GPU-based inference workloads' -- 'circuit-breaker-based rate limiting in Knative is incompatible with LLM's token-based inference constraints, and QPS-based autoscaling cannot accurately capture GPU-bound resource usage patterns such as KV cache memory pressure.' Traditional ML-serving frameworks (KServe, RayServe) 'lack deep integration with LLM inference engines' and 'do not provide specialized optimizations for efficient batch scheduling, KV cache coordination, or heterogeneous GPU utilization' -- this is AIBrix's own documented evidence of the capability gap these established platforms left unfilled before AIBrix (and the comparable llm-d) existed.\n\nTable 1 gives exact benchmarked numbers (Bird-SQL benchmark, 4x NVIDIA A10 GPUs) for AIBrix's distributed KV cache layered onto three vLLM baseline configurations: vs vLLM Default, +129.34% decoding throughput, 73.08% avg TTFT reduction, 78.80% P99 TTFT reduction, 52.51% avg ITL reduction, 73.80% P99 ITL reduction; vs vLLM Chunked Prefill, +82.40% decoding throughput, 50.34% avg TTFT reduction; vs vLLM Prefix Caching, +51.64% decoding throughput, 65.06% avg TTFT reduction, 77.26% P99 TTFT reduction. (Earlier blog posts rounded these to '50%/60-70%' -- this paper is the tabular source of the precise percentages.) Autoscaling (KPA/APA vs native HPA): 11.5% latency reduction, 11.4% token throughput increase, 33% fewer scaling oscillations. Routing strategy selection: 19.2% mean latency reduction, 79% P99 latency reduction. States AIBrix's control plane 'does not build any abstractions for base models, it still leverage kubernetes deployment for basic lifecycle operations' -- a direct admission of scope limitation. Cites DistServe (Zhong et al., OSDI'24), PagedAttention/vLLM (Kwon et al., SOSP'23), Melange (Griggs et al.) and QLM (Patke et al.) as the academic lineage behind its heterogeneous-serving and disaggregation design. `evergreen` (4563 words)
- [[ais-impact-on-banking-use-cases-for-credit-scoring-and-fraud-detection]] — AI’s impact on banking: use cases for credit scoring and fraud detection — ECB Banking Supervision newsletter (20 Nov 2025) on a 2025 supervisory exercise: workshops with 13 significant European banks on AI use for credit scoring and fraud detection, based on annual STE (short-term exercise) data collection from 107-110 significant institutions. Explicitly states 'No banks reported using GenAI in the context of credit scoring, citing challenges such as development time, cost and trustworthiness' and that 'revolutionary techniques like GenAI have not yet been adopted in fraud detection use cases by any bank participating in the exercise' -- these two specific regulated-sector use cases run on decision trees/gradient boosting/neural nets, not LLMs. Findings are anonymized and aggregated ('should not be generalised for the entire sector'); no bank is named. `review` (1858 words)
- [[api-overview-kubernetes-gateway-api-inference-extension]] — API Overview - Kubernetes Gateway API Inference Extension — GIE concepts/api-overview doc. Confirms the extension is built on Envoy's External Processing Protocol (ext-proc), extending any gateway supporting both ext-proc and Gateway API (named examples: Envoy Gateway, kgateway, GKE Gateway) into an Inference Gateway. States InferencePool 'represents a set of Inference-focused Pods' and is explicitly positioned as a drop-in replacement for a Kubernetes Service ('you'd replace a Kubernetes Service with an InferencePool') -- reinforcing Pod-only selector semantics at the spec level. Introduces InferencePoolImport: a cluster-local, CONTROLLER-managed (not user-authored) representation of an InferencePool imported from another cluster, populated by a multi-cluster controller, allowing traffic to be routed to model servers running in a remote cluster -- this is the one documented mechanism by which InferencePool-adjacent routing can reach endpoints outside the local cluster's Pod set, worth investigating further as a potential integration seam for non-k8s backends. Also notes self-hosted models are meant to be composed with model-as-a-service providers via higher-level AI Gateways (LiteLLM, Gloo AI Gateway, Apigee) sitting above the Inference Gateway -- i.e., non-k8s/external providers are explicitly pushed to a different layer (an AI Gateway), not into InferencePool itself. `evergreen` (346 words)
- [[api-reference-agent-router]] — API Reference | Agent Router — Full CRD API reference (v1.1, project renamed to Agent Router / Agentic AI Foundation, formerly Envoy AI Gateway) for aigateway.envoyproxy.io v1alpha1/v1beta1. Load-bearing for the layered-backend question: AIGatewayRouteRuleBackendRef.BackendRefs can point at EITHER an AIServiceBackend (default; group=aigateway.envoyproxy.io) OR an InferencePool (group inference.networking.k8s.io) from Gateway API Inference Extension -- but only one InferencePool is allowed per rule, InferencePool cannot be mixed with AIServiceBackend refs in the same rule, and 'Fallback behavior is handled by the InferencePool's endpoint picker' whereas AIServiceBackend fallback is done via Envoy Gateway's BackendTrafficPolicy (failover/retry). The backend-level 'priority' field (sets Envoy upstream priority, overrides Envoy Gateway's 'failover' property) is explicitly 'ignored when referencing InferencePool resources' -- confirming InferencePool backends are opaque to the gateway's own priority/fallback mechanics. AIServiceBackendSpec.BackendRef today must be an Envoy Gateway Backend resource; the doc notes 'k8s Service will be supported as a backend in the future' citing envoyproxy/ai-gateway issue #902 -- i.e., even the external/self-hosted (non-InferencePool) backend path is still k8s-object-shaped today. BackendSecurityPolicy covers auth to external managed providers (API key, AWS IAM/STS, Anthropic, Azure AD/API key, GCP credentials, OIDC). Long/dense reference (~18k words) -- flagged for possible source-analyst follow-up on the full CRD field list. `evergreen` (18117 words)
- [[awq-quantization-guide-deploy-llms-at-half-the-gpu-cost-2026-spheron-blog]] — AWQ Quantization Guide: Deploy LLMs at Half the GPU Cost (2026) | Spheron Blog — Vendor engineering blog from Spheron (a GPU-rental/cloud provider), dated content current to April 2026. Despite the hardware-rental framing, it contains a specific, checkable claim about the quantization TOOLCHAIN that is directly on-topic: AutoAWQ (casper-hansen/AutoAWQ), the original reference implementation for running AWQ quantization, "was archived in May 2025 and is no longer maintained," and the post names vllm-project/llm-compressor as "the recommended replacement" for ongoing support -- i.e., the vLLM project's own compressor has effectively become the maintained successor toolchain for producing AWQ (and other) quantized checkpoints, not just a runtime consumer of them. The post frames AWQ as "the default INT4 format for production inference" as of 2026, with major model families shipping pre-quantized AWQ checkpoints on Hugging Face and vLLM, SGLang, and TensorRT-LLM all shipping optimized AWQ kernels (vLLM: `--quantization awq` flag auto-loads AWQ weights; SGLang auto-detects AWQ from the HF config; TensorRT-LLM supports AWQ via NVIDIA's `modelopt` toolkit with a more involved build pipeline but reportedly higher peak throughput).

Software-side comparative claims (self-reported/aggregated, not independently verified by this source): AWQ INT4 gives "1-3% quality degradation on most tasks" vs BF16, slightly better than GPTQ INT4's "2-5% degradation" at the same bit-width, attributed to AWQ's activation-aware calibration vs. GPTQ's layer-wise Hessian-based reconstruction; a worked accuracy table cites Llama 3.3 70B MMLU dropping ~85% to ~83% (-2%) and HumanEval ~72% to ~70% (-3%) under AWQ INT4, and Mistral 7B WikiText-2 perplexity rising from ~5.25 to ~5.40 (+2.9%) -- explicitly flagged by the source itself as "community-sourced estimates," not an official/MLPerf benchmark. The piece explicitly recommends validating AWQ quality on reasoning/math-heavy tasks before production use, since those degrade more (3-5%) than instruction-following or summarization (1-3%). It also distinguishes AWQ from bitsandbytes NF4 (used for QLoRA fine-tuning, not calibration-based, not optimized for inference quality) and from vendor-trained QAT checkpoints (e.g., a cited Gemma 4 QAT release), which it says outperform post-hoc AWQ at the same bit-width because "error compensation is trained into the weights rather than patched on top." Flags common operational pitfalls: forgetting the `--quantization awq` flag silently falls back to BF16; mismatched `q_group_size` (standard is 128) produces garbage output; domain-mismatched calibration data (128-512 samples) degrades quality on the target distribution; AWQ's throughput benefit shrinks or reverses on compute-bound (large-batch, small-model) workloads due to dequantization overhead. `review` (4056 words)
- [[awq-activation-aware-weight-quantization-for]] — AWQ: ACTIVATION-AWARE WEIGHT QUANTIZATION FOR — AWQ (Activation-aware Weight Quantization), Lin, Tang, Tang, et al., MIT/NVIDIA/MIT-IBM, MLSys 2024 Best Paper (arXiv:2306.00978v6). Proposes a post-training, weight-only low-bit (INT4/INT3) quantization method for LLMs that requires no backpropagation or reconstruction. Key insight: roughly 0.1-1% of weight channels are "salient" and disproportionately affect output quality; salience is identified by activation magnitude (not weight magnitude), and AWQ protects salient channels by per-channel scaling (scale weights up, scale corresponding activations down by the same factor) before quantizing everything uniformly to INT4/INT3 -- avoiding the hardware-inefficient mixed-precision kernels that a naive "keep salient weights in FP16" approach would require. Because it does no reconstruction against a calibration set, the paper argues AWQ generalizes better out-of-domain than GPTQ, whose Hessian-based reconstruction can overfit the calibration set.

Measured accuracy: on OPT-6.7B under INT3 with group size 128, naive round-to-nearest gives perplexity 43.2; keeping the top 1% salient weights in FP16 (mixed precision) drops this to 13.0; AWQ's scaling approach matches that 13.0 PPL without mixed precision. On Wikitext-2, AWQ beats both plain GPTQ and GPTQ-with-reordering on LLaMA and Llama-2 models at INT4-g128. On a multimodal benchmark (OpenFlamingo-9B on COCO captioning, 32-shot), quantization-induced degradation drops from 4.57 (baseline PTQ approaches) to 1.17 under INT4-g128 -- "4x model size reduction with negligible performance loss" is the paper's own characterization, not an independent audit.

System contribution: TinyChat, a 4-bit-native inference engine (on-device/edge-focused, built for Jetson Orin Nano, laptop/mobile GPUs, Raspberry Pi) using kernel fusion and platform-aware weight packing to turn AWQ's ~4x memory reduction into "3.2-3.3x average speedup" over the HuggingFace FP16 baseline across model families, and to make Llama-2-70B deployable on a single Jetson Orin (64GB). This is explicitly an edge/on-device framing, distinct from the data-center server-inference stacks (vLLM, SGLang, TensorRT-LLM) that later adopted the AWQ format.

Ecosystem adoption claimed by the paper (2026 revision): AWQ is used by HuggingFace Transformers, NVIDIA TensorRT-LLM, Microsoft DirectML, Google Vertex AI, Intel Neural Compressor, Amazon SageMaker, AMD, FastChat, vLLM, and LMDeploy, and is credited with enabling Falcon-180B on a single H200 GPU. Code: github.com/mit-han-lab/llm-awq. `review` (10607 words)
- [[about-gke-inference-gateway-powered-by-llm-d-gke-networking-google-cloud-documen]] — About GKE Inference Gateway powered by llm-d  |  GKE networking  |  Google Cloud Documentation — Google's ground-truth doc on GKE Inference Gateway 'powered by llm-d' confirms the llm-d Router architecture: an L7 Proxy (Envoy/GKE Gateway) that calls a separate llm-d Endpoint Picker (EPP) service via the Envoy ext-proc protocol per request, then forwards directly to the chosen Pod -- Gateway (data plane) and EPP (routing intelligence) are decoupled processes. InferencePool is explicitly defined as 'a group of Pods that share the same compute configuration'; it supports up to 8 targetPorts, and each port in each zone generates a dedicated Network Endpoint Group (NEG), capped at 50 NEGs per GCP Backend Service -- e.g. an 8-port pool across 3 zones (24 NEGs) can only be aggregated from 2 clusters before hitting the limit, a concrete multi-cluster scaling ceiling. Also documents EPP scoring signals (prefix-cache match, KV-cache/queue load, LoRA-adapter affinity), the numeric Priority/Criticality field (values <0 dropped first, returning 429), and that streaming responses are never retried on error. `evergreen` (2747 words)
- [[accelerate-multi-turn-llm-workloads-on-openshift-ai-with-llm-d-intelligent-routi]] — Accelerate multi-turn LLM workloads on OpenShift AI with llm-d intelligent routing | Red Hat Developer — Red Hat Developer hands-on benchmark (Jan 13, 2026) comparing vanilla vLLM (4 replicas, round-robin K8s Service) vs. llm-d intelligent routing on a multi-turn conversational workload, using a custom multi-turn benchmarking tool (rh-aiservices-bu/multi-turn-benchmarking) on OpenShift AI 3.0 with 4x NVIDIA L4 GPUs. Round-robin vLLM: P50 TTFT 123ms, P95 TTFT 727-745ms, P99 TTFT 814-841ms, prefix-cache speedup only 1.70-1.79x, KV cache hit rate ~62%, imbalanced GPU utilization. llm-d intelligent routing: P50 TTFT 92ms (25% faster), P95 TTFT 237-272ms (63% faster), P99 TTFT ~700-674ms (20% faster), cache speedup 3.84-3.95x (2.1x better), KV cache hit rate ~90% (45% improvement), balanced GPU utilization, no latency spikes. States the mechanism plainly: 'llm-d addresses this problem directly with KV-cache-aware routing, ensuring follow-up turns land on replicas that already contain the relevant context' since round-robin 'destroy[s] KV cache locality.' Explicitly caveats results as single-node 4xL4 demonstration, not production-grade benchmarks. `evergreen` (2434 words)
- [[add-initial-gateway-api-inference-extension-support-issue-10411-kgateway-devkgat]] — Add Initial Gateway API Inference Extension Support · Issue #10411 · kgateway-dev/kgateway — Closed kgateway (formerly Gloo Edge/k8sgateway) meta-issue #10411 (Dec 2024 - Sep 2025) scoping initial GIE support, confirming kgateway's implementation is fully Kubernetes-CRD-driven: it adds controllers to reconcile InferencePool CRs, an ext-proc server deployment managed by kgateway's 'deployer' package, and translator/proxy_syncer logic that turns GIE CRs into Envoy proxy config — auto-enabled only when the InferencePool CRDs exist in-cluster. No standalone or non-k8s mode is scoped anywhere in this issue. Two details are directly relevant to the header-override/ORIGINAL_DST routing question: (1) a struck-through line notes an abandoned plan to 'Implement Override Host LB policy' referencing Envoy PR #38757 and GIE PR #761, explicitly 'no longer being pursued since the Envoy-based data plane is being deprecated in v2.1' — i.e., kgateway considered and dropped an Envoy load-balancer-level override-host mechanism for honoring EPP destinations; (2) a tracked follow-up item for 'failureMode' support, confirming fail-open/fail-closed configurability was treated as a distinct, later-scoped feature rather than baseline behavior. kgateway is moving off its Envoy-based data plane in v2.1, which undercuts any recommendation to reuse kgateway's Envoy config approach going forward. `evergreen` (1385 words)
- [[add-standalone-agentgateway-proxy-support-by-danehans-pull-request-2882-kubernet]] — Add standalone agentgateway proxy support by danehans · Pull Request #2882 · kubernetes-sigs/gateway-api-inference-extension · GitHub — GIE PR #2882 (danehans, opened Apr 24 2026, fixes issue #2883, approved) adds standalone Helm chart support for running agentgateway as the EPP sidecar proxy: 'Adds standalone Helm chart support for using agentgateway as the EPP sidecar proxy. This includes proxy preset selection, agentgateway validation, generated service-backed agentgateway local config, a generated model Service, release-tooling tag replacement, and standalone guide updates.' User-facing change: 'The standalone Helm chart can now run agentgateway as the sidecar proxy by setting inferenceExtension.sidecar.proxyType=agentgateway.' This is the concrete, mergeable GIE-side chart change that wires up agentgateway's PR #1658/#1429 passthrough destinationMode work into the official standalone-EPP Helm chart -- confirming 'proxyType=agentgateway' is now a first-class, officially supported sidecar option for standalone EPP deployments, alongside the earlier Envoy-based default. Testing was done live via a kind e2e cluster using the vLLM simulator and a patched agentgateway image built from PR #1658, directly cross-linking this PR to the destination-trust/passthrough work as its validation vehicle. Notably still generates 'a generated model Service' -- i.e. even this agentgateway-based standalone chart path still creates a Kubernetes Service object for the model backend, reinforcing that full non-k8s backend support requires further work beyond this chart (a Service is still the addressing anchor). `evergreen` (2152 words)
- [[add-standalone-inference-routing-local-config-by-danehans-pull-request-1429-agen]] — Add standalone inference routing local config by danehans · Pull Request #1429 · agentgateway/agentgateway · GitHub — agentgateway PR #1429 (merged, danehans/solo.io, part of umbrella issue #620) adds local static-config support for standalone inference routing on service backends, ships a user-facing examples/standalone-epp config example, and deliberately keeps standalone inference routing fail-closed 'for now' (i.e., if the EPP call fails, the request is rejected rather than falling back to a default backend -- the safer default prior to #1658's later passthrough/trust work). Maintainer howardjohn pushed back on adding a separate LocalInferenceRouting-vs-InferenceRouting split meant to prevent inference routing on non-Service backend types, noting agentgateway already permits inference routing on non-Service types elsewhere (e.g. LocalRouteBackend) and that guarding it in only one place added ~190 LOC for '0.01% of people' -- danehans agreed to keep the old (permissive) behavior instead of adding the guard. Author's stated next steps included documenting 'how this fits into the upstream GIE EPP standalone chart' and documenting required Kubernetes deps such as 'model workloads require a k8s Service' -- direct evidence that even standalone/non-pod routing in this design still assumes a k8s Service object exists for the backend group, not necessarily individual pod endpoints. `evergreen` (2235 words)
- [[amend-the-endpoint-picker-protocol-to-support-multiple-fallback-endpoints-by-wbp]] — Amend the endpoint picker protocol to support multiple fallback endpoints by wbpcode · Pull Request #761 · kubernetes-sigs/gateway-api-inference-extension · GitHub — GIE PR #761 (wbpcode, opened Apr 30 2025, part of issue #414) amends the endpoint-picker-protocol to change the x-gateway-destination-endpoint header value format from a single ip:port to a LIST of endpoints, making the previous single-endpoint format a special case of the new list format. Author's stated rationale, in direct response to reviewing the Envoy dynamic_forwarding LB policy work (envoyproxy/envoy#38757): the existing design only supported one fallback endpoint, and if EPP needs to communicate multiple fallback candidates, it is cleaner to send one ordered list in one header than to split primary/fallback across two headers or metadata entries -- 'from the proxy's perspective, I will prefer only one source to get the external specified endpoints... the proxy could iterate the endpoints list in order and select the first valid one. If the retrying happens, the proxy could continue the iteration from the position where it stopped last time.' This simplifies both EPP and proxy implementations by eliminating the need to handle multiple endpoint-source encodings. A key open design question raised by reviewer LiorLieberman: how does an ordered-endpoint-list header interact with proxy implementations that use Envoy subsets (cited: an internal Istio implementation doc of this same endpoint-picker API) -- i.e. subset-based routing and explicit destination-list routing are two different mechanisms for reaching a picked endpoint and may not compose cleanly. This PR is the header-protocol-level precursor to the Envoy dynamic_forwarding LB policy consuming that list. `evergreen` (4998 words)
- [[announcing-kserve-v017-production-ready-llm-serving-with-llminferenceservice-kse]] — Announcing KServe v0.17 - Production-Ready LLM Serving with LLMInferenceService | KServe — KServe v0.17 release notes (published March 13, 2026; KServe joined CNCF) announcing LLMInferenceService reaching production readiness, 'built on the llm-d framework.' This is the single strongest documented case of a downstream product reusing llm-d's EPP as-is rather than wrapping it: the LLMInferenceService controller auto-creates a full stack including InferencePool, InferenceModel, and 'EPP (Endpoint Picker Pod)' -- integrating Gateway Inference Extension (GIE) v1.3.0 and citing the llm-d-inference-scheduler repo directly for its plugin architecture. Gives the exact EndpointPickerConfig CRD shape used to configure the EPP plugin pipeline (plugins: single-profile-handler, prefix-cache-scorer, load-aware-scorer with a 'threshold' parameter; schedulingProfiles with weighted pluginRefs, e.g. prefix-cache-scorer weight 2.0, load-aware-scorer weight 1.0, max-score-picker) and shows it can be supplied inline in the LLMInferenceService spec or referenced from a ConfigMap for platform-wide standardization. Documents that KV-cache tracking works via ZMQ events (BlockStored, BlockRemoved) building an index mapping {ModelName, BlockHash} -> {PodID, DeviceTier}. Also covers disaggregated P/D via NixlConnector/RDMA-RoCE, TP/DP/EP parallelism via LeaderWorkerSet, Envoy AI Gateway token-based rate limiting, the Workload Variant Autoscaler (WVA, from github.com/llm-d/llm-d-workload-variant-autoscaler) with cost-aware variantCost scaling, EPP horizontal scaling/HA support, and the new modular Helm chart split (kserve core / llmisvc controller / localmodel) across 10 independent charts as of this release. `evergreen` (2486 words)
- [[announcing-the-llm-d-community-llm-d]] — Announcing the llm-d community! | llm-d — llm-d.ai founding blog post 'Announcing the llm-d community' (banner notes v0.9 was current at fetch time) frames llm-d as 'a Kubernetes-native high-performance distributed LLM inference framework' built as a layered stack on vLLM + Kubernetes + Inference Gateway (IGW/GIE), explicitly co-designed and integrated with IGW rather than a standalone product. States design principles of Operationalizability ('modular and resilient architecture with native integration into Kubernetes via Inference Gateway API'), Flexibility (cross-platform NVIDIA/TPU/AMD/Intel with 'extensible implementations of key composable layers of the stack'), and Performance. Names the 'vLLM Optimized Inference Scheduler' as llm-d's key contribution to IGW's Endpoint Picker Protocol (EPP) pattern -- implementing filtering/scoring for disaggregated serving, prefix-cache-awareness, and load-awareness, while explicitly inviting customization: 'Advanced teams can also tweak or implement their own scorers and filterers to further customize for their use cases, while still benefiting from upcoming operational features in the inference gateway, like flow control and latency-aware balancing.' This is the earliest public articulation of llm-d's own stated extensibility point being the EPP's scorer/filterer plugin layer, not the proxy/router layer itself -- i.e., the founding design assumed customization happens inside the EPP, with the proxy (Envoy/gateway) treated as a fixed IGW-compliant component. Also documents llm-d's disaggregated-serving (P/D) and disaggregated prefix-caching designs via vLLM's pluggable KV Connector API (NVIDIA NIXL transport), and a planned traffic/hardware-aware variant autoscaler drawing on AIBrix collaboration. Notably, this founding post makes NO mention of non-Kubernetes deployment modes, standalone EPP, or static/file-based discovery -- those concepts (per releases-llm-dllm-d and GIE issue #1838) emerged later (standalone EPP issue opened Nov 2025; llm-d default-to-standalone-mode UX change shipped May 2026), confirming non-k8s-backend support was NOT part of llm-d's original architecture and was added as the project matured in response to adoption friction. `evergreen` (2188 words)
- [[application-gateway-for-containers-inference-gateway-microsoft-learn]] — Application Gateway for Containers - Inference gateway | Microsoft Learn — Microsoft Learn docs for Azure Application Gateway for Containers' inference gateway (preview, last updated 2026-06-24) -- an implementation of GIE. Lists current v1 resources: InferencePool (backend resource: pod group + EPP reference), InferenceObjective (the v1 successor to the earlier InferenceModel CRD; represents per-request priority objectives shared across an InferencePool via header x-gateway-inference-objective), Endpoint Picker (EPP, customer-provided, scores candidate pods via pluggable scorers -- queue depth, KV cache utilization, prefix-cache affinity -- and returns the selected endpoint), and Body-Based Routing (BBR, a managed request processor that extracts the `model` field from an OpenAI-compatible JSON body and injects it as header `X-Gateway-Model-Name` for HTTPRoute matching). States InferencePool 'includes a pod selector, target port information, and an endpoint picker reference' -- reconfirms Pod-only selector semantics. Documents both EPP failure modes: FailOpen (gateway falls back to standard endpoint selection if EPP is unavailable/unresponsive -- preserves availability, may reduce routing quality) and FailClose (gateway rejects the request instead -- prevents routing without the required decision). CRITICAL SCOPE STATEMENT (verbatim): 'The inference gateway focuses on self-hosted inference workloads running on Kubernetes. Routing directly to public or managed model provider endpoints is outside the scope of this integration.' This is a vendor implementation's explicit statement that GIE/InferencePool targets self-hosted, cluster-local model servers -- not external/managed model providers -- reinforcing that a non-pod backend must still present itself as a Kubernetes-addressable, self-hosted-style endpoint to fit the model, even if the underlying compute is not a literal Pod. `evergreen` (1855 words)
- [[architecture-llm-d]] — Architecture | llm-d — llm-d top-level architecture overview (v0.9). Defines InferencePool as a label-selector grouping of Model Server pods for the same base model, serving as the router's discovery target, with 'Variant' as a pod-label-based sub-grouping (prefill/decode role, cost/perf profile) rather than a dedicated CRD. Lists advanced patterns built on the core Router/InferencePool/Model-Server trio: KV-cache management (prefix-aware routing, KV-indexer, KV offloading), disaggregated P/D serving, latency-predictor sidecar routing, batch inference (Batch Gateway + Async Processor), and autoscaling (HPA/KEDA using EPP-exported metrics like queue depth, or the Workload Variant Autoscaler). `evergreen` (602 words)
- [[arize-phoenix-or-arize-ax-phoenix]] — Arize Phoenix or Arize AX - Phoenix — Arize's own FAQ contrasting Phoenix (OSS) with Arize AX (commercial): states explicitly that Phoenix has 'no closed core and no paywalled tier' — 'no Phoenix feature is gated by the commercial offering, and Phoenix will never decline to build a feature because Arize AX offers it.' This is a direct contrast to Langfuse's ee/-folder gating. Comparison table: Phoenix is self-hosted on 'your laptop, your cloud, your air-gapped network,' free, with your team provisioning/scaling and community support; Arize AX is fully managed SaaS (self-hosting available only under a commercial license) with auto-scaling and enterprise SLAs. Both share the same OpenTelemetry + OpenInference instrumentation core, so traces can be pointed at either destination (or both) without re-instrumenting. Explicitly recommends Phoenix for 'the most regulated, air-gapped environments' where 'a commercial offering is largely off the table.' `review` (1105 words)

- [[atlassians-ai-gateway-best-in-class-model-garden-inside-atlassian]] — Atlassian’s AI Gateway: Best in Class Model Garden - Inside Atlassian — Atlassian engineering blog (Aug 22 2025), companion piece to the Inference Engine post, describing a separate component: the AI Gateway, a vendor-abstraction/routing layer (distinct from the self-hosted Inference Engine) that gives a single consistent API contract across third-party model providers (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, Meta) plus Atlassian-hosted models. Built in-house by the AI & ML Platform team, with an accompanying SDK and CLI for integration. - [[atlassians-inference-engine-our-self-hosted-ai-inference-service-inside-atlassia]] — Atlassian’s Inference Engine, our self-hosted AI inference service - Inside Atlassian — Atlassian engineering blog (Jul 22 2025) on 'Atlassian's Inference Engine,' a self-hosted inference platform built after off-the-shelf (third-party API) hosting hit latency SLOs, vendor lock-in, and pricing-control problems. Stated results: up to 40% P90 latency reduction (LLM, vs 3rd-party hosting) and >60% cost reduction for LLM workloads; 63% P90 latency reduction and >80% cost reduction for non-LLM workloads; a specific example of 72% latency reduction for query-rewriting LLM workloads via model/instance sizing, gRPC, and client-side tokenization. Runs on KITT (Atlassian's internal Kubernetes platform) with Kubernetes, Karpenter (node autoprovisioning), ArgoCD (GitOps), Helm, and Kustomize for deployment — explicitly OSS/off-the-shelf, chosen over FluxCD/plain-YAML-CI (less control/auditability) and over Cluster Autoscaler (Karpenter has faster provisioning for heterogeneous GPU types). Inference backend: NVIDIA Triton Inference Server with TensorRT-LLM and vLLM as backends — vLLM praised for batching performance and run-time deployability of new models; TensorRT-LLM customization credited for the 72% query-rewriting latency win. Routing: Istio ingress (chosen over Linkerd/NGINX for retry/observability/traffic-splitting control); autoscaling via KEDA (chosen over HPA/cron scale-ups for clean Prometheus-metric integration); observability via Prometheus + SignalFx. A control plane 'Optimization Service' compiles/tunes/benchmarks uploaded model artifacts (GPU-provisioned via Karpenter) — this compilation/optimization service itself is described as custom-built, as is the overall Inference Engine; gRPC + client-side tokenization are called out as in-house optimization techniques layered onto the OSS Triton/TensorRT-LLM stack. The piece is vendor-authored marketing-adjacent content (Atlassian's own 'How We Build' blog) so cost/latency percentages are self-reported without third-party verification. `review` (2274 words)
- [[automated-dynamic-ai-inference-scaling-on]] — Automated Dynamic AI Inference Scaling on — MIND '25 workshop paper (Trappen, Keßler, Pabel, Achter, Wesner; Ruhr Univ. Bochum / Univ. of Cologne), arXiv 2511.21413, describing a production system on the RAMSES supercomputer that bridges Kubernetes and Slurm for LLM inference, analogous to (but independent of) llm-d. Architecture: a Kubernetes-hosted 'Web Gateway' (OpenAI-API-compatible, bearer-token auth cached in a distributed memory cache) looks up available vLLM endpoints from a central PostgreSQL table (ai_model_endpoints) rather than from Kubernetes Pod objects, and forwards requests to a matching node; a long-running 'Job Worker' background process is the bridge -- it reconciles the ai_model_endpoint_jobs table against ai_model_configurations, submits/removes vLLM-serving Slurm jobs accordingly, and is the sole component that talks to Slurm. Apptainer is the container runtime for HPC compatibility. Reported benchmark: the architecture scales to 100/500/1000 concurrent requests with only ~500ms added end-to-end latency overhead versus direct vLLM access. This validates a 'discovery via external database/registry populated by a bridge process' pattern as a real, benchmarked precedent for exactly the kind of custom (non-K8s-pod) EndpointDiscovery plugin a customized llm-d-router would need -- though this system is a bespoke gateway, not built on llm-d/GIE, so it offers an architecture pattern rather than reusable llm-d-router code. `evergreen` (5745 words)
- [[automatic-prefix-caching-vllm]] — Automatic Prefix Caching - vLLM — vLLM design doc for Automatic Prefix Caching (APC), the v1 hash-based KV block reuse mechanism llm-d's routing decisions are built on top of. Each KV cache block is hashed as hash(parent_hash, block_tokens, extra_hashes) where extra_hashes covers LoRA IDs, multimodal input hashes, and cache_salt for multi-tenant isolation. Only full blocks are cached (default block size 16 tokens). As of v0.11 the default hash algorithm is sha256 (collision-resistant); vllm serve --prefix-caching-hash-algo also supports sha256_cbor (reproducible/cross-language), xxhash and xxhash_cbor (faster, non-cryptographic, collision risk). Describes the KVCacheBlock data structure (block pool, doubly-linked free queue for O(1) LRU operations), the scheduler workflow for get_computed_blocks()/allocate_slots(), block allocation/free/LRU-eviction semantics, and a worked example tracing block hits/evictions across four requests. Cache salting via a per-request cache_salt field injected into the first block's hash prevents timing-based side-channel attacks that could otherwise let an attacker infer cached content across tenants. `evergreen` (2226 words)
- [[automatic-prefix-caching-vllm-2]] — Automatic Prefix Caching - vLLM — vLLM's official Automatic Prefix Caching (APC) feature docs, the foundational mechanism llm-d's prefix-cache-aware routing depends on: APC caches KV cache of existing queries so new queries sharing the same prefix can skip recomputation, enabled via enable_prefix_caching=True. Documents a hybrid Mamba model extension: under --mamba-cache-mode align, Mamba state is stored only on the Mamba block grid so prefix-cache hits can resume only at block boundaries; --enable-mamba-fine-grained-prefix-cache adds a checkpoint at the shared-prefix junction so requests whose shared prefix ends mid-block can still reuse it, requiring --prefix-match-unit smaller than the Mamba block size plus EAGLE/MTP speculative decoding on the Mamba group. Cites two canonical use cases (long document query, multi-round conversation) and states APC's key limitation: it only reduces prefill time, not decode time, so it brings no benefit when answers are long or new queries don't share a prefix with cached ones. `evergreen` (588 words)
- [[autoscaling-with-keda-production-stack]] — Autoscaling with KEDA — production-stack — Official vLLM Production Stack project documentation (docs.vllm.ai, Helm chart v0.1.11+) -- a first-person primary source on the exact open-source composition teams use to autoscale vLLM on Kubernetes: KEDA + Prometheus + the vLLM Production Stack Helm chart, with kube-prometheus-stack bundled as an optional sub-chart dependency since v0.1.11 (so a separate observability stack install is no longer required). Walks through the full working configuration: deploy vLLM (example: meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct via lmcache/vllm-openai image) with serviceMonitor enabled so Prometheus scrapes the engine's native vllm:num_requests_waiting metric; install KEDA via its official Helm chart; wire a KEDA ScaledObject trigger of type 'prometheus' directly against that metric (no prometheus-adapter / Kubernetes Custom Metrics API needed -- KEDA's Prometheus scaler queries Prometheus directly; prometheus-adapter is only needed if using a standard HPA with the Custom Metrics API instead of KEDA). Concrete example config: minReplicaCount 1, maxReplicaCount 3, pollingInterval 15s, cooldownPeriod 360s, scale-up threshold of 5 pending requests on vllm:num_requests_waiting. Documents scale-to-zero explicitly as an advanced, non-default configuration: setting minReplicaCount: 0 requires pairing the queue-depth trigger with a second 'traffic-based keepalive' Prometheus trigger (querying sum(rate(vllm:num_incoming_requests_total[1m])) > 0) specifically to prevent the deployment from scaling to zero while traffic is still arriving -- i.e. the project's own docs treat zero-replica scaling as something that needs an extra safety trigger bolted on, not a default-safe behavior. Also documents custom HPA scaleDown behavior policies (stabilizationWindowSeconds, percent-based step-down) and a fallback block (failureThreshold, static fallback replica count) for when the Prometheus metric is unavailable. Verification steps use kubectl get scaledobjects / kubectl get hpa and a bundled Python load generator (tutorials/assets/example-10-load-generator.py) to trigger and observe scale-up. `review` (954 words)
- [[behind-the-platform-the-journey-to-create-the-linkedin-genai-application-tech-st]] — Behind the platform: the journey to create the LinkedIn GenAI application tech stack — Primary first-person LinkedIn engineering blog (Nov 26, 2024; authors Karthik R., Xiaofeng Wang, Sandeep Jha) on LinkedIn's GenAI application tech stack, confirming and sourcing the ZenML database summary. Verbatim: LinkedIn's 'GenAI application framework is now a thin wrapper atop LangChain, bridging it with LinkedIn infrastructure for logging, instrumentation, storage access and more. It is vended as a versioned internal library that is now mandated for use by all new GenAI applications at LinkedIn.' The Java-vs-Python fragmentation was deliberate and temporary: 'Rather than be blocked by the onerous task of rebuilding this tooling in Java or getting our online serving stack to support Python, we made a conscious call to start with fragmented online and offline stacks.' Python infra investment was selective -- e.g., they skipped building a native Python client for their Espresso document store and instead reused an existing REST proxy, and built Python RPC support only for the emerging gRPC transition target, not the legacy rest.li. Prompt management runs on a Jinja-templated 'Prompt Source of Truth' component (originally a Java resolution library, later rewritten in Python once the LangChain framework existed), converging on the OpenAI Chat Completions message format. Task automation uses a 'Skill Inversion' pattern -- a centralized skill registry service plus build plugins that auto-register downstream API endpoints as LLM-callable skills (JSON schemas compatible with LangChain's tool API) -- replacing an earlier approach where each GenAI product hand-wrote and duplicated skill wrappers. Conversational memory reuses LinkedIn's production messaging stack via the LangChain Conversational Memory abstraction rather than a new vector-store build. Model inference: initial GenAI apps used LLMs 'provided by the [Azure OpenAI service]' (word redacted/garbled in the scraped HTML but context and the ZenML summary confirm Azure OpenAI) behind a centralized GenAI proxy (Trust/Responsible-AI checks, streaming, quota management); increasingly, inference/fine-tuning depends on LinkedIn's internal AI platform 'built atop open source frameworks like PyTorch, DeepSpeed and vLLM' -- confirms vLLM by name as the open-source inference engine underneath LinkedIn's in-house serving layer. All LLMs (internal and external) are exposed behind a single OpenAI Chat Completions-compatible API with configuration-driven routing between on-prem and external models. Migration from the legacy Java stack proceeded incrementally, component-by-component, with a lean team pairing senior Java engineers with Python engineers. `review` (3454 words)
- [[benchmarks-litellm]] — Benchmarks | liteLLM — LiteLLM's own published performance-test results page -- the primary source behind the README's '8ms P95 latency at 1k RPS' headline claim, and directly responsive to the task's ask for stated performance limits of the proxy under load. Critical nuance the README strips out: the 8ms P95 gateway-overhead figure comes from the 'Short-prompt chat' test running FOUR gateway instances (1,170 RPS, 8ms p95 overhead, 150ms p95 request latency); the SAME test with only TWO instances produces materially worse results -- 1,035.7 RPS, 29ms p95 gateway overhead, and 630ms p95 total request latency -- showing per-instance throughput does not scale linearly and horizontal replication is required to hit the advertised headline number, not single-process performance. A separate high-throughput profile (3,000 RPS, 13.5B tokens/minute, p95 latency 54ms) required 33 gateway pods (4 workers/4 vCPU/16GiB each) behind an AWS ALB, with virtual-key budgets, token counting, and spend tracking all enabled, Postgres+Redis backing the proxy, and response caching disabled -- i.e. the full multi-tenant accounting machinery evaluated in this batch was active during the throughput test, not stripped out for the benchmark. All tests use in-process mock models or a mock OpenAI/realtime endpoint, so these are gateway-overhead-only numbers, excluding real upstream LLM latency. A realtime-API (WebSocket) test reached 1,207 RPS with 67ms p95 end-to-end latency on 4 instances. A logging-callback overhead test found GCS bucket logging and LangSmith logging added negligible overhead versus the base proxy (~1,133-1,137 RPS, ~132-140ms median latency across all three configs). `review` (871 words)
- [[best-of-both-worlds-cloud-native-ai-inference-at-scale-using-kserve-and-llm-d-ks]] — Best of Both Worlds: Cloud-Native AI Inference at Scale using KServe and llm-d | KServe — KServe blog post (post-v0.16, referenced by the Red Hat June 2026 article) providing the clearest concise layer-responsibility table for the KServe+llm-d stack: KServe = model lifecycle/scaling/governance; LLMInferenceService = generative API abstraction; vLLM = efficient execution inside the runtime; llm-d = cross-runtime routing and cache awareness; Kubernetes = resource orchestration. States 'llm-d provides the cluster-wide optimization' while KServe/LLMInferenceService supplies the OpenAI-compatible API surface (/v1/chat/completions, streaming, multi-turn) -- confirming llm-d's router role is scoped strictly to cross-instance scheduling, not API surface or lifecycle, which clarifies exactly which slice a non-k8s-backend replacement needs to reproduce. Cites llm-d benchmark numbers (noted as sourced from the llm-d project, not independently reproduced by KServe): up to ~57x faster P90 TTFT, throughput improving from ~4,400 to ~8,730 tokens/sec (~2x) in a baseline test cluster, sustained 4.5k-11k tokens/sec at scale, and ~50% P95/P99 tail latency reduction versus round-robin load balancing. `evergreen` (1693 words)
- [[best-self-hosted-ai-models-for-regulated-industries-financial-services-and-gover]] — Best self-hosted AI models for regulated industries: Financial services, and government — Prediction Guard (AI governance vendor) marketing/technical guide, updated May 11 2026 -- vendor content promoting its own 'sovereign control plane' product, but contains concrete, checkable technical tables. VRAM at Q4_K_M quantization: 8B~6GB, 14B~10GB, 32B~20-22GB, 70B~43GB; full FP16 70B needs ~4x A100-80GB (2x with reduced context, or 2x with AWQ/GPTQ quantization). Licensing specifics: Llama 3's Meta Community License has a 700M MAU threshold above which the free license expires plus a competitor-use restriction and an Acceptable Use Policy that outright bans ITAR-covered use; Qwen 2.5's 72B variant carries a 100M MAU threshold under the separate Qwen License (smaller variants are Apache 2.0). Recommends composing multiple self-hosted models under one OpenAI-compatible API gateway rather than picking a single model; lists vLLM and Hugging Face TGI as example inference-serving layers in a production architecture (Kubernetes + GPU device plugins, or VPC/air-gapped). `review` (4314 words)
- [[beyond-gateway-inference-traffic-control-practice-with]] — Beyond Gateway: Inference Traffic Control Practice with … — Secondary (Nov 14, 2025) blog walkthrough of Gateway API Inference Extension, useful mainly for its plain-language restatement of the resource model and its own caveat that terminology has shifted (InferenceModel+InferencePool -> InferenceObjective+InferencePool with optional InferencePoolImport) and the project is 'Alpha' at v1.1.x. States plainly: InferencePool 'is similar to a Service with a selector, responsible for selecting a group of model service Pods' -- i.e. it selects Pods directly by label selector, not Services or EndpointSlices, corroborating that a selectorless-Service/manual-EndpointSlice trick cannot be swapped in as an InferencePool backend without upstream changes. Also describes InferencePoolImport as the mechanism for 'importing InferencePools defined in remote clusters into the local cluster' for multi-cluster/multi-region governance. Recommends production users prefer managed implementations (GKE Inference Gateway) over the raw OSS extension while it is Alpha. As a secondary/derivative source (not primary docs or code), treat its API-shape claims as needing corroboration against gateway-api-inference-extension.sigs.k8s.io and the repo itself. `evergreen` (2172 words)
- [[blocking-queries-consul-hashicorp-developer]] — Blocking Queries | Consul | HashiCorp Developer — Official Consul blocking-queries semantics. Mechanism: endpoints that support blocking return header X-Consul-Index (a raft-index snapshot marker); client resends the same request with query param index=<last X-Consul-Index> plus wait=<duration> (max 10m, default 5m, plus up to wait/16 jitter) and the server holds the HTTP request open until a change occurs past that index or the timeout elapses -- long polling, not a persistent stream. Quote: 'the return of a blocking request is no guarantee of a change' (timeout or an idempotent write can return with no actual change). Implementation-detail requirements a client MUST follow: (1) reset index to 0 if a returned index is LOWER than the previous value (raft snapshot restore, KV list edge cases, Consul upgrades changing watch granularity) -- failing this can miss updates indefinitely or busy-loop; (2) sanity-check index > 0 after each response, since index=1 is always safe to request for not-yet-existent data; (3) rate-limit the blocking loop client-side (token-bucket, burst ~2) to avoid degrading into a busy loop when data churns fast, rather than sleeping (which delays delivery). Streaming backend (Consul 1.10+) is a materialized-view alternative to long polling, engaged automatically when index or cached params are set if the endpoint supports it; it omits X-Consul-LastContact/X-Consul-KnownLeader and marks responses with X-Consul-Query-Backend: streaming. A minority of agent-local endpoints (state not raft-indexed) instead use hash-based blocking: header X-Consul-ContentHash + query param hash=<value>, opaque (not orderable), otherwise same wait/timeout contract. RELEVANCE TO LOCUS: this is the direct analog to a poll-vs-watch decision for a Consul-backed EndpointDiscovery plugin -- a plugin would issue a blocking GET against /v1/health/service/:service with index tracking (see companion note health-http-api-consul-hashicorp-developer for the passing=true health-filter parameter), not a bare interval poll, to get near-real-time updates without tight polling. `evergreen` (1145 words)
- [[blog-agent-router]] — Blog | Agent Router — Blog index for the Envoy AI Gateway project, which rebranded to 'Agent Router' under the Agentic AI Foundation as of Sept 9 2026 ('same code, same maintainers, same APIs'). Post-1.0 development pivots toward agentic/MCP concerns: per-user identity at the gateway layer via header forwarding for multi-tenant MCP (v1.2, Oct 9 2026), credential isolation between teams sharing one gateway, and a separate benchmarking post measuring control-plane scaling to 2,000 AIGatewayRoute resources (latency/CPU/memory). Confirms the project has multiple named individual maintainers/authors (Erica Hughberg, Xunzhuo 'Bit' Liu, Takeshi Yoneda, Ignasi Barrera, Adrian Cole, Mohit Gurnani, Hrushikesh Patil) rather than a single corporate voice. `review` (578 words)

Also covers new distributed tracing (E2E trace_id across Gateway->EPP->KV-cache->P/D proxy->vLLM) and fine-grained first-party spans.
