- [[both-endpoints-and-endpointslices-not-working-with-service-abstraction-for-exter]] — Both Endpoints and EndpointSlices not working with Service abstraction for external IPs · Issue #123954 · kubernetes/kubernetes — GitHub issue #123954 (kubernetes/kubernetes, opened Mar 15 2024, closed Apr 18 2024) is direct adversarial evidence of a real failure mode in the selectorless-Service + manual-EndpointSlice pattern. A user created a selectorless Service (port name "mysql") and a matching EndpointSlice, but kube-proxy silently rejected all traffic to it (iptables rule: "has no endpoints", REJECT with icmp-port-unreachable) even though kubectl get endpointslice showed the correct backend IP. Root cause identified by maintainer aroradaman: the EndpointSlice port was named ("mysql") while the Service port was unnamed, breaking the internal Service-to-endpoint port-name mapping kube-proxy relies on; the fix is to make both named or both unnamed consistently. A second, still-present quirk: kubectl describe service continues to show "Endpoints: <none>" even after the fix, because kubectl only reads the legacy Endpoints object, which is never auto-populated when EndpointSlices are created manually (only the reverse Endpoints-to-EndpointSlice mirroring exists) -- so manually-managed EndpointSlice backends can look empty/broken in standard tooling even when actually routing correctly. This is a concrete, code-level gotcha anyone using selectorless Service + manual EndpointSlice as a router backend must account for. `evergreen` (2206 words)
- [[bring-back-kubernetes-service-support-in-aiservicebackends-ref-issue-902-theagen]] — Bring back Kubernetes Service support in AIServiceBackend's ref · Issue #902 · theagentrouter/agent-router — GitHub issue #902 (theagentrouter/agent-router, formerly envoyproxy/ai-gateway, opened Jul 17 2025, still open/past-due as of Jul 2026) reveals that plain Kubernetes Service support in AIServiceBackend's BackendRef was not merely unimplemented but 'temporarily disabled' -- it existed and was pulled. Maintainer mathetake's investigation (Jul 24 2025) found the blocker is structural, not superficial: Envoy Gateway propagates Service backends via EDS (Endpoint Discovery Service) separately from CDS, so the ai-gateway's own extproc server (internal/extproc/server.go) never receives the individual endpoint list for a Service-typed backend -- only the aggregate BackendRef identity via 'xds.upstream_host_locality_metadata' (added in envoyproxy/gateway#5875 and envoyproxy/envoy#39604), which carries name/namespace/kind/port but not the per-rule backend-reference index needed to support per-backendRef priority/modelNameOverride. Fix was deferred first to v0.4.0 then v0.5.0 then v0.6.0 (past due by Apr 25, 2026), pending EG v1.5 shipping endpoint-list support directly to the extension server so ai-gateway can build its own metadata instead of relying on EG's locality metadata. Interim mitigation landed as PR #1328 'enforce the limitation on k8s service at CRD' (Oct 2025) -- i.e. the CRD now explicitly rejects/validates against plain Service refs rather than silently mis-routing. This is direct evidence that even the REFERENCE two-tier gateway implementation cannot cleanly front a generic (non-InferencePool, non-Envoy-Gateway-Backend) backend today; the constraint originates in Envoy Gateway's control-plane data model, not in ai-gateway's own code, which matters for anyone building a custom router against a non-k8s backend outside this stack entirely. `evergreen` (619 words)
- [[building-the-silicon-brain-with-drew-houston-of-dropbox]] — Building the Silicon Brain - with Drew Houston of Dropbox — Primary transcript of the Latent Space podcast interview with Dropbox CEO Drew Houston (17,885 words; long source, flagged for source-analyst depth pass). Confirms, with exact quotes, the personal multi-backend local inference stack the ZenML summary paraphrased: 'I have my own like LLM inference stack. I mean, it uses the backend somewhat interchangeable. So everything from like XLlama to VLLM or SGLang, there's a bunch of these different backends you can use.' Run on a gaming laptop with a plug-in GPU for offline/airplane use, serving an 8B Llama model for autocomplete/transcription. On RAG vs. long context: 'models don't always use... the full context well fully yet, and actually if you provide too much irrelevant context, the quality degrades a lot... in the open source world, like we're still just getting to the cusp of... full context [being] usable' -- hence Dropbox Dash needs RAG because enterprise content 'is not going to fit into your context window,' analogized to RAM vs. hard disk. States frontier/large models aren't used for most product requests: 'I'm probably not using Sonnet 3.5 for all of your normal product use cases. You're going to use like a fine tuned 8 billion model or sort of the minimum model that gets you the right output' -- smaller models have materially better cost/latency. Frames model economics bluntly: 'large language models are a pretty bad business... models only have value if they're kind of on this... Pareto frontier of size and quality and cost... the second the frontier moves out... your model literally has zero economic value' -- so value accrues at the compute layer (Nvidia/semiconductors) and the application/customer-relationship layer, not mid-stack model ownership. Names Groq and Cerebras explicitly as 'non-NVIDIA stacks' that 'outperformed NVIDIA stack in terms of latency.' Attributes a 10-100x year-over-year price-performance improvement in AI infra partly to open-source competition ('you wouldn't see all this... flourishing of research and... democratization of access to compute without open source'), and frames current build-vs-buy posture as 'rent, don't buy' given how early-stage/volatile the hardware and model landscape still is -- explicitly rejecting owning dedicated capacity the way Dropbox did for storage, because unlike storage cost curves, AI cost/capability curves are not yet predictable. `review` (17904 words)
- [[building-the-foundation-for-running-extra-large-language-models]] — Building the foundation for running extra-large language models — Cloudflare's 2026-04-16 first-person follow-up (Chen/Flansburg/Krasnov) describing Infire's extension to multi-GPU (pipeline/tensor/expert parallelism) for trillion-parameter models like Kimi K2.5, plus PD disaggregation with token-aware load balancing, Mooncake-based cross-GPU KV-cache sharing, and EAGLE-3 speculative decoding; recovered via Wayback Machine after bot-wall blocked the live URL. `evergreen` (978 words)
- [[burstgpt-a-real-world-workload-dataset-to]] — BurstGPT: A Real-World Workload Dataset to — Academic paper (Wang et al., HKUST/Huawei/Tsinghua, 2025, presented at a Conference'17-style venue) introducing BurstGPT, a real-world LLM serving workload dataset comprising 10.31 million request traces from regional Azure OpenAI GPT services over 213 days. Captures four characteristics: (1) user request concurrency and burstiness variation across services/model types, (2) user conversation patterns (turn counts, intervals), (3) model response length distributions and their statistical relation to request length, and (4) system response failure rates, revealing intensive resource demands and limited availability in production Azure LLM services. The authors' demo evaluation using BurstGPT shows that frequent real-world traffic variation degrades efficiency, stability, and reliability in ways that synthetic/unrealistic workload assumptions miss, and explicitly identifies KV-cache management, scheduling, and disaggregation optimizations as needing evaluation under realistic (bursty, heavy-tailed) workloads rather than idealized traffic. BurstGPT is publicly released (github.com/HPMLL/BurstGPT) and cited by the llm-d project as evidence motivating adaptive, latency-predicted routing over fixed-weight heuristic KV-cache-aware schedulers, since production traffic's burstiness and unstable cache-hit-rate patterns make static tuning brittle. `evergreen` (7255 words)
- [[cacheroute-kv-cache-aware-routing-for-llm-backend-services-on-kubernetes-semanti]] — CacheRoute: KV-Cache-Aware Routing for LLM Backend Services on Kubernetes | Semantic Scholar — CacheRoute (Pi, Zhang, Shawulieti — CMU/Northeastern/UC, EITCE 2026, IEEE DOI 10.1109/EITCE70137.2026.11634378) is an independent, non-llm-d-maintainer KV-cache-aware router implemented as its own Endpoint Picker compatible with the Kubernetes Gateway API Inference Extension — a different work from the same-named Meta arXiv:2608.19677 paper. It jointly scores prefix similarity, real-time GPU memory utilization, and predicted TTFT. Evaluated on a 4-node GPU cluster against 5 baselines using LMSYS-Chat-1M prompts across short-QA, multi-turn-chat, and RAG-like long-context workload profiles: at 8 req/s mixed workload it reduces p95 TTFT by 11-43% (up to 54% at high load), improves KV-cache hit rate by 7-51 percentage points, and lowers peak GPU memory by 3-13 percentage points versus baselines, with comparable or higher throughput. The fetched page is a Semantic Scholar reference/citation listing (34 references, 7 forward citations), not the full paper text — IEEE Xplore and ResearchGate both blocked automated fetch and no open-access copy exists per Unpaywall. The abstract reports uniformly positive results; it contains no mention of skewed/low-recoverable-reuse adversarial conditions, hotspotting, or underperformance versus plain load balancing. `evergreen` (1280 words)
- [[cacheroute-planned-prefix-affinity-routingfor-large-scale-llm-serving]] — CacheRoute: Planned Prefix-Affinity Routingfor Large-Scale LLM Serving — CacheRoute (Meta, Aug 2026) is a planned prefix-affinity router that produces strong negative-result evidence on when cache-affinity routing fails. Core claim: cache-blind balancing disperses prefix reuse (return time to cache lengthens); fixed/pinned affinity restores locality but maps key skew directly onto server queues, overloading whichever server holds a hot key; reactive cache-aware routers (citing Preble and DualMap/Yuan-2026 as examples) still forfeit the warm prefix once a destination fills and traffic spills over. CacheRoute resolves this with a periodic offline routing plan: it admits only the highest-rate keys into a warm-prefix slot allocation, gives overloaded keys multiple destinations sized to cap expected per-destination load, places assignments via longest-processing-time-first (LPT) list scheduling, and falls back to cache-blind power-of-two-choices for unadmitted traffic; the table stays fixed for a control interval rather than remapping per request. On Llama-3.3-70B fp8 across 60 H100 GPUs (30 tensor-parallel-2 destinations), CacheRoute sustains 176 QPS at p99<=3.5s SLO vs 76 QPS for the strongest baseline (Preble) -- 2.3x -- and raises served KV-cache hit rate from 64.1% (cache-blind) to 93.2%. Ablation on an 8B model isolates the two mechanisms: affinity alone raises hit rate to 88% but creates 3.46x load imbalance; adding LPT placement keeps 90% hit rate while cutting imbalance to 1.24x, moving the SLO knee from 240 to >=500 QPS. CRITICALLY, two 32B counterexample workloads show affinity routing can actively HURT: when affinity recovers little KV work (hit rate only rising from 1.1% to 11.8% or 0.8% to 8.5%), the residual load skew from pinning is not offset, and capacity falls to 0.50-0.67x of flat load balancing (a straight loss) or merely ties it. The paper explicitly recommends gating any cache-affinity deployment with a shadow replay rather than enabling it from workload statistics alone, since model size alone does not predict which regime a workload falls into. Additional negative results: bursty arrivals (Gamma process, CV=1.9) cut CacheRoute's 3.5s capacity by one step (180->160 QPS); stale routing plans (one interval old under a lognormal rate-drift random walk) lose up to 3.0 points of KV hit and 858ms of p99 in worst-case transitions, and installing a freshly recomputed plan causes a transient 13.6-point KV-hit drop during cache rewarming. `evergreen` (6696 words)
- [[cast-ais-2026-state-of-kubernetes-optimization-report-reveals-gpu-utilization-at]] — Cast AI's 2026 State of Kubernetes Optimization Report Reveals GPU Utilization at 5% - Cast AI — PRIMARY SOURCE, but note Cast AI is a Kubernetes/GPU optimization VENDOR with a direct commercial interest in publicizing low utilization (it sells the fix). Press release (April 21, 2026) for Cast AI's '2026 State of Kubernetes Optimization Report,' based on 'tens of thousands' of non-optimized Kubernetes clusters (elsewhere reported as ~23,000 clusters across AWS/Azure/GCP). Confirms the headline figure cited by Forte Group: average GPU utilization across analyzed clusters was 5%, down to 2% on AKS, 5% on EKS, 6% on GKE. CPU utilization fell to 8% (from 10%), memory to 20% (from 23%). Quotes Cast AI co-founder Laurent Gil: 'A GPU sitting idle costs dollars per hour. A CPU sitting idle costs cents. And 95% of GPU capacity is doing nothing,' and notes cloud vendors raised H200 prices 15%, breaking a 20-year trend of falling compute costs. States fewer than 2% of GPUs in the analyzed clusters run on Spot instances despite 60-91% savings potential, and that one high-performing cluster with 136 H200s sustained 49% utilization -- a 10x gap versus the fleet average of 5%, showing the problem is operational/configuration rather than an inherent hardware constraint. Lists customers (BMW, Cisco, FICO, Hugging Face, Swisscom). This is the primary source behind the single most load-bearing utilization-reality statistic in this batch. `review` (2720 words)
- [[centralized-routing-for-external-and-self-hosted-llms-on-openshift-ai-red-hat-de]] — Centralized routing for external and self-hosted LLMs on OpenShift AI | Red Hat Developer — Red Hat Developer article (published May 25, 2026, updated Jun 1 2026) demonstrates the generic 'gateway abstraction' half of the layered pattern -- NOT llm-d/EPP specific. Shows deploying LiteLLM Proxy (OpenAI-compatible, docker.litellm.ai/berriai/litellm:main-stable) as a standard OpenShift Deployment that routes by logical model_name in a YAML model_list to either external providers (OpenAI gpt-5.4-mini, Gemini) via api_key/api_base, or a self-hosted vLLM model served via KServe InferenceService (llama-3.1-8b-instruct) reached over a plain in-cluster Service URL -- no InferencePool/EPP involved here, self-hosted backend is addressed as an ordinary ClusterIP endpoint. Notes that OpenShift AI 3.4 also ships a built-in Models-as-a-Service (MaaS) capability powered by Red Hat Connectivity Link that natively unifies self-hosted (vLLM) and external-provider routing behind one endpoint, and that OpenShift AI additionally supports Portkey AI Gateway as an alternative standalone gateway. Useful as a contrast case: this is the pattern without EPP-based intra-pool picking, i.e., what a gateway looks like when the self-hosted side is a single flat backend rather than an InferencePool-fronted pool. `evergreen` (2265 words)
- [[chapter-7-configuring-scheduler-settings-for-llm-inference-services-deploy-model]] — Chapter 7. Configuring scheduler settings for LLM inference services | Deploy models using Distributed Inference with llm-d | Red Hat OpenShift AI Self-Managed | 3.4 | Red Hat Documentation — Red Hat OpenShift AI 3.4 docs (KServe LLMInferenceService CRD layer, not raw llm-d) on configuring EPP scheduler settings two ways: inline (router.scheduler.config.inline.<EndpointPickerConfig>) or via a Kubernetes ConfigMap referenced from the LLMInferenceService spec (router.scheduler.config.ref.{name,key}) -- the two are mutually exclusive. Confirms the EPP's two sequential layers -- Flow Control (when/order: priority queuing, saturation detection, load shedding) and Scheduling (where: routing plugins choosing the backend pod) -- and that Scheduling is always on while Flow Control is opt-in via the flowControl feature gate. Documents the OpenShift-default scheduling profile: queue-scorer (weight 2) + prefix-cache-scorer (weight 3), prioritizing KV-cache reuse over even load. Note: this is a downstream KServe-wrapped EPP config surface, one layer removed from raw llm-d-router EndpointPickerConfig YAML. `evergreen` (1601 words)
- [[clear-prefill-target-header-if-set-in-incoming-request-by-elevran-pull-request-2]] — Clear prefill target header if set in incoming request by elevran · Pull Request #244 · llm-d/llm-d-router · GitHub — PR #244 (merged Jul 16 2025, elevran), the primary fix for issue #242: sets the prefill-target header to an empty string if present on an incoming request, before the scheduler/EPP sets its own value after routing decisions are made. Author's rationale, quoted: 'A possibly better fix would have been to respond to the gateway (Envoy) with remove_headers mutation but GIE currently supports only set_headers mutations, so clearing the user provided values seems like the best option given the available functionality.' This is EPP/scheduler-side logic (not the sidecar), meaning the SSRF defense-in-depth model is: (1) EPP/gateway strips attacker-supplied x-prefiller-host-port on ingress [this fix] and (2) sidecar-side InferencePool allowlist as a secondary check. If a non-Kubernetes deployment keeps the EPP/gateway path unchanged and only replaces backend discovery, mitigation (1) still applies as long as no untrusted client can reach the sidecar directly -- a network-topology requirement, not a Kubernetes-specific one. `evergreen` (702 words)
- [[cloud-native-system-for-llm-inference-serving]] — Cloud Native System for LLM Inference Serving — Xu, Liao, Wu, He, Ye (Shenzhen Institute of Advanced Technology, CAS / Univ. of Macau), arXiv 2507.18007, J.ACM 2025 — a short (~4,700-word), position-paper-style article arguing that cloud-native primitives (containerization, microservices, Kubernetes-based dynamic scheduling) should underpin LLM inference serving, rather than a report of an operator's actual production stack. RELIABILITY CAVEAT: this is weak evidence relative to the other papers in this batch — it is not first-person deployment evidence from an operator running LLM inference at scale, its empirical validation is a single small-scale lab experiment, and its central proposed technique (decomposing each individual Transformer layer of LLaMA-2-13B into its own Kubernetes microservice, connected via gRPC and Istio service mesh) is not how any disclosed production LLM serving stack in this research area actually operates — no major open-source engine (vLLM, SGLang, TGI) or named operator (Mooncake/Kimi, DistServe) partitions at per-layer granularity; this is a research proposal, not observed industry practice. Cites standard figures as motivation (inference is 'up to 90%' of total ML cloud cost; training GPT-4-class models needs '25,000 A100s for 90-100 days'; GPU clusters reportedly run at '~30% utilization' with '>70% of allocated resources idle outside peak') without primary sourcing for these numbers. Names PagedAttention, continuous batching, Llumnix, and DistServe as the state-of-the-art request-migration/latency-reduction techniques it positions its proposal against. Experimental setup: 3-node Kubernetes cluster, each node one NVIDIA A100-80GB (NVLink), LLaMA-2-13B with each of 40 Transformer layers run as a separate microservice, Prometheus/Grafana monitoring, Locust-generated load (50-2048 token inputs). Finds one bottleneck layer (Layer 27) with max latency over 230x that of another layer (Layer 30) under load, then shows Kubernetes HPA-based autoscaling of just that bottleneck layer's microservice reduces its latency from 15.23s to 12.28s and raises overall system throughput from 4.07 to 5.05 QPS at batch size 62 — a single-configuration, single-model result with no comparison to standard (non-decomposed) serving baselines like vLLM running the same model, and no discussion of the cross-microservice communication overhead that per-layer gRPC calls would add in a real deployment. Net assessment: useful only as an illustration of the general cloud-native-primitives framing (load balancing, autoscaling, migration, load prediction, profiling, fine-grained modularization) that NVIDIA Dynamo, llm-d, and AIBrix implement in production-proven form at the service-replica level (not per-layer); should not be cited for production-stack claims. `review` (4733 words)

- [[configuration-llm-d]] — Configuration | llm-d — llm-d dev docs, the canonical EndpointPickerConfig schema/mental-model reference (fetched Sep 2026) -- the single richest source on plugin taxonomy and wiring rules. Top-level schema: apiVersion (llm-d.ai/v1alpha1, constant), kind: EndpointPickerConfig, plugins, featureGates, requestHandler, flowControl, schedulingProfiles, dataLayer. Config is explicitly NOT a reconciled CRD -- read once at startup, no hot reload. Models the config as a graph: Plugins are 'Nodes' (instantiated by type+parameters), structural sections like schedulingProfiles/flowControl are 'Edges' (wiring by pluginRef name), and flat fields (maxBytes, defaultRequestTTL) are static runtime config. Validation enforces valid pluginRef references, unique names, and that metrics extractors form a DAG (no circular data dependencies). KEY MECHANISM for the plugin-registration question: 'Auto-Wiring' -- if a plugin declared in the top-level plugins list implements a specific Go interface (Admitter, DataProducer, or advanced hooks PreRequest, ResponseHeaderProcessor, ResponseBodyProcessor), the system automatically discovers and binds it to its lifecycle role WITHOUT an explicit wiring edge in schedulingProfiles/flowControl/etc -- i.e. plugin role dispatch is interface-based reflection over Go types registered at compile time, not purely declarative. Documents Admitters (early accept/reject before queueing) and Data Producers (per-request context gathering, e.g. predicted-latency-producer feeding latency-slo-admitter) as this auto-wired category. Documents Parsers (requestHandler.parsers, default openai-parser/anthropic-parser/vllmhttp-parser dispatched by request path, e.g. /v1/chat/completions) and notes the older single 'parser' field is deprecated in favor of the list (llm-d-router#1308). Scheduling Profile execution order is fixed: Filters -> Scorers -> Picker; a profile can have at most one Picker (runtime error otherwise); when schedulingProfiles is omitted, an auto-generated 'default' profile references ALL Filter/Scorer/Picker plugins, which errors if multiple Pickers exist top-level. Profile Handler plugin is required only when multiple SchedulingProfiles exist (e.g. disaggregated prefill); a single profile implicitly uses SingleProfileHandler. dataLayer.sources[].pluginRef must implement the DataSource interface and dataLayer.sources[].extractors[].pluginRef must implement the Extractor interface -- default metrics-data-source+core-metrics-extractor injection is ADDITIVE (appended to user-supplied sources) unless injectDefaults:false or a metrics-data-source is already present. Notably this dev-docs page does NOT yet mention the 'dataLayer.discovery' section or multicluster-file-discovery seen in the llm-d-router repo's own docs -- evidence the llm-d.ai site lags the repo's Aug 25 2026 discovery-consolidation commit. HA is active-passive: EPP replicas>1 elect a single leader via leader election; only the leader serves traffic. EPP exposes Prometheus metrics on port 9090 at /metrics. `evergreen` (3363 words)
- [[configuring-the-endpoint-picker-via-configuration-yaml-file-kubernetes-gateway-a]] — Configuring the EndPoint Picker via configuration YAML file - Kubernetes Gateway API Inference Extension — Ground-truth EPP configuration-YAML reference (Gateway API Inference Extension docs, archived 2026-06-14 snapshot since live URL 404s). Documents the EndpointPickerConfig schema (apiVersion inference.networking.x-k8s.io/v1alpha1): plugins, schedulingProfiles, saturationDetector, flowControl, data, parser, featureGates sections. The prefix-cache-scorer plugin (type: prefix-cache-scorer) takes blockSize (default 64), maxPrefixBlocksToMatch (default 256), and lruCapacityPerServer (default 31250) parameters and 'scores pods based on the amount of the prompt believed to be in the pod's KvCache.' Separately documents kv-cache-utilization-scorer (scores by KV cache utilization, no params) and lora-affinity-scorer. Config is read only at EPP startup via --config-file or --config-text flags, NOT reconciled like a K8s CRD. Details the saturationDetector's utilization-detector plugin (queueDepthThreshold default 5, kvCacheUtilThreshold default 0.8, metricsStalenessThreshold default 200ms) which gatekeeps centralized queuing when flowControl feature gate is enabled, or triggers HTTP 503 shedding of negative-priority requests when disabled. Data layer section documents built-in vLLM Model Server Protocol metrics collected via Prometheus: vllm:num_requests_waiting, vllm:num_requests_running, vllm:kv_cache_usage_perc, vllm:lora_requests_info, vllm:cache_config_info -- with SGLang defaults auto-selected via pod label inference.networking.k8s.io/engine-type: sglang. Parser section governs payload understanding for prefix-cache-aware routing (openai-parser default, vllmgrpc-parser alternative). featureGates flags (dataLayer, flowControl) gate experimental functionality, all disabled by default. `evergreen` (3814 words)
- [[confirmation-of-self-hosted-oss-usage-without-ee-license-production-observabilit]] — Confirmation of Self-Hosted OSS Usage Without EE License — Production Observability Use Case · langfuse · Discussion #13737 · GitHub — Langfuse GitHub Discussion #13737 (May 2026): a practitioner (poojabh91) runs an internal OSS-compliance review and asks Langfuse to confirm, for an internal production LLM-observability deployment (self-hosted Docker image, v3.173.0, no LANGFUSE_EE_LICENSE_KEY configured), whether core features are fully MIT-covered and whether EE code ships in but stays dormant in the official Docker image. The question remained formally 'Unanswered' by a human maintainer, but an automated Inkeep AI support-bot reply (citing Langfuse's own docs and a GitHub issue #9521) confirms: (1) all core features — tracing, evaluations, prompt management, experiments, annotation, playground, dashboards — are MIT licensed with no usage limits; (2) everything outside /ee is MIT (OSI-approved); EE-gated features are specifically Project-level RBAC Roles, Protected Prompt Labels, Data Retention Policies, Audit Logs, Server-Side Data Masking, UI Customization, Organization Creators, Org Management API/SCIM, and Instance Management API; (3) the official prebuilt Docker image DOES include the EE code paths, but they stay inert without a license key — self-hosters are not required to strip EE code. Useful as a practitioner-sourced confirmation of the OSS/commercial seam, though the thread itself got no official written sign-off from a Langfuse maintainer, which was exactly what the asker needed for compliance purposes — an audited absence worth flagging. `review` (874 words)
- [[cost-efficient-large-language-model-serving-for-multi-turn-conversations-with]] — Cost-Efficient Large Language Model Serving for Multi-turn Conversations with — CachedAttention / AttentionStore (Gao, He, Sharma, Kang, Jevdjic, Deng, Yang, Yu, Zuo — National University of Singapore + Shanghai Jiaotong + Huawei Cloud, arXiv 2403.19708, USENIX ATC'24) is the system Mooncake's own related-work section names as its closest concurrent architecture ('a concurrent work with us... The architecture of Mooncake shares many design choices with AttentionStore'). Motivating data from real ShareGPT conversation logs: 73% of real ChatGPT conversations are multi-turn, and because serving engines discard a session's KV cache when it goes idle and recompute it from scratch on the next turn, up to 99% of prefilling cost in multi-turn conversations is pure repetitive recomputation of KV cache the engine already computed once. AttentionStore is the hierarchical KV-cache storage system (GPU HBM -> host memory -> disk) that persists KV caches across turns instead of discarding them; CachedAttention is the attention mechanism/scheduling layer built on top of it. Three concrete engineering techniques, each solving a problem the paper demonstrates empirically: (1) layer-wise pre-loading and asynchronous saving overlap KV-cache transfer with GPU compute to hide access latency to slower storage tiers (motivated by the paper's own measurement that LLaMA-65B's free HBM space is fully consumed by KV cache in 14 seconds if caches are simply retained in GPU memory, ruling out an HBM-only retention strategy); (2) scheduler-aware fetching/eviction pre-fetches KV caches likely to be accessed from disk into host memory using hints from the inference job scheduler, because most caches end up on disk (tens of TBs capacity vs. host memory's hundreds of GBs) where random-access latency is poor; (3) decoupled positional encoding lets saved KV caches survive context-window truncation (e.g., LLaMA-2's 4K limit) by re-embedding position information only at load time, rather than invalidating the entire cached session when old tokens are dropped. Evaluated on the real ShareGPT dataset: reduces TTFT by up to 87%, improves prompt-prefilling throughput by up to 7.8x, and cuts end-to-end inference cost by up to 70% for multi-turn conversations. Authored primarily by Huawei Cloud interns/researchers, making it an institutional sibling to Mooncake and TetriInfer (also Huawei-affiliated) within this corpus's disaggregation literature. `review` (12848 words)
- [[data-layer-llm-d]] — Data Layer | llm-d — llm-d dev docs, canonical Data Layer design page -- the single most direct answer to 'where do pod/Kubernetes assumptions live and what are the plugin interfaces.' Defines the Data Layer as a Source -> Extract -> Attribute pipeline enriching thread-safe/type-safe Endpoint objects. Names THREE extension-point interface pairs: (1) PollingDataSource + Extractor -- periodic pull, e.g. metrics-data-source (polls a Prometheus-compatible /metrics endpoint of a model server) + core-metrics-extractor (multi-engine-aware, maps engine-specific metric names to standard internal keys like KVCacheUsagePercent/WaitingQueueSize); a Collector goroutine is started per endpoint by the runtime. (2) NotificationSource + NotificationExtractor -- reacts to Kubernetes resource changes; concrete k8s-notification-source watches a single configurable Kubernetes GVK (CRD or core API) and is bound to the controller-runtime manager, dispatching NotificationEvents on create/update/delete (docs note: 'There are currently no upstream extractors that require this source' as of this snapshot). (3) EndpointSource + EndpointExtractor -- reacts to EPP-internal endpoint lifecycle events (add/update/delete) via endpoint-notification-source, independent of Kubernetes, for cache init/cleanup. CRITICAL FINDING for non-k8s backends: only the NotificationSource/k8s-notification-source pairing is intrinsically Kubernetes-coupled (it binds to controller-runtime and watches a GVK); the PollingDataSource and EndpointSource interfaces are backend-agnostic by design -- metrics-data-source polls whatever URL/address an Endpoint object carries, and endpoint-notification-source only reacts to endpoints already in the EPP's own datastore regardless of how they got there. This means the actual Kubernetes-pod dependency is concentrated in whichever DataSource populates the endpoint datastore in the first place (the pod/endpoint discovery mechanism itself), not in the metrics-polling or endpoint-lifecycle machinery. `evergreen` (697 words)
- [[deep-dive-into-the-gateway-api-inference-extension-cncf]] — Deep Dive into the Gateway API Inference Extension | CNCF — CNCF blog (April 21, 2025) by Christian Posta (Solo.io), technical deep dive into the PRE-GA v1alpha2 API shape (uses `inference.networking.x-k8s.io/v1alpha2`, InferencePool field `extensionRef` and `targetPortNumber` -- both since renamed to `endpointPickerRef` and `targetPorts` in the v1 GA spec seen elsewhere in this batch; flag as historical/superseded API shape, not current). Explains the Endpoint Selection Extension (ESE) filter chain in detail with worked numeric examples: for Critical requests, filters are (1) queue < 50 wait threshold, (2) LoRA adapter affinity, (3) least queuing, (4) least KV cache; for Sheddable requests, filter requires KV cache < 80% AND queue < 5, else the request is dropped entirely (load shedding). Provides three worked routing scenarios with explicit pod queue/KV-cache/LoRA state showing exactly how the filter chain selects a winning pod. Confirms ESE extracts `modelName` from an OpenAI-formatted request body to match against InferenceModel objects. `evergreen` (1479 words)
- [[designing-mcp-gateway-ubers-mcp-management-platform]] — Designing MCP Gateway Uber's MCP Management Platform — Uber engineering blog (Oct 1 2026) on the MCP Gateway, a custom-built microservice orchestrating Model Context Protocol tool access for AI agents at Uber -- this is an agent-tool-orchestration layer, not an LLM inference-serving stack, and the post names no inference engine, model-serving framework, or GPU scheduling component (flagged: does not update the stale 2024 GenAI Gateway inference-stack description; it is a related but distinct system). Scale: hosts 800+ MCP servers and 5,000+ tools. Architecture: MCP Registry (control plane, catalog/governance) + Proxy Gateway (data plane, protocol translation). AutoCrawler, a Cadence-powered (OSS workflow engine, cadenceworkflow.io) distributed workflow, continuously scans Uber's internal IDL (Protobuf/Thrift) registry to auto-generate MCP server/tool definitions, using an LLM to write agent-friendly tool descriptions from schemas/comments -- tools register disabled-by-default pending owning-team review. Native MCP servers are built via Uber's in-house MCPFx framework and auto-discovered via heartbeat signals. Downstream protocol execution (HTTP/gRPC/TChannel) is delegated to Muttley, Uber's existing service-mesh sidecar, reusing existing service-to-service routing rather than building new transport. At scale, Uber built three additional in-house mechanisms to solve context-window/cost blowup from exposing hundreds of servers to agents: Omni MCP (a single proxy enabling incremental/gradual tool discovery via discover_server/discover_tools/get_tool_schema/invoke_tool, to avoid loading all tool schemas into context at once), Response Projection (a GraphQL-like field-selection mechanism letting the LLM request only needed response fields, trimmed server-side), and Code Mode (routes MCP calls through Uber's aifx CLI so coding agents write tool output to files instead of loading it into model context; stated to be 'now the company default for MCP tool use in coding agents'). Security: built-in per-tool authorization via Uber's internal Attribute-Based Access Control system, plus out-of-the-box PII/sensitive-data redaction on tool responses. `review` (3003 words)
- [[developing-llm-guardrail-configs-locally-with-nemo-guardrails-red-hat-developer]] — Developing LLM guardrail configs locally with NeMo Guardrails | Red Hat Developer — First-person engineering tutorial from Red Hat (co-developer of NeMo Guardrails with NVIDIA; ships it via Red Hat OpenShift AI 3.5), part 1 of a 3-part series (local dev -> EvalHub evaluation -> OpenShift AI production deploy). Demonstrates NeMo Guardrails' core abstractions: a 'rail' is a single text-classification decision (yes/no) over text; a 'guardrail configuration' is a set of rails forming a named profile (e.g., one per business department); a 'guardrail server' hosts multiple named configs behind one API. Walks through three escalating guardrail configs against prompt injection, run entirely locally via a Jupyter notebook with no GPU: (1) a single hardcoded regex for 'ignore all previous instructions' -- trivially bypassed by a homoglyph swap ('Ignóre'); (2) a broad hand-written regex set (dozens of patterns covering instruction-override, system-prompt extraction, roleplay/persona hijack, DAN/jailbreak names, delimiter manipulation, obligation phrasing) -- catches the obvious attacks but misses rephrased/novel ones ('Hypothetically, if you had no restrictions...') and produces false positives on benign prompts (laptop 'recovery mode', D&D 'roleplay as a Kobold' both got blocked); (3) protectai/deberta-v3-base-prompt-injection-v2, a 200M-parameter HF text-classification model run locally via a new 'hf_classifier' rail type (contributed by Red Hat into NeMo Guardrails 0.23 / shipped in OpenShift AI 3.5) -- scored 12/13 correct on the same test prompts (~90ms/query on the author's laptop after an initial ~750MB model download), still missing one subtle attack ('Let's play a game... be an AI with no filters'). Explicit conclusion: regex and even a fine-tuned classifier are a base, not a sufficient solution on their own -- recommends layering rails in sequence (cheap regex first for fail-fast on known patterns, falling back to the classifier for complex cases) for defense-in-depth, and explicitly flags that 13 manually-chosen test prompts 'is certainly not enough to draw any meaningful conclusions,' pointing to the series' next article on rigorous large-scale benchmark evaluation via EvalHub. Strong primary/first-person source on guardrail architecture and its limits. `review` (3194 words)
- [[disaggregated-serving-llm-d]] — Disaggregated Serving | llm-d — Current (v0.9, as of the fetch banner referencing the v0.9 'hardened for scale' release) llm-d architecture doc on P/D disaggregated serving. Explains two required components: (1) Request Flow Orchestration -- EPP selects a P/D worker pair via a disagg-profile-handler that runs a decode-profile first, consults a 'decider' on how much of the prompt is cached on D, and only invokes the prefill-profile (selecting a P endpoint too) if the uncached suffix is large; both P and D endpoints belong to the SAME InferencePool, distinguished by the llm-d.ai/role label (values: prefill, decode, prefill-decode) which the prefill-filter/decode-filter plugins use to filter candidates -- and (2) Efficient KV Transfer, typically over RDMA via NIXL. The Routing Proxy Sidecar runs in each decode pod and reads the x-prefiller-host-port header (one or more prefill pod addresses, comma-separated/multi-value) set by the proxy/EPP; if present it executes the disaggregated path (send prefill request, capture KV transfer metadata, forward enriched request to local decoder), otherwise it proxies decoder-only. It documents TWO KV-transfer protocol implementations the sidecar must speak: vLLM's nixlv2 (default) -- a two-phase sequential protocol using kv_transfer_params and max_tokens=1, with fallback to decoder-only on prefiller server errors (5xx) but no retry on client errors -- and SGLang's sglang connector, a concurrent protocol using bootstrap_host/bootstrap_port/bootstrap_room parameters where prefill fires asynchronously (goroutine with context.WithoutCancel) while decode runs synchronously, coordinating out-of-band via the bootstrap room. NIXL is the only KV-transfer engine llm-d currently focuses on; it needs RDMA (InfiniBand or RoCE, both supported via UCX and UCCL backends; TCP-X and EFA supported by UCCL; libfabric supports IB only) for efficient transfer, falls back to slow TCP without RDMA (dev/test only), and NVLink transfer works only for local development since 'it cannot cross pod boundaries' in Kubernetes deployments. With GPUDirect RDMA, GPU memory is registered directly with the NIC so transfers bypass the CPU. `evergreen` (1527 words)
- [[discovery-plugins-k8s-and-file-based-endpoint-discovery-by-ezrasilvera-pull-requ]] — Discovery Plugins: K8s and File-based Endpoint Discovery by ezrasilvera · Pull Request #1007 · llm-d/llm-d-router · GitHub — CORRECTION (verified 2026-09-11 via GitHub API): PR #1007 was NEVER merged. GitHub API confirms merged=false, merge_commit_sha=null, state=closed, closed_at=2026-05-25T10:35:40Z. The PR proposed two Kubernetes-based EndpointDiscovery plugins (inference-pool-discovery, static-selector-discovery) under pkg/epp/framework/plugins/datalayer/discovery/k8s/, but the author put a /hold on it: comment (2026-05-13) 'Don't merge this PR for now. Instead we will introduce the change in stages starting from the file discovery... Replace with the file discovery PRs', and closed it (2026-05-25) with 'Replace with teh file discovery PRs' [sic]. Only the file-discovery plugin actually shipped, via PRs #1080 and #1081 (both merged 2026-05-14), landing at pkg/epp/framework/plugins/datalayer/discovery/file/. Verified via `git/trees/main?recursive=1`: NO pkg/epp/framework/plugins/datalayer/discovery/k8s/ directory exists at HEAD -- only discovery/file/ (README.md, multicluster.go, multicluster_test.go, plugin.go, plugin_test.go) exists. IMPLICATION FOR THE LOCUS: Kubernetes pod discovery in llm-d-router today is NOT an EndpointDiscovery plugin at all -- it remains a separate, hardcoded controller-runtime path in cmd/epp/runner/runner.go (the ctrl.GetConfig() branch, distinct from the runWithFileDiscovery branch taken when dataLayer.discovery.endpoints is configured). The file-discovery plugin (#1080/#1081) is therefore the ONLY EndpointDiscovery plugin implementation that exists anywhere in the codebase as of HEAD -- there is no watch-based (as opposed to poll/fsnotify-based) reference implementation to copy for a custom (e.g. Consul- or Dynamo-backed) discovery plugin. Prior summary on this note incorrectly stated the PR was 'merged May 25 2026' -- that claim is superseded by this correction. `evergreen` (5444 words)
- [[distserve-disaggregating-prefill-and-decoding-for-goodput-optimized]] — DistServe: Disaggregating Prefill and Decoding for Goodput-optimized — DistServe (Zhong et al., Peking University / UC San Diego / StepFun, OSDI'24, arXiv 2401.09670) — the origin paper for prefill/decode disaggregation, directly cited as precedent by Mooncake, llm-d, and NVIDIA Dynamo. Diagnoses two root causes of poor goodput in colocated serving: (1) prefill-decoding interference — a single prefill job added to a decoding batch measurably slows both (Figure 2: adding one prefill job to a 13B-model decoding batch significantly elongates TPOT, worse with longer prompts), and chunked-prefill-with-piggyback (cites vLLM/Sarathi-style approaches [3,9]) only partially masks this at the cost of O(N^2) redundant KV-cache HBM->SRAM reloads when prefill is split into N chunks; (2) resource/parallelism coupling — colocation forces one parallelism plan to serve both a compute-bound prefill phase (benefits from intra-op parallelism) and a memory-bandwidth-bound decode phase (benefits from different scaling), forcing over-provisioning. DistServe's fix: assign prefill and decode to physically separate GPU instances, each independently scaled/parallelized, with a bandwidth-aware placement algorithm that colocates same-pipeline-stage prefill/decode segments on one node so KV-cache transfer rides NVLINK rather than cross-node links; decode instances 'pull' KV cache from prefill instances (buffering burstiness in the prefill GPU's memory) rather than prefill 'pushing' it. Reports a simple FCFS online scheduler, periodic workload-pattern-triggered replanning (placement algorithm reruns in seconds; weight reload takes minutes — both far under the hourly timescale at which real traffic patterns shift), and explicitly defers preemption and fault tolerance to future work, naming the specific failure mode (a single decode instance fault can cripple multiple dependent prefill instances). Implementation: ~6.5K LOC Python (placement algorithm, OpenAI-API-compatible frontend, orchestration layer) + ~8.1K LOC C++/CUDA execution engine, using Ray actors for distributed GPU workers, NCCL for cross-node and async CudaMemcpy for intra-node KV transfer; integrates continuous batching, FlashAttention, and PagedAttention, and serves OPT/LLaMA. Evaluated on a 4-node/32-GPU A100 cluster across three real workload shapes with measured token-length distributions — ShareGPT chatbot traces (avg input 755.5 / output 200.3 tokens), HumanEval code completion (avg input 171.3 / output 98.2), LongBench summarization (avg input 1738.3 / output 90.7) — against vLLM and DeepSpeed-MII baselines. Headline result: serves 7.4x more requests or sustains 12.6x tighter SLOs than state-of-the-art colocated systems (vLLM/DeepSpeed-MII) while keeping >90% of requests within TTFT/TPOT constraints; against vLLM specifically, DistServe sustains 1.8x-3.2x tighter SLO and up to 4.6x higher request rate in various configurations, and up to 3x-8x higher throughput in a related-work comparison table. Explicitly names Splitwise and TetriInfer as concurrent disaggregation papers it does not claim precedence over. `review` (13152 words)
- [[distributed-llm-inference-with-llm-d-moncef-abboud]] — Distributed LLM Inference with llm-d | Moncef Abboud — Named-practitioner (Moncef Abboud) walkthrough of llm-d architecture: EPP-as-Envoy-ext_proc, scoring/picking pipeline, and disaggregated P/D serving. States plainly that llm-d has a mode where it runs outside Kubernetes via a 'file discovery' plugin where vLLM endpoints are hardcoded/listed instead of discovered via the k8s API, linking to https://github.com/llm-d/llm-d-router/blob/f7e8852479d6b2902d3bd89d8d252187211ff801/docs/discovery.md. Documents the request path in detail: Envoy's ext_proc streams RequestHeaders/RequestBody to the EPP's StreamingServer.Process, which calls director.HandleRequest -> admission plugins -> flow control (priority/fairness via x-gateway-inference-fairness-id, x-gateway-inference-objective headers) -> data-producer plugins (tokenizer, prefix matcher) -> scheduler (filter -> weighted scorers -> weighted-random picker) -> EPP returns x-gateway-destination-endpoint header with IP:port, and Envoy forwards there. Gives a worked numeric scoring example (prefix-cache weight 3.0, kv-cache-utilization weight 1.0, queue-depth weight 1.0) showing weighted-random picking avoids thundering-herd on the single top-scored pod. Confirms the EPP gathers node metrics by scraping each backend's Prometheus /metrics endpoint (vllm:num_requests_waiting, vllm:kv_cache_usage_perc, etc., with an analogous sglang:* config), and that precise KV-cache tracking subscribes directly to vLLM's ZMQ KV-event stream per instance via the llm-d-kv-cache library (github.com/llm-d/llm-d-kv-cache). Also notes RDMA/NIXL-based direct KV-cache transfer between prefill and decode pods over InfiniBand/RoCE for disaggregated serving. `evergreen` (2431 words)
- [[dropbox-building-a-silicon-brain-for-universal-enterprise-search-zenml-llmops-da]] — Dropbox: Building a Silicon Brain for Universal Enterprise Search - ZenML LLMOps Database — ZenML summary of a Latent Space podcast interview with Dropbox CEO Drew Houston (primary at https://www.latent.space/p/drew-houston). Describes Houston's personal local-inference stack spanning multiple backends -- 'XLlama, VLLM, and SGLang' -- run on a gaming laptop to serve 8B-parameter Llama models offline for autocomplete/transcription. Dropbox Dash (enterprise search) combines RAG with long-context models since no single context window can hold 'everything your company's ever written'; production systems use hybrid architectures and purpose-fit fine-tuned 8B models rather than routing every request to frontier models. Articulates a 'rent, don't buy' build-vs-buy philosophy toward LLM infra given rapid hardware/model turnover, and names Groq and Cerebras as non-NVIDIA inference-latency alternatives. Reports 10-100x YoY price-performance improvement partly attributed to open-source model competition, and that ~80% of Dash's engineering effort is data ingestion/format-translation rather than the LLM layer itself. `review` (1462 words)
- [[dynamic-speculative-ttl-for-unified-prefix-cache-scoring-issue-544-llm-dllm-d-kv]] — Dynamic Speculative TTL for Unified Prefix-Cache Scoring · Issue #544 · llm-d/llm-d-kv-cache — GitHub issue #544 (opened Apr 27, 2026, llm-d/llm-d-kv-cache, part of #353) is a design proposal for 'speculative' KV-cache indexing with dynamic TTL, motivated by the unreliability of modeling engine-internal LRU cache state for agentic/multi-turn/tool-calling/branching inference workloads. Proposed mechanism: a per-endpoint 'KV Events Health Monitor' (PR 1, in llm-d-inference-scheduler) tracks last-event-received time, last-routed time, event rate, and speculative-to-confirmed confirmation rate to distinguish 'pipeline broken' (routing without events) from 'idle' (no routing). A dynamic TTL scorer (PR 3) then adapts automatically: no TTL config means permanent entries relying on index LRU capacity; when KV events are configured and flowing, short/precise TTL; when routing continues but events are delayed, TTL gradually increases; new config fields maxSpeculativeTTL and adaptiveTTL. PR 4 extends PodEntry with a Kind field distinguishing entry provenance and scoring weight: kv-event (confirmed from engine, weight 1.0), speculative (predicted from routing, weight 0.95), approximate (self-reported from response capture, weight 0.7) -- entries get promoted to kv-event once a real event confirms them. PR 2 (depends on #437's gen-counter) calls Index.Clear() when the health monitor detects a pod has disappeared, covering crash/scale-down cases where no AllBlocksCleared event arrives. PR 5 hashes response output tokens into approximate index entries on completion to capture multi-turn context. As of Sep 2026 the issue is stale/unimplemented (no linked merged PRs), flagged with an open question on whether it's a 'must have' for the next release. `evergreen` (720 words)
- [[dynamic-forwarding-load-balancing-policy-by-yanavlasov-pull-request-38757-envoyp]] — Dynamic forwarding load balancing policy by yanavlasov · Pull Request #38757 · envoyproxy/envoy · GitHub — Envoy PR #38757 (yanavlasov/Google, opened Mar 15 2025, merged) adds a new 'dynamic_forwarding' load-balancing-policy extension that lets an external endpoint picker (ext_proc filter) set PRIMARY and FALLBACK/retry candidate endpoints via request header or dynamic metadata, consumed at LB-selection time via chooseHost(), with a configurable fallback LB policy (e.g. round robin) used only if the ext_proc-set header/metadata is absent or fails to parse. This directly targets the InferencePool/EPP endpoint-picker-protocol use case (author links docs/proposals/004-endpoint-picker-protocol). The PR body contains the exact architectural debate the router-customization question hinges on: reviewer wbpcode initially argued the existing setUpstreamOverrideHost() API (single-host override, honored by all LB policies unless the host fails health checks) already does what's needed and a whole new LB policy was unnecessary complexity. Author yanavlasov countered that override-host + an HTTP filter cannot satisfy BOTH requirements simultaneously: (1) ext_proc-driven selection of primary AND retry/fallback hosts (avoiding 'stale retry endpoints' when only one override host is set) and (2) the longer-term goal of a full 'ext_lb' remote-callout protocol for asynchronous external load balancing. A second reviewer (posting independently, having built similar functionality) reiterated the override-host critique nearly verbatim: 'I didn't find anything setUpstreamOverrideHost cannot do (except the retry)... the story could [be] much simpler, the ext proc filter could set these endpoint candidates by the setUpstreamOverrideHost. This only enhancement necessarily is making setUpstreamOverrideHost could accept multiple addresses to support retry.' The fallback-endpoint header is later identified in the thread as x-gateway-destination-endpoint-fallback, distinct from the primary x-gateway-destination-endpoint header. CORRECTED FOLLOW-UP (per kgateway-dev/kgateway#11824, fetched separately): kgateway initially worked around the absence of override_host support with a 'static cluster + subset LB' hack to pass the EppUnAvailableFailOpen conformance test, and explicitly planned to refactor to override_host LB once its Envoy pin reached v1.35 (which ships this PR's feature) -- but that refactor issue was closed in Sep 2025 not because kgateway adopted override_host, but because 'the community decided to deprecate the Envoy-based data plane in favor of agentgateway for AI-related features.' So the override_host-vs-dynamic_forwarding debate was ultimately superseded, in at least one major GIE-integrated gateway, by abandoning Envoy-as-data-plane for AI/inference routing altogether in favor of agentgateway. This is a load-bearing fact for router customization strategy: it suggests that for llm-d + GIE, the durable extension point for non-pod-destination routing may increasingly be agentgateway's own destinationMode/passthrough config (PR #1658/#1429) rather than either of these two Envoy LB mechanisms. `evergreen` (6224 words)
- [[epp-ha-deployment-issue-692-kubernetes-sigsgateway-api-inference-extension]] — EPP HA deployment · Issue #692 · kubernetes-sigs/gateway-api-inference-extension — GIE GitHub issue #692 (Apr-Aug 2025, closed completed): tracks adding HA support for the Endpoint Picker (EPP), which runs as a single replica by default. Maintainers explicitly flag that 'with multiple active EPP replicas, we expect decreased performance on stateful operations such as queuing and prefix cache aware routing' because EPP state (in-flight counts, prefix-cache maps) is in-memory and per-replica; sharding InferencePools/InferenceModels across active-active EPPs is flagged as imperfect since multiple LoRA adapters can co-reside on the same pod. Resolved short-term via active-passive leader election (#1337); active-active sharding deferred to #1406. Maintainer-authored, not an independent benchmark, but a first-party admission that EPP horizontal scaling degrades the accuracy of the load/cache-affinity signals the router depends on. `evergreen` (1242 words)
- [[epp-http-headers-reference-llm-d]] — EPP HTTP Headers Reference | llm-d — llm-d Router v0.9 canonical EPP HTTP header reference. Priority/objective routing uses x-llm-d-inference-objective (names an InferenceObjective resource in the same namespace as the InferencePool, looked up by EPP for priority/performance goals); fairness/tenant grouping uses x-llm-d-inference-fairness-id (defaults to 'default-flow' if omitted); model-name routing/override uses x-llm-d-model-name-rewrite (an explicit per-request alternative to the server-side rule-based InferenceModelRewrite API). SLO headers x-llm-d-slo-ttft-ms and x-llm-d-slo-tpot-ms drive admission control. Deprecated aliases (x-gateway-inference-objective, x-gateway-inference-fairness-id, x-gateway-model-name-rewrite, x-slo-ttft-ms, x-slo-tpot-ms) are still accepted for reads in v0.9.0+ with canonical value winning on conflict; no removal date announced. Explicitly states GAIE Endpoint Picker Protocol headers like x-gateway-destination-endpoint* are unchanged/untouched by this rename and belong to a separate spec. Implementation notes state header lookups are case-insensitive and 'these values are typically provided as standard HTTP headers in the incoming request' -- i.e., the doc frames header origin generically as request headers, not as something exclusively derived from a Kubernetes CRD reconciler, though the InferenceObjective/InferenceModelRewrite names they carry still resolve against namespaced K8s custom resources. `evergreen` (759 words)
- [[efficient-memory-management-for-large-language]] — Efficient Memory Management for Large Language — The original PagedAttention/vLLM paper (Kwon et al., UC Berkeley/Stanford/UCSD, SOSP 2023, arXiv 2309.06180) — foundational prior art for all KV-cache-aware routing discussed elsewhere in this batch, and the mechanism llm-d's own KV-cache manager builds on. Proposes PagedAttention, an attention algorithm inspired by OS virtual memory paging: KV cache is stored in fixed-size, non-contiguous 'pages' (blocks) referenced via a block table, eliminating internal/external memory fragmentation and enabling near-zero KV cache memory waste. This block-based storage also enables flexible KV cache sharing within and across requests (e.g., parallel sampling, beam search, shared system prompts) via copy-on-write block reference counting. Built on top of PagedAttention, vLLM improves throughput of popular LLMs by 2-4x at the same latency compared to then-SOTA systems FasterTransformer and Orca, with the improvement more pronounced for longer sequences, larger models, and more complex decoding algorithms. This block/page abstraction and the block-hash-based cache lookup it enables is the direct ancestor of the block-hashing and prefix-tree matching schemes used by SGLang RadixAttention, NVIDIA Dynamo's KV router, AIBrix, and llm-d's KV cache manager. `evergreen` (13082 words)
- [[endpoint-picker-epp-llm-d]] — Endpoint Picker (EPP) | llm-d — llm-d docs page describing the EPP ('brains' of llm-d) as extensible via Envoy ext-proc, with 4 pluggable components: Request Handler (Parser plugins for OpenAI/vLLM-gRPC formats, custom parsers supported), Flow Control (SaturationDetector, FairnessPolicy, OrderingPolicy plugins), Request Scheduler (Filter -> Score -> Pick pipeline orchestrated by a ProfileHandler plugin), and Data Layer (async, watches the Kubernetes API server for InferencePool/Pod updates, probes Model Server metrics, maintains prefix-cache trees, and integrates 'consultant' sidecars like latency predictor/kv-indexer/tokenizer). States the Kube API watch and Pod-based endpoint discovery explicitly as the Data Layer's job. EPP is configured via EndpointPickerConfig YAML passed with --config-file/--config-text, read only at startup (no hot reload). `evergreen` (1085 words)
- [[endpoint-metadata-updates-do-not-trigger-endpoint-notification-events-issue-1403]] — Endpoint metadata updates do not trigger endpoint notification events · Issue #1403 · llm-d/llm-d-router — GitHub issue #1403 (llm-d-router, opened May 30 2026, closed Jun 1 2026 via PR #1404, shipped in v0.9.0): the datastore only dispatches the EventAddOrUpdate notification through endpoint-notification-source when an endpoint is FIRST added (via Runtime.NewEndpoint()); when an existing endpoint's metadata changes (address, labels, rank, ports), the datastore calls Endpoint.UpdateMetadata(meta) but returns without dispatching any event. Plugins that subscribe to endpoint-notification-source — explicitly including precise-prefix-cache components that install/refresh per-endpoint KV-events (ZMQ) subscribers keyed on endpoint address/port — can silently hold stale state after such changes. Fix (merged): dispatch EventAddOrUpdate on metadata change too, skipping dispatch only when new metadata is identical. Directly relevant to a custom (non-k8s) EndpointDiscovery/datastore implementation: it must fire update notifications on metadata mutation, not just on add, or downstream plugins (esp. KV-cache-event subscribers) will not know an endpoint's address/port changed. `evergreen` (419 words)
- [[endpointslices-kubernetes]] — EndpointSlices | Kubernetes — Canonical Kubernetes docs on the EndpointSlice API (stable since v1.21). Key facts for building selectorless-Service backends: EndpointSlices are auto-created only for Services that have a selector; for a selectorless Service you must create EndpointSlice objects directly, and the docs explicitly say to do this rather than relying on the deprecated (since v1.33) Endpoints-to-EndpointSlice mirroring path. EndpointSlices are grouped by IP family/protocol/port/Service name, capped at 100 endpoints per slice by default (configurable up to 1000 via kube-controller-manager --max-endpoints-per-slice flag), and are the literal source of truth kube-proxy reads for routing (not the legacy Endpoints object). Each endpoint carries serving/terminating/ready conditions and optional nodeName/zone topology fields. Ownership is via a kubernetes.io/service-name label plus owner reference, and multiple controllers can co-manage EndpointSlices for one Service via the endpointslice.kubernetes.io/managed-by label. `evergreen` (1344 words)
- [[enterprise-litellm]] — Enterprise | liteLLM — LiteLLM's own canonical statement of the OSS/Enterprise boundary -- the single most direct piece of boundary evidence in this batch. Architecture is layered, not forked: 'Enterprise is a license key on the same Gateway image you may already run... each layer includes everything below it' (Python SDK OSS -> AI Gateway OSS self-hosted, adds budgets+rate limits -> Enterprise license key, adds org/team admins + per-team logging). Enterprise is explicitly scoped for '100+ users or 10+ production AI use cases'; SSO is free for up to 5 users, beyond that an Enterprise license is required. Full enterprise feature list spans four buckets: (1) Security/access -- SSO (Okta/Azure AD/Google Workspace/OIDC/SAML), JWT auth, audit logs with retention, RBAC (Organizations/teams/roles), IP allowlisting, automated virtual-key rotation, external secret managers (AWS KMS/Secrets Manager, Azure Key Vault, Google KMS, HashiCorp Vault, CyberArk); (2) Governance/cost -- Multi-tenant Architecture (orgs/teams/projects/keys), Project Management (budget+owners+rate-limits+model-allowlist per project), Tag-based Budgets, Model-specific Budgets per Virtual Key, Temporary Budget Increases, Soft Budget Email Alerts, programmatic Spend Reports; (3) Observability/compliance -- SOC 2 Type II audit, Team-Based Logging (route each team's logs to its own Langfuse project), per-team logging opt-out (GDPR), log export to GCS/Azure Blob, several named enterprise-only guardrail integrations; (4) Operations/branding -- custom API-docs branding, custom email branding, request/response size limits, team-managed models (bring-your-own-key/fine-tune). Explicitly names which built-in guardrail callbacks require a license: llmguard_moderations, llamaguard_moderations, hide_secrets, openai_moderations, google_text_moderation, lakera_prompt_injection, aporia_prompt_injection (Presidio PII masking and custom guardrails remain OSS). Confirms fully self-hosted/air-gapped operation is explicit: 'No data leaves your environment,' SOC 2 Type II audited, Docker images cryptographically signed and independently verifiable, procurable via AWS/Azure Marketplace or direct invoice -- this is the closest any source in this batch comes to an explicit air-gap claim for a self-hosted LLM gateway. `review` (1357 words)
- [[envoy-ai-gateway-hits-10-what-an-llm-aware-kubernetes-gateway-actually-load-bala]] — Envoy AI Gateway Hits 1.0: What an LLM-Aware Kubernetes Gateway Actually Load-Balances On | bex.co — Practitioner deep-dive (bex.co, published ~Sept 2026) framing Envoy AI Gateway's v1.0 GA (June 23, 2026) around the question of what an inference-aware gateway sees that a generic HTTP LB does not. Gives a named table of 6 routing signals with explicit contrast against generic L7: per-backend KV-cache utilization (generic LB sees 'nothing -- cache state is invisible outside the model server'), queue depth/pending tokens (generic LB sees only connection count), prompt-prefix affinity ('nothing -- request bodies are opaque to L7 routing'), loaded LoRA adapter ('nothing -- same endpoint, different weights'), prefill vs decode role in a disaggregated fleet ('nothing -- all pods look identical'), and predicted time-to-first-token (generic LB has only lagging past response times). Cites two quantified benchmarks: llm-d project reports up to 57x faster time-to-first-token and 2x throughput vs round-robin under high prefix reuse on 8 pods; a separate 16-GPU case study found inference-aware routing doubled served users on the same hardware with zero new hardware purchased. Describes the three-part GIE architecture (InferencePool + InferenceModel CRDs, an out-of-process Endpoint Picker/EPP scoring service, and Envoy ext-proc as the glue) and names conforming gateways: Envoy Gateway, kgateway, and GKE Gateway (Google's managed offering routes on KV-cache usage across clusters). Names the single production adopter explicitly: LY Corporation runs Envoy AI Gateway in front of multi-tenant self-hosted LLM traffic, citing unified routing, token-based rate limiting, authentication, and InferenceExtension alignment. Documents ecosystem breadth of independent EPP implementations: llm-d's inference scheduler (prefix-aware KV-cache routing, prefill/decode disaggregation, pluggable scorers), NVIDIA Dynamo's own EPP (scores on KV-cache hit probability), and Microsoft KAITO which overrides the default EPP image with the llm-d scheduler for managed inference. Reports Istio shipped alpha GIE support in v1.27 and announced beta (with ambient multicluster beta) at KubeCon EU 2026 (March 25), gated behind the ENABLE_GATEWAY_API_INFERENCE_EXTENSION pilot flag -- meaning inference-aware routing is now a service-mesh feature, not gateway-only. Explicitly scopes what the gateway does NOT do: clarifies 'AI Gateway' here is a self-hosted-model routing layer, not a hosted-API reseller/proxy -- it does not call third-party model APIs or mark up tokens; states inference-aware routing gains concentrate in three situations (shared prompt prefixes, mixed request lengths, GPU-constrained fleets) and is pointless for single-replica or pure-hosted-API traffic. `review` (2160 words)
- [[envoy-ai-gateway-reaches-v10-establishing-the-open-source-standard-for-enterpris]] — Envoy AI Gateway Reaches v1.0, Establishing the Open Source Standard for Enterprise AI Traffic — Official press release (PRNewswire, June 23 2026, issued by Tetrate) announcing Envoy AI Gateway v1.0 GA -- the primary corporate-voice source for who built and runs this project. Names the exact corporate maintainer set explicitly: Tetrate (primary upstream Envoy contributor), Bloomberg (proposed the effort to the Envoy Gateway community 'to address its own challenge: routing generative AI workloads'), and Nutanix; project first released February 2025, reaching v1.0 after '16 months' of collaboration. Two named production adopters with direct quotes: Bloomberg runs it in production ('demonstrating the same open source code available to the community is stable and scalable enough to power demanding enterprise workloads' -- quote from Dan Sun, Envoy AI Gateway/KServe co-founder) and LY Corporation, quoted via principal AI-infrastructure architect Shingo Omura, uses it to manage 'multi-tenant, self-hosted LLM traffic' citing 'a unified API for flexible routing, token-based rate limiting, authentication and authorization, monitoring and extensibility, aligned with open standards like the Kubernetes Gateway API Inference Extension.' Nutanix is incorporating it into its own Nutanix Agent Gateway and Nutanix Enterprise AI products. Lists the v1.0 feature set verbatim by category: unified OpenAI-compatible API across OpenAI/Anthropic/Bedrock/Vertex/Gemini/Azure OpenAI/Groq/Together/Mistral/Cohere/DeepSeek/SambaNova; a native MCP Gateway (server multiplexing, tool include/exclude filtering, OAuth2 + JWT claim forwarding, CEL-based fine-grained authz, per-tool observability); token-aware traffic management (separate cost attribution for input/output/cached/reasoning tokens, route-scoped costs, a QuotaPolicy API for quota-aware routing around rate-limited upstreams); centralized upstream credential management (API keys plus AWS SDK default credential chain -- IRSA, EKS Pod Identity, instance profiles); AI-native observability (OpenInference tracing + OpenTelemetry GenAI semantic conventions); and a stable v1 CRD surface: AIGatewayRoute, AIServiceBackend, BackendSecurityPolicy, GatewayConfig, MCPRoute, MCPRouteSecurityPolicy. Explicitly states what's still roadmap, not shipped at v1.0: dollar-based (not just token-based) spend-limit cost governance, and deeper MCP authorization/identity (OIDC token exchange for MCP backends, finer-grained policy across tools/resources/prompts) -- confirming per-team budget caps and granular MCP identity were gaps as of the v1.0 GA date. `review` (1461 words)
- [[evaluate-llm-d-router-both-epp-and-the-standalone-proxy-scale-limits-issue-1290]] — Evaluate llm-d Router, both EPP and the standalone proxy, scale limits · Issue #1290 · llm-d/llm-d-router — Scale/benchmark evaluation issue (opened May 2026, closed Jul 21 2026), NOT a design doc — contains no statement that the precise prefix-cache index is partitioned across replicas and no mention of peer coordination/peer discovery being required for Active-Active correctness. It is the target of docs/operations.md's 'For more technical details and context on EPP replica state sync and scaling limitations, see Issue #1290' but the issue itself never uses the phrase 'replica state sync' nor discusses index partitioning; it is pure QPS/latency benchmarking. Key benchmark data (madhugoutham, EPP v0.8.0, llm-d-inference-sim v0.8.0, real vLLM v0.19.1 Qwen2.5-7B): Active-Active CPU-simulator scaling is near-linear to ~1,650 QPS at 4 replicas (1r ~478, 2r ~959, 3r ~1,308, 4r ~1,650) but collapses at 2,000 target QPS (only 1,642 achieved, 4,767 failures, TTFT P50 4,565ms) -- a capacity ceiling, not a correctness failure. Critically, the 'Precise prefix cache active-active scaling' table with real GPU vLLM backends shows failures at 500 QPS DECREASING as replicas increase: 1r=broken/3,148 failures, 2r=57, 3r=31, 4r=18, with TTFT P50 dropping from 165ms(1r) to 30ms(4r) -- empirical evidence that precise prefix-cache routing scales well and gets BETTER (not worse) with more Active-Active replicas, which supports rather than overturns the draft's claim. Separately documents a 'Leader election trap': the standalone Helm chart auto-injects --ha-enable-leader-election when replicas>1, silently forcing active-passive behavior unless explicitly disabled. Session-affinity-filter (#815) was tested only with synthetic INDEPENDENT requests (no x-session-token echoing) -- the issue explicitly flags that validating session-pinning correctness across active-active replicas 'requires trace replay with session header echoing, which is a separate evaluation' that was never done in this issue. No mention anywhere of LRU/no-hit-lru scorer state, predicted-latency training state, or saturation-view state differing per replica beyond what docs/operations.md itself already states (flow-control/saturation state is per-replica, not shared). `evergreen` (1639 words)
- [[external-processing-envoy-1400-dev-13b0a8-documentation]] — External Processing — envoy 1.40.0-dev-13b0a8 documentation — Envoy reference docs for the ext_proc HTTP filter, the wire protocol GIE's EPP uses to intercept requests: a bidirectional gRPC stream where the external processor (EPP) receives ProcessingRequest and replies ProcessingResponse, able to mutate headers/body/trailers or return an immediate response. Documents session-affinity configuration (RING_HASH/MAGLEV hashing on a copied header or cookie so repeated calls land on the same EPP instance) and the filter's exposed statistics, notably 'failure_mode_allowed' (count of times an ext_proc error was ignored due to configuration) and 'message_timeouts' — these are the Envoy-level counters that make ext_proc's fail-open/fail-closed behavior observable, directly relevant to whether an EPP outage causes the gateway to pass traffic through unchecked (fail-open) or reject it (fail-closed). Access-log filter-state fields (request_header_call_status, latencies) let operators trace whether/why a given request's endpoint-picker call failed. `evergreen` (1174 words)
- [[filediscovery-plugin-44-wiring-into-the-runner-by-ezrasilvera-pull-request-1218]] — FileDiscovery plugin (4/4) - wiring into the runner by ezrasilvera · Pull Request #1218 · llm-d/llm-d-router · GitHub — PR #1218 'FileDiscovery plugin (4/4) - wiring into the runner' (opened May 19 2026 by ezrasilvera), the PR that first wired an EndpointDiscovery plugin into cmd/epp/runner.go and made 'run EPP/llm-d without Kubernetes' real. Shows exactly what any custom discovery plugin (K8s or non-K8s) must satisfy: (1) implement the fwkdl.EndpointDiscovery interface (pkg/epp/framework/interface/datalayer/discovery.go), which as of this PR requires both Start(ctx, notifier DiscoveryNotifier) error and a newly-added Ready() <-chan struct{} method -- Ready's channel must be closed exactly once, only after a successful initial sync (e.g. first file load or first K8s list+watch reconcile), and must stay open if Start errors before that point; (2) plug into TypedName()/plugin.Plugin via a Factory(name, parameters, handle) function registered with fwkplugin.Register. When dataLayer.discovery(.endpoints).pluginRef is set, runner.go's Run() skips controller-manager setup entirely, runs discovery + ext_proc/health servers together under a RunnableGroup, and gates request-serving (epp-server, health) -- but not /metrics -- on Ready() closing; on initial-load failure the runnable group cancels via ctx.Done() instead. The FileDiscovery reference implementation (pkg/epp/framework/plugins/datalayer/discovery/file/plugin.go) satisfies Ready() with a sync.Once-guarded channel closed right after its first f.load(notifier) call succeeds. A reviewer flagged (and the PR discussion addresses) a double-instantiation risk: the discovery plugin is already created via loader.InstantiateAndConfigure and registered in r.PluginHandle during parseConfigurationPhaseTwo, so resolveDiscovery() must look it up rather than re-invoke its factory. `evergreen` (3575 words)
- [[fineserve-a-fine-grained-dataset-and-characterization-of]] — FineServe: A Fine-Grained Dataset and Characterization of — Tianjin University researchers with PPIO Cloud (a Chinese multi-model GPU-serving marketplace) release FineServe, an in-the-wild, multi-model LLM serving workload dataset: 4 months, 55 models, 10 model series, 1.48 billion requests, covering both Dense Transformer and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures across 4 model-scale tiers and 10 distinct task intents (writing, coding, role-playing, etc.). This is a multi-tenant commercial GPU marketplace trace (not single-enterprise internal use) but directly informs capacity-planning/routing design that internal operators running heterogeneous multi-model fleets face. Comparison table against prior trace releases: ServeGen (4 months, 12 models, 3 series, 3.54B requests, no architecture/task-intent labeling) and BurstGPT (4 months, 2 models, 2 series, 5.29M requests, no architecture/scale/task labeling) -- FineServe is the only one of the three to label model architecture (dense vs MoE), scale tier, and task intent per request.

KEY FINDINGS: workload arrival and token-length fluctuation regimes differ fundamentally by model architecture and scale -- dense vs MoE models exhibit distinct prefill/decode system behavior (MoE exploits expert parallelism for higher prefill utilization but suffers expert-imbalance/cross-device-communication contention during memory-bandwidth-bound decoding), and smaller models sustain higher concurrency with larger batches but show more demand-driven, volatile traffic with sharper and more frequent spikes than larger models. Task intent (chat/role-play vs coding vs others) materially changes prompt/response token geometry and latency sensitivity (e.g., multi-turn chat/role-play has stricter perceived TTFT requirements; coding requests show templated prompts and longer context carry-over), leading the authors to conclude that characterizing serving workloads by aggregate arrival rate alone is insufficient for capacity planning. The paper builds a workload generator from these fine-grained patterns for benchmarking multi-model routing/scheduling/capacity-planning strategies. Dataset and generator released at github.com/hihiztc1/FineServe. `review` (9353 words)
- [[flash-indexer-a-story-of-inter-galactic-kv-routing-nvidia-dynamo-documentation]] — Flash Indexer: A Story of Inter-Galactic KV Routing | NVIDIA Dynamo Documentation — NVIDIA Dynamo engineering blog documenting the design evolution of the Flash Indexer, the default global KV-block index shipped in Dynamo v1.0.0, which sustains over 100 million operations/second and is presented as an alternative KV-event-synchronization architecture to compare against llm-d's own routing/indexer design. Every cached block carries a local (chunk) hash (position-independent, content-only) and a sequence hash (rolling hash over the full prefix, position-dependent) plus a worker ID; a sidecar publisher per engine emits explicit Store(worker_id, local_hash, seq_hash) and Remove(worker_id, seq_hash) events over pub/sub because eviction policies (LRU sweeps, memory pressure, preemption) are opaque and cannot be inferred from request/response cycles alone. The design went through six iterations: (1) a Python nested dict, O(W*D) per query; (2) a single-threaded Rust actor eliminating interpreter overhead but serializing all reads behind writes; (3) an inverted index (local_hash -> seq_hash -> worker set) cutting query cost to O(D+W) but reintroducing rolling-hash computation on reads and expensive full-index scans on removes; (4) a radix tree keyed by parent-child local-hash relationships (requiring a new parent-hash field in KV events) that scopes hash-collision risk to shared-prefix siblings only; (5) a concurrent radix tree using Arc<RwLock> plus DashMap-sharded per-worker lookup tables and sticky per-worker-ID thread routing for writes, removing the actor bottleneck for reads (parking_lot::RwLock cited as 2-3x faster than std due to no syscall on uncontended paths); (6) the Flash Indexer itself, a Vec<DashMap> positional index enabling O(1) random access to any prefix position and a 'jump search' that skips ahead by a tunable jump_size (default 64) and only backtracks to find exact worker drain points on a partial-cardinality mismatch, giving amortized O(D/J + W) query cost. Benchmarked on a 24-core Arrow Lake desktop replaying the public Mooncake FAST'25 production trace (16,384 mock GPU blocks, 24 concurrent event-processing threads), the Flash Indexer sustains 170M ops/s -- 42x faster than the radix tree shipped in Dynamo v0.1.0 (4M ops/s) and 440x faster than the naive nested-dict implementation (385K ops/s). `evergreen` (3460 words)
- [[flow-control-5-minute-pluginstate-staleness-reaper-destroys-in-flight-accounting]] — Flow Control: 5-minute PluginState staleness reaper destroys in-flight accounting for live requests, collapsing the saturation gate under sustained load · Issue #2295 · llm-d/llm-d-router — GitHub issue #2295 on llm-d/llm-d-router (opened Aug 6 2026, contributor-authored incident report, closed as bug fix): a detailed, instrumented reproduction of catastrophic prefix-cache-affinity-driven hotspotting under sustained load in llm-d's Flow Control system. Setup: 8-replica vLLM pool (Qwen3-Coder-480B-A35B-Instruct-FP8, TP=4 on GB200), 1 EPP replica, concurrency-detector saturation gate (maxConcurrency=12/endpoint, 96 aggregate), agentic multi-turn traces up to 262k-token contexts, session arrival 1qps->2qps burst->1qps->0.1qps->1qps over ~2.5 hours. Root cause: a hardcoded 5-minute PluginState 'staleness reaper' evicts in-flight-accounting entries for requests still queued past 5 minutes, decrementing counters while the request still occupies the server; the saturation gate then sees phantom free capacity and dispatches more work, deepening queues further -- a self-accelerating loop that does not recover when load drops.
