Documented numbers: engine occupancy (running+waiting across 8 pods) rose from a healthy steady-state of 23-32 to ~1000 within 55 minutes of a 10-minute 2x burst and ~1700 by run end; EPP's own in-flight count read only 40-60% of reality, decaying ~90/minute with no completions; 24% of 18,699 requests (4,485) ended with TTFT above 300s; a 15-minute near-idle period (0.1 qps) failed to drain the backlog (occupancy only fell from ~1000 to ~700). Critically, the excess load was NOT spread evenly: prefix-cache-affinity concentrated it onto specific pods because every session turn's full conversation prefix pulls it back to the pod holding those KV blocks -- per-pod waiting depth data shows one pod's waiting queue growing from 2 to 64 to 159 to 201 while others emptied to near-zero, because the affinity scorer's load signal was itself corrupted by the same reaping bug (a reaped hot pod 'looks lightly loaded,' so both the affinity trade-off and the load scorer keep selecting it). The author states this is a real mechanism, not a synthetic corner case: 'in 51 minutes of healthy-plus-burst operation, 0 of 8545 requests waited [5 minutes]... The 5-minute waits were then manufactured by the gate's own burst-overshoot behavior.' This is the single most detailed, numerically load-bearing evidence in the corpus of llm-d's cache-affinity routing catastrophically concentrating load onto specific replicas under sustained/bursty conditions, though the trigger is a specific flow-control accounting bug (fixed in this issue) rather than a fundamental property of affinity routing itself. `evergreen` (3298 words)
- [[from-llm-inference-to-agentic-workloads]] — From LLM Inference to Agentic Workloads: — HKUST researchers with Alibaba Group and ByteDance engineers build AgentSysBench, a measurement toolkit + benchmark of 10 representative agentic applications (coding, research, RAG, search, assistant agents) with unified systems-level instrumentation, combining controlled deployments (4,641 benchmark requests, 64,924 LLM calls, 118,274 tool calls) with real production traces (178,799 production sessions in a single day, including 373,678 search queries from one agentic-search application and 4,389 web fetches from an Openclaw-like application).

SIX MEASURED PROPERTIES THAT DISTINGUISH AGENTIC WORKLOADS FROM CONVENTIONAL LLM SERVING (the paper's central contribution, each independently load-bearing):
(1) Execution is heavyweight and stateful -- non-LLM components (retrieval, sandboxes, tool execution) dominate end-to-end latency in 5 of the 10 benchmarked applications, and sandbox working-set memory peaks at 28 GB per session, meaning model inference is 'no longer the sole cost center.'
(2) Components have heterogeneous resource affinity (GPU-bound inference, memory-bound retrieval, CPU-bound sandboxes) whose task latencies diverge by up to 32x within a single application pipeline -- e.g. a RAG pipeline's bottleneck shifts between embedding and vector-database operations as inputs change, so application-level latency averages mask large per-request variance.
(3) Bottlenecks shift across requests, models, and deployments rather than sitting at a fixed stage.
(4) Production sessions hold state idle for minutes to hours between active steps (observed only in production traces, not controlled benchmarks) -- these sessions are neither finished nor actively consuming compute, but treating them as finished loses resumable state, while treating them as live wastes memory/disk.
(5) A 'control-plane tax': auxiliary LLM calls plus context overhead from tool schemas, raw observations, and safety checks crowds out productive compute and context, growing over multi-step sessions.
(6) Heavy cross-request redundancy in production: among 373,678 search queries from one production agentic-search app, 27% of unique queries recur and account for 67.3% of all search API calls; in a separate production app issuing 4,389 web fetches, 24% of distinct URLs recur and account for 64% of fetch invocations -- redundancy invisible to single-task synthetic benchmarks.

FOUR DESIGN INTERVENTIONS DERIVED FROM THESE FINDINGS, WITH MEASURED GAINS: task-disaggregated/task-aware serving cuts latency 29-40%; communication-aware (agent-aware) co-location placement improves up to 4.5x; state offloading reduces memory usage by 4.6x; tool-result caching removes 35.2% of redundant search calls and saves 19.3% of aggregate search latency. The paper's implication for serving-system design: systems built on conventional LLM-inference assumptions (model inference as sole cost center, request-level statelessness, fixed bottleneck location) misallocate resources for agentic workloads; serving systems need context-budget-aware tool interfaces, a first-class 'waiting' lifecycle state distinct from 'done', and query/object-level caching for external tool calls. `review` (13577 words)
- [[from-predictive-to-generative-how-michelangelo-accelerates-ubers-ai-journey]] — From Predictive to Generative – How Michelangelo Accelerates Uber’s AI Journey — Uber AI Platform blog (May 2, 2024 — stale by the run's 18-24mo recency window; mostly an 8-year history of the predictive-ML platform Michelangelo, with only a brief closing section on GenAI/LLMs). Historical/architectural context: Michelangelo serves 5,000+ production models, 10M real-time predictions/sec peak; control plane follows the Kubernetes Operator pattern; offline pipelines run on Ray or Spark; DL model serving migrated from the deprecated in-house Neuropod engine to NVIDIA Triton Inference Server (OSS) because Neuropod lacked continued community support. GPU scheduling is migrating from Peloton/Mesos to Kubernetes, with an in-house 'job federation layer' hiding region/zone/cluster details across multiple Kubernetes clusters (built as a job CRD controller). For the 2023-era GenAI phase, the article states Uber built the Gen AI Gateway (same project profiled in Uber's July 2024 genai-gateway post) and extended Michelangelo for LLMOps: a Ray-based LLM trainer integrating Hugging Face Hub models and PEFT library; DeepSpeed added for model parallelism (previously capped at ~4B params on a 16GB GPU); Ray clusters provisioned on GPUs via the Michelangelo job controller for elastic LLM training. States explicitly: 'fine-tuned open-source models are hosted in-house' (e.g., Llama2) alongside third-party APIs (GPT-4, PaLM). Does not name the in-house LLM inference serving engine used for the generative workloads beyond this training-side detail — flagged as an unstated component. States a build-vs-buy principle: 'OSS solutions may be prioritized over proprietary solutions' and that XGBoost outperforms DL in several cases on both cost and performance. `review` (6108 words)
- [[from-production-traffic-to-post-training-building-a-self-hosted-llm]] — From Production Traffic to Post-Training: Building a Self-Hosted LLM — T-Tech (Russian fintech, formerly Tinkoff) describes consolidating 200+ internal applications' LLM traffic onto a single self-hosted Qwen3-32B-based model to fight GPU-pool fragmentation under data-residency constraints. The serving stack itself is off-the-shelf: single-GPU FP8 replicas behind vLLM (Kwon et al. 2023), scaled 16-48 pods, serving 116M requests/month (45 avg / 110 peak RPS) with p95 latency 3.2s and TTFT 0.3s. The custom engineering is entirely on the model/eval side, not the serving engine: production-traffic error taxonomy (instruction-following/formatting failures = 37.9% of failures, tool-calling requests = ~12% of traffic), in-house stratified benchmarks scored by deterministic verifiers or calibrated LLM judges, and a per-axis GRPO expert (instruction-following, function-calling, task-distribution) merged via two-stage SLERP to avoid cross-domain reward interference. The consolidated model absorbed 50% of platform traffic within 6 months and cut per-token serving cost 2.8-3.9x (up to 4-9x versus prior largest-model deployments) while matching or beating a ~7x larger baseline (Qwen3-235B-A22B-Instruct-2507) on in-house Arena (69.6 vs 65.8), instruction-following (0.85 vs 0.83) and function-calling (0.79 vs 0.77). Rollbacks came only from teams needing frontier-scale agentic capability beyond a 32B dense model. `review` (18521 words)
- [[gke-inference-gateway-kv-cache-aware-llm-routing-explained-spheron-blog]] — GKE Inference Gateway: KV-Cache-Aware LLM Routing Explained | Spheron Blog — Vendor blog (Spheron, low authority) on Google's GKE Inference Gateway (announced Google Cloud Next 2025), explicitly stating it is 'powered by the llm-d Endpoint Picker (EPP)' and that 'the EPP is not proprietary. It ships in the open-source gateway-api-inference-extension repository under kubernetes-sigs... The routing logic, the CRDs, and the vLLM integration are identical whether you run on GKE or on your own Kubernetes cluster' -- direct evidence that GCP wraps/deploys the same upstream EPP binary rather than reimplementing it, with only the managed control plane, GCLB provisioning, and IAM integration being GCP-specific. Documents EPP's fallback behavior: when no replica has a strong prefix match, it falls back to routing on vllm:kv_cache_usage_perc. Provides a worked cost example: round-robin across 8 replicas yields ~12.5% per-replica cache hit rate for a shared system prompt; a 128K-token system prompt on a 70B FP8 model costs ~11s prefill, so 1,000 req/hr with round-robin wastes ~11,000 GPU-seconds/hr vs <2,000 GPU-seconds/hr with KV-aware routing. Notes cache-hit-rate advantage of KV-aware routing improves (not degrades) as replica count scales up, the opposite of round-robin. `evergreen` (3810 words)
- [[gpu-cloud-finops-for-ai-teams-cost-allocation-per-project-chargeback-and-tag-bas]] — GPU Cloud FinOps for AI Teams: Cost Allocation, Per-Project Chargeback, and Tag-Based Budgeting (2026) | Spheron Blog — Spheron (GPU cloud vendor) FinOps guide on cost allocation for GPU/AI workloads -- scope-filtered here to the SOFTWARE attribution mechanism only (per the research scope excluding hardware/pricing/SKU content, though the source is heavy on Spheron's own GPU rate cards, which are disregarded). Names four cost-allocation models: per-namespace (one K8s namespace per team, DCGM metrics relabeled on the namespace label -- simplest, works for 3-8 teams, formula team_cost = namespace_gpu_seconds/total_gpu_seconds * cluster_cost); per-tenant (multiple teams sharing inference pods behind a proxy layer -- names LiteLLM, Kong, or nginx injecting tenant headers before requests reach vLLM, attribution either token-weighted or request-weighted, stating token-weighting is 'almost always more accurate' since context length varies cost per request); per-token (most granular, reads vLLM's /metrics endpoint counters vllm:prompt_tokens_total and vllm:generation_tokens_total labeled by model name); and per-experiment (training-run attribution via K8s Job labels, Slurm --comment JSON parsed with sacct, or Ray runtime_env env vars). Prescribes a five-dimension tagging schema (team, project, environment, model-name, cost-center) applied consistently from K8s labels through DCGM Exporter through Prometheus to Grafana, explicitly warning that 'DCGM Exporter does not automatically join Kubernetes pod labels onto GPU metrics' -- it requires an explicit Prometheus relabeling rule. For the shared-vLLM scenario it describes a concrete architecture: 'LiteLLM proxy in front of vLLM, configured with virtual keys per team. Each virtual key carries a x-team-id and x-project-id header that LiteLLM injects on every request before forwarding to vLLM,' with LiteLLM's request logs joined to vLLM's Prometheus metrics for per-team token volumes. Flags prefix-caching as an attribution edge case: cache-shared prompt-prefix tokens are processed once but logged against whichever request triggered the cache write, so the source recommends routing prefix-cached tokens to a platform-overhead pool rather than crediting/charging any single team. Recommends showback (attribution-only reporting, no money movement) for 4-6 weeks before chargeback (real budget transfer), citing tagging coverage below 80% as the main signal chargeback is premature. `review` (3805 words)
- [[gpu-memory-snapshots-supercharging-sub-second-startup]] — GPU Memory Snapshots: Supercharging sub-second startup — Modal's own engineering blog post announcing GPU memory snapshots (alpha feature, 2026), the primary first-person source for the checkpoint/restore cold-start mitigation. IMPORTANT PROVENANCE CORRECTION: this post's own measured vLLM example is Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct cold boot dropping from 45s (P0) to 5s (P0) with GPU memory snapshots -- NOT the '460s -> ~70s' figure that the secondary dreaming.press article attributed to 'Modal's GPU memory snapshots.' That 460s/70s number more likely traces to Modal's earlier, separate CPU-only 'Memory Snapshots: Checkpoint/Restore for Sub-second Startup' post (linked from this one, introduced January 2025, https://modal.com/blog/mem-snapshots) rather than this GPU-snapshot feature -- the secondary source appears to have conflated the two Modal posts. Treat the 460s/70s figure as unverified pending that earlier post, and cite this post's own numbers (45s->5s Qwen2.5-0.5B vLLM; NVIDIA Parakeet ASR 20s->2s; ViT inference with torch.compile 8.5s->2.25s) as the directly sourced data. Technical mechanism: GPU memory snapshots extend Modal's existing CPU memory-snapshot checkpoint/restore system using NVIDIA's CUDA checkpoint/restore API (available on driver branches 570/575) to capture device VRAM contents (including model weights), CUDA kernels, CUDA objects/streams/contexts, and memory mappings -- not just CPU state -- so a restored container skips torch.compile recompilation (hardware-dependent optimized code is restored as-is), CUDA kernel loading, and CUDA graph capture entirely, not just weight loading. Checkpoint sequence: lock all CUDA processes (cuCheckpointProcessLock, blocking new calls until in-flight CUDA streams finish) -> checkpoint GPU+CPU memory and CUDA state to host memory, releasing GPU resources (cuCheckpointProcessCheckpoint) -> on restore, reverse via cuCheckpointProcessRestore + cuCheckpointProcessUnlock; integrated with Modal's existing gVisor-based checkpoint/restore system, with cross-host restore supported given compatible GPU hardware. Prior to this feature, GPU state had to be rebuilt post-restore via a two-stage snap=True/snap=False lifecycle (load to CPU, snapshot, then separately move to GPU) -- broken by any torch.cuda.is_available() check or CUDA session creation before restore; this is explicitly replaced by a single-stage model: load directly to GPU, snapshot everything. States Functions started 'up to 10x faster than baseline' across tested workloads; explicitly frames scale-to-zero viability as the business case: 'you can now really take advantage of serverless and scale down to zero while still maintaining great user experience.' `review` (1330 words)
- [[gartner-predicts-ai-inference-costs-per-agentic-workflow-will-increase-more-than]] — Gartner Predicts AI Inference Costs Per Agentic Workflow Will Increase More Than Fivefold Through 2028 — Gartner press release (Aug 17 2026) previewing an analyst report, forecasting that AI inference costs per agentic workflow will increase more than fivefold through 2028. Names this dynamic the 'Inference Paradox': falling per-token prices are not reducing total AI spend because (1) foundation-model cost economics are improving, but (2) that efficiency unlocks deployment of more powerful, more expensive models for higher-value applications, and (3) agentic workflows consume far more tokens than simple chatbot interactions -- so capability/cost growth outpaces the falling token-price curve. Quantifies that routing a task to an agentic reasoning model increases provider inference cost by 'at least five times' a basic chatbot interaction, more as task complexity grows. Gartner analyst Will Sommer (Sr. Director Analyst) is quoted arguing there is 'no reliable, economical one-size-fits-all model on the horizon' and that competitive AI products will require 'complex multimodel ecosystems' -- i.e., inference-tiering, routing, and orchestration across multiple models of varying cost/capability rather than a single frontier model, to avoid 'unbounded costs orders of magnitude higher than optimized product ecosystems.' `review` (991 words)
- [[gateway-api-inference-extension-gaie-nvidia-dynamo-documentation]] — Gateway API Inference Extension (GAIE) | NVIDIA Dynamo Documentation — NVIDIA's own operator documentation (Dynamo platform, docs version v1.3.0) for integrating Dynamo's model-serving framework with Gateway API Inference Extension (GAIE) -- a first-person vendor account of exactly how a third-party inference engine plugs into the GIE abstraction, confirming the EPP extension point is a real, actively-maintained integration surface rather than a one-off. Describes two routing topologies: Dynamo-native Frontend routing (Dynamo's own integrated router selects workers directly) versus 'Gateway API routing with GAIE' (a Kubernetes Gateway receives traffic, GAIE calls the Dynamo Endpoint Picker Plugin/EPP for endpoint selection, and the selected worker's Frontend sidecar forwards in 'direct' routing mode -- i.e., the worker trusts the EPP's choice rather than re-routing itself). States explicitly: 'Dynamo is independent of the Gateway implementation' and verifies two specific Gateway API implementations for this integration: agentgateway (Rust-based AI gateway, GatewayClass agentgateway, enabled via inferenceExtension.enabled=true) and Istio (uses Envoy in its data plane, enabled via ENABLE_GATEWAY_API_INFERENCE_EXTENSION=true, requires an EPP DestinationRule when mesh TLS policy applies). Names the exact component ownership split: user/platform team creates Gateway and HTTPRoute; Dynamo operator creates the EPP Service, worker pods, Frontend sidecars, and the generated InferencePool that binds the route to the EPP. Documents two concrete routing modes the EPP can run in: 'KV cache aware routing' (default, uses worker-published KV cache events delivered through Dynamo's own NATS-Core-based event plane for cache-locality-informed endpoint selection) versus 'Approximate routing' (fallback using only endpoint availability plus local request-lifecycle bookkeeping when precise KV events are unavailable). Gives a pinned compatibility table for a specific release line: Gateway API CRDs v1.5.1, GAIE CRDs v1.2.1, agentgateway v1.0.0, Istio 1.29.2, Dynamo images 1.2.1 -- concrete versioned evidence of the stack maturity as of this doc's publication. Lists exact scorer/picker plugin config knobs (schedulingProfiles[].plugins[].weight, label-filter for worker topology labels, DYN_MODEL_NAME, DYN_KV_CACHE_BLOCK_SIZE) showing GAIE's plugin architecture supports per-deployment custom scoring strategies beyond the reference implementation. `review` (2921 words)
- [[gateway-api-inference-extension-with-llm-d-router-kaito]] — Gateway API Inference Extension with llm-d Router | KAITO — KAITO official docs (v0.12.x, ground-truth/docs tier) describing how KAITO integrates Gateway API Inference Extension (GWIE) with 'the llm-d Router EPP.' States plainly: 'the EPP is provided by the llm-d Router project, which builds on the GWIE EPP with advanced scheduling plugins including KV cache-aware routing, prefill/decode (P/D) disaggregation, and pluggable filters/scorers' -- i.e. KAITO does not reimplement scheduling logic, it deploys the upstream llm-d-router-gateway artifact unmodified. Gives exact reuse mechanics: the KAITO InferenceSet controller uses Flux to deploy the llm-d-router-gateway Helm chart from 'oci://ghcr.io/llm-d/charts/llm-d-router-gateway' pinned to version v0.9.0, and the EPP container image is pinned to 'mcr.microsoft.com/oss/v2/llm-d/llm-d-router-endpoint-picker:v0.9.0' to match. This chart packages InferencePool + EPP together. KAITO explicitly configures the EPP to listen on plaintext gRPC (router.epp.flags.secure-serving=false) requiring an Istio DestinationRule with tls.mode: DISABLE for the Gateway->EPP ext_proc connection. Documents the full quickstart wiring: Istio Gateway with ENABLE_GATEWAY_API_INFERENCE_EXTENSION=true, InferenceSet CR triggering OCIRepository+HelmRelease creation, HTTPRoute targeting the InferencePool via .spec.endpointPickerRef, and optional Body-Based Routing (BBR, from kubernetes-sigs/gateway-api-inference-extension) that extracts model names from OpenAI-style request bodies into an X-Gateway-Model-Name header for multi-model routing without client changes. This is one of the cleanest documented examples of 'wrap, don't fork' reuse: KAITO's only original code is the InferenceSet controller and Flux wiring; the router/EPP itself is consumed as a versioned, pinned upstream artifact. `evergreen` (1635 words)
- [[gateway-api-inference-extension-nginx-documentation]] — Gateway API Inference Extension | NGINX Documentation — NGINX Gateway Fabric (NGF) how-to for GIE integration, confirming NGF's Inference Extension support is also Kubernetes/InferencePool-bound: enabled via a Helm value ('nginxGateway.gwAPIInferenceExtension.enable=true') or a container flag plus RBAC granting access to the 'inferencepools' CRD, and the InferencePool is installed as a Helm chart ('oci://registry.k8s.io/gateway-api-inference-extension/charts/inferencepool') that auto-deploys the Endpoint Picker Extension and selects pods via 'inferencePool.modelServers.matchLabels'. No standalone/non-k8s backend mode is documented; endpoint selection is pod-label-based only. Explicitly warns that NGF disables TLS certificate verification by default when talking to the third-party Endpoint Picker Extension and disclaims responsibility for risks from that EPP application — a relevant security caveat given the broader concern about trusting EPP-returned destinations. Links to llm-d's llm-d-router repo for Endpoint Picker documentation and llm-d-inference-sim for the reference vLLM simulator used in the walkthrough. `evergreen` (954 words)
- [[getting-started-kubernetes-gateway-api-inference-extension]] — Getting started - Kubernetes Gateway API Inference Extension — Implementer's Guide for building a Gateway controller/data-plane that supports InferencePool. Confirms two explicitly sanctioned integration paths for the EPP component: (1) 'Implement your own EPP: Create a custom external processing service that implements the Endpoint Picker Protocol specification' or (2) use a third-party/production EPP — i.e. the project's own docs treat a from-scratch custom EPP as a first-class, supported option, not a hack. Notes the repo's own pkg/lwepp 'Lightweight Endpoint Picker' is reference-only, 'not recommended for production... where high performance and advanced scheduling are required.' Adds one wire detail not in proposal 004 as fetched: a fallback endpoint CAN be set via x-gateway-destination-endpoint-fallback in the same envoy.lb metadata namespace. For endpoint tracking, gives Gateway-controller implementers two options: mirror InferencePool's pod selector into a shadow k8s Service, or track pods directly via the InferencePool's label selector (app: label) 'to discover the pods matching the criteria, and get their endpoints (IP and port)' — both endpoint-tracking options as documented assume Kubernetes pods as the backend unit, though the ext_proc protocol itself (per proposal 004) is IP:port-based and backend-agnostic at the wire level. Actively maintained: 9.5k stars, 67 watchers, 1.4k forks, latest release v0.17.0 (Sep 9 2026), commits as recent as Sep 3 2026 -- was originally at github.com/leondz/garak before moving to the NVIDIA org (repo explicitly instructs users who cloned pre-move to update their git remote). CLI usage pattern: "garak --target_type <family> --target_name <model>" runs all known probes by default against a target, or a specific probe family/plugin via "--spec probes.<family>.<Plugin>" (e.g. --spec probes.encoding for encoding-based prompt injection, or --spec probes.dan.Dan_11_0 to test DAN 11.0 jailbreak resistance). Results are reported per-probe-per-detector as pass/fail with a failure rate. Worked example in the README demonstrates garak finding that a more recent OpenAI model (ChatGPT) was MORE susceptible to encoding-based injection attacks than an older model (text-babbage-001, vulnerable only to quoted-printable encoding) -- a concrete illustration that newer/more capable models are not automatically more robust to a known attack class, directly relevant to why continuous automated red-teaming (not a one-time check) is needed when rolling out model upgrades. `review` (3104 words)
- [[github-safeailabeagle-official-implementation-of-eagle-1-icml24-eagle-2-emnlp24]] — GitHub - SafeAILab/EAGLE: Official Implementation of EAGLE-1 (ICML'24), EAGLE-2 (EMNLP'24), and EAGLE-3 (NeurIPS'25). · GitHub — Official academic repo for EAGLE/EAGLE-2/EAGLE-3 speculative decoding (EAGLE-1 ICML'24, EAGLE-2 EMNLP'24, EAGLE-3 NeurIPS'25; papers arXiv:2401.15077, 2406.16858, 2503.01840). EAGLE predicts next-token feature vectors (extrapolating second-top-layer contextual features) rather than tokens directly; EAGLE-3 removes the single-layer feature-prediction constraint and fuses low/mid/high-level semantic features via training-time testing. Measured on Vicuna-13B, fp16, 2x RTX 3090: EAGLE-3 is 5.6x faster than vanilla autoregressive decoding and 1.8x faster than EAGLE-1; EAGLE-2 is 4x faster than vanilla and 1.4x faster than EAGLE-1. Already merged into mainstream serving stacks: AWS NeuronX Distributed Core, Intel Extension for Transformers, Intel LLM Library for PyTorch, NVIDIA TensorRT Model Optimizer, and vLLM (via a 2024 PR); SGLang's own SpecForge project is recommended for EAGLE-3 training. Pre-trained EAGLE-3 draft-model weights exist on HF for LLaMA-3.1/3.3, LLaMA-4 Scout/Maverick, Qwen3 family, GPT-OSS-120B, and more -- corroborating Cloudflare's choice of EAGLE-3 as its speculative-decoding draft model for Kimi K2.5. `evergreen` (1813 words)

Slurm node states (Idle, Allocated, Mixed, Down, Drain, Not Responding) are surfaced as native Kubernetes pod conditions/status on each NodeSet pod. Explicitly supports 'Hybrid' deployments where only some Slurm components run in Kubernetes. This is the INVERSE integration strategy to a custom llm-d-router discovery plugin: instead of teaching the EPP to discover non-pod backends, Slinky makes the Slurm daemons themselves real Kubernetes Pods (via NodeSet), so a stock, unmodified InferencePool / K8s-based EndpointDiscovery can select them with zero router customization -- the tradeoff being that Slurm workers must run inside the K8s cluster's pod networking/scheduling domain rather than on an independently-managed HPC allocation. `evergreen` (3267 words)
- [[github-ai-dynamodynamo-a-datacenter-scale-distributed-inference-serving-framewor]] — GitHub - ai-dynamo/dynamo: A Datacenter Scale Distributed Inference Serving Framework · GitHub — ai-dynamo/dynamo GitHub repo README (fetched at HEAD, v1.4.2 latest release, Rust 52.7%/Python 34.6%/Go 10.5%). Dynamo is NVIDIA's orchestration layer above vLLM/SGLang/TensorRT-LLM providing disaggregated P/D, KV-aware routing, multi-tier KV offload (KVBM: GPU->CPU->SSD->remote), and SLA-driven autoscaling (Planner). Directly documents non-K8s worker discovery: a 'Service Discovery and Messaging' table states etcd and NATS are NOT required for local development (pass --discovery-backend file) or for Kubernetes (native CRDs+EndpointSlices handle it over TCP); etcd or NATS JetStream-backed modes are needed only 'for Slurm or other distributed deployments' that aren't K8s-native — etcd can run directly as ./etcd, NATS needs '-js' for JetStream. Also documents two routing topologies: (1) Dynamo-native Frontend routing, where the integrated Dynamo Router (client -> Frontend -> Router -> workers) makes worker-selection decisions with no external gateway required — the K8s-independent path; and (2) Gateway API routing via GAIE, where a Kubernetes Gateway API Inference Extension gateway calls the Dynamo Endpoint Picker Plugin (EPP) before forwarding to a worker's Frontend sidecar in --router-mode direct. Cites benchmark claims (7x higher throughput/GPU on GB200 NVL72 vs B200 without Dynamo, 2x faster TTFT via KV-aware routing per a Baseten blog, 750x higher throughput on GB300 via InferenceX) sourced from third-party/vendor blogs, not the repo's own controlled benchmark harness. `evergreen` (3836 words)

- [[github-coreweavetensorizer-module-model-and-tensor-serializationdeserialization]] — GitHub - coreweave/tensorizer: Module, Model, and Tensor Serialization/Deserialization · GitHub — CoreWeave's own GitHub repo (coreweave/tensorizer) for Tensorizer, a model/tensor serialization format and loader, read directly as the primary source for the tool secondary articles cite as a vLLM weight-streaming integration. 327 stars, 541 network dependents, 31 tags, Python 93.2%. Explicit first-person rationale: CoreWeave uses KNative to deploy models as serverless functions, and 'how long a model takes to load is a major factor in the latency of KNative scale-up' -- i.e. this tool exists specifically because of the cold-start/scale-up problem, built by an operator (CoreWeave) for its own serverless GPU product. Serializes a model's tensors into a single file loadable directly off HTTP/HTTPS, Redis, or S3 endpoints -- explicitly NOT embedding the model in the container image, which both shrinks image size/pull time and decouples model updates from container rebuilds. States deserialization speed is primarily network-bound and quotes GPT-J (20GB) loading 'at wire-speed (~5GB/s) on a 40GbE network,' bottlenecked by the Linux kernel TCP stack rather than disk or GPU transfer -- the one concrete throughput figure in the repo (notably in GB/s, not the 'Gbps' framing used by a secondary source describing a different tool). Technical details: TensorSerializer/TensorDeserializer are not drop-in replacements for torch.save/torch.load (different API surface, more performance/resource parameters) though a torch_compat compatibility layer exists; supports lazy-loading via a context-manager pattern; supports bfloat16/complex32/float8 dtypes via int16/int32 casting workarounds since numpy itself lacks those types; supports fast tensor weight encryption/decryption at serialization time via libsodium (metadata such as tensor names/dtypes/shapes is NOT encrypted, only weight values); Redis support is explicitly marked preliminary and 'not recommended for model deployment due to lack of distributed caching,' intended instead for inter-pod state sharing. `review` (4669 words)

- [[github-interlink-hqinterlink-slurm-plugin-github]] — GitHub - interlink-hq/interlink-slurm-plugin · GitHub — interlink-slurm-plugin is the Slurm sidecar implementation for interLink: it submits Slurm batch jobs that run the pod containers via Singularity/Apptainer (configurable SingularityPath/SingularityPrefix), exports pod ConfigMaps/Secrets as mounted files, and supports HostPath volumes. Directly answers the "is the virtual pod reachable" question: the plugin ships a Tsocks config option (TsocksPath, TsocksLoginNode) described as enabling "proxy networking," documented as "your window to the external world" via SSH to a designated login node, and the docs state this is "only implemented for the Slurm sidecar at the moment." This confirms Slurm-backed virtual pods do not get native, directly-routable cluster-network IP addresses -- network access is asymmetric and egress-oriented, tunneled outward through an SSH proxy to a login node, not inbound-routable from the Kubernetes pod network. This is a hard blocker for using interLink/Slurm as a live routing target for an inference gateway, which needs to open a connection INTO the backend, not just let the backend reach out. `evergreen` (4588 words)

- [[github-kvcache-aimooncake-mooncake-is-the-serving-platform-for-kimi-a-leading-ll]] — GitHub - kvcache-ai/Mooncake: Mooncake is the serving platform for Kimi, a leading LLM service provided by Moonshot AI. · GitHub — Mooncake GitHub repository page (6.6k stars, 1.2k forks, v0.3.13.post1 latest release, ~79% C++/13.5% Python/2.2% CUDA) — the upstream project behind llm-d's MooncakeStoreConnector. README describes Mooncake as the serving platform for Kimi (Moonshot AI's LLM service) and details Mooncake Store: a high-performance distributed key-value cache storage engine built on the Mooncake Transfer Engine that stores and manages reusable KV caches and model weights across inference clusters. Key architectural claims: large-object striping, parallel I/O, and end-to-end zero-copy transfer fully utilize aggregated bandwidth across multiple NICs; a multi-level cache hierarchy spans DRAM and SSD/NVMe for larger capacity; storage is elastic and disaggregated from inference engines, so storage nodes can be added/removed independently of engine restarts, upgrades, or scheduling; and per-object policies (replica counts, preferred segments, soft pin, hard pin) give applications programmatic control over placement and eviction. States broad ecosystem adoption: integrations with SGLang's Hierarchical KV Caching (HiCache), vLLM's disaggregated prefill serving, and LMCache, plus adoption by TorchSpec and TransferQueue. Also documents newer components: Mooncake EP (DeepEP-style fault-tolerant expert-parallel dispatch/combine with active_ranks awareness for MoE inference) and Mooncake PG (a PyTorch distributed process-group backend with failure detection and elastic rank recovery). - [[github-llm-dllm-d-routing-sidecar-incubating-pd-sidecar-for-llm-d-github]] — GitHub - llm-d/llm-d-routing-sidecar: Incubating P/D sidecar for llm-d · GitHub — GitHub repo for llm-d-routing-sidecar (Go, Apache-2.0), now a public archive: the README's deprecation banner states the sidecar's code moved to llm-d-inference-scheduler under cmd/pd_sidecar and pkg/sidecar, with 'all future development' there and a request not to file issues/PRs against this repo. The sidecar is a reverse proxy that redirects requests to the prefill worker named in the x-prefiller-host-port HTTP header (host:port format, any port allowed on an allowed host). SSRF protection (PR #49, merged Jul 18 2025) is opt-in via --enable-ssrf-protection (disabled by default): when enabled it watches a specific InferencePool CRD (via INFERENCE_POOL_NAMESPACE/INFERENCE_POOL_NAME env vars or CLI flags) and builds a host/IP allowlist from the pods matching that InferencePool's selector; unauthorized targets get HTTP 403; the allowlist auto-updates on pod add/remove/update. This makes the SSRF protection mechanism intrinsically Kubernetes-coupled -- it requires a real InferencePool CRD and a pod-watching client (RBAC for pod/InferencePool list-watch, namespace field via k8s Downward API) to compute the allowlist; without SSRF protection, the sidecar accepts any host:port in the header, so a non-k8s backend can run the sidecar unprotected but loses the built-in allowlist defense entirely. Quick-start requires 2 GPUs and runs vLLM with NixlConnector (kv_role=kv_both) for KV transfer between prefill and decode; supported --connector values are nixl (deprecated), nixlv2 (default as of PR #53, merged before Jul 22 2025), and lmcache (deprecated). Latest tagged release is v0.3.0 (Sep 25, 2025); repo archived Feb 3, 2026. `evergreen` (1960 words)

- [[github-michaelfeilinfinity-infinity-is-a-high-throughput-low-latency-serving-eng]] — GitHub - michaelfeil/infinity: Infinity is a high-throughput, low-latency serving engine for text-embeddings, reranking models, clip, clap and colpali · GitHub — GitHub repo for Infinity (michaelfeil/infinity, MIT license), a high-throughput low-latency REST API server for serving text-embeddings, reranking models, CLIP, CLAP and ColPali -- the embeddings/reranking-layer counterpart to generation-focused engines like vLLM/TGI, directly relevant to the claim that internal RAG services serve far more embedding/rerank calls than generation calls. 3.0k stars, 22 watchers, latest tagged release 0.0.77 (Aug 22, 2025) -- a release gap of roughly 14 months as of this Oct 2026 fetch, worth flagging as a possible slowdown signal even though a '2025/07 Blackwell support' changelog entry suggests some continued activity after that tag. Technical design: deploys any embedding/reranking/CLIP/sentence-transformer model directly from HuggingFace; built on PyTorch, Optimum (ONNX/TensorRT), and CTranslate2 with FlashAttention, supporting NVIDIA CUDA, AMD ROCm, CPU, AWS Inferentia2 (INF2), and Apple MPS backends; uses dynamic batching and dedicated tokenizer worker threads; multi-model orchestration lets one server instance mix-and-match several embedding/rerank/CLIP models simultaneously; exposes an OpenAPI surface aligned to OpenAI's embeddings API spec so it is a drop-in replacement at the API-contract level; ships specialized Docker images per backend (latest-cpu for Optimum/ONNX, latest-rocm, latest-trt-onnx with CUDA/TensorRT execution providers and flash-attn). CLI v2 supports launching multiple models at once via repeated --model-id flags or semicolon-delimited env vars (INFINITY_MODEL_ID, INFINITY_BATCH_SIZE), and a separate infinity_client pip package (since 2024/10) decouples client code from the server. Core contract: a provider implements PodLifecycleHandler (CreatePod/UpdatePod/DeletePod/GetPod/GetPodStatus/GetPods, all operating on real corev1.Pod/PodStatus objects) plus NodeProvider (Ping, NotifyNodeStatus); virtual-kubelet itself imposes no constraint on what PodIP the provider reports in PodStatus -- that value, and whether it is actually reachable, is entirely up to the provider implementation, not the framework. README explicitly states VK "is explicitly not intended to be an alternative to Kubernetes federation," ruling it out as a multi-cluster pattern. Documents a load-balancer integration quirk under "Known quirks": because virtual-kubelet nodes are not normal kubelets, Kubernetes control-plane versions before the ServiceNodeExclusion feature-gate (K8s 1.9+) may try to add virtual-kubelet nodes to cloud Load Balancer pools incorrectly; the documented workaround is enabling --feature-gates=ServiceNodeExclusion=true on the controller-manager. Liqo provider is noted as the one implementation that explicitly extends real pod-to-pod networking across peered clusters ("enabling Pod-to-Pod traffic and multi-cluster east-west services, supporting endpoints on both clusters"), unlike most other providers. `evergreen` (3146 words)

- [[github-vllm-projectproduction-stack-vllms-reference-system-for-k8s-native-cluste]] — GitHub - vllm-project/production-stack: vLLM’s reference system for K8S-native cluster-wide deployment with community-driven performance optimization · GitHub — vLLM production-stack GitHub repo (Apache 2.0, 2.7k stars, 29 watchers, vllm-project org), released 22 Jan 2025 per its own release blog at lmcache.ai -- the third 'alternative' named in llm-d's founding proposal alongside Dynamo and AIBrix, maintained by the LMCache team (contacts @ruizhang0101, @ApostaC, @YuhanLiu11, @Shaoting-Feng; blog hosted at lmcache.github.io, confirming University of Chicago LMCache-team governance claimed elsewhere in this batch). Self-describes narrowly as 'a reference implementation on how to build an inference stack on top of vLLM' that lets users 'scale from a single vLLM instance to a distributed vLLM deployment without changing any application code.' Architecture is three parts: Serving engine (vLLM), Request router (routes by routing key/session ID to maximize KV cache reuse; supports round-robin, session-ID-based, and prefix-aware routing marked Work-In-Progress), and Observability stack (Prometheus+Grafana). At fetch time its own Roadmap lists autoscaling, disaggregated prefill support, and router improvements (non-Python router, KV-cache-aware routing, better fault tolerance) as NOT YET BUILT ('We are actively working on this project and will release the following features soon') -- confirming production-stack is deliberately the lightest-weight of the four Kubernetes-native options discussed in this batch (llm-d/AIBrix/Dynamo/production-stack), explicitly scoped to 'the easiest way to deploy vLLM on Kubernetes' per llm-d's own characterization, not a full control plane with disaggregation or advanced routing yet. - [[github-vllm-projectrouter-a-high-performance-and-light-weight-router-for-vllm-la]] — GitHub - vllm-project/router: A high-performance and light-weight router for vLLM large scale deployment · GitHub — Primary GitHub repo for vLLM Router (vllm-project/router, 401 stars, 159 forks, fetched at HEAD Sept 2026), the code behind the Dec 2025 blog announcement. README's basic/'Standard' usage examples are entirely static-URL-based and Kubernetes-independent: '--worker-urls http://worker1:8000 http://worker2:8000 --policy consistent_hash' for standard DP routing, and for P/D disaggregation '--prefill http://127.0.0.1:8081 --decode http://127.0.0.1:8083 ...' with an explicit '--intra-node-data-parallel-size' flag — no K8s API calls involved. Kubernetes Service Discovery ('--service-discovery --selector app=vllm-worker role=inference --service-discovery-namespace default') is documented separately under 'Advanced Features,' i.e. opt-in, not the default or required path. Documents three distinct backend-discovery mechanisms for P/D disaggregation depending on the vLLM KV connector: (1) NIXL connector — static prefill/decode URL lists passed as CLI flags; (2) NCCL connector — 'ZMQ based discovery is supported' via a '--vllm-discovery-address' endpoint; (3) Mooncake connector — the router itself 'queries each prefill node's Mooncake bootstrap server at startup to learn engine_id per DP rank, and injects transfer_id / remote_bootstrap_addr / remote_engine_id into each request's kv_transfer_params' — a router-initiated pull-based discovery pattern with no Kubernetes or etcd involvement. Confirms the load-balancing policies (cache-aware, power-of-two, consistent hashing, random, round robin) are implemented independently of which discovery/backend-addressing mode is in use. `evergreen` (4381 words)
- [[governing-the-kv-cache]] — Governing the KV Cache: — Presents KVGov, a governance layer defending multi-tenant vLLM/SGLang/LMCache deployments against KV-cache timing side-channel attacks (PROMPTPEEK, EarlyBird, InputSnatch) that reach up to 100% attack success rate (ASR) by probing time-to-first-token (TTFT) to infer cache hit/miss and reconstruct victim prompts. Core mechanism is HMAC-keyed namespace isolation: a per-principal salt seeds the block-hash chain (sigma_p = HMAC_K(secret, principal_id)), making cache keys cryptographically disjoint across tenants; an ablation (N=1000 trials) shows HMAC-salt alone is necessary and sufficient to drive all three attacks to 0% ASR, with per-request HMAC overhead of only 1.3us. A 'boundary salting' variant seeds the chain only at the point where a shared preamble diverges into private content, retaining an estimated 93% of prefix-cache benefit (92.8% at a 2000-token shared preamble with 119-token private tail) instead of destroying all cross-tenant reuse. The paper also proposes ORIGAMI, a Stackelberg water-filling audit scheduler (12.6% reduction in adversary expected utility at Gini=0.63 enterprise heterogeneity) and an evolutionary-stability analysis identifying a 31.6% adversary-prevalence tipping point below which global (shared) caching remains stable versus per-session ephemeral caching. Real hardware measurements on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct/vLLM 0.26.0/A100 show a gate-verified cold/cached TTFT ratio of 0.22 (149.6ms cold vs 32.8ms cached at a 2119-token shared prefix; 0.37 ratio at 1447 tokens), replicated on llama.cpp/Apple Metal (ratio 0.093), confirming the channel is a property of prefix caching generally, not one engine's allocator. Critically, the paper shows vLLM 0.26.0's removal of block-size=1 (raising EarlyBird's search cost to infeasibility for free-text) provides NO protection against template-structured enterprise prompts, where membership/confirmation queries against a known field remain O(1) regardless of block size — a ~75-order-of-magnitude gap between reconstruction and confirmation cost on the identical cache. `evergreen` (7948 words)

This is either a linking error in Forte's article (pointing to the wrong CloudZero URL) or the cited report is a separate, unlinked CloudZero publication -- flagging as an audited absence rather than silently treating the citation as confirmed. The page DOES contain two in-scope operational/FinOps points, extracted as claims: GPU spend has a visibility problem because teams renting from multiple providers in different billing units (per GPU-hour, per instance-hour, per GPU-second) cannot determine true end-to-end cost per training run; and utilization is framed as 'the one variable' a buyer actually controls once market GPU rental rates are treated as given, since idle time at 40% utilization makes an H100's effective hourly cost nearly 2.5x its nominal rate. - [[harvey-tenet-research-preview]] — Harvey Tenet Research Preview — Harvey's own first-person technical blog (Aug 20 2026) introducing 'Harvey Tenet,' its first post-trained open-weight legal model. Base model: Kimi K3 (Moonshot's open-weight model), post-trained jointly with Fireworks Research via asynchronous reinforcement learning (GSPO -- group-sequence policy optimization) on ~1,750 agentic legal-task environments combining synthetic, public, and human-expert data (explicitly: 'We did not use any customer data in any of our post-training efforts'). Training used a rank-64 LoRA over the full Kimi K3 network (~500k expert tensors) on approximately 150 NVIDIA B300 GPUs over two months; 150 optimizer steps per epoch across 10,000+ rollouts; reward = weighted rubric-criteria satisfaction + holistic issue-solving + perfect-score bonus, graded by Kimi 2.6 as LLM-judge (chosen over heavier frontier judges after ablation). Results: solves nearly 2x as many held-out tasks on Harvey's own Legal Agent Benchmark (LAB) and 20% more on LAB Contracts versus the Kimi K3 base, achieving state-of-the-art on LAB Contracts and second place on LAB overall; gains transfer to third-party benchmarks the model never saw during training (Mercor APEX Agents corporate-law leaderboard, Crosby's RedlineBench). Cost-efficiency achieved by reward-shaping for token-efficient trajectories at equivalent quality, not just cheaper open-weight per-token pricing. Describes three specialized capability modules built on top: (1) M&A Diligence via Recursive Language Models (RLM) harness co-developed with Baseten -- a GLM-5.2 orchestrator with RLM lifts LAB Diligence pass rate from a baseline <=43.8% to 46.1%, and post-training via self-distillation SFT within the RLM harness pushes it to 60.1%; (2) Review Table structured extraction, where a GLM-5.2 model post-trained with Applied Compute improves answer quality by 3.6 points and citation quality by 12.1 points at roughly one-tenth cost per cell versus the strongest baseline; (3) Firm Knowledge retrieval, where a Qwen3.8-27B model trained with Engram to internalize ~100M tokens of synthetic firm-matter context into parametric memory improves criteria pass rate >15%, task completion ~10%, cuts total trajectory tokens by 58%, and cuts cost per query by 90%, reaching an intelligence-per-token score of 190.8 versus 129.3 for the best frontier configuration. Full methodology appendix documents benchmark-harness divergences versus Artificial Analysis and Vals third-party reporting, underscoring that cross-report benchmark comparisons are not apples-to-apples. `review` (4075 words)
- [[health-http-api-consul-hashicorp-developer]] — Health - HTTP API | Consul | HashiCorp Developer — Official Consul Health HTTP API docs, fetched as a companion to the blocking-queries page (cross-referenced from it) specifically for health-check filtering semantics. Key parameter, on /v1/health/service/:service (and related health endpoints): 'passing (bool: false) - Specifies that the server should return only nodes with all checks in the passing state. This can be used to avoid additional filtering on the client side.' This is a server-side filter -- the client does not need to fetch all instances and filter locally; it maps directly onto llm-d-router's Kubernetes-discovery behavior of only Upserting pods that satisfy the Ready condition. A Consul-backed EndpointDiscovery plugin should therefore call /v1/health/service/:service?passing=true (combined with blocking-query index/wait params from the companion blocking-queries note) to receive only healthy endpoints on each poll/watch cycle, mirroring the 'only Upsert healthy endpoints' contract. Also documents /v1/health/state/:state (state = any|passing|warning|critical) as an alternative check-centric view, and per-endpoint filter expressions (Status: Equal/Not Equal/In/Not In/Matches/Not Matches) for finer-grained filtering beyond the boolean passing shortcut. `evergreen` (3180 words)

- [[how-llm-d-prefix-cache-routing-made-qwen-7b-on-eks-23x-faster-dev-community]] — How llm-d Prefix-Cache Routing Made Qwen 7B on EKS 2.3x Faster - DEV Community — Independent dev.to benchmark (Andy Golubev, run 2026-06-15) of Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct on 8x g5.xlarge (1 NVIDIA A10G each) on AWS EKS, comparing a plain Kubernetes ClusterIP Service (round-robin) against llm-d's precise prefix-cache-aware EPP router, both fronting the identical 8 vLLM decode pods. At 512 concurrency with a repeated-prefix workload (150 prefixes x 2048 tokens, vllm bench serve, 9000 prompts): llm-d finished in 358.7s vs 840.2s round-robin (2.3x faster); output token throughput 6,423 tok/s vs 2,742 tok/s (+134%); mean TTFT 863ms vs 19,029ms (-95%, using precise TTFT numbers, blog title cites 2.3x); median TTFT 340ms vs 18,458ms (-98%); P99 TTFT 12,544ms vs 36,739ms (-66%); prefix cache hit rate ~93% vs ~11%; GPU KV cache usage 64-71% vs 98-99% (avoided saturation); waiting-request queue dropped from ~180 to 0. Setup used vLLM's --block-size=64, KV events over ZMQ, and llm-d's precise prefix-cache scorer (router.values.yaml); notes the vLLM --block-size and router scorer block size must match. Includes a rate-ladder (20/40/60 req/s) showing round-robin saturates near 8-9 req/s delivered regardless of requested rate while llm-d scales to ~22-25 req/s delivered. Full setup/code published at github.com/andygolubev/aws-eks-inference-llmd-vllm-benchmark-qwen-7b. `evergreen` (1795 words)
- [[how-llm-d-routes-model-inference-traffic-on-amazon-eks-red-hat-developer]] — How llm-d routes model inference traffic on Amazon EKS | Red Hat Developer — Red Hat Developer article (Aug 13, 2026) traces a live request through llm-d on Amazon EKS via KServe LLMInferenceService, Istio, and Gateway API Inference Extension -- the most concrete component-level wiring diagram in this batch. Versions pinned: llm-d inference scheduler 0.7.1, Gateway API 1.4.0, Gateway API Inference Extension 1.3.1, Istio 1.27.8_ossm, KServe (LLMISvc controller) 0.17.0, Red Hat AI Inference 3.4.0, EKS 1.34.8-eks. Key architecture facts: the KServe-generated scheduler pod ('test-kserve-router-scheduler') runs TWO containers sharing state over ZeroMQ -- 'main' (image odh-llm-d-inference-scheduler-rhel9) implementing the EPP with routing plugins queue-scorer, prefix-cache-scorer, max-score-picker, exposing gRPC on port 9002; and 'tokenizer' (image odh-llm-d-kv-cache-rhel9) tracking prefix-cache state across vLLM replicas. The EPP acts as an Envoy ext-proc external processor: Envoy pauses the request, calls the scheduler over gRPC, and forwards to the returned pod. InferencePool.spec.endpointPickerRef points the gateway at the EPP Service (failureMode: FailOpen falls back to standard LB if EPP is unreachable; FailClose drops requests instead -- explicit availability/correctness tradeoff). InferenceObjective (group inference.networking.x-k8s.io, distinct from InferencePool's inference.networking.k8s.io group) sets per-request priority via the client header 'x-gateway-inference-objective'; requests without the header default to priority zero. RBAC: the EPP's Role is scoped to get/list/watch only on core pods/endpoints plus inferencepools/inferenceobjectives -- no write access. Critically, only the scheduler (EPP) and istiod watch the InferencePool CR directly; the Envoy gateway pod itself has NO Kubernetes API access -- istiod compiles the InferencePool into gateway config and the gateway only calls the EPP over the compiled ext-proc endpoint. The InferencePool auto-creates a headless backing Service so the gateway has a stable address even though routing is decided by the EPP. `evergreen` (3212 words)
- [[how-to-build-an-inference-platform]] — How to Build an Inference Platform — Intility (Norwegian managed-IT/cloud provider) engineering blog (Jun 5 2026) — a detailed first-person build log for an internal, privately-hosted LLM inference platform, built for data control, cost, compliance, availability, and pricing predictability versus third-party APIs. Serves models up to 744B parameters (GLM 5.1, FP8, ~756GB weights) via a Kubernetes cluster running Talos Linux (minimal, immutable, API-driven, declarative/GitOps-managed OS, chosen for its 'no shell, no SSH' design). Inference engine: vLLM, chosen over Ollama (deemed not enterprise-grade) and SGLang, citing vLLM's 'fastest and broadest model support' and 'most active community'; engine deployed via vllm/vllm-openai:v0.21.0 container image pulling weights directly from Hugging Face, with --enable-chunked-prefill and --max-num-batched-tokens=8192 to balance prefill/decode scheduling (verbatim: 'Without chunked prefill, a user sending a 100,000-token prompt would freeze the GPU for everyone else'). Storage: Longhorn (OSS distributed block storage), chosen for simplicity, backing a single ReadWriteMany volume per model so only one pod downloads weights and others mount the same copy — avoiding per-node weight replication. Routing: adopted the Kubernetes-native EndPointPicker from the Gateway API Inference Extension SIG for cache-aware/prefix-aware load balancing based on scraped vLLM queue-depth/cache-utilization metrics — explicitly chosen over round-robin (measured 'staggering results like 50% slower and more expensive requests') and judged sufficient versus heavier alternatives like NVIDIA Dynamo or llm-d (reserved for larger-scale disaggregated prefill/decode, which Intility has not yet implemented). Gateway: Envoy Gateway plus the Envoy AI Gateway extension, which injects a sidecar to adjust headers, enforce per-user rate limits (keyed on identity, not IP, since billing is token-based not request-based), and extract token usage for billing; introduces the custom AIGatewayRoute resource as the central per-model exposure unit. All of engine, storage, routing, and gateway are explicitly named OSS (vLLM, Longhorn, EndPointPicker, Envoy AI Gateway); Intility's own contribution is the integration/GitOps wiring and the developer-platform self-service layer that provisions AIGatewayRoutes, not a custom engine. Notes KubeCon '26 Amsterdam had 5+ talks on cache-aware inference routing. Roadmap: moving to NVIDIA Blackwell/FP4, eventually disaggregated prefill/decode. `review` (2620 words)
- [[how-we-built-the-most-efficient-inference-engine-for-cloudflares-network]] — How we built the most efficient inference engine for Cloudflare's network — Cloudflare's first-person account (Vlad Krasnov, Mari Galicer, 2025-08-27) of building Infire, a Rust LLM inference engine, to replace vLLM for edge-network inference; recovered via Wayback Machine after the live URL repeatedly tripped bot detection. `evergreen` (1770 words)
- [[i-know-what-you-asked-prompt-leakage-via]] — I Know What You Asked: Prompt Leakage via — Primary source (NDSS 2025) for PROMPTPEEK, the third timing side-channel attack cited by KVGov. First security investigation of multi-tenant LLM serving's KV-cache sharing (vLLM, SGLang), showing that sharing KV cache across users for identical token prefixes creates a side channel: an attacker can monitor changes in serving order/latency to detect KV cache hits and extract another user's prompt one token at a time, iteratively reconstructing the full prompt (rather than only approximating content, as prior work did). Evaluated on Llama2-13B on an A100 80GB GPU across three adversary-knowledge scenarios and four datasets: with knowledge of the prompt template, average success rate of 99% fully/partially reversing the prompt input; with knowledge of the prompt input, 98% reversing the prompt template; and with no background knowledge at all, 95% success. Identifies three factors governing attack feasibility: GPU memory capacity (sets whether the attack is feasible at all), concurrent user request volume, and attacker request volume (both accelerate memory-driven cache eviction/exposure), plus attacker background knowledge (reduces the number of requests needed). Demonstrates low attack cost in a concrete case: with knowledge of the prompt template, an adversary can uncover a victim's gender, age, weight, and height fields using just 60 total requests. The authors responsibly disclosed findings to SGLang (primary target), OpenAI, ByteDance, and Anthropic, and report ByteDance acknowledged the new side-channel vector. `evergreen` (13416 words)

Requests started timing out.' This triggered a comparative test of Ollama vs vLLM vs llama.cpp whose headline claim is that Ollama 'collapses at 5 concurrent users.' - [[i-see-a-lot-of-hate-for-ollama-doing-this-kind-of-thing-but-also-they-remain-one]] — I see a lot of hate for ollama doing this kind of thing but also they remain one... | Hacker News — Hacker News comment thread (on the 'Ollama Turbo' submission, item 44802414) containing substantive first-person technical claims from named/pseudonymous practitioners, including Ollama maintainer 'mchiang' (Michael Chiang, Ollama co-founder) responding directly. Treat all claims as gray literature from pseudonymous or semi-identified commenters, not verified fact.\n\nKey technical claims (with speaker attribution; evidentiary caveats noted):\n- mchiang (Ollama maintainer): 'Since the new multimodal engine, Ollama has moved off of llama.cpp as a wrapper. We do continue to use the GGML library... Ollama might look like a toy... to keep its simplicity, we go through a deep amount of struggles.' He also states Ollama implemented its own MXFP4 kernel for gpt-oss and benchmarked against OpenAI's reference implementation and against vLLM's published harmony-format benchmarks to validate parity -- this is a first-party maintainer claim, not independently verified in-thread.\n- Commenter 'buyucu' disputes mchiang's framing as 'gaslighting,' asserting 'GGML library is llama.cpp. They are one and the same... developed by the same people.' Another commenter 'scosman' flatly rejects this ('Nope...'). This is an active, unresolved technical disagreement about whether Ollama's GGML dependency still constitutes a 'llama.cpp wrapper' -- both sides are pseudonymous/semi-identified, so the underlying technical reality (not just the framing dispute) should be checked against Ollama's own source tree (linked in-thread: https://github.com/ollama/ollama/tree/main/model/models and https://github.com/ggml-org/ggml) rather than taken from either side's assertion.\n- Commenter 'dcreater': explicit production positioning claim -- 'AWS, vLLM, SGLang etc would be the solutions for enterprise' vs. Ollama being 'meaningful for hobbyists and individuals.' Cites an anecdote (unverifiable, pure anecdote) of a startup deploying Ollama on customer premises 'because it was easy' with 'no good reason.'\n- Commenter 'evilduck': concrete friction claims for llama.cpp as the 'easy' alternative -- no prebuilt CUDA binaries for Fedora-based distros ('losing an hour or more'), llama-swap requiring a 'new custom yaml config,' and manual systemd/user/group setup for boot-time startup, each costing 'another hour.'\n- Commenter 'steren' (claims internal benchmarking, unpublished at time of posting): 'We benchmarked vLLM and Ollama on both startup time and tokens per second. Ollama comes at the top' -- caveat: no published data, no stated concurrency/multi-user condition, and 'apitman' immediately asks whether multi-user cases were tested (unanswered in-thread).\n- Commenter 'sbinnee': architectural framing claim -- 'vllm and ollama assume different settings and hardware. vLLM backed by paged attention expect a lot of requests from multiple users whereas ollama is usually for single user on a local machine' -- this is the clearest single-sentence articulation in the thread of why Ollama is considered unsuited to concurrent/production serving.\n- Commenter 'miki123211': niche production counter-case -- Ollama is 'your best bet' when an org has no datacenter GPU access and must do desktop/clientside deploys ('a desktop with a 4090 or two is all you can get in some organizations'). `review` (1466 words)
