This is generic cross-institution advisory/survey content from an aggregator body (IFC/BIS), not any single central bank's first-person account of its own LLM serving architecture -- reinforces that even within the government/central-bank space, the UK DBT and GovTech Singapore disclosures are exceptional rather than the norm; aggregator-body publications about the sector don't substitute for an institution's own disclosure. `review` (9067 words)
- [[in-house-llm-serving-at-netflix-by-ai-platforms-model-runtime-team-and-by-netfli]] — In-House LLM Serving at Netflix. By AI Platform’s Model Runtime team and… | by Netflix Technology Blog | Netflix TechBlog — Netflix AI Platform's Model Runtime + Inference teams detail their in-house LLM serving stack (published Jul 17 2026): a unified JVM serving system fronts both gRPC and HTTP/OpenAI-compatible paths, delegating GPU inference to the shared Model Scoring Service (MSS), which runs NVIDIA Triton Inference Server under a Java control plane for deployment/versioning/autoscaling. Netflix migrated its paved-path engine from TensorRT-LLM to vLLM in summer 2025 for operational fit (no multi-step compilation, logits-processor extensibility, debuggability, practitioner familiarity). They package models via Triton's vLLM backend (dynamic I/O specs) but keep the Python backend as an escape hatch for custom pre/postprocessing; they hit a Triton 25.09 vs vLLM 0.11.2 API-mismatch bug requiring pinned versions. They git-subtreed and patched NVIDIA's Triton OpenAI-compatible frontend because it silently dropped response_format before reaching vLLM. They built a custom Prometheus-merging proxy because Triton's built-in bridge surfaces only 9 of 40+ vLLM metrics (missing token throughput, KV cache utilization, prefix cache hit rate). Constrained decoding is implemented via vLLM's custom logits-processor interface as per-request state machines; migrating from vLLM V0 (GIL-serialized, CPU-bound) to V1 (batch-level, C++/multithreaded) was necessary to scale, with new handling for chunked-prefill partial state and KV-cache preemption. Models are pre-staged on Amazon FSx to avoid S3/HF cold-start latency. Explicitly OSS: Triton, vLLM, PyTorch. Explicitly in-house: JVM serving system, MSS, Java control plane, the OpenAI-frontend patch, the unified metrics proxy, and the constrained-decoding logits processors. `review` (2510 words)
- [[inference-economics-of-enterprise-coding-agents]] — Inference Economics of Enterprise Coding Agents: — Pegatron/PEGAVERSE case study (N=1 developer, two contiguous 28-day periods on one production monorepo) directly compares a cloud-API coding-agent stack (Claude Code + Claude Opus 4.7/4.8) against an on-premise stack (Opencode + self-hosted vLLM serving NVFP4-quantized GLM-5.1/5.2 on a Blackwell 4xB200 tray). METHODOLOGY CAVEATS (load-bearing): single hypothesis-aware developer self-benchmarking own commits (bounded by a deterministic keyword fix-label rule and enforced CI/review gate, but still N=1); Period B (local) succeeded Period A (cloud) sequentially, not randomized/counterbalanced; authors explicitly flag this and propose a cross-over multi-developer replication as future work; data = >44,000 LLM requests and 613 non-merge commits over 56 days on one corporate AI PaaS monorepo.

COST FINDING (the pivotal number): prompt caching on the Claude API achieved a 99.3% hit rate, cutting realized API cost 88.6% to an effective /bin/bash.57/million tokens -- BELOW the .83/million-token amortized unit cost of the shared on-premise slice. This is explicitly flagged as a 'utilization-dependent inversion': the on-premise per-token figure divides a time-billed GPU resource by one developer's token volume, so it is inflated by low single-tenant utilization. The authors state total realized spend and TCO, not the per-token rate, are 'the robust quantities.' The Claude configuration processed 16.9x more tokens; its larger total bill is a volume effect, not a per-token price effect.

TCO / BREAK-EVEN MODEL: Taiwan-market GPU rental-equivalent pricing of .00/GPU-hour (6.00/hour for a 4xB200 tray; June-July 2026 on-demand B200 rates quoted at .70-5.90/hour). Two scenarios modeled: (1) Dedicated Reservation = 24h x 28d = 672 hours = 0,752 for the period -- this scenario is 'dominated throughout': True TCO exceeds the cached API's at every point of a parameter sweep (10% above in the most favorable case to >2x in the most adverse), so dedicated reservation only pays off when the tray serves substantially more than one developer's workload. (2) Shared Allocation = charged as a standard 160-hour/month developer seat under a shared-cluster chargeback model = ,560 for the period. Under Taiwan-market parameters and a symmetric labor-cost model, shared-allocation on-premise deployment saves 40.1% of true TCO versus the cached API, while dedicated reservation costs 43.8% MORE than the cached API. The shared-allocation saving is robust to per-developer attribution up to ~405 of the 672 tray-hours (~2.5 standard developer seats). Extrapolated (with explicit caveat about footprint scaling) to 100 developers: ~79,000/month pure-API vs ~56,000/month shared on-premise -- but the paper cautions this assumes per-developer amortization holds at fleet scale, since one 4xB200 tray cannot actually serve 100 concurrent developers; the extrapolation compares amortization RATES, not a fixed footprint.

QUALITY/DEFECT FINDING: at comparable gross code churn, on-premise GLM had far higher Fix Commit Ratio (FCR) = 74.9% vs Claude's 45.9%; odds of a commit being a repair were 2.6-4.9x higher within every difficulty tier (Mantel-Haenszel OR=3.61), ruling out task-difficulty confounding. Per-tier fix rates: Claude 15.1%/61.2%/63.4% and GLM 46.5%/80.6%/86.9% for High/Medium/Low difficulty tiers respectively. Under shared allocation the 'genuine penalty is not monetary but a measurable developer-experience burden' -- more time trapped in debugging spirals, slower commit cadence.

HYBRID ROUTING: an offline counterfactual replay over the pooled 613 commits, amortizing each backend's measured period total (API ,785.21; shared GPU+ops ,453) per commit, shows that routing policies which send only high-difficulty tasks to the cloud API and routine tasks to the local model trade defect rate for infrastructure savings along a cost-quality frontier -- no routing policy dominates the pure-API baseline. Proposed future design: complexity pre-filtering routes high-stakes tasks to cloud API; routine tasks start locally and escalate to cloud after a bounded number of failed self-healing iterations.

THROUGHPUT CONTEXT (not a hardware recommendation, included only as serving-economics context): hosted GLM-5.2 sustains 214.9 output tok/s at Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index 51 vs Claude Opus 4.8's 66.0 tok/s at Index 56; the self-hosted NVFP4 GLM-5.2 deployment observed ~185 tok/s under interactive agent load (single-tenant observation, not a controlled benchmark), ~2.8x Opus 4.8's API throughput -- but faster raw generation did not translate to faster task completion given the elevated local defect rate. `review` (15610 words)
- [[inference-serving-with-superpowers-agentgateway-agent-connectivity-solved]] — Inference Serving with Superpowers – agentgateway | Agent Connectivity Solved — Vendor blog (agentgateway, Mar 23 2026) positioning agentgateway + llm-d + Gateway API Inference Extension (GIE) as a composable inference stack: agentgateway is the Rust traffic layer (GA v1.0.0, first GIE v1.4.0-conformant gateway), GIE's InferencePool/endpoint-picker-protocol is the shared contract, and llm-d supplies the scheduling intelligence (prefix-cache-aware routing, P/D disaggregation, tiered KV caching) via the llm-d-router / llm-d inference scheduler which 'extends GIE.' Notes GIE v1.4.0 added standalone EPP support and introduced 'appProtocol, FailOpen, and ALPN h2' at the InferencePool/Helm layer, and that llm-d recently merged agentgateway+GIE integration support (llm-d/llm-d PR #421) plus a reproducible demo repo (solo-io/agentgateway-llm-d). Also flags llm-d's move into CNCF Sandbox for vendor-neutral governance. This is promotional content from agentgateway's own blog, so architecture claims should be cross-checked against primary docs/code, but it is useful for locating the demo repo and confirming GIE v1.4.0 introduced a FailOpen concept at the InferencePool API level (distinct from agentgateway's own unresolved fail-open issue). `evergreen` (959 words)
- [[inference-routing-agentgateway-agent-connectivity-solved]] — Inference routing – agentgateway | Agent Connectivity Solved — Agentgateway docs describe two inference-routing deployment modes: Kubernetes Gateway API mode (InferencePool + HTTPRoute + EPP) and 'standalone request scheduler mode', where agentgateway runs as a localhost sidecar to the EPP and does NOT support InferencePool. In standalone mode the config defines a synthetic top-level 'services' entry (e.g. 'default/my-model') that the route backend references; when the EPP owns endpoint discovery, operators set 'destinationMode: passthrough' under policies.inferenceRouting so EPP-selected ip:port destinations are forwarded to directly without matching them against locally known workload endpoints. This is the exact mechanism for routing to non-pod/non-k8s backends: the synthetic service is a placeholder, and passthrough mode tells agentgateway to trust the EPP's returned destination rather than validating it against known endpoints. `evergreen` (506 words)
- [[inference-without-interference]] — Inference without Interference: — TetriInfer (Hu, Huang, Xu, Chen, Xu, Chen, Feng, Wang, Wang, Bao, Sun, Shan — University of Chinese Academy of Sciences/ICT-CAS + Huawei Cloud, arXiv 2401.11181) is named as concurrent disaggregation work by Mooncake, the Taming the Titans survey, and discussed directly by DistServe's related work. Built directly on top of vLLM ('We implement TetriInfer's disaggregated prefill and decode instances based on vLLM'), with most modules in Python plus a C++ network-stack module for KV-cache transfer and HuggingFace Trainer APIs for its length-prediction fine-tuning — making it one of the clearest documented examples of an operator extending vLLM's internals rather than replacing them. Motivating measurement: the paper runs its own interference experiments mixing prefill/decode workloads and reports specific slowdown figures — mixing prefill requests together causes up to 10x slowdown, combining prefill and decode requests causes up to 5x slowdown, and mixing decode requests of different lengths causes a 16% throughput hit — establishing empirically (not just theoretically) that colocation interference is severe and multi-dimensional, not limited to the single prefill-vs-decode axis emphasized in DistServe. Three-pillar design: (1) partitions/pads input prompts into fixed-size chunks sized to keep the accelerator at its 'computation-saturated limit' (a chunked-prefill variant, independently motivated); (2) disaggregates prefill and decode into separate, independently-scalable instances that can dynamically flip roles under load; (3) a two-level scheduling algorithm using an LLM-based length-prediction model to forecast decode-token counts and avoid decode scheduling hotspots. Because the authors lacked access to high-end interconnect hardware, they implemented a mock network-bandwidth emulator (200Gbps-300GBps range) rather than testing on real RDMA/InfiniBand fabric — a notable methodological limitation flagged transparently in the paper itself. Benchmarked against vanilla vLLM using public conversation/summarization/writing datasets: for light-prefill/heavy-decode workloads, TetriInfer improves performance-per-dollar by 2.4x; for common mixed workloads, it improves average TTFT by 85% and average job completion time by 50%, using 38% fewer resources overall while cutting average TTFT and JCT by 97% and 47% respectively in the paper's headline figures. The paper explicitly reports a negative/null result for heavy-prefill/heavy-decode workloads, where disaggregation's overhead is not offset by the gains — a documented boundary condition for when phase disaggregation stops paying off. `review` (10953 words)
- [[inference-refactor-plugin-to-use-override_host-lb-issue-11824-kgateway-devkgatew]] — Inference: Refactor Plugin to Use Override_Host LB · Issue #11824 · kgateway-dev/kgateway — kgateway issue #11824 (opened Jul 30 2025 by danehans, closed Sep 30 2025) documents kgateway's Inference (GIE) plugin evolution: PR #11810 shipped EPP-fail-open conformance ('EppUnAvailableFailOpen') using 'a static cluster + subset LB' as a workaround; once kgateway's Envoy pin advanced to v1.35 (via PR #11784), which ships Envoy's new override_host LB feature (linking directly to envoyproxy/envoy#38757), the plan was to refactor the plugin away from the subset-LB hack to use override_host LB. The issue was closed WITHOUT that refactor happening, with the closing comment stating plainly: 'Closing since the community decided to deprecate the Envoy-based data plane in favor of agentgateway for AI-related features.' This is a highly load-bearing, dated (Sep 30 2025) data point: kgateway -- a production, GIE-integrated gateway -- abandoned Envoy as its AI/inference data plane entirely in favor of agentgateway, rather than adopting either dynamic_forwarding or override_host LB as its long-term mechanism for honoring EPP-picked destinations. Referenced cross-project context: Istio tracked a related override_host need at istio/istio#56230, and Envoy Gateway discussed it at envoyproxy/gateway#6458. Net effect for router-customization strategy: agentgateway's own destinationMode/passthrough mechanism (not either Envoy LB approach) is the mechanism kgateway's maintainers bet on going forward for GIE-integrated non-pod destination routing. `evergreen` (346 words)
- [[inference-support-agentgateway-as-a-standalone-provider-issue-620-agentgatewayag]] — Inference: Support agentgateway as a Standalone Provider · Issue #620 · agentgateway/agentgateway — Closed agentgateway design/implementation issue (#620, Nov 2025 - Apr 2026) tracing the full origin of standalone (non-Gateway-API) EPP support, which is the load-bearing design history for routing to non-k8s backends. Key facts: (1) the GIE community pursued a 'standalone' mode dropping the Gateway API dependency specifically to support non-k8s orchestrators like Slurm and RL/batch-inference workloads (design doc linked); (2) danehans's Mar 15 2026 implementation plan states the intended behavior as 'default target = stable model Service DNS/port, EPP override = chosen pod IP:port,' giving compatibility with pod-selector discovery while preserving a future FailOpen fallback; (3) it documents the exact request flow: client -> agtw sidecar -> agtw calls EPP over ext-proc -> EPP returns 'x-gateway-destination-endpoint=<ip:port>' -> agtw forwards directly to that endpoint; (4) an example standalone config shows 'policies.inferenceRouting.endpointPicker.host' plus a 'failureMode: failOpen' field that maintainer howardjohn immediately questioned ('if the EPP is bundled, should we expose failureMode at all? Seems you would not want failOpen') and danehans agreed to remove pending user feedback; (5) InferencePool is confirmed NOT required in standalone mode — endpoint discovery instead uses a label/pod selector, explicitly to 'support non-k8s environments' later; (6) closed via merged PRs #1429 ('Add standalone inference routing local config') and #1658 ('Trust standalone EPP-selected destinations'), landing in agentgateway v1.2.0-alpha.1; (7) notes llm-d has begun documenting standalone mode as a supported gateway deployment option, and GIE has since published an upstream standalone guide at gateway-api-inference-extension.sigs.k8s.io/guides/standalone/. Maintainer howardjohn's initial skepticism ('made up use case') was overturned once ahg-g explained the batch-inference/Slurm rationale and confirmed the ext-proc/EPP wire protocol does not change between modes. `evergreen` (1865 words)
- [[inferencepool-kubernetes-gateway-api-inference-extension]] — InferencePool - Kubernetes Gateway API Inference Extension — GIE docs page for InferencePool v1 (GA since v1.0.0). InferencePoolSpec has 3 major parts: selector (label selector matching Pod labels, e.g. matchLabels app: vllm-qwen3-32b — pods only, selection is by Kubernetes label match, no other backend kind is mentioned), targetPorts (port numbers on selected Pods the gateway routes to, e.g. number: 8000), and endpointPickerRef (references the EPP Service; became OPTIONAL as of v1.5.0 — before that it was required — to allow InferencePool without a user-managed EPP deployment). Example config shows failureMode: FailOpen on endpointPickerRef: if EPP fails/is unresponsive, request is forwarded to a gateway-chosen endpoint instead. States any Pod joining a pool must implement the Model Server Protocol so the EPP can route intelligently. Frames InferencePool as pool-level replacement for a Kubernetes Service, explicitly Pod-centric ('defines a group of Pods'). `evergreen` (504 words)
- [[inputsnatch-stealing-input-in-llm-services-via-timing-side-channel-attacks]] — InputSnatch: Stealing Input in LLM Services via Timing Side-Channel Attacks — Primary source for the InputSnatch attack cited by KVGov. Proposes a timing-based side-channel attack against two cache mechanisms (prefix/KV caching and semantic caching) using two components: an input constructor (ML models + LLM-based vocabulary-correlation learning with optimized search) that generates candidate inputs likely to hit the victim's cached state, and a time analyzer that applies statistical time fitting with outlier elimination to distinguish cache hits from misses and feeds results back to refine the constructor's search. Against prefix/KV caching, the timing analyzer achieves 87.13% accuracy in determining cache-hit prefix length. Against static-template medical question-answering systems, the attack achieves a 62% success rate extracting exact disease-name inputs and 13.5% for exact symptom descriptions; against legal-consultation RAG services using semantic caching, semantic-level extraction success rates range from 43% to 100% across thirteen legal domains. Overall the framework reports 62% success in exact partial input recovery, 12.5% in exact complete input extraction, and 79.5% effectiveness in semantic-level content reconstruction under realistic deployment constraints. Distinguishes itself from prior prompt-theft work (adversarial prompting, embedding inversion, next-token-probability exploitation) by requiring neither white-box model access nor unlimited querying across the full vocabulary, and by targeting structured/templated enterprise prompts where the unknown content is a bounded field rather than open vocabulary. `evergreen` (13972 words)
- [[inside-vllms-new-kv-offloading-connector-smarter-memory-transfer-for-maximizing]] — Inside vLLM’s New KV Offloading Connector: Smarter Memory Transfer for Maximizing Inference Throughput | vLLM Blog — vLLM blog (Jan 2026) explaining the KV cache offloading connector introduced in vLLM 0.11.0, focused on CPU DRAM offload. Motivates offloading via two use cases: (1) reusing KV across requests sharing a prefix, and (2) preserving KV for preempted requests that would otherwise need full recomputation. The Connector API was extended in vLLM 0.9.0 to support asynchronous load/store; the offloading connector uses this async path so GPU-CPU transfer does not block request handling. Enabled via --kv_offloading_backend native --kv_offloading_size <GB> (PR #24498, targeted for 0.14.0) or the older --kv-transfer-config JSON with OffloadingConnector/num_cpu_blocks. Reports microbenchmarks on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct/NVIDIA H100: CPU cache loading cuts TTFT by 2x-22x depending on prompt size, and CPU-cache-hit throughput for 10,000 concurrent 512-token prefill requests increases up to 9x. States the offloading connector's performance improved substantially in 0.12.0 (up to 4x TTFT reduction, 5x throughput) after a KV cache memory layout change (PR #27743) that made each logical block's KV data one contiguous physical block across all layers instead of fragmented per-layer/per-K/V, increasing effective physical block size by a factor of 2*num_layers (e.g., Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct block size grew from 32KB to 2MB). Deep-dives GPU<->CPU transfer mechanics: DMA via cudaMemcpyAsync vs a custom CUDA-core copy kernel, showing DMA wins on larger blocks (bi-directional: DMA 83.4 GB/s vs custom kernel 68.5 GB/s) while the custom kernel wins on very small (<0.5MB) blocks but interferes with concurrent GPU compute (yielding up to 6% throughput regression at 0% cache hit rate). Next milestone stated: using the CPU KV cache as an intermediate tier for storage offloading. `evergreen` (2830 words)
- [[inside-vllms-new-kv-offloading-connector-smarter-memory-transfer-for-maximizin-2]] — Inside vLLM’s New KV Offloading Connector: Smarter Memory Transfer for Maximizing Inference Throughput | vLLM Blog — vLLM engineering blog (Dec 2025) technical deep dive into the KV offloading feature introduced in vLLM 0.11.0, the mechanism llm-d's native offloading path builds on. Explains motivation: KV offloading helps even without shared prefixes, because preempted requests under GPU memory pressure can be spilled to CPU DRAM instead of discarding KV data and recomputing on reschedule. Describes the Connector API, extended in vLLM 0.9.0 to support asynchronous KV load/store so the engine isn't blocked during external transfers; the new offloading connector exposes a pluggable backend API bundled with a native CPU backend, enabled via '--kv_offloading_backend native --kv_offloading_size <GB>' (PR #24498, targeted for 0.14.0) or the older '--kv-transfer-config' JSON form. Reports concrete micro-benchmarks on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct/NVIDIA H100: loading KV from CPU cuts TTFT by 2x-22x depending on prompt size versus GPU recomputation, and CPU cache hit rate increases concurrent-request throughput by up to 9x on a 10,000-request/512-token batch, while cache-miss TTFT is essentially unaffected since offloading writes run asynchronously. States the offloading connector's performance was 'dramatically improved' in vLLM 0.12.0 — up to 4x TTFT reduction and 5x throughput increase for the same model/GPU — attributed to a GPU memory layout change (PR #27743) that creates one contiguous physical block spanning all layers instead of the previous per-layer/per-K-V fragmentation, increasing effective physical block size by roughly 2*num_layers (e.g., Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct grows from 32KB to 2MB blocks; provides a full 15-model comparison table of old vs new block sizes). Presents its own microbenchmark comparing DMA (cudaMemcpyAsync) vs a custom CUDA-core copy kernel: DMA wins for larger blocks and achieves 83.4 GB/s bidirectional vs the custom kernel's 68.5 GB/s, though the custom kernel is faster for very small (<~64KB) block sizes; end-to-end, DMA gives up to 32% better throughput than the custom kernel for Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct while matching TTFT. Full benchmark hardware/software setup is disclosed (Ubuntu 24.04.1, Intel Xeon SapphireRapids 8-core limit, NVIDIA H100 80GB HBM3, 500GB DRAM, CUDA 12.9, specific vLLM commit hash, Flash Attention backend, 16-token GPU/CPU block size, GPU prefix caching disabled to isolate CPU-hit effects). Flags CPU-tier-as-intermediate-for-storage-offloading as the next roadmap milestone. `deprecated` (2842 words)
- [[integrations-phoenix]] — Integrations - Phoenix — > Fetch the complete documentation index at: /llms.txt `review` (549 words)
- [[intelligent-inference-scheduling-with-llm-d-on-red-hat-ai-red-hat-developer]] — Intelligent inference scheduling with llm-d on Red Hat AI | Red Hat Developer — Red Hat Developer article (June 11 2026) demonstrating that OpenShift AI / Red Hat AI Inference embed llm-d's EPP by exposing it through a single LLMInferenceService CRD with a 'router' stanza containing 'route: {}', 'gateway: {}', 'scheduler: {}' sub-fields left empty to accept operator-managed defaults -- i.e. the EPP is deployed and wired automatically rather than reimplemented. Confirms EPP is built on Gateway API Inference Extension and plugs into standard gateways (Istio, Envoy Gateway) via the ext-proc protocol. Details the 3-stage Filter->Score->Pick pipeline, default scorers (prefix-cache-scorer + queue-scorer, with optional load-aware-scorer), and cites upstream benchmarks: TTFT dropped from 35s to 120ms (>99% reduction), throughput +151%, latency down 35% on 8 vLLM replicas / 16 H100 GPUs, Qwen3-32B, 60 RPS shared-prefix workload. Also documents predicted-latency scheduling via an XGBoost model (~5% MAPE) exposed through x-slo-ttft-ms SLO headers, flow control (priority bands/fairness/backpressure), and that the identical LLMInferenceService router config is portable from OpenShift AI to third-party managed Kubernetes (AKS, CoreWeave) as a Technology Preview -- meaning the abstraction boundary Red Hat exposes for router customization is the LLMInferenceService CR itself, not the EPP binary. `evergreen` (3113 words)
- [[interim-report-affinity-helps-or-hurts]] — Interim report — affinity-helps-or-hurts — Dialectical synthesis: llm-d's affinity-filter+load-scorer+saturation-gate design is architecturally positioned to avoid CacheRoute's capacity-loss regime (never ships affinity-only, per-request threshold self-degrades under low reuse, saturation override empirically fires under load) but this is an architectural inference, not a head-to-head benchmark against CacheRoute-scale adversarial conditions. `evergreen` (3282 words)
- [[interim-report-approximate-vs-precise-index]] — Interim report — approximate-vs-precise-index — Dual-key design means the router does NOT need to match vLLM's hash algorithm bit-for-bit (block-size and tokenizer identity are what must match, with one unresolved HashSeed/PYTHONHASHSEED tension); precise routing is production-ready only for vLLM today — SGLang emits the right event schema but salted/multimodal blocks travel in a deliberately-separate wire tag llm-d's adapter may not parse, and TensorRT-LLM now has a real (Sep 10 2026, prototype) streaming KV-event publisher but llm-d has built no consumer for it; the corpus's 9.97% figure measures P2P's lift under precise vs approximate routing, not precise vs approximate routing itself. `evergreen` (3777 words)
- [[interim-report-availability-and-lifecycle-off-k8s]] — Interim report — availability-and-lifecycle-off-k8s — RESOLVED: llm-d-router's default EPP HA (--ha-enable-leader-election) literally uses Kubernetes coordination.k8s.io/Lease (confirmed in docs/operations.md); Fail-Open Resiliency (router.proxy.failOpen=true default) already scopes fail-open to the lease-transition window, resolving the fail-open/fail-closed dispute for llm-d-router's own Envoy path -- it remains genuinely unresolved only in agentgateway's separate standalone-EPP passthrough integration (#1661, open). All 3 documented HA modes (Lease leader-election, StatefulSet Priority Routing, Active-Active) are Kubernetes-coupled; non-k8s deployments get single-instance EPP for free but must engineer HA themselves. x-gateway-destination-endpoint client-spoofing is resolved in current code (test proves client value discarded). `evergreen` (3142 words)
- [[interim-report-cache-salt-lora-multitenant-routing]] — Interim report — cache-salt-lora-multitenant-routing — llm-d's precise-prefix-cache-producer is salt- and LoRA-aware for vLLM (recomputes block keys from the same extra_keys vLLM emits, preserving affinity without defeating engine-enforced isolation), but this requires explicit opt-in; the default approximate scorer is completely tenant/salt/LoRA-blind, and llm-d itself enforces no isolation policy of its own. `evergreen` (2488 words)
- [[interim-report-control-plane-convergence-or-proliferation]] — Interim report — control-plane-convergence-or-proliferation — llm-d/KServe/AIBrix/Dynamo converge at substrate (K8s+vLLM) and API contract (Gateway API Inference Extension, EPP) but fork at KV-routing logic and governance; KServe literally runs llm-d's EPP, AIBrix has its own non-LMCache cache and ByteDance-dominated governance, Dynamo forked its own EPP and benchmarks only on NVIDIA's newest hardware, and the EPP reference impl's move out of neutral kubernetes-sigs governance (issue #2430) plus KAITO's stalled migration PR show sponsor choice, not design choice, is what's actually being decided. `review` (3586 words)
- [[interim-report-custom-discovery-plugin-design]] — Interim report — custom-discovery-plugin-design — Custom EndpointDiscovery plugin design: llm-d-router's only shipped plugin is file-discovery (poll+fsnotify); the K8s watch-based plugin PR (#1007) was abandoned unmerged (verified via GitHub API), so there is zero in-repo watch-based reference. Recommends a 3-tier design (poll+diff / long-poll via e.g. Consul blocking queries / true persistent watch) with concrete effort estimates, using Dynamo, production-stack, Consul, and RAMSES as cross-checked references. `evergreen` (3520 words)
- [[interim-report-default-scoring-configuration]] — Interim report — default-scoring-configuration — llm-d's default scoring config: v0.9 blog explicitly states sticky-until-saturated (prefix-cache-affinity-filter@0.8 + token-load/active-request scorer, tau-gated) is now default across all well-lit-path guides, superseding the v0.4-originated legacy weighted blend; but the canonical EndpointPickerConfig schema docs still show the legacy blend as their recommended example (documentation lag). New design is more portable/calibratable, not strictly better -- concedes TTFT-p90 and hit-rate to the legacy blend on the b2b-saas workload per the source's own benchmark. `evergreen` (2730 words)
- [[interim-report-end-to-end-reference-architecture]] — Interim report — end-to-end-reference-architecture — Assembles the internal LLM-serving reference architecture (gateway/control-plane, Kubernetes+KEDA+Karpenter orchestration, vLLM vs Triton-fronted engine fork, cache-aware routing, and the streaming-vs-snapshot weight-loading fork) from first-person accounts (Thomson Reuters, T-Tech, Tesla/llm-d, Netflix, Atlassian, practitioner GitOps stack), and confirms via primary sources that the '460s->70s' Modal figure and '~80Gbps' Run:ai figure circulating in secondary literature are fabricated (Modal's real number is 45s->5s; NVIDIA's own post never states a Gbps figure). `review` (5765 words)
- [[interim-report-extension-mechanism]] — Interim report — extension-mechanism — Out-of-tree EndpointDiscovery plugin (thin downstream main() importing cmd/epp/runner) beats in-tree fork and custom-router embedding for non-pod backends; file-discovery-by-generator covers the static-list case with zero Go; upstream in parallel. Registry mechanism is source-verified sound but undocumented and unprecedented -- audited-absence search confirmed zero downstream examples and zero CONTRIBUTING.md guidance -- and open contributor issue #2105 is the closest thing to a policy statement found, proposing in-tree as "the supported surface" and out-of-tree as "at-your-own-risk" (unratified). Discovery's config surface has reshaped three times in about four months. `evergreen` (3543 words)
- [[interim-report-index-freshness-and-recovery]] — Interim report — index-freshness-and-recovery — llm-d's index-restart blindness (issue #372) is fully fixed as of PR #1887 (merged Aug 6, 2026, dependency vLLM #45177 merged Jul 7, 2026, both verified this session), but pod-loss invalidation (issue #544's Index.Clear() proposal) remains unimplemented and stale-bot-flagged as of Sep 4, 2026, and offloaded-tier invalidation ownership is an unresolved vLLM-side contract as of Jul 2026 — the freshness guarantee is scoped, not blanket. `evergreen` (3129 words)

- [[interim-report-topology-and-k8s-shims]] — Interim report — topology-and-k8s-shims — Topology decision rule: EndpointSlice-shimming is a category error (InferencePool/EPP discovery watches Pods directly, confirmed at pod_reconciler.go HEAD, never EndpointSlices); virtual-kubelet is architecturally viable for the EPP (admission = label match + aggregate Ready only, PodIP unvalidated) but no production HPC provider (interLink) delivers the needed inbound reachability; Slinky answers a different question (convert the estate) not this one; the real fork is router-in-cluster vs out-of-cluster (organizational), both served identically by file/custom EndpointDiscovery plugins. `evergreen` (2996 words)

- [[introducing-aibrix-a-scalable-cost-effective-control-plane-for-vllm-vllm-blog]] — Introducing AIBrix: A Scalable, Cost-Effective Control Plane for vLLM | vLLM Blog — vLLM project's own Feb 21 2025 release announcement for AIBrix, confirming it as 'a battery-included vLLM Kubernetes serving stack developed by Bytedance', started early 2024, 'successfully deployed to support multiple business use cases across ByteDance' (first-party ByteDance-in-production claim, not third-party). Quotes Clayton Coleman (Google, GKE Inference Lead) and Robert Nishihara (Anyscale co-founder, Ray co-creator) endorsing the project, confirming Google and Anyscale as external validators/collaborators, not just ByteDance-internal. `evergreen` (747 words)
- [[introducing-aibrix-cost-effective-and-scalable-control-plane-for-vllm-aibrix-blo]] — Introducing AIBrix: Cost-Effective and Scalable Control Plane for vLLM | AIBrix Blogs — AIBrix's own deep-dive architecture blog (Feb 2025), the most detailed primary description of its layer boundary: explicitly frames a 'Three-Layer Approach' of Open-Source Models / Inference Engines (vLLM) / System-Level Orchestration (AIBrix), stating 'system-level orchestration is where true cost efficiency is unlocked' and that AIBrix 'operates at the orchestration level, serving as vLLM's control plane.' States AIBrix only supports vLLM as inference engine at time of writing ('Now, only vLLM is supported' for the Unified AI Runtime's vendor-agnostic engine abstraction) -- i.e. engine-agnosticism was aspirational, not yet real, at v0.1-0.2. Gives numeric results from ByteDance's own production/benchmark use: LoRA management delivers '4.7x cost reduction in low-traffic scenarios and 1.5x savings even under high demand'; routing strategy selection 'reduce mean latency by 19.2% and P99 latency by 79% on public datasets'; LLM-specific autoscaling (KPA/APA replacing Prometheus polling with sliding-window metric aggregation) achieves '11.5% reduction in latency, 11.4% increase in token throughput, and 33% fewer scaling oscillations compared to native HPA'; distributed KV cache pool combined with vLLM prefix caching 'improves peak throughput by ~50%, reduces average and P99 TTFT by ~60% and ~70%'. Documents the capability gap explicitly: notes vLLM's built-in KV cache has 'key limitations: constrained memory capacity, engine-specific storage that prevents sharing across instances, and difficulty supporting scenarios like KV migration and prefill-decode disaggregation' -- AIBrix's distributed KV cache pool was built to fill exactly this gap. Also documents a hybrid-orchestration design gap: 'Kubernetes operators can be overly complex for fine-grained scheduling, while Ray excels in distributed communication but lacks broader resource management' -- informed by 'ByteDance's internal experience in hosting Kubernetes and Ray workloads.' Credits academic lineage directly: features inspired by Preble, Melange, QLM, and MoonCake research papers. States governance: 'Originally open-sourced by ByteDance, AIBrix has rapidly evolved into a fully open-source project with contributions from the University of Michigan, University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign, University of Washington, Google, DaoCloud, and other industry and academic partners' -- distinguishes founding (ByteDance) from the broader multi-institution contributor base. `evergreen` (2288 words)
- [[introducing-disaggregated-inference-on-aws-powered-by-llm-d-artificial-intellige]] — Introducing Disaggregated Inference on AWS powered by llm-d | Artificial Intelligence — AWS ML blog (joint effort with Red Hat's llm-d team, credits Greg Pereira and Robert Shaw) announcing ghcr.io/llm-d/llm-d-aws container image with AWS-specific libraries (EFA, libfabric) for llm-d on SageMaker HyperPod EKS / Amazon EKS. Explains llm-d's four well-lit paths: (1) intelligent inference scheduling -- the scheduler 'maintain[s] visibility into the cache state across the serving replicas and rout[es] requests accordingly' without requiring direct introspection of KV cache state, guessing locality instead; (2) prefill/decode disaggregation, where a sidecar next to decode instances instructs vLLM to perform point-to-point KV cache transfers over fast interconnects from prefill servers; (3) wide expert parallelism for MoE models (DeepSeek-R1, Qwen3.5, Minimax, Kimi K2.5); (4) tiered prefix caching, offloading KV cache entries from GPU memory to CPU memory or local disk to expand effective cache size beyond GPU memory limits. Architecture detail: NVIDIA Inference Xfer Library (NIXL) provides the point-to-point KV-cache transfer abstraction (vs. NCCL's collective-only patterns), using RDMA over AWS Elastic Fabric Adapter (EFA) via a libfabric plugin/transport layer for GPU-Direct, kernel-bypass, zero-copy transfers between prefill and decode nodes. Deployment uses llm-d's Gateway API Inference Extension (Istio or Kgateway) plus helmfile-based well-lit-path guides (guides/inference-scheduling, guides/pd-disaggregation) with vLLM configured via --kv-transfer-config '{kv_connector:NixlConnector, kv_role:kv_both, kv_connector_extra_config:{backends:[LIBFABRIC]}}'. Benchmark: GPT-OSS on vLLM tensor-parallel-4 on ml.p6-b200.48xlarge vs. llm-d P/D disaggregation (4 prefill pods TP=1, 1 decode pod TP=4) connected via NIXL/Libfabric/EFA -- disaggregation increased tokens/sec by up to 70% as concurrency rises, at 1024-in/1024-out tokens up to concurrency 128. Recommends EFA allocation of 4 interfaces per GPU on p5.48xlarge (8 H100s, 32 EFA interfaces). `evergreen` (3417 words)
- [[introducing-gateway-api-inference-extension-kubernetes]] — Introducing Gateway API Inference Extension | Kubernetes — Kubernetes.io blog (June 5, 2025) introducing GIE, authored by maintainers from Solo.io, Google, Bytedance. Describes two CRDs at the time: InferencePool (platform-admin owned, groups pods on shared compute, similar to Service but model-serving aware) and InferenceModel (AI/ML-owner owned, maps a public model name like 'gpt-4-chat' to actual models in a pool, supports traffic splitting/prioritization -- this CRD was later renamed/restructured into InferenceObjective in the v1 GA API per other sources in this batch). Request flow: Gateway routing (HTTPRoute match) -> Endpoint Selection Extension (ESE) examines live pod metrics (queue length, memory, loaded adapters) -> forwards to chosen pod. Benchmarks: H100 GPU pods running vLLM v1, 10 Llama2 replicas, ShareGPT dataset, 100-1000 QPS via Latency Profile Generator -- found comparable throughput to a plain Kubernetes Service but significantly lower p90 per-output-token and overall latency at 500+ QPS. - [[introduction-kubernetes-gateway-api-inference-extension]] — Introduction - Kubernetes Gateway API Inference Extension — Official GIE project homepage defining the terminology used throughout the ecosystem. Defines Inference Gateway (a proxy/LB coupled with an Endpoint Picker extension), Inference Router (the decision-making component), 'Metrics and Capabilities' (model-server-reported data like Prefix Cache status and LoRA Adapter availability used to optimize routing), and Endpoint Picker/EPP (reference implementation of an Inference Router, provided here only for conformance testing -- production users are told to implement their own EPP or use an existing one like llm-d-router). Explains the mechanism is built on Envoy's External Processing Protocol (ext-proc) layered onto any gateway supporting Gateway API + ext-proc; names Envoy Gateway, kgateway, and GKE Gateway as gateways this extends into Inference Gateways, and names LiteLLM, Gloo AI Gateway, and Apigee as higher-level 'AI Gateways' this can sit underneath. States project is driven by WG-Serving and SIG-Network (official Kubernetes governance groups), not a single vendor. Lists 5-step request flow: Gateway selects InferencePool via HTTPRoute match -> forwards request info to EPP -> EPP fetches live metrics from pool endpoints -> EPP instructs Gateway of chosen endpoint -> Gateway routes. Key features named: model-aware routing (including to LoRA fine-tunes), serving priority (criticality-based), model rollouts (traffic splitting), extensibility for bespoke routing, and customizable inference-optimized load balancing that reduces latency and improves GPU utilization versus traditional LB. `review` (952 words)
- [[introduction-interlink]] — Introduction | interLink — interLink project docs (v0.6.x, fetched as substitute for a paywalled CHEP2025 conference paper on the same project) describe a Virtual-Kubelet-based system letting a 'local' k8s cluster offload container execution to remote systems (another k8s cluster, HPC/SLURM/HTCondor batch systems, on-demand VMs, or lambda-like services) via a pluggable interLink API + provider-plugin spec. Directly relevant to router-customization for non-pod backends: interLink explicitly states in a 'NOT a target' section that it is NOT designed for long-running, continuously available services -- 'Our solution is not designed for services that need to run continuously for extended periods. It is optimized for tasks that have a defined start and end, rather than persistent services exposing intra-cluster communication endpoints.' This is a load-bearing negative finding: interLink's virtual-pod abstraction targets batch/job-style HPC offload (AI training, ML optimization jobs, GPU-heavy on-demand runs), NOT stable, continuously reachable inference-serving endpoints that an EPP/router would pick as a routing destination -- so interLink is not a fit for making a long-lived non-k8s inference backend directly reachable through the standard virtual-kubelet Pod abstraction; a custom EPP/router backend-discovery plugin talking to the remote scheduler directly (Slurm, VM, etc.) is a better-aligned integration point than routing traffic through an interLink virtual pod. Three deployment topologies are documented (edge-node, in-cluster, tunneled), the last supporting secure tunneled communication to remote interLink API/plugin servers behind NAT or on shared login nodes. `evergreen` (916 words)

- [[istio-kubernetes-gateway-api-inference-extension]] — Istio / Kubernetes Gateway API Inference Extension — Istio's official GIE integration task doc, confirming Istio's Inference Extension support is entirely Kubernetes-native and InferencePool-bound: it requires installing Gateway API + Gateway API Inference Extension CRDs, enabling Pilot env flags 'SUPPORT_GATEWAY_API_INFERENCE_EXTENSION'/'ENABLE_GATEWAY_API_INFERENCE_EXTENSION', and wiring an InferencePool resource with a 'selector.matchLabels' targeting Kubernetes Pods plus an 'endpointPickerRef' Service. - [[istio-brings-future-ready-service-mesh-to-the-ai-era-with-new-ambient-multiclust]] — Istio Brings Future Ready Service Mesh to the AI Era with New Ambient Multicluster, Gateway API Inference Extension and More | CNCF — Official CNCF press release (KubeCon + CloudNativeCon Europe, Amsterdam, March 25 2026) announcing Istio's beta release of Gateway API Inference Extension support, alongside Ambient Multicluster beta and experimental agentgateway integration. Confirms inference-aware routing is moving from gateway-only into service-mesh-wide scope: the extension 'integrates machine learning inference directly into mesh traffic flows, offering a consistent developer experience... for platform teams familiar with the Kubernetes standard.'
