Cites CNCF's own Annual Cloud Native Survey finding that 66% of organizations run GenAI workloads on Kubernetes, yet only 7% achieve daily deployment velocity for AI workloads -- and that service-mesh-in-production adoption correlates with cloud-native maturity (self-described 'innovators' are nearly 3x more likely than 'explorers' to run service mesh in production). Quotes Istio maintainer Keith Mattix and CNCF CTO Chris Aniszczyk framing the updates as meeting rising demand to run AI workloads 'with confidence' atop existing service-mesh security/observability investments rather than building a parallel AI-specific control plane. `review` (797 words)
- [[kv-cache-events-synchronization-aibrix]] — KV Cache Events Synchronization — AIBrix — AIBrix (ByteDance's Kubernetes-native vLLM control plane) documentation for KV Cache Events Synchronization, an alternative to llm-d's KV-event design: vLLM instances publish BlockStoredEvent (BlockHashes, TokenIDs, ModelName, LoraID, SourcePod, ParentBlockHash) and BlockRemovedEvent messages over a ZMQ pub/sub pattern (ports 5557 for events, 5558 for replay) to an in-gateway KV Event Manager, which feeds a Sync Prefix Cache Indexer maintaining global prefix cache state for the Gateway Router. The feature has a strict hard dependency on a remote tokenizer being enabled (AIBRIX_PREFIX_CACHE_USE_REMOTE_TOKENIZER=true) to guarantee identical tokenization between gateway and vLLM workers — the system refuses to initialize KV sync without it — and requires vLLM >=0.7.0 with KV cache events support plus gateway-plugins built with libzmq3-dev (-tags="zmq"). Routing selects pods by prefix-match percentage (descending) then running-request count (ascending), picking the first pod satisfying running_requests <= mean + STANDARD_DEVIATION_FACTOR * sigma, falling back to the globally least-loaded ready pod if no matched pod clears that bar; a separate, centrally-applied load-imbalance gate runs ahead of whichever routing strategy is active. Documented resource costs: ~1MB/s ZMQ traffic per vLLM pod at high load, ~64 bytes per prefix entry in the sync indexer, and <1% CPU overhead per pod. `evergreen` (1273 words)
- [[kv-cache-events-lmcache]] — KV Cache Events | LMCache — LMCache official docs (in-process mode, now deprecated in favor of LMCache MP mode) specifying the wire format for KV-cache lifecycle events used by KV-aware routers. Events follow vLLM's BlockStored class schema (block_hashes, parent_block_hash, token_ids, block_size, lora_id, and an LMCache-added 'medium' field of GPU/CPU/disk for offload tier). LMCache generates the events and hands them to vLLM (needs vLLM v0.13.0+, LMCache v0.3.11+) or SGLang to publish over their existing messaging system (ZMQ, topic 'kv-events') — LMCache does not run its own separate transport. Requires explicit config: enable_kv_events: true in the LMCache YAML, plus vLLM's --kv-events-config flag with publisher/topic; multi-worker vLLM deployments need a non-default pre_caching_hash_algorithm (e.g., sha256_cbor_64bit) per worker or hash collisions cause duplicate events across workers. SGLang's BlockStored payload omits the medium and lora_name fields that vLLM's includes, so a consuming subscriber script needs to branch on backend. Gives a worked example with Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B showing the actual emitted event payloads for both backends. `evergreen` (706 words)
- [[kv-cache-management-llm-d]] — KV Cache Management | llm-d — llm-d dev-version overview page defining KV cache management as three composable architectural pillars: (1) Prefix-Cache Aware Routing — the EPP-managed intelligence layer that routes requests to replicas already holding the relevant KV cache, offered in Approximate (heuristic) and Precise (event-driven) variants; (2) KV-Cache Indexing — the observability layer consuming high-frequency KVEvents from engines like vLLM to track block movement/eviction across offloaded tiers; (3) KV Offloading — the capacity layer that spills cache to CPU memory or SSD beyond GPU HBM limits. Also introduces a fourth layer, P2P Sharing, which composes the other three into a fleet-wide cache: the index identifies which peer holds a prefix, the router stamps the request, and the model server pulls blocks directly from the peer's CPU offload tier over NIXL without touching the source pod's GPU; a promised-but-undelivered block degrades to normal recompute rather than failing the request. `evergreen` (491 words)
- [[kv-cache-offloading-production-stack]] — KV Cache Offloading — production-stack — vLLM Production Stack official tutorial (v0.1.2) demonstrating KV cache offloading via LMCache in a Kubernetes/Helm deployment. Confirms vLLM Production Stack uses LMCache (not a built-in mechanism) for moving KV caches from GPU memory to CPU/disk to increase cache-hit opportunities. Config example disables vLLM's native chunked-prefill and prefix-caching (enableChunkedPrefill: false, enablePrefixCaching: false) and instead sets lmcacheConfig.enabled: true with cpuOffloadingBufferSize: 20 (GB), using the lmcache/vllm-openai container image. Validation is via log lines from lmcache_connector.py and LMCacheEngine confirming LMCache (not vLLM) is handling the store/retrieve path. `review` (373 words)
- [[kv-offloading-usage-guide-vllm]] — KV Offloading Usage Guide - vLLM — vLLM docs reference for configuring the OffloadingConnector (single-tier CPUOffloadingSpec or multi-tier TieringOffloadingSpec). Only the CPU primary tier has direct GPU access; secondary tiers (fs, obj, p2p) never touch GPU memory -- all GPU<->secondary transfers stage through CPU. Config surface includes cpu_bytes_to_use (total host bytes, not per-worker), block_size/blocks_per_chunk, eviction_policy (built-in lru/arc or custom via cache_policy_module_path), offload_prompt_only (default true -- only prefill blocks offloaded, not decode), and self_describing_kv_events (opt-in richer BlockStored/BlockRemoved payloads with block hashes, token_ids, block_size, parent hash, LoRA/group metadata for external KV-event consumers to index offloaded blocks). Filesystem and object-store secondary tiers publish hash-only BlockStored KV events with a 'locality' field (LOCAL/REMOTE relative to the publishing instance) that is NOT inferred from tier type -- an obj tier is not implicitly REMOTE. Cross-process/cross-instance KV sharing (shared filesystem mount, shared object bucket, or P2P) works by default because block content hashes are deterministic across instances via a fixed NONE_HASH seed, EXCEPT under the non-cryptographic xxhash/xxhash_cbor prefix-caching-hash-algo, which requires manually matching PYTHONHASHSEED across all instances or cross-instance cache sharing silently fails. Documents in detail the P2P orchestration-layer protocol most relevant to llm-d: the P2P secondary tier itself only executes lookups/transfers -- the router/EPP orchestration layer decides which peer to pull from and drives every transfer via three mutually-exclusive-ish keys in the request's kv_transfer_params (remote_decoder, remote_prefiller, remote_kv_source), each carrying a kv_request_id plus, for prefiller/kv_source roles, remote_host/remote_port of the specific peer. remote_decoder+remote_kv_source is the only legal dual-role combination. Gives the full runtime handshake: consumer sends LookupMsg (kv_request_id + block hashes) at on_schedule_end, producer replies with hit hashes, consumer calls submit_load for hits only, producer performs NIXL WRITE and sends TransferDone; classic P/D mode (remote_prefiller set) skips the lookup round-trip entirely since the decoder assumes the prefiller holds all its blocks. Also documents per-request max_offload_tokens to cap how many prefix tokens are eligible for offload (e.g., cache a shared system prompt but not user-specific tail tokens). `evergreen` (3708 words)
- [[kv-aware-routing-on-kubernetes-nvidia-dynamo-documentation]] — KV-Aware Routing on Kubernetes | NVIDIA Dynamo Documentation — NVIDIA Dynamo docs page (redirect landed on 'KV-Aware Routing on Kubernetes', current as of fetch) lays out three deployment topologies for KV-cache-aware routing, directly relevant to the GIE/EPP + non-pod-backend question. (1) 'Dynamo Frontend routing': the Dynamo Frontend itself tokenizes, scores workers, and forwards -- fewest components, no Gateway API needed, router flags live on the Frontend (--router-mode kv). (2) 'GAIE with Dynamo': a k8s Gateway calls a Dynamo Endpoint Picker Plugin (EPP) implementing the GAIE endpoint-selection protocol before forwarding to a worker pod; worker Frontend sidecars then run --router-mode direct since EPP already decided. (3) 'GAIE with vanilla vLLM': explicitly described as adding 'Dynamo's advanced KV-aware routing without migrating the fleet to Dynamo workers' via 'standalone Dynamo EPP support' with 'EPP with direct vLLM worker discovery and vLLM token rendering' -- i.e., this is NVIDIA's own standalone-EPP pattern for routing to a backend fleet (upstream vLLM pods) that is NOT running the Dynamo-specific worker stack, evidence that EPP-based routing to heterogeneous/non-native backends is an established, productized pattern beyond llm-d/GIE's own standalone-EPP work. Explicit anti-pattern warning: 'Do not configure the Frontend to select a worker after the EPP has already selected one' -- i.e. worker-selection authority must be singular per request path, a design constraint relevant to any router customization that layers additional logic on top of EPP's picks. `evergreen` (741 words)
- [[kv-cache-indexer-llm-d]] — KV-Cache Indexer | llm-d — v0.7 architecture doc (marked no-longer-actively-maintained but describes still-current design) for the KV-Cache Indexer, the EPP subcomponent enabling precise prefix-cache-aware routing. Three cooperating parts: an Index (block key → pods mapping built from KVEvents: BlockStored, BlockRemoved, AllBlocksCleared, published over ZMQ by vLLM/SGLang), a Data Producer (tokenizes prompts once per request so downstream components share the result), and a Scorer (precise-prefix-cache-scorer, finds the longest consecutive prefix of cached blocks per pod, since block i requires the unbroken prefix 0..i-1 due to causal attention; a worked example shows Pod A scoring 4/5 blocks, Pod B 2/5 due to a chain break, Pod C 0). Tier-weighted scoring defaults to gpu=1.0, cpu=0.8, taking the max weight if a block is cached on multiple tiers; scores normalize to [0.0, 1.0] before combining with other scorers (queue depth, KV utilization) in the Filter-Score-Pick pipeline. Two event delivery modes: Centralized (all pods PUB to one EPP SUB endpoint) and Pod discovery (each pod binds its own socket; every EPP replica subscribes to every pod, enabling active-active multi-EPP HA). Three Index backends: in-memory two-level LRU (default, ~100M keys x 10 pod entries), Ristretto-based cost-aware memory (byte-budgeted, useful for variable-size multimodal/LoRA entries), and Redis/Valkey (external, adds network hop, rarely needed). Speculative Indexing (recommended for production) inserts short-lived predicted entries immediately after a routing decision with a default 2-second TTL to cover the gap before a confirming KVEvent arrives. Multimodal content hashes and LoRA adapter names are folded into block keys so cache hits are correctly scoped; hybrid-attention-aware scoring (full/sliding-window/linear layer groups evicting independently) is explicitly flagged as work in progress. `evergreen` (1721 words)
- [[kv-cache-offloading-llm-d]] — KV-Cache Offloading | llm-d — Deep-dive on llm-d KV-cache offloading, which extends effective cache capacity beyond GPU HBM by moving KV blocks to CPU DRAM or shared storage, reading up to 16x faster than recomputation for long prompts. Describes two integration patterns: (1) Native vLLM OffloadingConnector — dispatches blocks to CPU RAM (~250GB/GPU, low latency, per-node) via GPU DMA with pinned staging buffers (contiguous memory layout since vLLM 0.12.0 improves transfer throughput 4-5x), or to shared storage (TB+, cross-cluster) via the llm-d FS backend, which stores KV blocks as files on any POSIX filesystem (CephFS, Lustre, NVMe) and needs an external PVC Evictor for cleanup; (2) Out-of-tree connectors (LMCache, Mooncake, NVIDIA KVBM) that own their own indexing/memory/storage outside vLLM. Gives full architectural and config detail on MooncakeStoreConnector: a Mooncake Master (centralized metadata/eviction/leases, default eviction_high_watermark_ratio=0.95, eviction_ratio=0.05, default_kv_lease_ttl=5000ms, default_kv_soft_pin_ttl=1800000ms), an optional standalone Mooncake Client that owns DRAM/SSD outside the vLLM process, and a Transfer Engine moving bytes via RDMA/TCP; embedded vs standalone-store deployment modes trade simplicity for storage/compute decoupling. Notes that PYTHONHASHSEED must be fixed (e.g., 0) across all vLLM instances sharing a Mooncake store because block hashes are content-addressed keys. Offloading is explicitly described as complementary to (not a substitute for) prefix-cache-aware routing: routing picks replicas that can reuse cached blocks, offloading grows how much each replica can hold. `evergreen` (3268 words)
- [[kv-cache-wins-you-can-see-from-prefix-caching-in-vllm-to-distributed-scheduling]] — KV-Cache Wins You Can See: From Prefix Caching in vLLM to Distributed Scheduling with llm-d | llm-d — llm-d blog (well-lit path deep-dive) explains the KV-cache indexer's two-component write/read architecture, now shipped in the separate github.com/llm-d/llm-d-kv-cache library (distinct from the older llm-d-kv-cache-manager repo — worth flagging as a possible repo split/rename to verify against release notes). Write path: vLLM pods emit KVEvents over ZMQ on block create/evict; 'kvevents.Pool' (a sharded ZMQ worker pool) ingests the stream and updates a low-level KV-Block Index (block-hash -> pod + memory-medium GPU/CPU); a higher-level 'kvcache.Index' maps token-sequence prefixes to pods. Read path: scheduler queries kvcache.Index per candidate pod to get cache-hit percentage, used by the Precise Prefix-Cache Scorer alongside load-aware scores. States the design 'naturally supports active-active or active-passive deployments, with either full view duplication or sharding through configurations' for HA. Reports a controlled benchmark (8 vLLM pods, 16 H100s, Qwen3-32B, 150 simulated enterprise customers each with 6,000-token shared prefixes, 5 users/customer submitting 1,200-token queries, 3-60 QPS Poisson load, cache demand = 73% of cluster capacity = 6x any single pod's capacity): precise-scheduling achieved 8,730 output tok/s vs 6,944 (approximate) vs 4,429 (load/random); TTFT p90 of 0.542s vs 31.083s (approximate, 57x slower) vs ~93s (cache-blind, 170x slower). Appendix gives indexer memory overhead: DeepSeek-R1 FP8 on 8xH200 (365GB KV-cache pool, 128-token/8.6MB blocks) needs only ~339KB of index metadata (8-byte hash per block) — a 1,000,000:1 data-to-metadata ratio. Notes Alibaba Cloud (ACK Gateway with Inference Extension) and DaoCloud (d.run MaaS) are adopting/extending this precise-routing approach. `evergreen` (3968 words)
- [[kvcache-cache-in-the-wild-characterizing-and-optimizing-kvcache-cache]] — KVCache Cache in the Wild: Characterizing and Optimizing KVCache Cache — Shanghai Jiao Tong University researchers with Alibaba Group engineers present the first systematic characterization of KV-cache (KV$) reuse patterns from production traces at Alibaba Cloud (ALIYUN), covering two representative workloads: Trace A (a to-C workload -- browser/mobile chatbot users) and Trace B (a to-B workload -- developers sending OpenAI-compatible API calls). This is cloud-provider multi-tenant production data, useful as a proxy for how internal platform operators should think about prefix-cache sizing and eviction, not itself an internal-enterprise deployment.

KEY MEASURED FINDINGS: (1) Ideal (infinite-capacity) KV-cache hit rates are 62% (Trace A, to-C) and 54% (Trace B, to-B) -- lower than previously reported numbers on synthetic/ShareGPT-style datasets, meaning synthetic benchmarks overstate achievable cache benefit. (2) KV-cache reuse is highly skewed: 10% of KV-cache blocks contribute 77% of all reuses. (3) Contrary to the expectation that multi-turn conversations dominate cache reuse, single-turn requests account for 97% of KV-cache reuses in the to-B (API) workload -- single-turn reuse is NOT a negligible phenomenon and must be designed for, not just multi-turn session locality. (4) User-level reuse is also skewed: 19% of users in Trace A and just 4% of users in Trace B account for more than 90% of cache block hits, motivating the authors' recommendation to prioritize intra-user over inter-user KV-cache reuse in system design. (5) KV-cache lifespan is ephemeral: P99 lifespan of KV-cache entries in the to-B workload is 97 seconds, meaning a relatively small cache suffices -- specifically, cache capacity of about 2x per-GPU HBM is enough to approach the ideal infinite-capacity hit rate under common GQA configurations (for this workload). (6) Reuse-time and reuse-probability distributions vary by request category (API vs chat, and number of turns) but are predictable within a category from historical data, which the paper exploits to build a workload-aware cache eviction policy that outperforms generic (LRU-style) eviction, especially under limited cache capacity. `review` (12876 words)
- [[license]] — LICENSE — Confirms Arize-ai/openinference is Apache License 2.0 — a separate, more permissive license than Phoenix's own Elastic License 2.0, demonstrating that Arize splits its instrumentation layer (Apache-2.0 OpenInference) from its storage/UI platform (ELv2 Phoenix). `review` (1595 words)
- [[licensemd]] — LICENSE.md — Confirms EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness is MIT licensed (Copyright 2020 EleutherAI), fully permissive — no EE/enterprise carve-out, unlike Langfuse's ee/ folders. `review` (186 words)
- [[llm-autoscaling-and-cold-starts-in-kubernetes-architecture-keda-metrics-model-we]] — LLM Autoscaling and Cold Starts in Kubernetes: Architecture, KEDA Metrics, Model Weight Caching, and Ephemeral GPU Provisioning — LLMs Blog — Dense technical gray-literature post (llms.blog, Aug 2026) decomposing a K8s LLM cold start into 5 sequential phases with measured ranges: (1) node provisioning/driver init 120-300s via Karpenter/Cluster Autoscaler, (2) container image distribution 60-240s for 15-25GB images (CUDA/PyTorch/vLLM/SGLang/TGI), (3) weight transfer to host storage 30-180s (70B fp16 = ~140GB; 8B = 16GB; standard NFS/EFS throttles at 100-250MB/s), (4) host RAM to GPU VRAM over PCIe Gen4/5 (32-64GB/s) taking 5-40s, (5) runtime engine warmup + CUDA graph capture for discrete batch-size buckets (1,2,4,8,16,32) taking 15-60s -- cumulative unoptimized cold start exceeds 8 minutes. Argues hardware metrics (GPU core %, DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL) are misleading autoscaling signals under continuous batching because a pod with 2 requests and a pod with 50 queued requests both read ~100% GPU utilization; recommends scaling on engine-exposed application metrics instead: vllm:num_requests_waiting (queue depth), vllm:gpu_cache_usage_perc (PagedAttention KV-block occupancy), and p95 vllm:time_to_first_token_seconds. Provides a working KEDA ScaledObject YAML querying vllm:num_requests_waiting via Prometheus with asymmetric policies: aggressive scale-up (stabilizationWindowSeconds:0, +100% per 15s) vs conservative scale-down (300s stabilization window, 1 pod per 120s) to avoid discarding warm GPU contexts. Storage comparison table for loading 140GB: standard AWS EFS 9.3-23.3min, multi-threaded S3 1.5-2.9min, local NVMe direct 23-40s, host pinned memory 5-6s -- motivating hostPath NVMe caching with vLLM's mmap-based safetensors loading, distributed caching layers (Fluid/JuiceFS/Alluxio) for catalogs too large for local disk, and P2P image distribution (Spegel, Dragonfly) plus lazy/streaming image formats (eStargz, SOCI) to compress the image-pull phase. Describes 'pause pod' overprovisioning: low-priority placeholder pods hold pre-warmed GPU nodes that get evicted in seconds when KEDA triggers real inference pod creation, while Karpenter asynchronously provisions replacement nodes in under 45s. Cites AWS EKS KEDA docs, vLLM production-stack KEDA docs, a Red Hat Developer KServe+KEDA article, and a BentoML '25x faster cold starts' post as its own sources. `review` (1974 words)
- [[llm-cost-attribution-and-team-budget-tracking]] — LLM Cost Attribution and Team Budget Tracking — TrueFoundry (AI Gateway vendor) engineering blog on gateway-level LLM cost attribution, published Sep 11 2026. Core argument: provider-native billing aggregates at the credential boundary (Anthropic by API key, OpenAI by project, Azure by deployment/resource group, Bedrock by IAM role/account), never at the team/app/feature boundary organizations actually want to charge -- so attribution must happen at the gateway, the one point every provider call already routes through. Mechanism: an X-TFY-METADATA JSON header set per-request by the calling application (team/app/feature/env/user_id/etc.), with low-cardinality fields (team, app, feature, env) projected to metric labels and high-cardinality fields (user_id, pipeline_id) kept on the trace only to avoid metric-storage explosion; metadata auto-propagates across fallback and multi-turn/agent spans. Per-trace cost is computed at span close from a versioned (provider,model)-dated pricing table applied to gen_ai.usage token counts, explicitly subtracting both cache-read AND cache-write tokens from the 'fresh' input count before pricing (a documented common bug is subtracting only cache-read, double-counting cache-write). Aggregation is three-layer pre-aggregation (per-minute in-memory counters to hourly ClickHouse/TimescaleDB rollup to daily team x app x model materialized view) because querying raw trace volume (millions of spans/day) directly is cost-prohibitive. Budget enforcement is two-tier: soft alerts at 80% (PagerDuty/Slack, no blocking) and hard limits at 100% returning HTTP 429 with a budget_exhausted error; the check-then-spend race under concurrent requests is resolved with an atomic Redis INCRBYFLOAT counter incremented pre-dispatch and reconciled against actual cost at span close -- deliberately over-counts on aborted requests rather than under-counting. Budget-aware routing degrades gracefully (e.g. Sonnet to Haiku at 80% utilization, output-token cap added at 95%) rather than hard-cutting to 429. Chargeback report schema is the daily rollup grouped by team/app/model/provider with a cache_savings_usd column often representing a third to two-thirds of spend for agents with stable system prompts. IMPORTANT caveat: this is vendor content; it contains an unverified competitive claim stated as fact -- 'TrueFoundry AI Gateway delivers ~3-4ms latency, handles 350+ RPS on 1 vCPU... while LiteLLM suffers from high latency, struggles beyond moderate RPS, lacks built-in scaling' -- presented with no benchmark methodology, so treat as marketing, not independent evidence, regarding LiteLLM's actual throughput ceiling. `review` (3930 words)
- [[llm-inference-servers-compared-vllm-vs-tgi-vs-sglang-vs-triton-2026]] — LLM Inference Servers Compared: vLLM vs TGI vs SGLang vs Triton (2026) — Vendor comparison (PremAI blog) of vLLM, SGLang, TGI, and Triton as of 2026. States TGI entered maintenance mode on December 11, 2025, with Hugging Face now recommending vLLM or SGLang for new deployments and the TGI repo accepting only minor bug fixes/doc updates. NVIDIA Triton Inference Server was renamed NVIDIA Dynamo Triton in March 2025; for LLM workloads it typically pairs with the TensorRT-LLM backend and is positioned as a multi-model/ensemble platform rather than an LLM-specific server, with reported overhead vs standalone vLLM at higher QPS due to response-packaging in the backend layer. Cites software-behavior benchmarks: SGLang/LMDeploy ~16,200 tok/s vs vLLM (FlashInfer) ~12,553 tok/s on Llama-3.1-8B (H100, 1000 ShareGPT prompts) -- a 29% SGLang/LMDeploy advantage attributed to kernel co-design vs vLLM's plugin-based flexibility; vLLM concurrent-request saturation point given as 100-150 vs TGI's 50-75, and vLLM GPU utilization 85-92% vs TGI 68-74%. Notes vLLM p99 TTFT was 80% larger (less consistent) than competing engines in one cited benchmark. `review` (2634 words)
- [[llm-inference-a-guide-to-build-vs-buy]] — LLM Inference: A Guide to Build vs. Buy — VENDOR SOURCE (Forte Group, an AI/software consulting firm; authored by Forte's CTO Lucas Hendrich, Aug 2026) but the most data-dense and best-sourced piece in this batch, with external citations rather than just vendor assertion. Central claim: the standard build-vs-buy volume-threshold model is 'arithmetically clean and operationally wrong' because it assumes purchased hardware will be busy. Cites Cast AI's 2026 State of Kubernetes Optimization Report, drawn from ~23,000 production clusters across AWS/Azure/GCP, putting AVERAGE ENTERPRISE GPU UTILIZATION AT 5 PERCENT — called out explicitly as the number that 'is enough to break most build-versus-buy spreadsheets.' Cites Spheron's 2026 inference cost analysis: an 8-GPU H100 SXM5 pod (~$19.20/hr) serving Llama 3.1 70B FP16 via vLLM at 2,800 tokens/sec costs ~$1.90/million tokens AT SATURATION (a ceiling, not an expectation); the same analysis models a realistic internal deployment (500 daily active users, 9am-6pm weekday traffic, near-zero overnight/weekends) at 22% average GPU utilization — continuous batching lifted that to 68%, and FP8 quantization let the same 4-GPU footprint absorb 1.8x the traffic. Cites BenchLM's Token Price Index (July 2026, 130 models): median $1.00/M input, $4.00/M output tokens — a 22%-utilized self-hosted node does not undercut that; 'it is paying a premium and owning the outage as well.' Cites CloudZero's FinOps in the AI Era 2026 report: 78% of organizations cannot distinguish AI costs from general cloud spend. On compliance: cites Gartner Predicts 2026 (>75% of EU/Middle East enterprises will geopatriate workloads by 2030, vs <5% baseline today) and Deloitte's Dec-2025 AI Infrastructure Survey (86% expect AI infra budgets to more than triple in 3 years); argues most compliance obligations are CONDITIONAL (satisfiable via BAA, ZDR commitment, regional tenancy) rather than absolute prohibitions, with a short enumerated list of genuine prohibitions (ITAR, classified/air-gapped, German BSI KRITIS, certain GDPR special-category data without an adequate transfer mechanism); and distinguishes data residency / processing locus / jurisdiction as three separately-satisfied obligations (citing the US CLOUD Act as why EU residency does not solve US jurisdictional exposure). On capability: cites Epoch AI (Jan-May 2026 window) finding the best open-weight models lag the closed frontier by an average of 4 months / ~8 points on Epoch's Capability Index, and the Stanford AI Index 2026 (March 2026 snapshot) reporting 1,503 Arena points for the leading closed model vs 1,454 for the leading open-weight model — while flagging that Epoch itself believes this likely UNDERSTATES the gap. On hardware pricing volatility: H100 rental spanned $2.01-$12.29/GPU-hour as of July 2026 (CloudZero); SemiAnalysis's one-year H100 rental index rose ~40% from $1.70/GPU-hour (Oct 2025) to $2.35 (March 2026) even as mid-tier API pricing fell ~80% between early 2025 and early 2026 and frontier blended pricing roughly doubled after Jan 2026. Six concrete recommendations: instrument workloads for a full quarter before buying; classify compliance as absolute-prohibition vs conditional; sort workloads by sensitivity x capability and only self-host the sensitive/low-capability quadrant; fix batching/quantization before adding nodes; consolidate multiple applications onto one serving cluster to defend utilization; attribute cost per workload from week one. Explicitly states four unsolved limits: hybrid routers fail open on misclassification without a pre-agreed data classification policy; a shared vector index without per-requester permission filtering leaks regardless of self-hosting; public benchmark parity is not your parity; utilization forecasts are optimistic by default due to structural peak-to-average ratios. LONG SOURCE (2300 words), high citation density — strong candidate for primary-source chase of the Cast AI, Spheron, BenchLM, CloudZero, Epoch AI, Stanford AI Index, Gartner, and Deloitte reports it cites. `review` (2300 words)
- [[llm-model-serving-faster-inference-on-snowflake]] — LLM Model Serving: Faster Inference on Snowflake — Snowflake engineering blog (Apr 17 2026), vendor-authored product-launch post for Snowflake Model Serving's LLM support (extending Snowflake ML's Model Registry to bring your own fine-tuned/domain-specific/OSS models, distinct from Snowflake's managed Cortex AI for frontier models). Engine: vLLM, explicitly named and credited by mechanism -- "continue to double down on vLLM ... leveraging vLLMs PagedAttention and continuous batching capabilities" for dynamic GPU memory management and streaming output. Exposes models via the standard OpenAI protocol (chat completions, multimodal base64 input) callable from SQL, Python, or REST, using a reg.get_model(name).version(v).list_services() pattern and direct openai Python client usage against a Snowflake-issued endpoint. Supports one-click Hugging Face model import via a Model Garden and remote model logging via file I/O so GPUs are not needed just to log a model. Runs inside Snowpark Container Services (SPCS), an airgapped container runtime within the customers Snowflake account perimeter; co-engineered internal network routing solved an egress-proxy bottleneck, achieving a measured 700GB model download in 83 seconds at 67.5 Gbps. Claimed benchmark: 2.2x-2.3x P99 latency/TTFT improvement on average vs Databricks across Qwen3-4B, Gemma3-1B, Medgemma-27B, and favorable high-concurrency scaling vs Baseten; benchmark code published at github.com/Snowflake-Labs/sf-samples. Caveat stated by Snowflake itself: benchmarks were single-node, and GLM 4.5 (700GB model) scalability was tested in Snowflakes own internal environment -- self-reported, not third-party verified. Does not name the orchestration/gateway layer around vLLM beyond SPCS; flagged as an unstated component. `review` (1969 words)
- [[llm-serving-on-kubernetes-llm-d-aibrix-dynamo-compared]] — LLM Serving on Kubernetes: llm-d, AIBrix, Dynamo Compared — Dense practitioner comparison (mid-2026, builderai.tools) of the Kubernetes control-plane layer, explicit about layer boundaries: 'Kubernetes LLM serving in mid 2026 splits into three layers: an engine that runs the model, a control plane that decides how many replicas exist and where each request lands, and a gateway that understands what a KV cache is.' States flatly: 'none of the control planes discussed below replace the engine. llm-d, AIBrix and Dynamo all launch vLLM or SGLang processes in pods and orchestrate around them.' Documents the Gateway API Inference Extension as the converging standard layer ABOVE all three control planes: GA, v1 InferencePool API, Endpoint Picker (EPP) extension reading model-server metrics for KV-cache-locality/load/priority placement; InferenceObjective config object 'has moved into the llm-d Router repository' -- 'the routing contract is becoming portable even though the control planes are not.' Per-project governance/backing with specific dates: llm-d is Apache 2.0, 'launched in May 2025 by Red Hat, Google Cloud, IBM Research, CoreWeave and NVIDIA, later joined by AMD, Cisco, Hugging Face, Intel, Lambda and Mistral AI,' accepted into CNCF Sandbox 12 March 2026, announced at KubeCon Europe 24 March. AIBrix is Apache 2.0, lives inside vllm-project GitHub org, 'built by a team led by ByteDance engineers,' first public release Nov 2024, white paper Feb 2025, v0.7.0 (18 June 2026) had 242 merged PRs. NVIDIA Dynamo is Apache 2.0, Rust core + Python extensibility, v1.3.0 as of 22 July 2026, 'the most explicitly datacenter-scale of the three,' built for NVL72-class hardware with contractual latency SLAs via Grove (alpha, Volcano scheduler). Names llm-d's production-adjacent integration point: 'If you already run KServe... KServe's LLMInferenceService CRD is described in its own documentation as built on the foundation of llm-d.' Documents the pre-control-plane capability gap directly: cache-aware routing fixes what 'round robin across replicas is actively hostile to' (prefix cache fragmentation); disaggregation addresses prefill(compute-bound)/decode(memory-bound) interference that 'no amount of extra GPUs fixes' when colocated. Gives a concrete adoption heuristic: 'adopt a control plane when at least two of the following are true, not one: multiple models sharing a GPU pool, multiple teams needing isolation/fairness, a model too large for one node, a hard TTFT/ITL SLO, an idle-GPU bill large enough that someone has asked about it' -- implying a single vLLM box behind a plain Kubernetes Service is the default and these three control planes are deliberate escalations, not defaults. Comparison table covers 6 stacks (llm-d, AIBrix, Dynamo, GPUStack, Xinference, OpenLLM) across license/engines-driven/PD-disaggregation/cache-routing/K8s-requirement/operational-weight. `evergreen` (3825 words)
- [[llmops-in-production-457-case-studies-of-what-actually-works-zenml-blog]] — LLMOps in Production: 457 Case Studies of What Actually Works - ZenML Blog — ZenML LLMOps-database release notes (~28k words) presenting an alphabetical catalogue-style roundup of ~450+ individual case-study links (each a separate ZenML database entry, not fetched here) rather than aggregate statistics -- no 'X% of deployments use Y' figures with denominator appear anywhere in the piece despite its length. Relevant to inference tech-stack scope: an unnamed 'technology company' and separately 'Fuzzy Labs' (serving Mistral-7B for a tech company's developer-docs RAG system) both report using vLLM for inference plus Ray Serve for horizontal scaling, with Fuzzy Labs citing load-testing (via Locust) that cut response times from 11s to 3s. Faire (wholesale marketplace) self-hosts a fine-tuned Llama model on 16 GPUs serving 70M predictions/day, reporting 28% accuracy improvement over a prior GPT-based approach. Spotify uses vLLM to serve fine-tuned Llama models for music recommendations/AI DJ at scale. OLX's 5K/month API cost is cited as the driver of a prospective shift to self-hosted models. A Facebook AI Research/Unusual Ventures/Digits/Bountiful panel discussion explicitly frames 'API vs self-hosted trade-offs' as a central production decision, with Digits reporting 100M daily financial-transaction processing via LLMs. Discord's case study describes scaling via a mix of hosted and self-hosted models after initial commercial-LLM prototyping. `review` (28032 words)
- [[lmcache-vllm-kv-events-missing-cpu-blockstored-event-only-gpu-event-emitted-issu]] — LMCache + vLLM KV Events: Missing CPU BlockStored Event (Only GPU event emitted) · Issue #3532 · LMCache/LMCache — LMCache/LMCache GitHub issue #3532 (filed Jun 2026): LMCache's LMCacheConnectorV1Dynamic internally generates a CPU-medium BlockStored event but it never reaches the ZMQ subscriber -- only the GPU-tier event is published. Reporter cross-checked against vLLM's own OffloadingConnector (no LMCache), which correctly publishes medium='CPU'. Auto-closed 'not planned' Sep 4 2026; fix appears to be arriving via LMCache's newer MP-mode ZmqKVEventSink rather than a patch to the (deprecated) connector the bug was filed against. `evergreen` (1966 words)
- [[lmcache-an-efficient-kv-cache-layer-for-enterprise-scale-llm-inference]] — LMCache: An Efficient KV Cache Layer for Enterprise-Scale LLM Inference — LMCache paper (Tensormesh/UChicago, arXiv 2510.09665, Oct 2025) — the KV-offloading library llm-d integrates for cross-node/cross-engine cache sharing and PD disaggregation. LMCache extracts KV cache out of GPU memory to CPU/storage/network tiers and shares it across queries and across inference engines (vLLM and SGLang), supporting both cache offloading (prefix reuse across queries) and prefill-decode (PD) disaggregation (cross-engine/GPU cache transfer). Combined with vLLM it achieves up to 15x throughput improvement on workloads like multi-round QA and document analysis. Three core contributions: (i) optimized KV data movement via batched operations plus compute/I-O pipelining, (ii) a modular KV cache connector that decouples LMCache from inference-engine internals (vLLM-native design honors strict scheduler/worker separation; connector API splits into scheduler-side hooks — get_num_new_matched_tokens, update_state_after_alloc, build_connector_meta — and model-runner-side hooks — start_load_kv, wait_load_kv, plus per-layer pipelined loading/storing), and (iii) a first-class control API (pinning, lookup, cleanup, movement, compression) for orchestrating cache placement across GPU/CPU/storage/network. Reports two production insights from large-scale enterprise adoption: fetching KV cache from remote storage measurably improves prefill delay, and context truncation (a common industry technique) can cut prefix cache hit ratio by roughly half. `evergreen` (12312 words)
- [[lmcache-an-efficient-kv-cache-layer-for-enterprise-scale-llm-inference-2]] — LMCache: An Efficient KV Cache Layer for Enterprise-Scale LLM Inference — LMCache tech report (Tensormesh Inc. / University of Chicago; arXiv:2510.09665v2, Dec 2025), self-described as "the first and so far the most efficient open-source KV caching solution." Core thesis: engines' built-in prefix caching only keeps KV cache inside GPU memory (vLLM's native CPU offloading does page-by-page transfer at 88 Gbps and lacks a distributed/hierarchical storage backend); LMCache instead extracts KV cache out of the engine entirely and treats it as a first-class, externally-managed data structure that can be moved across GPU, CPU DRAM, local/remote disk, S3, and network links (achieving 400 Gbps loading from CPU vs vLLM native's 88 Gbps), shared across queries AND across separate engine instances (cross-engine PD disaggregation transfer), with explicit control APIs (pin, lookup, clear, move, compress/decompress) that neither vLLM's nor SGLang's native caching exposes.

Technical mechanisms: (1) chunk-based batched I/O (default 256 tokens/chunk vs. vLLM's native 16-token pages) via a streaming GPU buffer to saturate interconnect bandwidth -- prior work cited shows transfers need to reach ~16MB to saturate 400Gbps NICs and 1-2MB to hit 75-80% of PCIe 5.0 bandwidth; (2) a standardized "KV connector" interface (jointly designed/maintained by the LMCache and vLLM teams, live in vLLM for over six months at publication) that decouples LMCache from inference-engine internals that change with ~15-20 new open-weight models released per week; (3) a centralized KV cache controller (manager plus per-instance workers) exposing lookup/move/pin/compress APIs for cache-aware routing and migration; (4) layer-wise pipelining and async prefetch to overlap compute with I/O; (5) zero-copy reference counting and a dynamic-offloading three-pointer scheme to avoid duplicating free GPU pages.

Adoption/integration: the paper states LMCache is used in -- and is the actual KV-caching implementation inside -- vLLM Production Stack, NVIDIA Dynamo, llm-d (Red Hat), and KServe; it also lists AIBrix (ByteDance) and SGLang/OME as peer Kubernetes-native inference stacks in the same category but does not claim LMCache is used inside AIBrix. It explicitly contrasts itself with proprietary prefix caching at Fireworks AI and Together AI (closed, not available to self-hosters) and with storage layers like Mooncake, Redis, InfiniStore, 3FS (lack an engine-integration "glue" layer or are tied to one framework). Over roughly a year, community contributions expanded backend support from 3 (local CPU, local disk, Redis) to 11 (adding NFS, WEKA, GPU-Direct Storage, Mooncake Store, NIXL, S3, InfiniStore, Valkey) across four processor types (NVIDIA, AMD, Ascend, TPU) and two engines (vLLM, SGLang).

Measured gains (vLLM v0.10.2 baseline, LMCache v0.3.6, 8xH100 unless noted): vs basic vLLM, basic vLLM CPU offloading, and two unnamed commercial inference APIs on synthetic multi-round QA (10-20K token contexts; Llama-3.1-8B/70B, Qwen2.5-72B/Coder-32B, Qwen3-Coder-480B-FP8) -- "1.9-8.1x smaller TTFT" and "2.3-14x higher throughput" at matched TTFT; one commercial baseline couldn't serve Qwen3-Coder-480B at all. On two real anonymized enterprise traces ("Company F", "Company G"): 3.7-6.8x smaller TTFT and 19-58% smaller ITL at high QPS. Centralized remote-storage sharing (15 Gbps link, TriviaQA/LongBench): 1.3-3x throughput improvement. PD disaggregation vs. vLLM's native NIXL-based PD transfer: 1.53-1.84x lower mean TTFT, 1.12-1.66x lower mean ITL, and markedly better tail latency, because LMCache transfers chunk-by-chunk into a GPU buffer rather than scattered page-by-page. A sensitivity study found KV-cache loading only beats fresh prefill when network bandwidth is low (32 Gbps) AND context exceeds 256K tokens; at 64-128 Gbps it wins at all context lengths -- a load-bearing caveat that offloading is not unconditionally better than recomputation.

Reported production lessons (Section 9, directly load-bearing for "what engines don't give you"): (a) loading KV cache from remote object storage (e.g., Amazon S3 Express, now roughly 1GBps vs. historical roughly 100MBps) can be faster than full prefill, not just cheaper -- one customer ("Company C") saw 22-32% lower TTFT vs. full prefill; (b) context truncation (sliding-window handling of long inputs) cuts prefix-cache hit ratio roughly in half in production (observed 85% to 45% for "Company F"); (c) customers were surprised by high real-world hit rates (50% for "Company G") because dynamically reusable contexts (chat history, RAG context, coding-assistant turns) are reused far more than the classic fixed-system-prompt case; (d) nearly all production users deploy via Docker/Kubernetes and never touch LMCache's source; (e) industry prioritized throughput/stability/compatibility over flexible research APIs for custom attention variants, making LMCache comparatively less popular in academic research settings; (f) the team deliberately kept the implementation in Python with hand-optimized kernels rather than porting to Rust/C++, trading some raw performance for contributor velocity. Code: github.com/LMCache/LMCache. `review` (12312 words)
- [[linkedin-building-and-evolving-a-production-genai-application-stack-zenml-llmops]] — LinkedIn: Building and Evolving a Production GenAI Application Stack - ZenML LLMOps Database — ZenML summary of LinkedIn's Nov 2024 engineering blog (primary at linkedin.com/blog/engineering/generative-ai/behind-the-platform-...). LinkedIn ran a fragmented stack for ~2 years: Java midtier for online serving vs. Python for offline LLM workflows/evals, because AI engineers needed Python's OSS ecosystem but LinkedIn's RPC/storage/tracing infra was Java-only. Concluded Java-for-serving was unsustainable and invested in first-class Python online serving (building Python clients only for *future* infra like gRPC, not legacy rest.li). Adopted LangChain as the base framework after evaluating it on functionality/operability/community/roadmap; LinkedIn's internal GenAI framework is now a mandated thin wrapper over LangChain. Standardized prompts on Jinja templates plus a versioned 'Prompt Source of Truth' service, converging on the OpenAI Chat Completions API format. Built a 'skill registry' (Skill Inversion: downstream services define/expose skills rather than apps wrapping them) exposed to LangChain's tool API. Conversational memory reuses LinkedIn's existing production messaging stack (not a new vector DB) via the LangChain Conversational Memory abstraction. For model inference: initial LLMs came from Azure OpenAI via a centralized GenAI proxy (routing, streaming, quota, Trust/Responsible-AI checks); increasingly, in-house fine-tuning/serving runs on an internal AI platform built on open-source PyTorch + DeepSpeed + vLLM, with fine-tuned Llama reported to match or beat commercial frontier models at lower cost/latency for LinkedIn-specific tasks. All models (external and internal) are exposed behind a single OpenAI Chat Completions-compatible API for seamless on-prem/external switching. `review` (2065 words)
- [[litellm-ai-gateway-litellm]] — LiteLLM AI Gateway | liteLLM — LiteLLM's canonical gateway-overview doc. The AI Gateway is a self-hosted server giving one OpenAI-compatible endpoint to 100+ providers, MCP tools, and A2A agents; clients that speak the OpenAI API need no code changes. Core gateway functions enumerated: access control (per user/team/project virtual keys, each with its own models/budgets/rate limits), spend tracking (per key/team/user/tag), reliability (load balancing, fallbacks, retries across deployments), guardrails (content filters, PII masking), logging (Langfuse, Datadog, OpenTelemetry, Prometheus), and an Admin UI. Deployment path: Docker/Helm/Terraform + a connected Postgres database, i.e. a standard self-hostable stack with no mandated external SaaS dependency described on this page. States the gateway-vs-SDK decision rule explicitly: use the gateway once more than one application/person calls LLMs or you need virtual keys/spend tracking/centralized logs/guardrails; use the plain Python SDK for a single Python app that doesn't need those controls. `review` (779 words)
- [[llm-d-routerpkgeppframeworkinterfacedatalayerdiscoverygo-at-main-llm-dllm-dllm-d]] — Llm D Routerpkgeppframeworkinterfacedatalayerdiscoverygo At Main Llm Dllm Dllm D — Stub for [[llm-d-routerpkgeppframeworkinterfacedatalayerdiscoverygo-at-main-llm-dllm-dllm-d]] `draft` (21 words)
- [[local-llm-total-cost-of-ownership-2026-cloud-vs-self-hosted-promptcostorg]] — Local LLM Total Cost of Ownership 2026: Cloud vs Self-Hosted | PromptCost.org — Content/calculator site (promptcost.org; markets its own free 'AI cost calculator' tool, so has a mild traffic-driving interest but is not selling an inference platform or consulting). Claims analysis is 'based on 12 months of production deployment data' (hardware pricing from Lambda Labs/CoreWeave May 2026, electricity from US EIA, engineering rates from Glassdoor) though the underlying deployment logs are not published/linkable — an unverifiable but specifically-sourced claim. Headline break-even: local LLMs break even with cloud APIs at 500K-2M tokens/day depending on model size; below that cloud is cheaper counting all costs, above it local saves 60-80% on per-token cost. Worked hardware amortization: A100 80GB server ~$25,000 amortized 3yr/10% residual = $20.55/day + $3.50/day power = $24/day baseline before serving a single request; A10G server ~$7,000 = $5.75/day baseline. Ops overhead budget: 4-8 hrs/month software updates/monitoring + 2-4 hrs/month troubleshooting + 4-8 hrs/quarter major upgrades, at $75/hr = $450-900/month basic reliability labor, or $1,500-3,000/month for proper 24/7 on-call. Reports an actual 12-month incident log for a single-server deployment: months 1-3 had 12 hours unplanned downtime (SSD failure, driver update requiring reboot, CUDA version conflict), months 4-6 had 6 hours (overheating, ECC memory error), months 7-12 averaged 4 hours/month even once 'stable' — totaling 8-16 hours/month average downtime for a single-server setup; redundant failover doubles hardware cost. Model-update treadmill: upgrading a serving stack (e.g., vLLM 0.3 to 0.6) is 2-4 weeks of engineering time per cycle, 3-4 times per year, totaling 90-160 hours/year for a 3-engineer team = $13,500-24,000/year in engineering time at $150/hr. Worked break-even scenarios: 7B model at 1M tokens/day, cloud API $112.50/day ($3,375/mo) vs local A10G $17.11/day all-in baseline (6.6x cheaper locally at that volume, assuming the A10G can sustain 800K tokens/day at 24/7 and MLOps capability exists); 70B model at 3M tokens/day, cloud $1,200/day ($36,000/mo) vs local A100 $74/day (16x cheaper). States explicit per-model-size break-even bands: 7B/A10G under 500K tokens/day cloud wins, 500K-1M marginal, above 1M local wins definitively; 70B/A100 under 2M tokens/day cloud wins, 2M-5M local wins meaningfully, above 5M local is 10-20x cheaper. Recommends a 6-month phased migration path (parallel run -> route highest-volume/lowest-complexity workloads first -> expand to 80% local/20% cloud fallback -> full production with continuous A/B quality checks). States vLLM delivers 3-5x higher throughput than Ollama via PagedAttention/continuous batching (50-100+ req/sec/A100 vs Ollama's 10-20 req/sec) and calls Ollama 'better for local development and experimentation' rather than production. `review` (2629 words)
- [[locality-aware-fair-scheduling-in-llm-serving]] — Locality-aware Fair Scheduling in LLM Serving — UC Berkeley and LMSYS (the group behind SGLang/Chatbot Arena) researchers propose DLPM (Deficit Longest Prefix Match) and its distributed extension D2LPM, the first locality-aware FAIR scheduling algorithms for multi-tenant LLM serving. NOTE ON EVIDENCE TYPE: this is a benchmark/algorithmic systems paper evaluated on synthetic workload traces and single-/multi-GPU testbeds (A10 GPUs cited for Fig.1), not a production-measurement paper -- it belongs to the systems layer (vLLM/SGLang/RadixAttention lineage) rather than the measured-production-evidence theme, but documents the fairness-vs-locality tradeoff that any multi-tenant internal LLM platform must resolve.

PROBLEM STATEMENT: existing fair-scheduling algorithms for LLM serving (e.g., Virtual Token Counter/VTC) ignore prefix locality and thus under-exploit prefix/KV-cache reuse, while existing locality-aware schedulers in production serving frameworks (SGLang's RadixAttention-based scheduler, Preble) maximize prefix-cache hit rate without any fairness guarantee -- meaning a dominant or adversarial client's requests can starve other tenants' latency by monopolizing cache-friendly routing.

MEASURED RESULTS: DLPM/D2LPM achieve up to 2.87x higher throughput than VTC (the only other fairness-guaranteeing algorithm) and up to 7.18x lower latency for 'victim' (well-behaved, non-dominant) clients compared to the state-of-the-art locality-aware-but-fairness-blind scheduler Preble; on average D2LPM achieves 2.90x lower latency than Preble and 4.06x lower latency than round-robin+LPM. Without a fairness guarantee, locality-maximizing schedulers like Preble and round-robin+LPM can inflict up to 7.18x and 9.55x higher latency respectively on well-behaved clients when a misbehaving/dominant client is present in the mix, because these schedulers preferentially route to maximize cache hit rate for whichever client already dominates cache state. DLPM provides rigorous theoretical fairness bounds (service bound and latency bound across client types) while preserving a tunable relaxation parameter Q that trades fairness strictness for throughput, achieving a new Pareto frontier of fairness (Jain Index) vs. throughput relative to plain LPM and VTC. `review` (12589 words)
- [[master-kv-cache-aware-routing-with-llm-d-for-efficient-ai-inference-red-hat-deve]] — Master KV cache aware routing with llm-d for efficient AI inference | Red Hat Developer — Red Hat Developer article (Maroon Ayoub/IBM, Oct 7 2025) walks through KV-cache-indexer internals using the llm-d-kv-cache-manager repo (v0.4.0-era) and gives a concrete Kubernetes production example (87.4% cache hit rate, 88% TTFT improvement 2,850ms->340ms). Lists core internal components: kvcache.Indexer (orchestrator), kvevents.Pool (sharded ZMQ worker pool ingesting events), kvblock.Index (in-memory two-level LRU mapping block hashes to pods), tokenization.PrefixStore (LRU-cached tokenized prefixes), kvblock.TokenProcessor (chunking/hashing matching vLLM's algorithm), kvblock.Scorer (longest-consecutive-prefix-match scoring). CAUTION — conflicts with the more current (2026) llm-d/llm-d GitHub guide on ZMQ topic format: this article states 'Events parsed by topic format: kv@pod-id@model' whereas the current guide's --kv-events-config sets topic to 'kv@$(POD_IP):$(POD_PORT)@<model>' (i.e., keyed by literal pod IP:port, not a Kubernetes pod-id/name). Given the domain note that llm-d/GIE APIs 'changed quickly' over the last 12-18 months, and this article predates the guide by roughly a year, the IP:port-keyed format in the current repo should be treated as authoritative and this article's 'pod-id' phrasing as either simplified/inaccurate or reflecting an older topic-key scheme. Describes pod discovery as label-selector based: EPP auto-discovers decode-service endpoints via the Kubernetes Service label llm-d.ai/inferenceServing: "true", and shows a config snippet with a discovery.label_selector field — reinforcing that endpoint discovery in the shipped implementation is Kubernetes-label/selector driven rather than static config in this deployment path. `evergreen` (2410 words)
- [[metrics-llm-d]] — Metrics | llm-d — Official llm-d docs (v0.9, current) walkthrough of the metrics pipeline: model-server metrics (vLLM: num_requests_running, num_requests_waiting, kv_cache_usage_perc, time_to_first_token_seconds, inter_token_latency_seconds, prefix_cache_hits_total/queries_total, prompt/generation_tokens_total; SGLang: analogous sglang_* names) are scraped via Prometheus PodMonitors created by a Kustomize monitoring component (Kubernetes Prometheus-Operator CRD, decode-podmonitor / prefill-podmonitor). EPP (router) metrics are exposed via a ServiceMonitor (epp-servicemonitor) and enumerated in full: llm_d_epp_request_total/error_total/duration_seconds/size_bytes/input_tokens/output_tokens/cached_tokens/running/ntpot_seconds/ttft_seconds/streaming_tpot_seconds/streaming_itl_seconds, llm_d_epp_ready_endpoints, llm_d_epp_scheduler_attempts_total/e2e_duration_seconds, llm_d_epp_plugin_duration_seconds, llm_d_epp_disagg_decision_total, plus flow-control-specific gauges/histograms (queue_size, queue_bytes, request_queue_duration_seconds, dispatch_cycle_duration_seconds, request_enqueue_duration_seconds, pool_saturation, stale_endpoints, requests_total-by-outcome). Security-relevant for non-k8s backends: the whole scrape path is built on Kubernetes-native PodMonitor/ServiceMonitor CRDs, so a non-k8s backend loses this auto-discovery/scrape-config mechanism entirely and must supply its own static Prometheus scrape_configs; troubleshooting guidance shows the model-server /metrics endpoint is verified with a plain HTTP curl against port 8000 by default, and TLS between Prometheus and its targets is an explicit opt-in the operator must enable (an --enable-tls flag on the bundled install script) rather than a default -- i.e., EPP-to-model-server metrics scraping is unencrypted unless the operator deliberately adds TLS. `evergreen` (1868 words)
- [[milestones-llm-dllm-d-router-github]] — Milestones · llm-d/llm-d-router · GitHub — GitHub Milestones list for llm-d/llm-d-router, fetched 11 Sep 2026. Only 3 open milestones exist: 'v0.12' (no due date, 0/1 issues closed), '1.0' (no fixed date, due-by placeholder October 8 2026, 17/26 issues closed, 65% complete), and 'v0.11' (due September 30 2026, 4/20 issues closed, 20% complete). This means PR #2434 (dataLayer.discovery.endpoints.pluginRef consolidation, merged 25 Aug 2026) landed in the v0.11 development window, not a 'v0.10 targeted for September' as an earlier investigator framed it -- v0.10 is already closed and released. `evergreen` (110 words)
- [[monitor-llm-routing-with-the-kubernetes-inference-extension-datadog]] — Monitor LLM routing with the Kubernetes Inference Extension | Datadog — Datadog engineering blog (2026) explains Gateway API Inference Extension mechanics end-to-end and is explicit that the EPP is commonly 'powered by a dedicated, production-grade scheduler like the CNCF llm-d project' (linking github.com/llm-d/llm-d-inference-scheduler), and that the extension exposes a 'programmable plugin architecture' at llm-d.ai/docs/architecture/core/router/epp letting operators customize how each routing signal (queue depth, LoRA adapter state, KV-cache locality/prefix match, availability) is scored and weighted -- direct evidence for the IPP/plugin-framework customization seam this research is investigating. Documents the request lifecycle in three phases: gateway routing (HTTPRoute matches hostname/path, extracts model name directly or via a Body-Based Router (BBR) parsing the JSON payload, maps to an InferencePool), EPP endpoint selection (rejects payloads over capacity limit with 413, scores pods on availability/queue depth/adapter state/KV-cache locality, returns only an IP -- 'does not execute the physical routing'), and model serving (vLLM/Triton backends). Covers EPP flow control in detail: a central queue at the EPP (rather than backend-local queues) that dispatches by InferenceObjective priority and fairness, sheds low-priority traffic via 429/503 at configurable saturation thresholds (e.g., shed background jobs at 50% full while serving interactive traffic to 99%), and enables scale-to-zero for batch workloads since requests queue centrally while GPU pods spin up. Lists concrete Prometheus metric names for observability: inference_objective_request_duration_seconds, inference_pool_per_pod_queue_size, inference_extension_flow_control_request_queue_duration_seconds (labeled by outcome=Rejected/Evicted), plus vLLM metrics (gpu_cache_usage_perc, num_requests.running/waiting/swapped). `evergreen` (3526 words)
- [[mooncake-a-kvcache-centric-disaggregated]] — Mooncake: A KVCache-centric Disaggregated — Canonical academic paper (arXiv:2407.00079, Qin et al., Moonshot AI + Tsinghua University) establishing the KV-cache-centric disaggregated serving architecture that llm-d's EPP scoring and KV-connector design build on. Mooncake is the production serving platform for the Kimi LLM service. Core contributions: (1) a disaggregated architecture separating prefill and decoding clusters, distinct from monolithic serving; (2) a disaggregated KVCache pool that harvests underutilized CPU, DRAM, and SSD resources of the GPU cluster rather than confining KV cache to GPU HBM -- the direct ancestor of llm-d's tiered hot/warm/cold cache hierarchy; (3) a KVCache-centric global scheduler that balances maximizing effective throughput against per-request latency SLOs (TTFT and TBT); (4) a prediction-based early-rejection policy for overload scenarios, since Mooncake (unlike prior throughput-oriented work) must handle requests that cannot all be served under SLO. Reports up to 525% throughput increase over baseline in simulated long-context scenarios while meeting SLOs, and that the architecture enabled Kimi to handle 75% more requests under real production workloads. This is the paper any non-pod router replacement must architecturally preserve fidelity to: the global KV-cache locality view + SLO-aware admission/scheduling loop is the theoretical basis for both llm-d's precise-prefix-cache-scorer and the sourced-KV-event RFC pattern seen in the vllm-ascend RFC. `evergreen` (12154 words)
- [[mooncake-a-kvcache-centric-disaggregated-2]] — Mooncake: A KVCache-centric Disaggregated — Mooncake (Qin, Li, He, Zhang, Wu, Zheng, Xu — Moonshot AI + Tsinghua/MadSys, arXiv 2407.00079, tech report) is first-person production evidence: this is the actual serving platform for Kimi, Moonshot AI's commercial LLM chatbot, described by its own operators (not a paper about a hypothetical system). It is the direct origin of the open-source kvcache-ai/Mooncake project (6.6k GitHub stars per prior note) and its MooncakeStoreConnector is now integrated into llm-d and referenced by vLLM/SGLang KV-cache tooling. Architecture: KVCache-centric disaggregation that goes beyond simple prefill/decode splitting (as in DistServe/Splitwise/TetriInfer, all cited as prior art) by also disaggregating the KV cache itself into a separate pool spanning CPU/DRAM/SSD across the GPU cluster's otherwise-underutilized resources, connected via GPUDirect RDMA through a component called Messenger. A global scheduler, Conductor, selects prefill/decode instance pairs per request to jointly optimize cache reuse, instance load, and TTFT/TBT SLOs; KVCache blocks are identified by chained prefix hashes (block size 512 tokens) enabling exact prefix-cache matching, with a heuristic hot-spot migration scheme that replicates frequently-hit blocks without needing precise future-usage prediction. For long-context prefill, Mooncake introduces chunked pipeline parallelism (CPP) — explicitly novel ('to our knowledge, this is the first application [of pipelined parallelism] in the inference stage') — instead of cross-node tensor or sequence parallelism, because CPP needs cross-node communication only at pipeline-stage boundaries, overlapping with compute; combined with layer-wise asynchronous KVCache load/store that overlaps transfer with attention computation layer-by-layer. Explicitly chose NOT to adopt chunked-prefill-into-decoding-batch (SARATHI, cited) as its primary technique, because prefill nodes need different cross-node parallelism than decode nodes and because keeping prefill out of VRAM-constrained decode batches frees VRAM. Under severe overload (a documented real condition for Kimi, not a hypothetical), Mooncake implements a prediction-based Early-Rejection policy: naive early rejection (reject before prefill if no decode slot will be free) causes load oscillation, which the predictive version mitigates. PRODUCTION DATA: releases an open-sourced 1-hour real request trace (23,608 entries) with timestamp/input_length/output_length/block-hash fields (text scrubbed for privacy) — paper states average input length 8,590 tokens, average output length 182 tokens, input:output ratio ~720 (reflecting Kimi's long-context use case). Cache-hit analysis on this trace: LRU/LFU/length-aware caching all plateau around a 55% hit ratio as capacity grows past 50,000 blocks (34% hit ratio at 1,000-block capacity), and the paper separately states real reusability on Kimi's live traffic is markedly lower than open-source benchmarks suggest, reaching up to 90% only for specific high-repetition products like its chat-to-paper service (papers.cool). Cache-block popularity is extremely skewed: over 50% of blocks are never reused while a minority are hit tens of thousands of times. Benchmark numbers: against vLLM on simulated long-context workloads (16k-128k prompts), Mooncake shows 50%-525% higher throughput under identical TTFT/TBT SLOs; under a real-workload replay (10P+10D Mooncake vs 20-instance vLLM), both systems hit ~100% TTFT-SLO compliance but only 57% of vLLM requests met the TBT SLO versus ~100% for Mooncake, letting Mooncake handle 75% more real requests at the same SLO. In an 8P+8D cluster overload test replaying 23,000 real requests at 2x speed, the naive-reject baseline rejected 4,183 requests versus 3,771 (early rejection) and 3,589 (prediction-based early rejection). All experiments reportedly use a dummy LLaMA2-70B-architecture model over replayed real-trace timing/lengths/cache-hash metadata (no real user text) to protect customer data while preserving reproducibility; the trace is open-sourced at github.com/kvcache-ai/Mooncake. `review` (12154 words)
- [[moved-generative-ai-semantic-conventions-opentelemetry]] — Moved: Generative AI semantic conventions | OpenTelemetry — Redirect stub only: OTel's GenAI semantic conventions page confirms the content has moved to the separate open-telemetry/semantic-conventions-genai GitHub repository and is no longer maintained on opentelemetry.io. Does not itself contain the gen_ai.usage.input_tokens definition cited by TrueFoundry; the actual spec would need to be fetched from the linked GitHub repo (not fetched here, outside this batch's cap). `deprecated` (156 words)
- [[multi-tenant-architecture-with-litellm-litellm]] — Multi-Tenant Architecture with LiteLLM | liteLLM — LiteLLM's own architecture doc for multi-tenancy: a single proxy serves many internal tenants via a four-level nested hierarchy -- Organization > Team > User > (Virtual) Key -- where every request's spend is attributed simultaneously to its key, user, team, and organization, and budgets are enforced inward (a request is blocked the instant any level on its path is over budget; team budget cannot exceed org budget, user budget cannot exceed team budget). States explicitly: 'Teams and Virtual Keys are available in open source, and Teams alone can serve as your top-level tenant boundary. Organizations and Org Admins add a further layer of hierarchy on top and are an enterprise feature.' So in OSS, Teams are the top-level tenant boundary (no org-level rollup); Organizations/org-admin delegation require the 30-day-trial enterprise tier. Roles ship at two altitudes: platform-wide (proxy_admin, proxy_admin_viewer, internal_user) and scoped (org_admin, team_admin), letting a team admin manage only their team without touching the platform -- the delegation chain that lets the platform onboard thousands of users without central-admin bottlenecks. Virtual Keys can be scoped to a user, a team (service-account key that survives member turnover), or both. Documents two concrete patterns: Enterprise Departments (OSS flattens the org-rollup into teams-only; enterprise keeps a department-level org with a company-wide cap) and Environment Separation (prod/staging/dev as separate teams with stricter rate limits/approved-model lists on prod). `review` (1251 words)
- [[multi-cluster-gke-inference-gateway-helps-scale-ai-workloads-google-cloud-blog]] — Multi-cluster GKE Inference Gateway helps scale AI workloads | Google Cloud Blog — Google Cloud announces (Mar 17, 2026 preview) multi-cluster GKE Inference Gateway, built on multi-cluster Gateways. Each 'target cluster' still runs a normal InferencePool grouping Pods locally; that pool is exported and surfaces in the 'config cluster' as a new, GCP-proprietary GCPInferencePoolImport resource, to which a GCPBackendPolicy attaches for cross-cluster load-balancing signals (KV-cache metric, in-flight limits). Standard Gateway/HTTPRoute in the config cluster route to the imports. This is vendor-specific plumbing on top of the OSS InferencePool concept, not a portable non-k8s-backend mechanism -- InferencePool itself still only ever groups Pods within a single cluster; cross-cluster reach is solved by a new import CRD + GCP control plane, not by relaxing what InferencePool can select. `evergreen` (718 words)
- [[my-ai-gateway-added-400ms-to-every-request-heres-where-it-went-dev-community]] — My AI Gateway Added 400ms to Every Request. Here's Where It Went - DEV Community — LOW-AUTHORITY, ADVERSARIAL, NOT llm-d-specific: a generic "AI gateway" (LLM API proxy) latency-debugging checklist on dev.to, written in a template SEO-blog style with numerous suspicious near-identical bot-like comment exchanges (repeated agree-and-restate patterns from a small set of accounts) -- treat the article text as commentary of uncertain provenance, not a verified case study. Does not mention llm-d, Envoy ext_proc, EPP, InferencePool, or Kubernetes at all; it is about generic LLM-provider API gateways (auth/rate-limit/policy proxies), not inference-aware schedulers. Still useful as a generic latency-attribution checklist transferable to evaluating a custom (non-k8s) llm-d router/EPP: decompose overhead into connection setup (DNS/TCP/TLS, keep-alive reuse), authentication/lookup caching, synchronous logging/audit writes blocking the response path, serial vs. parallel policy checks (cites one commenter claim of 280ms to ~60ms after parallelizing 4 serial auth/rate-limit middleware calls), retries (compounding delay invisible to uptime dashboards), and streaming-buffer-induced gap between provider TTFT and client-visible TTFT. Recommends benchmarking gateway overhead against the SAME real upstream provider (not a mock) and tracking p50/p95/p99 rather than averages, since connection-pooling and retry costs concentrate in the tail. Gives illustrative (not measured, not llm-d-derived) target budget: gateway processing <10-20ms, auth <5ms warm, policy eval <10ms, audit/logging non-blocking. `evergreen` (5601 words)
- [[nvidia-dynamo-planner-brings-slo-driven-automation-to-multi-node-llm-inference-i]] — NVIDIA Dynamo Planner Brings SLO-Driven Automation to Multi-Node LLM Inference - InfoQ — InfoQ report (Jan 31, 2026) on NVIDIA/Microsoft's Part 2 Dynamo-on-Azure-Kubernetes-Service collaboration. First announcement claimed 1.2M tokens/sec raw throughput on distributed GPU systems; this release adds automated resource planning via two components: the Dynamo Planner Profiler (pre-deployment simulation tool, ~20-30s via an AI Configurator mode using pre-measured performance data, sweeps tensor-parallelism sizes for prefill vs decode to optimize 'Goodput' -- throughput within TTFT/ITL SLOs) and the SLO-based Dynamo Planner (runtime orchestration engine that watches decode-pool KV-cache load and prefill-queue depth to scale prefill/decode workers independently as traffic shifts). Illustrated with a Qwen3-32B-FP8 airline-assistant case: SLA of 500ms TTFT / 30ms ITL; a weather-driven spike to 200 concurrent complex-rerouting users triggers scale-out from 1 to 2 prefill workers (decode stays at 1), with new workers online within minutes. Confirms Dynamo's rate-matching problem in disaggregated prefill/decode serving is a first-class, actively-developed operational concern, not just a research curiosity. `review` (1344 words)
- [[nanoflow-towards-optimal-large-language-model-serving-throughput]] — NanoFlow: Towards Optimal Large Language Model Serving Throughput — NanoFlow (Zhu et al., University of Washington, arXiv 2408.12757, 2024) argues that end-to-end LLM serving is compute-bound (not memory-bound as commonly assumed) for most common workloads, because compute, memory, and networking operations are executed sequentially within a device rather than overlapped. Its core technique, intra-device parallelism, splits inputs into 'nano-batches' and duplicates operations to run heterogeneous resources concurrently on each portion, with NanoFlow automatically choosing nano-batch count, size, ordering, and GPU resource allocation. Cited by the SGLang v0.4 blog as the prior-art origin of the CPU/GPU scheduling-overlap idea underlying SGLang's zero-overhead batch scheduler. Tangential to KV-cache-aware routing specifically — this paper addresses intra-device compute/memory overlap and throughput, not cross-worker cache placement or prefix-aware request routing — included as background provenance for the scheduling-overlap technique rather than as a routing source. `evergreen` (11720 words)
- [[native-kv-cache-offloading-to-any-filesystem-with-llm-d-llm-d]] — Native KV Cache Offloading to Any Filesystem with llm-d | llm-d — Describes llm-d's native filesystem (FS) KV-cache offloading connector, which plugs into vLLM's Offloading Connector to store KV blocks as files on any POSIX-compatible shared filesystem (tested with IBM Storage Scale, CephFS, local NVMe). Unlike GPU HBM (tens of GB) or CPU DRAM (~250GB per GPU on an 8-GPU node), storage scales nearly infinitely and offers persistence across restarts/rescheduling plus cross-replica/cross-node KV sharing via a shared directory-as-index. Design choices: fully async I/O (non-blocking), parallel worker threads for throughput, and default GPU DMA transfers to avoid interfering with compute. Benchmarks on Llama-3.1-70B / 4x H100 with IBM Storage Scale show storage-tier KV loading achieves up to 16.8x TTFT speedup vs. prefill on long prompts (though GPU/CPU tiers remain faster for single requests); the key benefit is preventing throughput collapse at high concurrency/large working sets that exceed GPU+CPU capacity, sustaining ~185k tok/s in a mixed workload test vs. collapse for GPU-only. Positions storage as complementary to (not a replacement for) CPU offloading and prefix-aware routing. Next steps: tiered storage (second tier to CPU DRAM), NIXL backend integration, object storage, GPU Direct Storage (GDS) support. `evergreen` (2012 words)
- [[navigating-the-llm-landscape-ubers-innovation-with-genai-gateway]] — Navigating the LLM Landscape: Uber’s Innovation with GenAI Gateway — Uber Michelangelo team blog (Jul 11 2024 — outside the 18-24mo recency window, but the canonical first-person GenAI Gateway account) on the GenAI Gateway, a unified LLM access layer built because >60 disparate team-level LLM integration strategies had produced inefficiency/redundancy. Architecture: a Go service mirroring the OpenAI HTTP/JSON API shape, wrapping clients for third-party vendors (OpenAI, Vertex AI/PaLM2) plus Uber-hosted/in-house-served models — chosen specifically because OpenAI's API had become the de facto interface for the OSS LLM ecosystem (LangChain, LlamaIndex), reducing onboarding friction and avoiding a proprietary interface going stale. For OpenAI integration they maintain an internal fork of the community Go client (github.com/sashabaranov/go-openai); for Vertex AI, finding no adequate Go implementation available at the time, they built and open-sourced their own (github.com/uber/go-vertex-ai), continuing to maintain it even after Google later published an official Prediction Client. For Uber-hosted LLMs they state they built 'a robust serving stack built upon the STOA (state-of-the-art) inference libraries' but do not name the specific engine. In-house, explicitly custom-built components: the Go gateway service itself, a PII redactor/un-redactor (anonymizes categories like names/phone numbers with sequential placeholders before forwarding to third-party vendors, restores on response), auth/authz, metrics emission, and audit logging for cost attribution. Scale stated: ~30 customer teams, 16M queries/month, peak 25 QPS. Compared explicitly against Databricks' MLflow AI Gateway, which Uber says uses a non-OpenAI-compatible syntax (create_route/query) and lacks Uber's multi-language (Java/Go, not just Python) support. `review` (1772 words)
- [[nemo-guardrails-a-toolkit-for-controllable-and-safe]] — NeMo Guardrails: A Toolkit for Controllable and Safe — Original NVIDIA arXiv paper (Rebedea, Dinu, Sreedhar, Parisien, Cohen; arXiv:2310.10501, Oct 16 2023) introducing NeMo Guardrails, cited from the project's own GitHub README as its technical overview and evaluation. NOTE ON RECENCY: dated Oct 2023, outside this run's 18-24 month window -- read as the historical/architectural foundation document, not as evidence of the current (2026) feature set; cross-check against the live GitHub repo and Red Hat dev-blog tutorial for current state. Core architectural contribution: 'programmable rails' defined at runtime via a modeling language (Colang) are contrasted with 'embedded rails' baked in via model alignment/RLHF/instruction-tuning -- programmable rails are user-defined, independent of the underlying LLM, and interpretable, while the Guardrails runtime acts as a dialogue-manager proxy between application and LLM, using a three-step chain-of-thought prompting approach (generate user canonical form -> determine next step -> generate bot message). Reports concrete evaluation numbers: on a 231-sample/77-intent Banking NLU dataset, topical rails worked even with smaller open models (falcon-7b-instruct, llama2-13b-chat), given >=3 examples per canonical form in the vector DB; on an Anthropic Red-Teaming/Helpful moderation benchmark, combining both input AND output moderation rails with gpt-3.5-turbo blocked ~99% of harmful prompts (vs 93% with no rails) while allowing 98% of helpful ones (2% false-block) -- combining both directions is explicitly shown to be 'much more robust' than either alone; fact-checking rail against MS-MARCO reached 80% accuracy; a hallucination-deflection rail against 20 false-premise questions boosted text-davinci-003's deflection from near-0% to 70% and gpt-3.5-turbo's from 65% to 95%. Critically, the paper's own Limitations section (Section 7) states explicitly: 'we advocate that, whenever possible, our toolkit should not be used as a stand-alone solution, especially for safety-specific rails. Programmable rails complement embedded rails and these two solutions should be used together' -- a first-person, foundational-source confirmation that this OSS component is designed and positioned as a base layer, not a sufficient guardrail on its own. Also quantifies overhead directly: the three-step CoT runtime 'incurs extra costs and extra latency... about 3 times the latency and cost of a normal call to generate the bot message without using Guardrails' because the sequential CoT calls cannot be batched. `review` (10033 words)
- [[netflix-in-house-llm-serving-infrastructure-at-scale-zenml-llmops-database]] — Netflix: In-House LLM Serving Infrastructure at Scale - ZenML LLMOps Database — ZenML summary (very detailed, 2670 words) of Netflix's early-2026 tech blog on in-house LLM serving (primary at netflixtechblog.com/in-house-llm-serving-at-netflix-a5a8e799ea2c). Netflix unified LLM serving into its existing Model Scoring Service (MSS) rather than building a separate ML silo: a JVM-based frontend handles routing/A-B testing/feature-fetching, small CPU models run in-process, and GPU models delegate to MSS, which wraps NVIDIA Triton Inference Server behind a Java control plane (deployment, versioning, autoscaling, multi-region rollout). Exposes both a gRPC path (legacy ML workloads) and an OpenAI-compatible HTTP path (new LLM apps), reusing NVIDIA's Triton OpenAI-compatible frontend (FastAPI-based) alongside KServe HTTP/gRPC. Switched inference engines from TensorRT-LLM to vLLM in summer 2025 'based on operational fit rather than raw performance alone' -- citing ability to load custom model architectures without multi-step compilation, extensibility hooks for custom decoding, better debuggability, and research-community familiarity, after open-source engines had 'largely closed the performance gap with specialized stacks.' Had to fork/patch Triton's OpenAI-compatible frontend via git-subtree because response_format (structured-output) was silently dropped before reaching vLLM. Built a custom metrics proxy merging vLLM's Prometheus (.db files via PROMETHEUS_MULTIPROC_DIR) and Triton's native Prometheus endpoint because Triton's built-in bridge surfaced only 9 of 40+ vLLM metrics, missing token throughput, KV-cache utilization, and prefix-cache hit rate. Implements custom constrained decoding via vLLM's logits-processor interface as per-request state machines; migrating from vLLM V0 (per-request, GIL-serialized, CPU-bound at scale) to V1 (batch-level logits processing, reimplemented hot path in multi-threaded C++) kept latency flat as batch size grew. Future roadmap: system-prompt compression, async scheduling in vLLM V1, vectorized/GPU-kernel logits processors, lower-precision model variants. `review` (2670 words)
- [[networking-for-distributed-inference-in-llm-d-llm-d]] — Networking for Distributed Inference in llm-d | llm-d — Dense llm-d engineering blog post (co-authored with NVIDIA) on the networking stack underlying P/D disaggregation -- highly relevant to non-k8s backend feasibility because it describes NIXL/vLLM's NixlConnector mechanics as fundamentally pod-topology-agnostic.
