NIXL (NVIDIA Inference Xfer Library) is the sole KV-transfer layer llm-d uses; it exposes a unified transfer API abstracting UCX (RDMA), libfabric (AWS EFA), UCCL, and Mooncake backends behind a common plugin interface, over accelerators (CUDA/ROCm/XPU), fabrics (InfiniBand/RoCE/EFA/TCP), memory types (GPU VRAM/CPU DRAM) and storage (local SSD, HF3FS, S3). Backends declare capabilities (memory types, local/remote transfer, notification support) and implement registration/metadata-exchange/connection/prepare-post-check-release primitives; NIXL itself handles plugin discovery, descriptor validation, metadata exchange, backend selection and async request lifecycle -- none of this machinery references Kubernetes.

Critically for non-k8s feasibility: vLLM's NixlConnector operates pull-based (D reads KV blocks from P's GPU memory via one-sided RDMA READs) with LAZY, DECENTRALIZED pairing -- 'the first time D sees an unknown P engine it performs a background handshake that exchanges metadata over ZMQ, validates a compatibility hash, and bootstraps the RDMA path... New instances are paired automatically following the same mechanism, with no centralized service-discovery plane required.' Transfers are zero-copy. Additional connector features: heterogeneous TP between P/D (D computes which remote TP rank to read and fetches the matching slice), Hybrid Memory Allocator support for SSM-hybrid architectures (Jamba/Mamba), and fault tolerance via a configurable kv_lease_duration with D-side heartbeats (P auto-frees blocks if D crashes) and a kv_load_failure_policy (fail=default, error immediately; recompute=fall back to local prefill on D).

llm-d contributed a UCCL backend to NIXL starting in v0.5, targeting hardware UCX doesn't cover. Benchmarks (via nixlbench, 1-8GB payloads) compared UCCL/UCX/Mooncake across 100G RoCE, 400G InfiniBand, and 100G TCP: on RoCE all three converge near line rate (~12.0-12.2 GB/s of 12.5 GB/s theoretical); on 400G IB at batch=1000, UCCL and UCX both hit ~49.5 GB/s (~99% of 50GB/s line rate) while Mooncake saturates at ~42 GB/s (84%) due to higher per-block posting latency (2-17ms vs ~12us UCCL / ~283us UCX); at batch=10,000 Mooncake drops further to 38-42GB/s while UCX/UCCL hold ~49.5GB/s. Over TCP, GPU-to-GPU: UCCL achieves 4.7-4.9 GB/s (1GB in 211ms avg, P99 217ms) by leveraging NCCL CUDA kernels to chunk/pipeline GPU-to-CPU-to-network; UCX manages only 0.55 GB/s (1.8s for 1GB, 14.4s at 8GB, ~9x slower); Mooncake does not support GPU-to-GPU TCP at all. CPU-to-CPU over TCP at 1GB: UCCL 216ms, UCX 263ms (22% slower), Mooncake 298ms (38% slower); sustained throughput ~4.9/4/3.5 GB/s respectively.

Operationally, llm-d is adding 'preflight check' tooling (llm-d-preflight-checks.py) that gates vLLM startup inside the pod -- on failure the pod exits non-zero so Kubernetes reschedules it -- and a pause mode (LLMD_PREFLIGHT_CHECKS=pause) that holds vLLM startup for runtime networking tests, resumed via a POST to the script's /exit endpoint. This tooling is packaged as an agent skill in the llm-d/llm-d-pd-utils repo (/llm-d-preflight-checks, /llm-d-networking-tests) with a Helm-chart integration tracked as llm-d/llm-d#1580. Explicitly frames networking as the binding constraint on TTFT for disaggregated serving, and states 'considering KV Cache management' (CPU-memory offload paths) and observability (correlating network- and app-level metrics) as active/open work areas. `evergreen` (3384 words)
- [[no-kubernetes-no-problem-llm-d-now-runs-anywhere-llm-d]] — No Kubernetes? No Problem: llm-d Now Runs Anywhere | llm-d — llm-d.ai blog post (published alongside/before v0.9, references v0.9 release) introducing the EndpointDiscovery plugin interface that decouples endpoint discovery from routing logic in the llm-d router/EPP, enabling non-Kubernetes deployment (bare metal, HPC/Slurm, Ray, RL post-training frameworks like veRL/OpenRLHF/SkyRL/NeMo-RL). Gives the exact Go interface (EndpointDiscovery with Start/Ready, DiscoveryNotifier with Upsert/Delete) at pkg/epp/framework/interface/datalayer/discovery.go, describes the FileDiscovery plugin (YAML/JSON file, fsnotify watch, name+address:port+labels schema, llm-d.ai/role label for prefill/decode), the config flip (dataLayer.discovery.pluginRef: file-discovery), and named future plugins (SlurmDiscovery, RayDiscovery, Consul/etcd-backed). States limitations: InferenceObjective priority and InferenceModelRewrite still require Kubernetes CRDs and fall back to static config off-cluster; endpoint-lifecycle removal (dead worker eviction) is the orchestrator's job off-Kubernetes, not automatic. Links to upstream No-Kubernetes Deployment guide and companion architecture doc, both chased in Phase 2. `evergreen` (3177 words)
- [[ollama-vs-vllm-a-deep-dive-into-performance-benchmarking-red-hat-developer]] — Ollama vs. vLLM: A deep dive into performance benchmarking | Red Hat Developer — PRIMARY SOURCE: Red Hat Developer's own controlled benchmark (Aug 8, 2025; last updated July 13, 2026) directly substantiating the concurrency-collapse claims repeated across this batch's secondary sources. Controlled test on a single NVIDIA A100-PCIE-40GB GPU on OpenShift 4.17.15, using GuideLLM to drive concurrency from 1 to 256 simultaneous users against vLLM 0.9.1 (meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B-instruct) and Ollama 0.9.2 (llama3.1:8b-instruct-fp16). Headline result: vLLM peaks at 793 tokens/sec vs Ollama's 41 TPS, with P99 latency of 80ms vs 673ms at peak throughput. At Ollama's untuned default (max 4 parallel requests), throughput plateaus immediately while vLLM scales with concurrency. Even after tuning Ollama to its highest stable parallelism for this GPU (OLLAMA_NUM_PARALLEL=32), Ollama's throughput still plateaus well below vLLM's, and its inter-token latency becomes 'extremely erratic, with massive spikes at higher concurrency' -- explicitly attributed to head-of-line blocking where 'a single stalled request can slow down an entire batch.' vLLM's TTFT stays low and stable across all concurrency levels; Ollama's TTFT 'rises sharply' because requests queue before processing. Conclusion: Ollama is explicitly scoped for local development/prototyping/single-user use, not production; vLLM is 'unequivocally the superior choice for production deployment.' This is the primary engineering source behind the 'Ollama collapses at 10+ concurrent users' claims found in tianpan.co and other secondary sources in this batch -- though note the specific multiplier (20x/44x) cited by tianpan.co is not the exact figure in this benchmark (this benchmark reports ~19x TPS gap at peak: 793 vs 41), suggesting tianpan.co's figures may be rounded/approximated or drawn from a related but different Red Hat benchmark. `review` (1907 words)
- [[open-models-lag-state-of-the-art-closed-models-by-4-months-epoch-ai]] — Open models lag state-of-the-art closed models by 4 months | Epoch AI — PRIMARY SOURCE: Epoch AI (independent AI research nonprofit, no inference-platform commercial interest) data-insight page confirming the exact figure Forte Group cites. Since January 2026, the most capable open-weight models have lagged frontier closed models by an average of four months on the Epoch Capabilities Index (ECI), with an average ECI gap of 8 points -- 'similar to the gap between GPT-5 and GPT-5.5.' Epoch explicitly flags this gap 'may be understated: open-weight models tend to perform worse on private benchmarks compared to closed models, plausibly because they more aggressively optimize for public benchmarks' -- the same caveat Forte Group's article relays. The page is dominated (>95% of word count) by a large sortable data table of per-model ECI scores with confidence intervals (e.g., Gemini 3.5 Flash 156.31, Qwen 3.6 Max Preview 150.16, GLM-5.1 149.94) spanning dozens of open- and closed-weight models from Jan 2023 through mid-2026, which is reference data rather than narrative; the load-bearing textual claim is confined to the first ~150 words. LONG SOURCE BY WORD COUNT (~25,000 words / ~160KB) but almost entirely tabular model-score data, not prose -- flagging for awareness rather than recommending full source-analyst treatment, since the substantive claim is fully captured in the opening paragraph. `review` (25308 words)
- [[open-source-llm-apis-beat-self-hosting-heres-the-math-dev-community]] — Open-Source LLM APIs Beat Self-Hosting. Here's the Math. - DEV Community — STARTUP-SCALE (not personal hobby, but not enterprise either) first-person account on dev.to: author ran two A100s on Lambda Labs to serve Qwen3-32B for an internal summarization pipeline at ~$1,400/month for ~30M tokens of actual traffic, versus an estimated $8.40 via an open-weights API aggregator — closed the self-hosted setup that afternoon. Gives a dated (October 2025) open-weights model pricing table (DeepSeek V4 Flash $0.25/M output, Qwen3-32B $0.28/M, GLM-4-9B $0.01/M, etc.) against self-host GPU-only monthly cost ranges ($200-$3,000) and explicitly flags the GPU-only number as misleading. Hidden-cost breakdown for self-hosting: GPU compute $400-8,000, load balancer/gateway $50-200, monitoring $50-200, partial DevOps allocation $500-3,000/month (author's own prior company: ~$2,000/month for a part-time DevOps allocation), model-upgrade/dependency churn $100-500, electricity/cooling $200-1,000 — all-in $900-4,900/month, of which 'the DevOps line is the one that stung' and 'the GPU was the smallest line on the invoice if you count opportunity cost honestly.' Proposes three token-volume break-even buckets: under 10M tokens/day API wins ~32x; 10-100M tokens/day (grows to 50M tokens/day in ~9 months) API still 3-5x cheaper; 500M+ tokens/day self-hosting starts to pull ahead ONLY if capital is already sunk, a real infra team exists, and load is steady. NOTE INTERNAL INCONSISTENCY: the article's own 'bottom line' section states the break-even is '50M tokens/day for most teams,' which contradicts its own bucket analysis showing self-hosting only winning above 500M tokens/day — a tenfold discrepancy within the same source. Also contains production failover code sample routing across three OpenAI-compatible endpoints to argue vendor lock-in is 'overblown' because open-weights + OpenAI-compatible APIs preserve self-host optionality. `review` (1935 words)
- [[open-weight-models-are-gaining-ground-in-enterprise-ai-constellation-research]] — Open-Weight Models Are Gaining Ground in Enterprise AI | Constellation Research — Constellation Research analyst piece (Chirag Mehta, Aug 24 2026) built entirely on Vercel AI Gateway TELEMETRY (actual token-routing volume through one specific commercial LLM gateway, not a survey) — explicitly caveated as 'just one gateway' not overall market share. Open-weight SHARE OF TOKEN VOLUME rose from ~11% (April 2026) to 29% (June 2026), then to 28.4% on June 24 and a 'record' 62% by August 22; roughly half of token volume over the latest two-month window. Critically, open-weight models captured 29% of token volume on Vercel in June while accounting for under 4% of SPEND, versus Anthropic's 32% of tokens but 61% of spend — i.e., open models win volume/cheap-task share long before revenue share. Notes heavy multi-model usage scales with request volume (teams at >10M requests/month use ~35 models on average per Vercel data). Documents that Chinese labs (DeepSeek, Qwen, GLM, Kimi) are setting the pace in open-weight releases and gaining real production traffic on gateways; Hugging Face counted >151,000 downstream Qwen derivative repos, with ~180-210 new ones/day in H1 2026. Argues enterprises adopt open-weight models via managed inference providers (Together AI: ~30B to >400 trillion tokens/month in 9 months; Fireworks: >40 trillion tokens/day, >95% of traffic from customer-specialized models) FASTER than they adopt self-hosted inference — i.e. gateway/provider telemetry captures API-mediated open-weight consumption, not self-hosted internal deployments, which this metric structurally cannot see. `review` (1695 words)
- [[openai-compatible-frontend-for-triton-inference-server-nvidia-triton-inference-s]] — OpenAI-Compatible Frontend for Triton Inference Server — NVIDIA Triton Inference Server — Official NVIDIA documentation (Triton Inference Server v2.73.0) for the OpenAI-Compatible Frontend -- the component Netflix (per the Netflix in-house-LLM-serving post in this batch) forked via git-subtree and patched to fix a silently-dropped response_format parameter. Confirms the frontend supports both vLLM and TensorRT-LLM as backends behind a single /v1/chat/completions, /v1/completions, and /v1/embeddings OpenAI-compatible surface; vLLM backend additionally supports embeddings via vLLM's embedding-model list. Supports LoRA adapter selection at request time via a --lora-separator flag and <model>_<lora> naming convention, for both vLLM and TensorRT-LLM backends. Supports OpenAI-style tool calling (tools/tool_choice) via --tool-call-parser {llama3,mistral}, implemented through vLLM's guided/structured-decoding and TensorRT-LLM's guided decoding respectively -- directly corroborating the mechanism Netflix's logits-processor patch plugs into. Documents --model-control-mode=explicit for dynamic runtime model load/unload without server restart (useful for multi-model GPU sharing), per-endpoint access restriction via custom auth headers (--openai-restricted-api), and a configurable HTTP request body size limit (default 64 MiB). Does not mention or confirm the specific response_format bug Netflix describes -- the docs as fetched describe the intended/current behavior, not historical defects. High-authority primary/official documentation source (tier: ground_truth). `review` (3428 words)
- [[optimized-baseline-llm-d]] — Optimized Baseline | llm-d — v0.9 llm-d well-lit-path doc explaining why LLM request scheduling needs to diverge from standard round-robin HTTP load balancing: LLM requests are multi-turn (repeatedly resending a growing prefix), slow (single requests can exceed a minute), and non-uniform (1000s of reasoning tokens to 100k+ context). The llm-d Router's Optimized Baseline injects two signals into load balancing: Prefix-Aware Scheduling (EPP maintains an in-memory view of each endpoint's prefix-cache state and routes to the pod already holding the matching prefix — critical for multi-turn workloads to avoid recomputation at scale) and Load-Aware Scheduling (EPP scrapes each endpoint's /metrics at a default 50ms interval, scoring queue depth, running requests, and KV-cache utilization to avoid hotspots from heterogeneous request patterns). Notes the Router also supports session-affinity and active-request-based routing that make no assumptions about parsing requests or probing servers, and positions this baseline as distinct from the event-driven precise-prefix-cache-routing path. `evergreen` (343 words)
- [[optimized-baseline-llm-d-2]] — Optimized Baseline | llm-d — llm-d.ai docs page (dev branch) for the 'Optimized Baseline' well-lit path. States the llm-d Router injects LLM-workload awareness into load balancing via prefix-cache affinity and server load metrics, and that EPP maintains an in-memory view of each endpoint's prefix-cache state, routing to the pod already holding the matching prefix. Explicitly caveats that this is 'one approach' — the router also supports session affinity and active-request-based routing as alternate scorers (see router configuration doc). Notes load-aware scoring probes /metrics every 50ms by default, scoring on queue depth, running requests, and KV-cache utilization. Flags the peakPrefillThroughput calibration trap on non-default hardware as a documented failure mode. `evergreen` (409 words)
- [[pd-disaggregation-on-a-single-gpu-what-the-architecture-actually-requires-karthi]] — P/D Disaggregation on a Single GPU — What the Architecture Actually Requires - Karthika Raghavan — First-hand engineering post (Karthika Raghavan, April 2026) documenting a live deployment of llm-d's official P/D disaggregation guide (llm-d v0.4.0, NIXL sidecar v0.7.1, Qwen3-0.6B) on a single Lambda Labs GH200 480GB GPU, and the specific failure mode when the hardware precondition for NIXL is not met. The pods (gaie-pd-epp, infra-pd-inference-gateway-istio, decode, prefill) all ran healthy (1/1) with valid HTTPRoute and a responsive gateway, but the P/D Coordinator Grafana dashboard showed all NIXL KV-transfer metrics as 'No Data' -- Avg KV Transfer Time, Avg MB per Transfer, Total KV Transfers all empty, and Failed KV Transfers=0 not because transfers succeeded but because none were attempted. Root cause: NIXL requires GPU-to-GPU transfer over RDMA (InfiniBand or RoCE) using ibv_reg_mr to pin GPU memory for zero-copy transfer between SEPARATE physical GPUs; on a single GH200 with prefill and decode pods time-sliced onto the same physical GPU, there is no RDMA path -- both pods share the same physical memory space and NIXL's transport layer cannot initialize, so the sidecar opens port 5600 but nothing moves. The llm-d pd-disaggregation guide documents this requirement explicitly (its inference-scheduling guide's default benchmark config targets 16 GPUs across 8 model servers) but it is easy to overlook because every other layer (pods, EPP, HTTPRoute, Prometheus) still looks healthy. Consequently the system silently collapsed back into aggregated serving with WORSE performance than plain aggregated serving: end-to-end latency was 6.5x higher, TTFT 3.7x higher, and inter-token latency (ITL) 3.5x higher (ITL rose from 5ms to 17.5ms) than the prior aggregated-serving baseline, because (1) gpu-memory-utilization was halved to 0.45 per pod, shrinking the KV cache budget and increasing evictions, (2) both pods time-slice-compete for the same physical GPU cycles, and (3) the EPP's Ready Pods count showed only 1 of 2 pods as ready -- the prefill pod, despite being healthy, was never receiving routed traffic (0% KV cache utilization, near-zero throughput), so the decode pod handled 100% of both prefill and decode work under a halved memory budget. The author proposes a 5-signal diagnostic checklist (NIXL metrics=No Data, Prefill Worker Utilization~0%, decode pod handles all throughput, Prefill TTFT=Decode TTFT identical, EPP Ready Pods less than total running pods) to detect this failure mode before assuming disaggregation is functioning. `evergreen` (3393 words)
- [[pd-disaggregation-llm-d]] — P/D Disaggregation | llm-d — llm-d v0.9 'well-lit path' quickstart for P/D disaggregation (short, links to the fuller architecture doc). Gives a concrete reference topology: 2 vLLM Deployments in the same InferencePool -- prefill Deployment = 4 replicas of TP=1 vLLM labeled llm-d.ai/role=prefill, decode Deployment = 1 replica of TP=5 vLLM labeled llm-d.ai/role=decode, with routing proxy sidecars on all decode pods. Standard request flow: proxy forwards to EPP, EPP schedules using the role labels to pick decode+prefill variants, request routed to the sidecar which forwards to the prefill instance, prefill returns KV-block retrieval metadata, decode instance pulls KVs over RDMA (IB, RoCE, or EFA) via NIXL, then decode runs. States NIXL supports TCP transfer but high-bandwidth networking (IB/RoCE/EFA) is 'highly recommended' for production. Notes observability guidance treats P/D as independently scaled pools joined by NIXL KV transfer, so pool balance and transfer health (not aggregate latency) are the key signals -- points to a PromQL reference and the llm-d/llm-d guides/pd-disaggregation repo folder for manifests, troubleshooting and metrics. `evergreen` (400 words)
- [[pd-disaggregation-llm-d-2]] — P/D Disaggregation | llm-d — llm-d dev-branch overview of prefill/decode (P/D) disaggregation. Prefill is compute-bound (GPU flops); decode is memory-bandwidth-bound (KV-cache read from HBM). Recommended for long-context (10:1 ISL:OSL) medium/large-model workloads. Describes a reference topology: 4 prefill replicas (TP=1) and 1 decode replica (TP=5) in one InferencePool, labeled llm-d.ai/role=prefill|decode. Request flow: proxy -> EPP (schedules P/D using role labels) -> sidecar on the decode pod -> sidecar forwards to prefill instance -> prefill returns KV-block metadata -> decode instance pulls KV blocks over RDMA (IB/RoCE/EFA) via NIXL -> decode proceeds. Notes NIXL supports TCP but high-bandwidth RDMA networking is 'highly recommended' for production. Observability guidance: because P/D runs two independently scaled pools joined by a NIXL transfer, operators should watch pool balance and transfer health rather than one aggregate latency metric. `deprecated` (414 words)
- [[pd-sidecar-allowlist-stores-only-pod-ip-not-hostport-issue-979-llm-dllm-d-router]] — P/D sidecar allowlist stores only pod IP, not host:port · Issue #979 · llm-d/llm-d-router — GitHub issue #979 (opened May 3 2026, status OPEN as of this research), ported from llm-d-inference-scheduler#1365, with a full live-cluster reproduction against pkg/sidecar/proxy/allowlist.go. Reports TWO bugs bundled together: BUG A -- updatePodsForPool read spec.selector flat instead of spec.selector.matchLabels for the GA InferencePool API, so the pod-selector label set was malformed and the pod informer's reflector failed every list/watch, leaving the allowlist permanently empty (net effect: --enable-ssrf-protection=true made every P/D request 403, i.e. secure but completely broken). BUG B -- the allowlist stores only bare pod IPs and pod names with NO PORT, and IsAllowed() strips the port from the input before comparing, so 'an attacker with direct sidecar pod-network reach can send X-Prefiller-Host-Port: <allowlisted-pod-ip>:<arbitrary-port> and the sidecar will dial that arbitrary port on the allowlisted IP' -- demonstrated end-to-end with a captured log of the sidecar POSTing a prefill body to an attacker-controlled port 9090 on an allowlisted IP. CROSS-CHECK AGAINST HEAD (2026-09-11 fetch of allowlist.go in this same session): BUG A's fix (matchLabels-aware selectorPath, GA vs alpha API branch) IS present at HEAD, matching PR #1647's merged code -- so bug A appears fixed. BUG B is NOT fixed at HEAD: addPodToAllowlist still inserts only podIP and podName with no port suffix, exactly matching the reported pre-fix behavior. This means the currently-merged allowlist, even when SSRF protection is fully enabled with correct InferencePool/RBAC setup, does not enforce the port component of x-prefiller-host-port -- a caller with any network reach to the sidecar can hit an arbitrary port on any allowlisted pod IP. `evergreen` (3942 words)
- [[preble-efficient-distributed-prompt-scheduling]] — PREBLE: EFFICIENT DISTRIBUTED PROMPT SCHEDULING — Preble (UCSD, 2024) is the first distributed LLM serving platform that co-optimizes KV cache/prompt-prefix reuse with compute load balancing across multiple GPUs, rather than treating prefix caching as a single-GPU optimization (as in SGLang RadixAttention or vLLM prefix caching). It introduces a distributed scheduling algorithm plus a two-tier hierarchical scheduling mechanism: a global scheduler assigns requests to GPU groups based on prefix-tree similarity, and local schedulers within a group balance load while preserving cache locality. Against real workloads and arrival patterns on two open-source LLMs, Preble outperforms SOTA serving systems (vLLM, SGLang) by 1.5x-14.5x on average request latency and 2x-10x on p99 latency, demonstrating that jointly optimizing cache reuse and load balance beats either alone. Directly relevant as academic grounding for llm-d's prefix-cache-aware scheduler design. `evergreen` (11959 words)
- [[preble-efficient-distributed-prompt-scheduling-2]] — PREBLE: EFFICIENT DISTRIBUTED PROMPT SCHEDULING — UC San Diego's Preble paper (ICLR 2024, arXiv 2407.00023) is the primary systems-paper source for the locality-vs-load-balancing tension discussed throughout this batch. It identifies that prior KV-cache-reuse work (including SGLang/RadixAttention) is confined to single-GPU optimization, while production LLM serving is inherently distributed across many GPUs -- so prefix-cache reuse decisions must be co-optimized with cross-GPU load balancing, not treated as a single-instance problem. Preble's architecture is a two-level hierarchical scheduler: a global scheduler makes request-level placement decisions across GPU instances using a new 'E2' (Exploitation + Exploration) algorithm driven by a prompt-aware load metric (accounting for historical load, eviction cost, and the cost of serving a new request), while a per-instance local scheduler handles iteration-level scheduling of the requests assigned to it. Motivation grounded in real usage: notes that production prompts have grown long relative to generated output (prefill now dominates total compute) and that prompts are frequently partially shared across requests -- citing Anthropic's own public reporting of widespread long/shared prompts in production as evidence this is not a synthetic concern. Evaluated on real workloads and arrival patterns with two open-source LLMs, Preble outperforms prior state-of-the-art serving systems by 1.5x-14.5x on average latency and 2x-10x on p99 latency. This paper is the system that the later Locality-aware Fair Scheduling in LLM Serving paper (DLPM/D2LPM, same batch) explicitly targets as the locality-optimal-but-fairness-blind baseline, showing Preble can impose up to 7.18x higher latency on well-behaved clients when a dominant client is present -- i.e., Preble solves the locality/load-balance tension but not the fairness dimension. `review` (11973 words)
- [[patreon-saves-nearly-600kyear-in-ml-resources-with-baseten]] — Patreon saves nearly $600k/year in ML resources with Baseten — Baseten vendor case study: Patreon deploys OpenAI's Whisper (off-the-shelf, unmodified) on Baseten for audio/video transcription and closed-caption generation at scale, reporting 440+ engineer-hours/year, $600k/year, and 70% GPU cost savings versus self-managed infra or OpenAI's hosted Whisper API. No fine-tuning, post-training, or distillation of any kind is mentioned anywhere in the case study -- Patreon explicitly 'deployed and massively scaled the Whisper transcription model' as-is. This is the clean NEGATIVE control for the managed-middle two-part test: part 2 holds (Baseten operates serving) but part 1 fails entirely (no weight customization) -- ordinary managed hosting of a stock open-weight model, not the managed-middle pattern. `review` (701 words)
- [[pinterest-cut-ai-costs-90-by-gutting-a-frontier-models-vision-layer-venturebeat]] — Pinterest cut AI costs 90% by gutting a frontier model's vision layer | VentureBeat — VentureBeat report (May 29 2026) on Pinterest CTO Matt Madrigal's own account (from the 'Beyond the Pilot' podcast) of customizing an open-weight model for Pinterest's 620-million-MAU visual discovery product. Pinterest's conversational shopping assistant, 'Navigator 1,' was built on Qwen3-VL; Madrigal's team 'ripped out' Qwen's native vision encoder layer entirely and replaced it with Pinterest's own proprietary multimodal embeddings (precomputed offline from pin/image metadata and regularly retrained), cutting AI costs 90% and improving accuracy 30%. Without precomputed embeddings, encoding each returned image at runtime individually would make inference latency '20 times worse.' Madrigal frames the rationale explicitly around licensing: 'Open-source models, especially with open Apache licenses where you can truly tweak a lot of open weights and customize for unique use cases -- that's where we've found open source to be so powerful for us.' Notes Pinterest's longer history of building on open models for visual search (Google's BERT, OpenAI's CLIP -- the latter fine-tuned in-house as 'Pin CLIP' with proprietary visual embeddings). Pinterest also runs a 'taste graph': a representational-learning structure of per-user embeddings capturing evolving preferences (not a social graph), continuously updated from activity signals, used to drive discovery-to-intent conversion. Madrigal's decision heuristic: for anything 'critical for end users, driving engagement, and needing to scale to over 600 million MAU,' Pinterest will either build in-house or take open source and 'customize the heck out of it' rather than default to a frontier-model API call per request. `review` (1212 words)
- [[poor-performance-with-bursty-workloads-issue-298-llm-dllm-d-router]] — Poor performance with bursty workloads · Issue #298 · llm-d/llm-d-router — GitHub issue #298 on llm-d/llm-d-router (opened Aug 18 2025 by Red Hat contributor dagrayvid, closed-not-planned Jan 2026, 17 comments): a maintainer's own performance experiment found llm-d's inference-scheduler underperforming plain Knative request-count-based load balancing. Setup: 8 replicas of Llama-3.3-70B-fp8 on 2xH100/replica (TP=2), prefix-caching/prefix-aware routing explicitly DISABLED (queue-scorer + kv-cache-scorer only, weight 1.0 each), guidellm workload at 1000 ISL/1000 OSL, concurrency=800 -- a deliberately bursty, fixed-sequence-length, concurrency-based traffic pattern. Result: llm-d gave slightly lower throughput and 'significantly worse TTFT' than Knative, with vllm:num_requests_waiting showing heavy queuing concentrated on a subset of replicas -- because metric refresh lag (50ms interval) meant a burst of near-simultaneous requests all landed on the same pod (0 KV utilization/queue depth at decision time) before the score updated. Root cause per maintainer liu-cong: 'metrics update delay for very bursty traffic ... this is a corner case ... only happens when you send a very bursty traffic with shared prefixes, which in practice is likely rare.' Quantified fix comparison (KServe baseline vs. llm-d max-score-picker vs. llm-d weighted-random-picker): TTFT mean 1413ms (KServe) vs. 2083.4ms (llm-d top-score-picker, 47% WORSE than plain LB) vs. 367.2ms (weighted-random fix); TTFT P99 6941ms vs. 7749ms (worse) vs. 2061ms; output tok/s 17509 vs. 16861 (slightly worse) vs. 17601. This is a first-party, maintainer-documented instance of llm-d's load-aware routing (with prefix-affinity off) actually losing to plain load balancing on a bursty, low-prefix-diversity workload. Follow-up experiments show the fix (weighted random sampling) trades off against multi-turn chat performance where prefix affinity matters: 'weighted-random-sampling significantly hurts performance compared to max-score for this use-case' (output tok/s drops from 12608 to 8292 at matched scorer weights) unless the prefix-scorer weight is substantially increased to compensate. `evergreen` (2647 words)
- [[post-training-open-legal-agents-with-baseten-research]] — Post-Training Open Legal Agents With Baseten Research — Harvey's own blog post (5/27/2026, predates the Kimi K3/Tenet announcement) describing a SEPARATE research collaboration with Baseten Research: post-training a 27B open-weight model (Qwen3.5-27B) using iterative SFT (iSFT) plus Baseten's KV-cache compaction technique ('STILL') inside a legal-agent compaction harness, reaching closed-source-frontier performance on Harvey's LAB benchmark. Explicitly framed as early-stage research ('base camp', 'more to come'), not a production deployment -- it names governance/self-hosting ('open-weight models can be hosted within a firm's own secure cloud environment') as a motivating benefit of post-training but never states where this Qwen3.5-27B model actually runs, and does not address the Tenet/Kimi K3 production-serving question at all. `review` (2521 words)
- [[post-training-kimi-k3-with-harvey-for-long-horizon-legal-work]] — Post-training Kimi K3 with Harvey for long-horizon legal work — Fireworks AI's own blog post (8/26/2026) on co-developing Harvey Tenet (Kimi K3 post-trained via async RL on the Fireworks Training API). States Tenet scores 19.7% all-pass on LAB vs 10.8% for base Kimi K3, at near-flat cost ($5.92 vs $5.62 per task) due to open-weight per-token pricing. Critically, it describes the Fireworks platform property that 'training and serving live on the same platform, a promising checkpoint can be promoted and validated while training continues, then moved into production without leaving the platform' -- the strongest available signal that Tenet is served in production on Fireworks infrastructure rather than self-hosted, though it stops short of an explicit first-person statement that Harvey's production traffic for Tenet runs on Fireworks. `review` (1009 words)
- [[potential-ssrf-via-x-prefiller-url-or-x-prefiller-host-port-header-issue-242-llm]] — Potential SSRF via `x-prefiller-url` or `x-prefiller-host-port` header · Issue #242 · llm-d/llm-d-router — GitHub issue #242 (opened Jul 16 2025 by pierDipi, closed Jul 18 2025), the original public SSRF disclosure against the routing sidecar: the sidecar honored client-supplied x-prefiller-url / x-prefiller-host-port headers with zero validation, letting an external caller through the Gateway force the sidecar to forward the prefill leg to an arbitrary internal address (demonstrated with curl against an internal pod IP, 200 OK, confirmed via sidecar logs). Maintainer elevran proposed two mitigations: (1) MUST-have -- have the scheduler/gateway/EPP always clear the incoming header from user requests and set its own value only after scheduling (implemented in PR #244); (2) additional safety net -- have the sidecar validate the target is in-cluster/non-Internet-routable, which elevran noted requires adding Kubernetes API access to the sidecar's pod (this became the InferencePool-watching AllowlistValidator, eventually landing via now-archived llm-d-routing-sidecar#48 and later reimplemented as PR #1647 in llm-d-router). The issue explicitly frames the sidecar-side allowlist as a secondary safety net, not the primary control -- the primary control is upstream header-stripping at the EPP/gateway. `evergreen` (1139 words)
- [[practical-guide-to-evaluating-open-models-achieving-sonnet-level-performance-wit]] — Practical Guide to Evaluating Open Models: Achieving Sonnet-level performance with Kimi — Amplitude's own first-person engineering blog (Jacob Newman, Principal PM), describing exactly how Amplitude deployed open-weight models in its 'Global Agent' product. Context: Microsoft's 'Open Weights and American AI Leadership' open letter (signed by Nvidia, Microsoft, Meta, Dell, IBM, Palantir, Mistral, Hugging Face initially, growing past 200 signatories within a week including OpenAI, Google, Amazon, Uber, Databricks, GitHub, Fireworks AI, and Amplitude itself); Amplitude's CEO Spenser Skates confirmed in his signing request that 'Kimi and GLM are already core to how we operate, both internally and in customer-facing AI features.' Cites Stanford HAI AI Index: the human-preference gap between open and closed models on Chatbot Arena fell from 8% to 1.7% in one year. Prior production stack: all Global Agent subagents (Global Chat, Chart Agent, Data Assistant, Session Replay SQL, Preference Extractor) ran on Claude Sonnet 4.6. Hosting choice: selected Fireworks' managed open-weight catalog (Kimi, GLM, gpt-oss billed per token) over Modal's raw GPU containers, because dedicated Fireworks GPU instances matched serverless exactly on Amplitude's quality suite with more consistent latency and fixed hourly cost. Model selection mapped three open-weight options against Claude's tiers: gpt-oss-120B ~ Haiku tier (11-25x cheaper, weak instruction-following, narrow subagent use only); Kimi K2.6/K2.7 ~ Sonnet tier (trained for 200-300-step tool chains, #1 on tau2-bench Telecom at 93%, ~2x thinking-token cost); GLM 5.2 ~ near-Opus (within ~4 points of Opus on SWE-bench/Terminal-Bench at ~6x lower cost but weaker on long-horizon tasks and blind preference). Chose Kimi K2.7 for the head agent. On an internal 186-case eval suite mirroring production traffic, Kimi K2.7 scored 64 unmodified vs Sonnet 4.6's reference; after system-level remediation (caching/parallelizing permission checks, single-agent compiler routing for chart work -15% latency, typed/validated chart-definition schemas, a loop-breaker for degenerate tool loops, a dedicated compute/date tool to remove hand-done arithmetic, grounding rules against capability hallucination, a reasoning-leakage sanitizer, and a context clamp tied to the model's real context window) it scored 73.7, edging past Sonnet's 72.7, at roughly one-third Sonnet's price (with the real gap possibly wider since Anthropic bills cache writes and Fireworks doesn't). ~80% of failed eval cases traced to the head agent. Global Chat alone is ~44% of all Global Agent spend. Amplitude built this evaluation using its own 'Agent Analytics' product (usage data, cost-per-session, failure taxonomy) and is now running a staged, customer-segment rollout rather than a single cutover, explicitly noting Kimi's launch benchmarks are vendor-reported and independent coverage is still thin. `review` (2174 words)
- [[precise-prefix-cache-routing-llm-d]] — Precise Prefix Cache Routing | llm-d — v0.9 (latest, well-lit-paths/foundations) doc describing precise prefix-cache routing as a deployable path built on model-server-emitted KV-events (vLLM, SGLang, and NVIDIA TensorRT-LLM all publish cache-change events) that llm-d subscribes to, building a near-real-time view of resident blocks across the fleet and scoring requests against it; the prefix-affinity score combines with standard load-aware scorers as in the Optimized Baseline path. Frames KV-events as the emerging ecosystem-standard substrate for exposing cache state, extensible to tier-aware tracking (GPU HBM/CPU DRAM/NVMe/shared storage), prompt-cache placement policies, dynamic KV-offloading, prefetching, agentic-workload retention/eviction, and hybrid-attention models. Architecture: model servers produce KV-events on every cache change; the llm-d Router's indexer maintains a block-key-to-pods mapping and its scorer derives block keys deterministically, returning the longest consecutive cached prefix per candidate pod weighted by tier. Events flow over ZMQ via pod discovery (each model-server pod binds its own socket; every Router replica subscribes to every pod independently), so all Router replicas converge to the same index for active-active HA with no extra coordination; the reference guide deploys two Router replicas behind one Service by default, scalable down to one for small fleets. `evergreen` (411 words)
- [[predicted-latency-based-scheduling-llm-d]] — Predicted Latency-Based Scheduling | llm-d — Official llm-d docs (v0.9) overview of predicted-latency-based scheduling: an alternative/complement to the optimized-baseline heuristic scorer, using an online-trained XGBoost model to predict per-pod TTFT and TPOT and route to the pod predicted fastest, optionally enforcing per-request SLOs via x-llm-d-slo-ttft-ms / x-llm-d-slo-tpot-ms headers. Explicitly answers the gap question about k8s coupling: "The setup deploys an EPP with the predicted latency sidecar containers" -- a Training Server (trains XGBoost on observed traffic) and Prediction Servers (serve TPOT/TTFT predictions from current server state) -- both colocated with the EPP, not with the model server. Request flow: proxy forwards to EPP, EPP queries the prediction server, EPPs latency-scorer picks the endpoint, proxy forwards to the vLLM endpoint, and results flow back to the training server to update the model online. Explicit limitation: only p90-percentile prediction and only streaming-mode training are currently supported; the predictor assumes a homogeneous pool (same GPU/model/serving-config) and produces inaccurate predictions on a heterogeneous pool. Links to github.com/llm-d/llm-d-latency-predictor for the training/prediction server source (Python) and to architecture/advanced/latency-predictor for the full plugin pipeline and metric reference. `evergreen` (372 words)
- [[predicted-latency-based-scheduling-for-llms-llm-d]] — Predicted-Latency Based Scheduling for LLMs | llm-d — Describes llm-d/Google's predicted-latency-aware scheduling system, which replaces manually tuned heuristic routing weights (queue depth, KV utilization, cache locality) with a lightweight XGBoost regression model trained online from live traffic to directly predict per-server Time to First Token (TTFT, prefill) and Time Per Output Token (TPOT, decode) for each candidate endpoint given a new request. Features: KV cache usage %, input length, queue depth, running requests, prefix cache match %, and input tokens in-flight. The model retrains continuously on a bucketed sliding window (stratified by KV-cache % and prefix hit-rate bands) to avoid forgetting rarer traffic regimes. Deployed as a training/prediction sidecar pair to the Gateway API Inference Extension's Endpoint Picker (EPP); a predicted-latency scorer computes 'headroom' (predicted latency minus SLO target, weighted 80% TTFT/20% TPOT by default) and does best-fit packing when SLOs exist, else routes to lowest predicted latency. A cache-aware affinity gate (epsilon-greedy, default affinityGateTau=0.80, epsilonExploreSticky=0.01, affinityMaxTTFTPenaltyMs=5000ms) prevents pure-greedy routing from fragmenting the cache: it exploits cache-warm pods 99% of the time and explores non-sticky pods 1% of the time, with a load override if the warm pod's predicted TTFT exceeds the best pod's by more than the penalty threshold. Achieves ~5% MAPE on TTFT/TPOT prediction. Across 5 synthetic benchmark scenarios (10x H100 pods, Qwen3-class models) spanning cache-friendly to cache-intensive regimes, predicted-latency scheduling beats or matches load+prefix-aware routing in 4/5 cases; only under extreme system-cache churn (Workload C) does it merely tie. On a production-derived Qwen3-480B workload (13 servers x 8 H200, ~94% peak cache hit rate with frequent collapses), predicted-latency routing cut E2E p50 latency 43% (9.06s vs 13.42s for hand-tuned weights) and TTFT p50 70% (0.97s vs 3.38s) versus the best heuristic scorer, without workload-specific tuning. Prediction serving scales to 10,000 QPS across 100 candidate pods using request coalescing (1ms batching window) and horizontally-scaled prediction servers (28 uvicorn workers each). `evergreen` (5526 words)
- [[prefix-scorer-should-consider-cache-freshness-issue-972-kubernetes-sigsgateway-a]] — Prefix Scorer should consider cache freshness · Issue #972 · kubernetes-sigs/gateway-api-inference-extension — GIE GitHub issue #972 (opened Jun 2025 by a maintainer, closed Sep 2025 as superseded by #1497): proposes that the prefix scorer weight cache hits by recency (freshest 80% of LRU insertion order counted as 'fresh', older 20% discounted), since an old 101-block hit is less valuable than a recent 100-block hit. Thin, maintainer-internal feature request, not an independent evaluation and not evidence of hotspotting or underperformance vs. plain load balancing — closed without implementation in favor of a follow-on issue. `evergreen` (633 words)
- [[prefix-cache-aware-routing-llm-d]] — Prefix-Cache Aware Routing | llm-d — llm-d docs (v0.9) describe two prefix-cache-aware routing implementations in the EPP Router. (1) Approximate: EPP-local, character-based hashing, no ZMQ/network connectivity to model servers required — 'no need for a tokenizer sidecar; no network connectivity required to model servers (ZMQ)'. (2) Precise: token-producer plugin calls vLLM's HTTP /v1/completions/render endpoint for exact token IDs; model servers emit KVEvents over ZMQ on block add/evict; the KV-Cache Indexer (EPP Data Layer component) subscribes to these events and builds a global index of which token blocks reside on which Pods. Precise mode explicitly 'requires model server support for emitting KV-cache events' and ZMQ connectivity, and natively supports Prefill/Decode disaggregation by identifying specific blocks for transfer. `evergreen` (803 words)
- [[prefix-cache-aware-routing-llm-d-2]] — Prefix-Cache Aware Routing | llm-d — Dev-version doc contrasting llm-d's two prefix-cache-aware routing implementations. Approximate implementation: lightweight, no external deps; the approx-prefix-cache-producer approximates tokens via character-to-token ratios, splits prompts into fixed-size blocks (e.g., 16-token-equivalent chunks), builds a rolling hash chain, and the EPP keeps an in-memory LRU index of which prefix hashes were recently routed to which pods, updating its own index optimistically after each routing decision (no KVEvents or model-server integration required); con is it can diverge from actual server state on eviction. Precise implementation: 100% accuracy via the token-producer plugin, which calls vLLM's HTTP render endpoint (/v1/completions/render, typically a loopback sidecar; legacy gRPC-over-UDS tokenizer is deprecated) for exact token IDs, combined with real-time KVEvents over ZMQ and the KV-Cache Indexer's global block-key-to-pod index; natively supports Prefill/Decode disaggregation by identifying specific transferable blocks, at the cost of requiring the render endpoint and ZMQ connectivity. Includes a comparison table (precision, state source, dependencies, use case, P/D disagg support) and notes the Precise path composes with both the KV-Cache Indexer and KV Offloading to manage cache across accelerator/host memory boundaries. `evergreen` (817 words)
- [[prefix-aware-routing-ray-2580]] — Prefix-aware routing — Ray 2.58.0 — Ray Serve docs (alpha API, v2.58.0) for PrefixCacheAffinityRouter, a comparison implementation of cache-aware routing to llm-d's KV-cache-aware scheduler. Explicitly builds in a load-imbalance escape hatch that llm-d's design documents reference conceptually: the router first checks whether queue-length difference across replicas exceeds an imbalanced_threshold (default: infinity, i.e. disabled unless configured); only when load is judged balanced does it proceed to prefix-tree-based routing. When routing on prefix, match rate >=10% (match_rate_threshold, default 0.1) routes to the replica with highest prefix match for cache hits; match rate <10% deliberately falls back to the replica with LOWEST prefix cache utilization to spread cold traffic and increase utilization; no prefix data falls back to Power-of-Two-Choices. When load actually is imbalanced (queue-length gap exceeds threshold), the router abandons cache locality entirely and reverts to plain Power-of-Two-Choices, prioritizing load balancing over cache hit rate. The prefix tree itself is a distributed, detached Ray actor with configurable eviction (default eviction_threshold_chars=400,000, eviction_target_chars=360,000 (doc example config differs: 500,000/400,000), eviction_interval_secs=10) to bound memory and approximate the underlying vLLM engine's own eviction policy. Requires vLLM's enable_prefix_caching=True (Automatic Prefix Caching, APC) to have any effect. `evergreen` (730 words)
- [[prefixwall-mitigating-prefix-caching-side-channels-in-shared-llm-systems]] — PrefixWall: Mitigating Prefix Caching Side Channels in Shared LLM Systems — Presents PrefixWall (IMDEA Software Institute / UPM), a vLLM-based system defending multi-tenant Automatic Prefix Caching (APC) against timing side-channel prompt-stealing attacks without the throughput loss of prior full user-level isolation defenses. Rather than isolating entire users, PrefixWall tags each KV cache entry with two metadata fields (immutable OwnerID set at allocation, and a mutable AttackFlag) and runs a Detector that flags a prefix as isolated the first time it is hit by a non-owning user; subsequent non-owner requests are blocked from reusing anything beyond the flagged boundary and are recomputed, while the original owner can continue extending their own path unrestricted even through flagged nodes. A second component, the Activator, continuously computes the Kernel Density Estimation (KDE) overlap between the hit-latency and miss-latency TTFT distributions over a sliding window; when overlap exceeds an admin-set threshold theta (meaning hits/misses are statistically indistinguishable, e.g. under high load/batching), isolation is deactivated entirely to preserve cache reuse, since the side channel is not exploitable in that regime. The paper gives a formal security guarantee: for any probing sequence targeting a secret entry with a non-empty known prefix, if the prefix's final entry is already flagged OR the attacker's first guess is wrong, all subsequent probes from a different principal return misses — with two explicit gaps: (1) no protection for attacks on the very first prompt entry (no parent node to flag; mitigated in practice because OpenAI-style deployments only cache prefixes above ~1024 tokens and hit/miss distributions overlap heavily for single-entry prefixes even at large model sizes), and (2) no protection if an attacker guesses the secret correctly on the very first probe. Evaluated on an A100 (40GB) running vLLM 0.8.5 (block size 16) across nine models (0.5B-13B, Gemma3/Llava/Llama2/Qwen2/Qwen2.5 families), PrefixWall achieves up to 70% higher cache reuse and 30% lower inference latency than full user-level-isolation baselines, with negligible runtime/memory overhead, while empirically closing the measured timing leak across cross-user requests. `evergreen` (14619 words)
- [[private-llm-inference-on-consumer-blackwell-gpus]] — Private LLM Inference on Consumer Blackwell GPUs: — Independent researchers benchmark self-hosted LLM inference for SMEs using vLLM (PagedAttention) as the serving engine, running four open-weight models (Qwen3-8B, Gemma3-12B, Gemma3-27B, GPT-OSS-20B) under multiple quantization formats (BF16, W4A16, NVFP4, MXFP4) across three workload classes: RAG, multi-LoRA agentic serving, and high-concurrency APIs, measured with AIPerf. NOTE ON SCOPE: this paper's primary content is GPU hardware selection/benchmarking across consumer NVIDIA Blackwell cards, which is explicitly OUT OF SCOPE per this research's hardware exclusion; this note captures only the software/operating-decision economics (self-hosted vs. cloud API), not the hardware comparisons themselves.

SOFTWARE/ECONOMICS FINDINGS RELEVANT TO THE SELF-HOST-VS-CLOUD-API DECISION: self-hosted inference achieves cost parity with commercial cloud APIs within 1-4 months at moderate usage (30M tokens/day), then operates at 40-200x lower electricity-only cost than budget-tier cloud APIs (self-hosted: /bin/bash.001-0.04/million tokens, electricity only; vs. 3,000-15,000x cheaper than frontier-model API pricing). The authors' break-even formula is TBE = Chw / (Vdaily x (Pcloud - Pself)) -- hardware cost divided by daily token volume times the per-token price gap. Worked examples at 30M tokens/day: vs. GPT-5 nano (/bin/bash.23/MTok) break-even is ~292 days; vs. Claude Opus 4.5 (5/MTok) break-even is ~4 days. At lower volume (1M tokens/day) break-even extends proportionally to ~8,772 days vs. GPT-5 nano -- i.e., the self-host decision is strongly volume-dependent, consistent with the utilization-dependence finding in the Pegatron coding-agent economics paper (inference-economics-of-enterprise-coding-agents) in this same batch. RAG workloads cost more per token (/bin/bash.03/MTok at 8k context, /bin/bash.14/MTok at 32k context) than simple API-style workloads, extending break-even 5-30x, though self-hosted RAG remains cost-competitive with budget-tier cloud APIs. These break-even figures use electricity-only self-hosted cost (not fully amortized TCO including depreciation/ops/multi-tenancy overhead), which likely understates true self-hosted cost relative to the amortized-TCO framing used elsewhere in this corpus. `review` (10058 words)
- [[production-metrics-vllm]] — Production Metrics - vLLM — vLLM exposes a number of metrics that can be used to monitor the health of the system. These metrics are exposed via ... `review` (1951 words)
- [[production-grade-llm-inference-at-scale-with-kserve-llm-d-and-vllm-llm-d]] — Production-Grade LLM Inference at Scale with KServe, llm-d, and vLLM | llm-d — llm-d blog post documenting Tesla's production adoption of KServe + llm-d + vLLM at scale (with Red Hat collaboration: Sergey Bekkerman, Greg Pereira, Robert Shaw et al. from Red Hat; Scott Cabrinha, Sai Krishna from Tesla). Tesla's initial naive vLLM-in-StatefulSet deployment hit three operational walls: NFS storage drag for hundreds-of-GB safetensors, node-to-pod affinity lock-in from local LVM PVs requiring manual PVC deletion on hardware failure, and naive round-robin load balancing that 'fails to utilize the critical KV-cache on the GPU.' Switching to KServe's LLMInferenceService/LLMInferenceConfig CRDs plus Envoy + Envoy AI Gateway + Gateway API Inference Extension enabled prefix-cache-aware routing. Reports a concrete production result: 'a 3x improvement in output tokens/s and a 2x reduction in time to first token (TTFT) after enabling prefix-cache aware routing,' measured serving Llama 3.1 70B on 4x AMD MI300X GPUs with tensor-parallel-size=4, gpu-memory-utilization=0.90, --max-model-len=65536. Also lists upstream KServe contributions from this production hardening (kserve#4901, #4900, #4898, #4899 feature requests; #4970 making storageInitializer optional for RunAI Model Streamer; #4886 adding support for the latest Gateway API Inference Extension). `evergreen` (961 words)
- [[productionising-large-language-models-in-government-by-ng-shangru-ai-practice-an]] — Productionising Large Language Models in Government | by Ng Shangru | AI Practice and Data Engineering Practice, GovTech | Medium — Detailed first-person engineering account (15-min read, Nov 2023, by Ng Shangru, SWE at GovTech, published under GovTech Singapore's 'AI Practice and Data Engineering Practice' Medium publication) of the Whole-of-Government (WOG) NLP team's roughly year-long journey hosting production-grade LLMs on-premise/in Government-on-Commercial-Cloud (GCC) for the Singapore government, written to avoid dependence on OpenAI/ChatGPT (US-hosted, proprietary, data-residency risk). Covers three serving-stack generations: (1) BLOOM-176B on AWS using 5x g4dn.metal (Nvidia T4, 16GB each) orchestrated via Ray + Alpa, constrained because model layer count must divide evenly into instance count (so only 1/2/5/7/10/14/35/70 instances work) and costing ~0,000 USD/month to run 24/7, with only 47% single-call GPU utilization and throughput degrading as concurrency rose; (2) a move to GCP A100 80GB nodes and HuggingFace's transformers-bloom-inference (using Accelerate + DeepSpeed), which quadrupled throughput versus Alpa (4.06 vs 2.23/1.17 tokens/sec) but still took 238s to load the model; (3) adoption of HuggingFace's Text Generation Inference (TGI, released July 2023) serving Llama-2-70b-chat-hf, which fully saturates GPU even on single calls via internal queuing/batching and gRPC to model shards, paired with a Qdrant + OpenSearch vector store and a self-hosted gte-large embedding model (replacing OpenAI's text-ada-embedding-002) served via Gunicorn/FastAPI with sentence-transformers, all fronted by a Chainlit UI with FastAPI backend. The on-premise A100 server (obtained via the Government Infrastructure Group) runs fully offline RAG serving Secret-classified workloads; a separate GCC setup handles Confidential workloads. Gives exact sizing math (1B params = 2GB storage/2GB GPU RAM at bf16; Llama-2-70B needs ~140GB, though their deployment used ~272GB GPU RAM at max context/concurrency) and names forward-looking exploration of vLLM, llama.cpp, exllama, ollama, quantization (GPTQ/NF4/GGUF/AWQ/EETQ), and NVIDIA Triton Inference Server as a possible TGI alternative. `review` (3567 words)
- [[proposal-to-move-epp-and-bbr-to-the-llm-d-repo-issue-2430-kubernetes-sigsgateway]] — Proposal to Move EPP and BBR to the llm-d Repo · Issue #2430 · kubernetes-sigs/gateway-api-inference-extension — GitHub issue #2430 (kubernetes-sigs/gateway-api-inference-extension, opened Feb 26 2026 by Google's ahg-g, closed completed May 13 2026) -- the single most load-bearing primary-source document in this batch for the governance question: it is the actual proposal and multi-week contested debate over moving the reference Endpoint Picker (EPP) implementation and the Body-Based Router (BBR) OUT of the neutral kubernetes-sigs org and INTO the llm-d project's own repo, with the InferencePool API, EPP protocol, and conformance tests staying in k8s/Gateway API.

Problem statement from the proposal author: two repos (upstream IGW and llm-d-inference-scheduler) were each shipping an EPP image, with 'advanced plugins (like P/D and precise kv-cache aware scheduling)... mostly driven by the llm-d project,' creating 'fragmented code and duplicate maintenance and documentation' and confusing both developers and end users about 'what to put where.' The rationale frames IGW as three separable layers -- (1) the InferencePool API + endpoint-picker protocol that stays neutral and Gateway-API-owned, (2) a conformant gateway ecosystem (named explicitly: GKE Gateway, Istio, kGateway, NGINX Gateway Fabric), and (3) the EPP implementation itself, proposed to move -- with the explicit analogy 'why should k8s pick a winning EPP?' the same way it doesn't pick a winning proxy.

Captures a sharp, named, unresolved governance dispute that is direct evidence of what 'neutral OSS infrastructure' costs an adopter: Microsoft engineer keithmattix (Istio maintainer) argues the move destroys GAIE's core value proposition -- 'What LLM-D doesn't provide that GAIE did is neutral governance. Other inference frameworks that want to create an EPP would be faced with starting from scratch or importing a competitor's (i.e. llm-d's) EPP' -- and explicitly names NVIDIA Dynamo and Ray/KubeRay as non-llm-d inference frameworks with a stake in EPP neutrality ('the latter [dynamo] already has an EPP derived from the library that's present in GAIE'). llm-d maintainer kfswain counters that llm-d itself is becoming foundation-governed ('soon to be in the same foundation as K8s') and that a lightweight reference EPP remains for conformance testing, so 'anyone can implement their own EPP and either use the lightweight version or the llm-d EPP as a reference.' A third commenter (dims) flags mid-thread that NVIDIA Dynamo is independently vendoring its own EPP fork with 3 added plugins (github.com/ai-dynamo/dynamo/tree/main/deploy/inference-gateway/epp) rather than depending on the shared upstream code -- direct evidence that major vendors are already forking/duplicating EPP logic rather than trusting a single shared implementation.

Resolution: the proposal author confirms 'The move happened' (May 13 2026) and closes the issue as completed. A late comment (May 26, after close) flags a concrete regression risk the debate anticipated: 'How can we still use envoy gateway? Llm-d does not reference it as supports igw, seems pretty regression if that is the case' -- i.e., Envoy Gateway support for the EPP protocol may have been weakened or lost in the migration, an unresolved/open concern as of the note's capture date. Also surfaces, in passing, that the EPP spec added a conformance-test MUST requirement (an Envoy metadata field, x-gateway-endpoint-served or similar) before any open-source gateway implementation other than Istio could actually satisfy it -- evidence that conformance requirements have at times been driven by one implementation's needs ahead of the broader ecosystem being able to comply. `review` (3391 words)
- [[published-as-a-conference-paper-at-iclr-2023]] — Published as a conference paper at ICLR 2023 — GPTQ (Frantar, Ashkboos, Hoefler, Alistarh; IST Austria/ETH Zurich/NeuralMagic; ICLR 2023, arXiv:2210.17323v2). The baseline post-training weight quantization method that AWQ, llm-compressor, and every production quantization guide in this batch define themselves against. GPTQ is a one-shot, layer-wise weight quantization method based on approximate second-order (Hessian) information: it quantizes GPT-family models to 3- or 4-bit weights with claimed "negligible accuracy degradation" relative to FP16, more than doubling the compression-vs-accuracy tradeoff of prior round-to-nearest (RTN) methods at the time. Headline result: GPTQ quantizes OPT-175B or BLOOM-176B in approximately four GPU-hours, enabling OPT-175B to run for the first time on a single NVIDIA A100 (or two NVIDIA A6000 GPUs) instead of a multi-GPU cluster, with measured end-to-end inference speedups of approximately 3.25x on A100 and 4.5x on A6000 from custom GPU kernels that accelerate memory loading of the compressed weights. The paper is explicit about its own limitations, directly relevant to why AWQ and later methods were developed: GPTQ "currently does not provide speedups for the actual multiplications, due to the lack of hardware support for mixed-precision operands," and it does not quantize activations (weight-only, W4A16-style), leaving that to orthogonal techniques. This is the mechanism AWQ's authors cite when arguing GPTQ's Hessian-based reconstruction can overfit the calibration set -- GPTQ's own paper does not test or claim out-of-distribution generalization, which is the specific gap AWQ's non-reconstruction, activation-aware scaling approach was designed to close. Code: github.com/IST-DASLab/gptq. `review` (8923 words)
- [[published-as-a-conference-paper-at-iclr-2026]] — Published as a conference paper at ICLR 2026 — DualMap (ICLR 2026, Huawei + HUST) formalizes the cache-affinity vs. load-balancing conflict in KV-cache-aware LLM serving routers: pure cache-affinity routing (e.g., consistent hashing on prefix) causes severe load imbalance under skewed prompt popularity, overloading nodes serving hot prefixes while cold-prefix nodes idle, driving TTFT SLO violations; pure load-balancing (Least Loaded) scatters shared prefixes and destroys cache hit rate. Prior systems (Mooncake, Preble, Dynamo) only partially resolve this by routing a subset of requests by cache-affinity and the rest by load, operating in a single mapping space. DualMap instead hashes each request's partial prefix with two independent hash functions to get two candidate instances (power-of-two-choices), then picks the better one via three mechanisms: SLO-aware routing (switch to load-aware once predicted TTFT exceeds SLO), hotspot-aware rebalancing (migrate requests off overloaded instances), and dual-hash-ring scaling (avoid full remap on cluster resize). On Mooncake's real-world Conversation and Tool&Agent traces with Qwen2.5-7B/14B on Ascend NPU clusters, DualMap raises effective request capacity (fraction of requests meeting 5s TTFT SLO) by up to 125% and goodput by 16.7-48% versus the best baseline (Preble/Min TTFT/Cache Affinity), with Cache Affinity alone collapsing under skewed Tool&Agent prefixes due to load imbalance. `evergreen` (12920 words)
- [[pull-requests-llm-dllm-d-router-github]] — Pull requests · llm-d/llm-d-router · GitHub — GitHub PR search on llm-d/llm-d-router for 'consul OR etcd OR slurm OR ray discovery', fetched 11 Sep 2026 (first page, ~20 results, GitHub free-text OR semantics). Chronologically newest discovery-adjacent PRs: #2716 (Sep 6 2026, 'Expose session-managed prefix-cache lookup from KV events', area/datalayer), #2701 (Sep 4 2026, 'Add discovery-backed vLLM render balancing', area/epp+scheduling), #2642 (closed Sep 7 2026, 'docs: describe file discovery validation behavior' -- FileDiscovery docs, not a new backend), #2612/#2611 (Aug 28 2026, EPP harness/discovery test-framework exports, not new plugins), #2604 (merged Sep 2 2026, 'docs: document that endpoint discovery honours custom pod readinessGates' -- confirms current EndpointDiscovery is pod-readiness-gate aware, i.e. still Kubernetes-Pod-centric), #2501 (opened Aug 21 2026, still open, 'feat: pod watch peer discovery plugin' -- k8s-native, see separate note), #2445 (Aug 18 2026, 'Honor podLabelSelector for KV-event subscriptions'), #2434 (merged Aug 25 2026, discovery consolidation -- see separate note), #2341 (merged Aug 16 2026, peer discovery infra -- see separate note). CRITICAL ABSENCE: none of the returned PRs, despite the search including 'consul', 'etcd', 'slurm', and 'ray' as literal terms, actually implements a Consul, etcd, Slurm, or Ray discovery backend -- every discovery-related PR in this list targets files (FileDiscovery), Kubernetes Pods/EndpointSlices/readinessGates, or KV-event-driven cache lookups. No non-Kubernetes discovery plugin has been proposed or merged in llm-d-router as of 11 Sep 2026. `evergreen` (1514 words)
- [[pull-dont-recompute-peer-to-peer-kv-cache-sharing-in-llm-d-llm-d]] — Pull, Don't Recompute: Peer-to-Peer KV Cache Sharing in llm-d | llm-d — Introduces P2P (peer-to-peer) KV cache sharing in llm-d, an opt-in scheduling mechanism that adds a third option beyond 'wait for the cache owner' or 'recompute': route to the best endpoint for load and copy (pull) the finished KV over the network. Any vLLM instance can act as Consumer (pulls matching KV blocks from a peer instead of recomputing) or Producer (serves blocks from its CPU offload tier). Mechanically, the router's Endpoint Picker (EPP) compares the peer with the most cached tokens against the load-selected destination; if the peer holds enough more cached prefix (minCachedTokenDelta) to beat the measured transfer crossover, it names that peer as KV source, and the producer writes matching blocks over NIXL (CPU-to-CPU, no GPU compute involved) — a normal cache miss still falls back to full computation. Transfer-vs-recompute economics were measured directly: on gpt-oss-120B/H200, pull beat recompute at every measured prefix length from 2K tokens (-56%) to 49K tokens (-88%); on a 753B GLM MoE testbed (KV footprint ~93KB/token) the crossover was ~8.7K tokens, with pull 61% faster than recompute at 24K tokens. Production-shaped benchmark: on GLM-5.2-FP8 across 32x H200 P/D-disaggregated at concurrency 64 (AIPerf trace replay, SemiAnalysis Weka coding-agent corpus), P2P improved successful throughput by 4.6% with approximate routing and 9.7-11.1% with precise routing (average +9.6% successful throughput, +11.8% input-token throughput across 3 repeats), while median TTFT fell up to 25%. A document-Q&A workload (192 48K-token docs, 128 active conversations, gpt-oss-120B/16 H200 pods) showed load-aware placement + P2P beating pure prefix affinity by +35% throughput and lower p99 TTFT (16.6s vs 25.2s) at the cost of higher median TTFT (0.6s vs 0.3s). For P/D session continuity, P2P let a prefill worker pull decode-generated history instead of recomputing it: on an agentic-serving workload (24 conversations, prompts averaging 61.9K tokens), median TTFT dropped 6.3x (6.83s to 1.09s) and throughput rose 50%. P2P is off by default because the crossover threshold is deployment- and fabric-specific and must be calibrated (recommended via the p2p-kv-cache-sharing guide) to avoid pulls costing more than recomputation. `evergreen` (3355 words)
- [[readmemd]] — README.md — README for llm-d-router's built-in 'file-discovery' EndpointDiscovery plugin (type 'file-discovery', interface EndpointDiscovery, disabled by default). Loads a YAML/JSON endpoints file (cap 1 MiB) at startup and optionally hot-reloads via fsnotify (watchFile: true), reconciling adds/deletes through DiscoveryNotifier. Entry schema is exactly {name (required, unique), namespace (optional, default 'default'), address (required, must be literal IPv4 -- hostnames/IPv6 unsupported), port (required string 1-65535), labels (optional free-form map)} -- CRITICAL FOR GAP-5: there is no rankIndex/dp-rank/'llm-d.ai/rank' field anywhere in this schema, and cross-referencing plugin.go confirms the word 'rank' does not appear in the implementation at all. This means every endpoint discovered by the built-in file-discovery plugin gets EndpointMetadata.RankIndex left at its Go zero-value (0) -- multi-DP-rank backends (vLLM data-parallel deployments needing multiple KV-event ZMQ ports per pod) are NOT supported out of the box by this plugin; a custom discovery plugin author would have to add their own rank field to the entry schema and set RankIndex themselves, since neither a label convention nor the shipped schema carries it. Explicitly documents what breaks running EPP without a Kubernetes controller manager: 'the InferenceModelRewrite and InferenceObjective reconcilers do not run, and any k8s-notification-source plugin in the data layer config will not bind. The runner emits a startup log naming the inactive features.' Metrics are scraped from address:port only (no separate metrics endpoint support) in single-cluster file-discovery. A distinct 'multicluster-file-discovery' variant exists for cluster-scoped discovery, allowing RFC-1123 hostnames (not just IPv4) since it discovers whole peer clusters via gateway host, and supports per-entry metricsAddress/metricsPort labels for out-of-band metrics scraping. `evergreen` (670 words)
- [[recover-kvblockindex-from-a-disruptive-event-issue-372-llm-dllm-d-kv-cache]] — Recover kvBlock.Index from a disruptive event · Issue #372 · llm-d/llm-d-kv-cache — GitHub issue #372 (opened Mar 1, 2026, llm-d/llm-d-kv-cache) documents a real bug in kvBlock.Index recovery after a disruptive event (e.g. GAIE/EPP pod restart clears the in-memory index while vLLM pods still hold the actual KV blocks). Sequence: after restart, a BlockStored event for a child block [B1]B3 arrives whose parent B1 is not in the freshly-cleared index, so pkg/kvevents/pool.go:297 fails with 'Failed to get request key for parent block' / 'engine key not found' and DROPS the block -- meaning any descendant blocks (e.g. [B3]B4) are dropped too, since their parent chain was never captured. Impact: KV-cache-aware routing silently loses visibility into cache-resident blocks after any indexer restart until the affected prefix ages out and is regenerated. Root-cause discussion: vLLM's KV-event replay buffer holds the last 10,000 events and could theoretically rebuild the hash chain on subscriber reconnect, but (per RishabhSaini, Jun 2026) a bug in vLLM's replay event protocol prevented this, requiring vllm-project/vllm#45177 (include topic frame in replay responses) to merge first. As an interim/permanent fallback (PR #663, merged), blocks with unrecoverable parents are now indexed using EmptyBlockHash instead of being dropped -- the system self-heals as new requests rebuild correct hash chains from scratch, and tail-first eviction lets stale malformed entries expire naturally. A full fix (PR #691) adds a replay-buffer consumer for KV-event index recovery on subscriber connect/reconnect. `evergreen` (1021 words)
- [[reducing-cold-start-latency-for-llm-inference-with-nvidia-runai-model-streamer-n]] — Reducing Cold Start Latency for LLM Inference with NVIDIA Run:ai Model Streamer | NVIDIA Technical Blog — NVIDIA Developer Blog primary engineering post (Sep 16, 2025; Omer Dayan, Noa Neria, Ekin Karabulut, all ex-Run:ai/now NVIDIA) introducing and benchmarking NVIDIA Run:ai Model Streamer (github.com/run-ai/runai-model-streamer), an open-source Python SDK with a C++ backend that concurrently reads model weights from storage and streams them directly into GPU memory, bypassing the sequential read-to-CPU-then-copy-to-GPU path. PROVENANCE CORRECTION: the secondary dreaming.press article's claim that Model Streamer 'sustains around 80 Gbps' is NOT substantiated anywhere in this primary source -- this post reports only GiB/s storage-throughput figures (~1 GiB/s on GP3 SSD, ~2 GiB/s observed on IO2 SSD, close to but below its 4 GiB/s theoretical max) and absolute load times in seconds, never a Gbps figure; treat the 80 Gbps claim as unverified/likely fabricated by the secondary source. Benchmark setup: Llama 3 8B (15GB, single safetensors file) on an AWS g5.12xlarge (1x A10G GPU used), vLLM 0.5.5, Model Streamer 0.6.0, Tensorizer 2.9.0, compared against the default HF Safetensors Loader and CoreWeave Tensorizer across three storage tiers. Concrete results: on GP3 SSD, raising concurrency from 1 to 16 cut Model Streamer's load time from 47.56s to 14.34s (saturating GP3's ~1GiB/s ceiling, matched by Tensorizer at 16.11s/16 workers, both ~3.3x faster than Safetensors' 47.99s baseline); on IO2 SSD, Model Streamer hit 7.53s at concurrency 8 (~6x faster than Safetensors' 47s), Tensorizer 10.36s at 8 workers; on Amazon S3 (which the default Safetensors Loader cannot read at all), Model Streamer reached 4.88s at concurrency 32 vs Tensorizer's best of 37.36s at 16 workers -- Model Streamer's largest relative advantage is specifically on S3, where it is the only loader with native object-store support. End-to-end vLLM engine-ready time (not just weight load) showed Model Streamer roughly halving total readiness versus Safetensors: 35.08s vs 66.13s on GP3, 28.28s vs 62.69s on IO2, and 23.18s on S3 vs Tensorizer's 65.18s. Notes a measurement artifact: repeated S3 benchmarks within the same session showed anomalously fast 'warm cache' results, so the authors enforced a 3-minute cooldown between S3 runs to preserve genuine cold-start conditions. Key architectural features: multi-threaded concurrent reading with balanced workload distribution across threads, native Safetensors format compatibility (no weight conversion/re-serialization step required, unlike Tensorizer which needs its own serialization step), and direct integration as a loader option inside vLLM and TGI. `review` (4169 words)
- [[refresh-support-for-kgateway-agentgateway-gie-and-gw-api-by-danehans-pull-reques]] — Refresh support for kgateway agentgateway GIE and GW API by danehans · Pull Request #421 · llm-d/llm-d · GitHub — llm-d PR #421 (danehans/solo.io, opened Oct 31 2025, targeted at llm-d v0.4/v0.5 release tracker, fixes issue #399) is llm-d's official gateway-provider refresh: it deprecates the legacy kgateway integration path (marking it for future removal, migrating remaining kgateway users to kgateway-dev's agentgateway charts v2.2.1) and adds first-class agentgateway v1.0.0 support across llm-d's provider assets, guides, recipes, and CI, while bumping GAIE (gateway-api-inference-extension) to v1.4.0 and Gateway API to v1.5.1. This is direct, dated evidence of llm-d's own strategic direction: agentgateway (not kgateway or generic Envoy Gateway) is becoming the reference/first-class proxy for llm-d's GIE-based routing, which matters for anyone building a router customization on top of llm-d components since agentgateway is where the standalone-EPP passthrough destinationMode work (PR #1658, #1429) landed. The PR stalled across multiple release cycles (v0.4 -> missed -> targeted for v0.5) due to reviewer bandwidth and needing a rebase; a related llm-d-infra issue 'Migrate kgateway Support to agentgateway' (llm-d-incubation/llm-d-infra#269) and a kgateway-side test issue 'agentgateway: test llm-d support' (kgateway-dev/kgateway#12451) show the migration was tracked in parallel across three repos. `evergreen` (2825 words)
- [[release-v0110-llm-dllm-d-router-github]] — Release v0.11.0 · llm-d/llm-d-router · GitHub — llm-d-router v0.11.0 release notes (27 Sep 2026): ships NEW scoring and filtering plugins plus a structured Helm pluginsConfig surface — the primary evidence falsifying a maintainer's earlier warning that llm-d might remove its EPP plugin extensibility model. `review` (5394 words)
- [[release-v0110-llm-dllm-d-router-github-2]] — Release v0.11.0 · llm-d/llm-d-router · GitHub — llm-d-router v0.11.0 release notes (published 2026-09-27), the EPP/router component that merged what used to be llm-d-inference-scheduler. Shows the plugin extensibility framework being actively extended, not removed: new scorer/filter plugins added this release (latency-observation-scorer for TTFT-based ranking, context-length-aware scorer, turn-priority fairness strategy, endpoint-attribute weight scorer), Helm gains a structured router.epp.pluginsConfig and per-EPP deploymentStrategy. Individual legacy plugins were deprecated/removed and replaced by newer ones in the SAME framework (pd-profile-handler -> disagg-profile-handler; precise-prefix-cache-scorer -> precise-prefix-cache-producer + prefix-cache-scorer; by-label/by-label-selector -> label-selector-filter; tokenizer -> token-producer), and endpoint-attribute-filter/scorer plugins now require a separate 'producer' parameter (breaking change for out-of-tree plugins using the old combined Name/Producer form). Stricter startup checks now fail on invalid plugin ordering. This is the most recent llm-d-router release as of fetch (2026-10-10); no later release exists. `review` (5394 words)
- [[release-v130-kubernetes-sigsgateway-api-inference-extension-github]] — Release v1.3.0 · kubernetes-sigs/gateway-api-inference-extension · GitHub — GIE v1.3.0 release notes, released by @kfswain on Jan 21 (2026), tag signed with verified SSH signature. Noteworthy section: (1) LoRA Syncer feature deprecated — the lora-syncer image will not ship with this or future releases (similar functionality moves to vLLM's filesystem LoRA resolver plugin), old images kept indefinitely for model servers using the discrete LoRA management endpoints, syncer code slated for full removal in the next release; (2) Flow Control gained Scale-from/to-Zero support, allowing requests to reach an EPP with zero model-serving endpoints behind it while emitting metrics an autoscaler can use to scale the pool back up; (3) 'Standalone EPP: This functionality allows the EPP to be deployed as a proxy, all contained within a single pod. This is achieved by the Envoy proxy having EPP as a sidecar container. This feature was developed for batch inference scenarios, and is currently considered experimental' — this is the first appearance of standalone mode, introduced as experimental at v1.3.0. Also fixed the approximate prefix-cache scorer's interaction with the llm-d P/D (prefill/decode disaggregation) setup. Merged PR list includes 'Execute prepare data plugins in topological order of data dependencies' (#1878, @rahulgurnani) and 'Implement EPP Plugins by datalayer objects' (#1901, @elevran) — early datalayer-based plugin refactor work, and 'SLO Aware Routing Sidecar + Plugin EPP Integration and Helm Deployment' (#1839, @BenjaminBraunDev). `evergreen` (1989 words)
- [[release-v140-kubernetes-sigsgateway-api-inference-extension-github]] — Release v1.4.0 · kubernetes-sigs/gateway-api-inference-extension · GitHub — GIE v1.4.0 release notes, released by @danehans on Mar 20 (2026). Release Highlights: standalone chart work landed and is included in release artifacts; conformance split into its own Go module; InferencePool/Helm/gRPC improvements including appProtocol, FailOpen, and ALPN h2; ongoing work in flow control, BBR, predicted latency, and datalayer internals. - [[releasing-ai-features-without-breaking-production-shadow-mode-canary-deployments]] — Releasing AI Features Without Breaking Production: Shadow Mode, Canary Deployments, and A/B Testing for LLMs — Named-practitioner engineering blog post (Tian Pan, tianpan.co, April 9 2026) giving a first-person technical treatment of shadow mode, canary deployment, A/B testing, and feature flags for LLM model rollouts -- the direct primary source underlying the Nadir vendor blog's numbers. Argues LLM releases differ structurally from code deploys on four axes: (1) non-determinism is irreducible even at temperature=0/greedy sampling because GPU floating-point ops aren't strictly associative and batch-size variance changes rounding -- cites documented accuracy variations of up to 15% across runs with identical inputs; (2) small changes (prompt reword, fine-tune data update, model version bump) have large, benchmark-invisible blast radii; (3) feedback is delayed (hours/days via support tickets, not immediate 500 errors); (4) cost is a rollout variable, not a constant (a 20%-better model might be 3x more expensive per call).
