Shadow mode definition matches the secondary sources: duplicate traffic to current (serving) + candidate (silent) models, compare via automated LLM-judge criteria (factual accuracy, tone, task completion, format compliance) plus token-count/cost/latency diffs; recommends replaying last week's historical traffic through the candidate before touching production infra at all; roughly doubles inference spend during the test window; reserved for major changes (model upgrades, prompt restructuring, new tool schemas), not minor tweaks. Canary deployment: ramp 1% -> 5% -> 20% -> 50% -> 100% (as low as 0.1% for high-stakes), holding >=24-48 hours per stage; flags consistent user-to-cohort assignment at the SESSION level (not per-request) as a 'critical infrastructure requirement' to avoid jarring mid-conversation model switches; tracks LLM-specific canary metrics distinct from typical service rollouts: latency percentiles (p50/p95/p99, since LLM latency is highly skewed), cost per request, error AND refusal rates, output-length-distribution shifts (mode collapse or runaway verbosity), and user feedback signals (thumbs-down, regeneration requests, session abandonment). Names concrete automated rollback thresholds as non-optional: p99 latency +40%, refusal rate +5 points, or cost-per-request budget overrun trigger an automatic 100%-back-to-baseline rollback with no human intervention required. Directly addresses model-pinning: explicitly recommends pinning flags to an exact model version/digest ('gpt-4o-2024-11-20 is a stable flag target. gpt-4o-latest is not') because loosely-specified model flags create 'implicit rollouts that bypass all your careful canary and A/B infrastructure' as providers silently update weights behind a 'latest' alias. A/B testing section: explicit signals (thumbs up/down) cover only 2-5% of responses; implicit signals (regeneration requests, abandonment, follow-up clarifications) are the more reliable real-time leading indicator; statistical power for a 5% minimum detectable effect at 80% power/95% confidence requires 'tens of thousands of sessions per arm' given LLM non-determinism inflating variance; recommends pre-stratifying analysis by request type since a model can be simultaneously better at factual Q&A and worse at creative tasks. Prescribes a concrete five-stage rollout sequence (shadow -> canary@1% -> gradual ramp -> A/B at 50/50 -> kill switch always available, togglable in under a minute without a code deploy) and names the required infrastructure most teams underestimate: a percentage-based session-sticky request router, a near-real-time automated evaluation/judge pipeline (calibrated per use case), and explicit LLM-specific instrumentation (cost/request, output-length distribution, refusal rate, regeneration rate) beyond standard APM latency/error metrics. `review` (2531 words)
- [[request-scheduler-llm-d]] — Request Scheduler | llm-d — Primary llm-d dev-branch docs for the EPP Request Scheduler: a plugin-based Filter -> Score -> Pick pipeline organized into SchedulerProfiles. Final endpoint score = sum over scorers of (scorer_score in [0,1] * weight), e.g. (0.8*2.0)+(0.5*1.0)=2.1. Lists concrete plugins: filters (prefix-cache-affinity-filter with a 'TTFT load gate' to break stickiness, label-selector-filter, prefill/decode-endpoints-filter), scorers (kv-cache-utilization-scorer preferring lower KV utilization; prefix-cache-scorer scored by prefix match length, backed by either approx-prefix-cache-producer or the more precise precise-prefix-cache-producer that tracks real-time cache state via model-server events; load-aware and session-affinity scorers), and pickers (max-score default, random, weighted-random/lottery). Documents the two-profile ProfileHandler used for P/D disaggregation: decode endpoint returned as primary destination, prefill endpoint injected as a request header that the sidecar reads to fetch a remote prefill. Also documents the full EPP metrics surface (llm_d_epp_average_kv_cache_utilization, llm_d_epp_disagg_decision_total, llm_d_epp_prefix_indexer_hit_ratio, etc.). `evergreen` (2061 words)
- [[request-routing-ray-2580]] — Request routing — Ray 2.58.0 — Ray Serve LLM architecture doc confirming the default request router (used when prefix-aware routing is not configured) is plain Power-of-Two-Choices -- randomly sample two replicas, route to the one with fewer ongoing requests -- with PrefixCacheAffinityRouter as an opt-in alternative that explicitly falls back to Power-of-Two-Choices whenever replica load is imbalanced (queue-length difference exceeds threshold). This is comparison context confirming that even Ray's cache-aware router treats load-balance-not-cache-locality as the default/fallback behavior, i.e. prefix affinity is deliberately subordinate to load balancing rather than the reverse. Also documents two architectural patterns for building custom LLM-aware routers: a centralized singleton metric-store actor (consistent cluster-wide state, but a coordination bottleneck) versus decentralized per-router state. `evergreen` (1482 words)
- [[roadmap-sglang-router-issue-10341-sgl-projectsglang]] — Roadmap: SGLang Router · Issue #10341 · sgl-project/sglang — GitHub roadmap issue (opened Sep 11, 2025, child of sgl-project/sglang #7736 'Development Roadmap 2025 Q3') tracking planned work on the standalone SGLang Router — a comparison point for alternative prefix/cache-aware routing designs. Planned workstreams: (1) multi-model + model-aware load balancing, where model-aware selection (by priority, cost, input complexity, capabilities) runs as a pre-filter ahead of existing worker-selection policies (cache-aware, power-of-two, round-robin, random), via a policy registry supporting multiple radix trees per model family; (2) a lightweight in-memory state layer plus a 'naive gRPC mesh' for high availability across router replicas without external Redis/etcd, synchronizing rate-limit buckets, tenant/key radix trees, model registry, and per-model/per-mode worker states with eventual consistency and fast convergence (explicitly prioritizing low overhead over strong consistency); (3) a gRPC router core connecting directly to the SGLang scheduler for request admission, with the router itself running tokenizer, detokenizer, tool parser, and reasoning parser inline behind a unified OpenAI-compatible entrypoint, supporting both monolithic and PD-disaggregated (prefill/decode) serving modes; (4) a Responses API, MCP module, and data connectors for enterprise integration. Confirms SGLang's router-side cache-awareness is implemented via radix trees (plural, one per model family under the policy registry) synchronized across replicas rather than a centralized KV-event bus. `evergreen` (2638 words)
- [[role-based-access-controls-rbac-litellm]] — Role-based Access Controls (RBAC) | liteLLM — LiteLLM's RBAC doc, and a more precise statement of the OSS/Enterprise boundary than the multi-tenant-architecture page: Global Proxy Roles (proxy_admin, proxy_admin_viewer, internal_user, deprecated internal_user_viewer) are platform-wide and OSS. But Organization/Team Specific Roles -- org_admin AND team_admin -- are both explicitly flagged '(Premium Feature)' here, meaning delegated TEAM administration itself, not just the Organization layer, is gated behind Enterprise (this sharpens/partially contradicts the multi_tenant_architecture page's framing that 'Teams alone can serve as your top-level tenant boundary' in OSS -- OSS teams exist and can hold budgets, but a non-proxy-admin cannot administer even a single team's membership/keys/budgets without the premium team_admin role). Documents three virtual-key scoping patterns with concrete lifecycle rules: user-only keys (deleted when user is deleted, tracked to the user -- for individual developer use), team/service-account keys (team_id but no user_id, persist across team member turnover, tracked to the team only -- for production apps/CI pipelines), and user+team keys (both IDs, tracked to both, deleted with the user -- for individual accountability inside a team budget). Usage-dashboard visibility is scoped to four levels (personal/team/organization/global) gated by role; notes the Admin UI has no custom-dashboard capability, so per-user-within-team usage requires querying the management API directly or exporting via Prometheus/OpenTelemetry to Grafana. `review` (3854 words)
- [[router-configuration-vllm-projectproduction-stack-deepwiki]] — Router Configuration | vllm-project/production-stack | DeepWiki — DeepWiki AI-generated wiki (low-authority secondary summary, indexed 12 Mar 2026 at commit 99ab33ab) of vllm-project/production-stack's router configuration, sourced from actual repo files (helm/values.yaml, src/vllm_router/service_discovery.py, routing_logic.py, parser.py) with line-numbered citations back to GitHub — usable as a pointer to primary code, not as authoritative prose itself. Documents the router's ServiceDiscovery abstraction with exactly two implementations: K8sServiceDiscovery (watches pods via the K8s API, selectable as 'pod-ip' direct-to-pod or 'service-name' via K8s Service, label selector auto-derived from servingEngineSpec.labels) and StaticServiceDiscovery, explicitly labeled 'for non-Kubernetes deployments or testing' — configured via routerSpec.serviceDiscovery: static plus comma-separated staticBackends/staticModels/staticAliases URL lists, matched positionally. Routing logic is a separate pluggable axis from service discovery (roundrobin, session with consistent-hash ring + QPS fallback, kvaware requiring an LMCache controller port, prefixaware with tokenization-based prefix matching, disaggregated_prefill / disaggregated_prefill_orchestrated requiring prefill/decode model-label args) — meaning any routing policy can in principle be paired with static (non-K8s) discovery except where the policy's docs imply a specific engine-labeling scheme. Router exposes /health for liveness/readiness/startup probes and is architected as a FastAPI app whose initialize_all() wires ServiceDiscovery + RoutingLogic + EngineStatsScraper + RequestStatsMonitor independently — i.e., the discovery backend and the routing algorithm are decoupled components in this codebase's own architecture diagram. `evergreen` (1600 words)
- [[router-design-nvidia-dynamo-documentation]] — Router Design | NVIDIA Dynamo Documentation — NVIDIA Dynamo's KV Router design doc — the closest direct architectural comparator to llm-d's inference-scheduler/EPP. The router tracks three per-worker metrics: potential decode blocks, potential new prefill blocks (computed as (total input tokens - overlap_blocks*block_size)/block_size), and active requests. Block tracking splits into locally-tracked 'active decoding blocks' and globally-tracked 'cached blocks' maintained by a KvIndexer that builds a prefix tree from worker-reported KV events (published by a KVPublisher on block-store and block-evict). Routing cost formula: cost = prefill_load_scale * adjusted_prefill_blocks + potential_decode_blocks + active_request_blocks, where adjusted_prefill_blocks subtracts a decaying overlap_score_credit (weighted separately for device/host/disk cache tiers) from raw prefill blocks, clamped at zero; the worker with lowest cost is selected (or softmax-sampled if router_temperature > 0). Gives a concrete worked example: Worker 2 with 10 raw prefill blocks, 5 device-overlap blocks, 5 decode blocks scores cost=10 (5+5), beating Worker 1 (cost 18) and Worker 3 (cost 11). KV block management flow: tokenize -> partition into fixed-size blocks (16 or 64 tokens) -> hash each block (LoRA-adapter-aware) -> lookup -> allocate or evict (LRU/LFU) -> compute. Event transport uses fire-and-forget pub/sub (NATS Core or ZMQ) with per-worker monotonic event IDs and gap detection triggering full-state resync; multi-router deployments optionally exchange AddRequest/MarkPrefillCompleted/Free lifecycle events for cross-replica active-load estimates without synchronizing prefix-cache state itself. Explicitly credits vLLM's PagedAttention and SGLang's RadixAttention as the two techniques underlying its KV cache optimization. `evergreen` (2288 words)
- [[routing-agent-traffic-is-really-three-decisions-aks-engineering-blog]] — Routing agent traffic is really three decisions | AKS Engineering Blog — AKS Engineering Blog (Jun 29, 2026), validated end-to-end mid-2026 against Gateway API Inference Extension v1.0.0 and agentgateway v1.3.1. Directly demonstrates the query's exact layered pattern with a concrete, non-Envoy-AI-Gateway data plane: agentgateway (OpenAI-compatible, agent-native, open-source AI gateway) fronts BOTH a 'strong' Azure OpenAI backend (declared as an 'ai:' backend block with provider.azure{resourceName, resourceType, model=deployment-name}, auth via a route-level policy sending a static or workload-identity api-key) and a 'weak' self-hosted KAITO/vLLM pool reached through a 'service' backend carrying a backend-level policy 'inferenceRouting: {endpointPicker: {host: <epp-svc>:9002}, destinationMode: passthrough}' -- i.e. agentgateway itself speaks ext-proc to the Endpoint Picker rather than requiring a separate Gateway API Gateway object; the article explicitly notes this lets you 'drop the Gateway API gateway entirely: one data plane instead of two.' The three decisions are explicitly decomposed by owning layer: Layer 1 (semantic, RouteLLm mf-router, picks WHICH model by content, ~95% of GPT-4 quality at ~26% of GPT-4 calls per the RouteLLM paper), Layer 2 (agentgateway: auth/budgets/guardrails/provider-abstraction, policy only, never reads prompt content), Layer 3 (Gateway API Inference Extension EPP: WHICH replica, GPU state only -- KV-cache occupancy, queue depth). Gotchas documented: EPP must run --secure-serving=false (plaintext gRPC) to match agentgateway's ext-proc client and must stay cluster-internal; InferencePool targetPorts must be the vLLM container port (5000) not the Service port (80) because the EPP routes to pod IPs directly, bypassing the Service; InferencePool lives in inference.networking.k8s.io while InferenceObjective (successor to InferenceModel, carries only an integer priority, no criticality enum) lives in inference.networking.x-k8s.io. Explicitly states 'the ext-proc call to the Endpoint Picker... has no managed equivalent today, so that piece stays yours to run whichever gateway fronts it' -- i.e. even swapping in a managed AI gateway still requires self-operating the EPP integration. Notes RouteLLM's strong/weak upstreams are themselves just agentgateway routes (/strong, /weak paths), so the semantic router composes on top of the gateway rather than replacing it. `evergreen` (4192 words)
- [[running-large-scale-gpu-workloads-on-kubernetes-with-slurm-nvidia-technical-blog]] — Running Large-Scale GPU Workloads on Kubernetes with Slurm | NVIDIA Technical Blog — NVIDIA technical blog on the SlinkyProject slurm-operator represents the OPPOSITE integration direction from interLink/virtual-kubelet: rather than making Slurm compute look like virtual K8s pods, Slinky runs Slurm daemons (slurmctld, slurmdbd, slurmd, slurmrestd) themselves AS real Kubernetes pods via CRDs, with a companion slurm-bridge project letting Slurm act as a scheduler for native K8s pods. Because slurmd workers are genuine Pods under this model, they get normal Pod IPs, normal Service/EndpointSlice membership, and would be selectable by a stock InferencePool selector with zero router changes -- this is a viable alternative strategy to backend-side "look like a K8s object" hacks: instead of customizing the router, make the non-native compute (Slurm) actually become native K8s pods. NVIDIA reports production use at 1,000+ worker nodes and 8,000+ GPUs, GPU communication (NCCL all-reduce/all-gather) matching bare-metal Slurm performance, integration with the NVIDIA GPU Operator and DCGM per-job GPU metrics, and PodDisruptionBudget-protected rolling upgrades. One documented constraint: slurm-operator currently assumes one worker pod per node, which over-provisions for workloads that are exclusively single-node Slurm jobs (slurm-bridge suggested as the lighter-weight alternative for that case). Article carries an "AI-Generated Summary" disclosure box, indicating parts of the piece are AI-generated content from NVIDIA, not pure staff-authored prose -- flag when quoting. `evergreen` (3181 words)
- [[s-lora-serving-thousands-of-concurrent-lora-adapters]] — S-LORA: SERVING THOUSANDS OF CONCURRENT LORA ADAPTERS — S-LoRA (Sheng, Cao, Li, Hooper, Lee, Yang, Chou, Zhu, Zheng, Keutzer, Gonzalez, Stoica — UC Berkeley/Stanford/Shanghai Jiao Tong, MLSys 2024, arXiv 2311.03285) is the origin paper for scalable multi-LoRA-adapter serving, generalizing PagedAttention into 'Unified Paging.' Problem framing: the original LoRA paper's own recommended inference strategy — either merging adapter weights into the base model, or swapping adapters in/out by adding/subtracting LoRA deltas — breaks down at multi-tenant scale because merging requires one full-model-sized weight copy per adapter and swapping serializes execution across adapters, killing both memory efficiency and batched throughput when many adapters must be served concurrently (e.g., one adapter per customer/task in a shared fine-tuning service). S-LoRA's fix: never merge adapters into the base model. Instead, keep the base model's batchable GEMM (xW) separate from each request's much cheaper per-adapter computation (xAB), computed on-the-fly with custom CUDA kernels operating directly on non-contiguous, heterogeneous-rank adapter weights (no padding), fetching only the active adapters for the current batch from CPU/host main memory into GPU memory. Core memory-management contribution, Unified Paging, extends vLLM's PagedAttention block-based allocator to manage a single unified memory pool holding both KV-cache blocks (varying per sequence length) and adapter-weight blocks (varying per LoRA rank) side by side, which is the direct lineage from PagedAttention this batch is tracking. Also introduces a custom tensor-parallelism strategy (S-LoRA TP) that schedules communication for the small LoRA intermediate tensors so they fuse with the base model's existing communication, keeping added cross-GPU overhead minimal. Evaluated serving Llama-7B/13B/30B/70B: S-LoRA improves throughput up to 30x over HuggingFace PEFT and up to 4x over vLLM's then-existing naive multi-LoRA support, while increasing the number of concurrently served adapters by 'several orders of magnitude' (the paper's own phrase) — demonstrating that thousands of adapters can be served on a single GPU or across multiple GPUs with small overhead. This is the technique AIBrix and vLLM's own multi-LoRA serving support build on, per the orchestrator's framing of the batch; the paper positions LoRA-adapter-per-tenant serving as the enabling mechanism for large-scale customized fine-tuning-as-a-service offerings. Code released at github.com/S-LoRA/S-LoRA. `review` (11532 words)
- [[sglang-joins-pytorch-ecosystem-efficient-llm-serving-engine-pytorch]] — SGLang Joins PyTorch Ecosystem: Efficient LLM Serving Engine - PyTorch — PyTorch Foundation blog (March 19, 2025) announcing SGLang's integration into the PyTorch ecosystem. First-party description of SGLang's architecture: RadixAttention for prefix caching, zero-overhead CPU scheduler, continuous batching, token (paged) attention, speculative decoding, tensor parallelism, chunked prefill, structured outputs, and FP8/INT4/AWQ/GPTQ quantization, plus a separate 'flexible frontend language' for programmatic LLM app construction. States named production adoption: xAI uses SGLang to serve Grok 3; Microsoft Azure uses SGLang to serve DeepSeek R1 on AMD GPUs. Claims SGLang is 'deployed to large-scale production, generating trillions of tokens every day' with 300+ GitHub contributors, and lists institutional backers/users: AMD, Atlas Cloud, Baseten, Cursor, DataCrunch, Etched, Hyperbolic, iFlytek, Jam & Tea Studios, LinkedIn, LMSYS, Meituan, Nebius, Novita AI, NVIDIA, RunPod, Stanford, UC Berkeley, UCLA, xAI, 01.AI. Includes a reference Docker launch command for serving DeepSeek-V3 with tensor-parallel=8. `review` (907 words)
- [[sglang-v04-zero-overhead-batch-scheduler-cache-aware-load-balancer-faster-struct]] — SGLang v0.4: Zero-Overhead Batch Scheduler, Cache-Aware Load Balancer, Faster Structured Outputs - LMSYS Org — LMSYS blog (Dec 2024) announcing SGLang v0.4, introducing a cache-aware load balancer ('sglang-router', written in Rust) that predicts prefix KV cache hit rates per worker via a locally maintained approximate radix tree and routes requests to the worker with the highest match, rather than round-robin. Benchmarks on 8xA100 show throughput rising from 82,665 to 158,596 tokens/s (1.9x) and cache hit rate from 20% to 75% (3.8x) on a workload with balanced long-prefix groups. The router is communication-free (no worker-to-worker cache-state sync; it simulates an approximate tree from passed request info), supports multi-node deployment, and is installable standalone via 'pip install sglang-router' as a drop-in for --dp-size. Also describes a zero-overhead CPU/GPU-overlapped batch scheduler (1.1x speedup) and DP attention for DeepSeek MLA models (1.9x decode throughput). Directly comparable prior art to llm-d's KV-cache-aware scheduler/EPP. `evergreen` (1432 words)
- [[sglang-v04-zero-overhead-batch-scheduler-cache-aware-load-balancer-faster-stru-2]] — SGLang v0.4: Zero-Overhead Batch Scheduler, Cache-Aware Load Balancer, Faster Structured Outputs - LMSYS Org — Primary source: SGLang team's own v0.4 release blog (Dec 4, 2024), cited directly by the PyTorch/SGLang ecosystem announcement in this batch. Gives first-party technical mechanism and measured numbers behind SGLang's scheduler-efficiency claims (software-only; no GPU-model comparisons). Zero-overhead batch scheduler: overlaps CPU scheduling with GPU computation one batch ahead (idea credited to the NanoFlow paper), verified via Nsight profiling to show zero GPU idle time across 5 consecutive decoding batches; delivers 1.1x speedup vs SGLang v0.3 and 1.3x vs other baselines, with the largest gains on small models and large tensor-parallel sizes. States the general problem this solves: 'An unoptimized inference engine can spend as much as half of its time on CPU overhead' (linking to the NanoFlow scheduling-overhead analysis) -- directly corroborating vLLM's own V1-issue claim (elsewhere in this batch) that Python/CPU-side scheduling, not GPU kernels, is the dominant bottleneck. Cache-aware load balancer: written in pure Rust, maintains a lazily-updated approximate radix tree mirroring workers' real prefix caches with 'almost no overhead' and no worker-to-worker synchronization; measured 82,665 tok/s (v0.3, naive round-robin) vs 158,596 tok/s (v0.4, cache-aware) -- a 1.9x throughput increase with cache hit rate rising from 20% to 75% (3.8x) on a workload with multiple long shared-prefix groups; claims 2x speedup vs Python-based load-balancer alternatives. Data-parallel attention for DeepSeek's multi-head latent attention (MLA): avoids KV-cache duplication that tensor parallelism would cause (DeepSeek models have only one KV head), redistributing data through an all-gather before the MoE layer; measured 1.9x decoding throughput vs v0.3 on 8xH100. Fast structured outputs: integrating the xgrammar grammar backend makes SGLang+xgrammar 'up to 10x faster than other open-source solutions for JSON decoding,' building on SGLang's earlier Compressed Finite State Machine approach. Roadmap for the next release names disaggregated prefill-decode, speculative decoding, multi-level radix cache, and sequence parallelism as priorities. `review` (1448 words)
- [[sglang-efficient-execution-of]] — SGLang: Efficient Execution of — The original SGLang paper (Zheng et al., Stanford/UC Berkeley/SJTU, 2023-2024, arXiv 2312.07104) introduces RadixAttention, the prefix-tree KV cache reuse technique that NVIDIA Dynamo's KV router and AIBrix's routing strategies build on conceptually. RadixAttention retains (rather than discards) the KV cache of every completed generation request in an LRU-cache radix tree, using the tree for efficient prefix matching, insertion, and eviction; KV tensors are stored in a non-contiguous, page-per-token layout compatible with continuous batching, PagedAttention, and tensor parallelism, adding only negligible overhead. A cache-aware scheduling policy built on top of this radix tree improves cache hit rate by prioritizing requests whose prefixes are already resident. Combined with a compressed finite-state-machine decoder for structured outputs and API speculative execution for API-only models (e.g. GPT-4), SGLang achieves up to 6.4x higher throughput and up to 3.7x lower latency versus vLLM, Guidance, and LMQL across agent control, logical reasoning, few-shot learning, JSON decoding, RAG, and multi-turn chat workloads on Llama-7B/70B, Mistral-8x7B, and LLaVA models on A10G/A100 GPUs. Footnote: as of publication, RadixAttention had been partially integrated as an experimental feature into vLLM, so an earlier vLLM version was used for the comparison. `evergreen` (12209 words)
- [[scale-and-serve-generative-ai-nvidia-dynamo]] — Scale and Serve Generative AI  | NVIDIA Dynamo — NVIDIA's own marketing page for Dynamo: an open-source, distributed inference-serving framework for multi-node/data-center-scale LLM serving. Supports SGLang, TensorRT-LLM, vLLM, and PyTorch as backends. Core components: disaggregated prefill/decode serving, an LLM-aware KV-cache router, KV Cache Manager with LMCache integration for offload to CPU/SSD/network storage, Grove (Kubernetes topology-aware orchestration), GPU Planner (dynamic prefill/decode worker allocation), NIXL (low-latency data-movement library), AIConfigurator and AIPerf (benchmarking/config tools). Marketing claims 'fully open source' and vendor-neutral multi-backend support, but headline performance numbers (7x MoE throughput, 15x compounding gains) are measured only on NVIDIA GB200 NVL72 vs B200, and Dynamo ships bundled with NIM microservices and AI Enterprise -- i.e., open-source core with NVIDIA-hardware-tied performance claims and a paid enterprise support tier layered on top. Named adopters/testimonials: CoreWeave, Together AI, Pinterest. `review` (3836 words)
- [[scale-to-zero-for-llm-inference-why-cold-starts-are-a-weight-loading-problem]] — Scale to Zero for LLM Inference: Why Cold Starts Are a Weight-Loading Problem — AI-authored (claude-sonnet) secondary blog post on dreaming.press arguing LLM cold starts are dominated by weight-loading bandwidth, not container init: a 70B fp16 model is ~140GB and must move disk/network -> host RAM -> PCIe -> VRAM before first token. Cites Baseten framing weight loading as the costliest cold-start phase; Anyscale measuring naive HF load of Llama-2-70B at up to 10 minutes vs 20x faster with a streaming chunked loader; NVIDIA's Run:ai Model Streamer sustaining ~80 Gbps streaming concurrently into VRAM; CoreWeave's Tensorizer doing zero-copy tensor-by-tensor deserialization direct from HTTP/S3/disk into GPU memory (integrated as a vLLM loader) with minimal host RAM use. Describes a second software mitigation beyond streaming: snapshotting post-warmup GPU/CPU memory state (Modal's GPU memory snapshots + vLLM sleep mode) to skip weight load, compilation, and CUDA graph capture entirely on restore, citing a 460s -> ~70s (6.5x) cold-start reduction, at the cost of needing a few cold invocations to build the snapshot first. States KServe/Knative guidance reserves scale-to-zero for CPU/lightweight workloads, not generative inference, because of this latency tail. Low-authority source (AI-generated listicle-style tech blog) -- value is chiefly as a pointer to five named primary engineering sources (Modal, NVIDIA, Baseten, CoreWeave, Anyscale) rather than as a primary source itself. `review` (1976 words)
- [[self-hosting-llms-hidden-costs-youre-missing]] — Self-Hosting LLMs: Hidden Costs You're Missing — VENDOR SOURCE: Azumo (AI consulting/dev shop that also sells its own GPU-brokering platform 'Valkyrie'); authored by Azumo's CTO. Argues self-hosting is underestimated by 3-5x and pushes a middle path (Azumo's own platform) as the economically superior option — a vendor arguing against pure build, with an interest in the 'buy our platform instead' conclusion, not in 'just use the frontier API' either. Cost breakdown: initial deployment realistically needs 2-4 full-time engineers for 3-6 months (loaded cost ~$250K/FTE/year) = $200K-300K one-time; ongoing on-call for a production fleet consumes 25-50% of 1-2 engineers indefinitely = $125K-150K/year; supporting infra (load balancers, GPU telemetry, secrets/config management) adds 20-40% on top of GPU cost in cloud, more on-prem; ML systems engineers command a 30-50% premium over standard senior engineers. States why initial cost estimates miss by 3-5x: deployment timelines run 12-16 weeks instead of an assumed 8, plus another 4-8 weeks of stabilization (total 4-6 months); and GPU utilization assumptions of 70-80% collapse to an ACTUAL 20-40% average because real traffic is peaky (5-10x average during peak hours) requiring 2-4x more GPU capacity than naive sizing. Worked example at 1B tokens/month: API $1,000-2,000/month with no other costs; pure self-host $62K-80K/month in deployment months then $22K-31K/month ongoing; Azumo's own platform positioned at $4,000-6,000/month as the 'sweet spot.' States the self-hosting scale threshold where cost clearly favors DIY is 10 billion+ tokens/month (not 1 billion), and the 500M-5B tokens/month range is where a managed platform (i.e., vendors like Azumo) captures 'most of the benefit' at 40-50% less than hyperscaler on-demand GPU pricing — a claim that directly serves Azumo's commercial interest and should be weighted accordingly. `review` (4641 words)
- [[self-hosting-vs-api-when-running-your-own-llm-pays-off-aitldr]] — Self-Hosting vs API: When Running Your Own LLM Pays Off | AI/TLDR — Third-party LLMOps educational reference (ai-tldr.dev glossary/learn section). Frames the cost decision as two different cost SHAPES: API is pure variable cost (linear through origin), self-hosting is a big fixed cost with near-free marginal requests, so cost-per-request falls as utilization rises. Gives an explicit worked utilization table for a single GPU at $2.00/hr ($1,440/month, 24/7) serving ~2 req/sec sustained, against an API at ~$0.00125/request: at 5% utilization self-host costs ~$0.0055/request (>4x the API); at 25% utilization the two are ~at parity (~$0.0011 vs $0.00125); at 60% utilization self-host drops to ~$0.00046/request; at 90% utilization ~$0.00031/request. States the break-even sits 'around 25% sustained utilization,' which already implies 'well over a million requests a month flowing steadily through that one box' — and explicitly flags that this number EXCLUDES engineer salary, standby failover GPU, and peak over-provisioning, so the true break-even volume is higher still. Lists concrete signals for choosing API vs self-host (volume, traffic pattern, team capacity, compliance). Names vLLM, TGI, SGLang as the serving engines that make high utilization achievable via continuous batching, and explicitly recommends hybrid routing (self-hosted for cheap bulk traffic + API for hard/rare queries) via an LLM gateway with model routing and provider failover. States the industry trend: falling API prices are pushing the break-even volume UPWARD over time, shrinking the pure-cost case for self-hosting, so durable reasons to self-host are increasingly about control (residency, latency, customization) rather than price. `review` (2428 words)
- [[self-hosted-llm-stack-a-solid-foundation-for-an-open-weight-platform-built-to-ev]] — Self-hosted LLM stack: a solid foundation for an open-weight platform built to evolve | Ogenki — Ogenki blog (personal/demo project, May 2026, 4739 words, primary first-person account, not a named company's internal production service but an unusually detailed reference architecture) documenting a full self-hosted open-weight LLM stack on Kubernetes (AWS EKS / GCP GKE). Three layers: (1) Storage -- model weights (HuggingFace) cached on a shared Amazon S3 Files (POSIX NFS, GA April 2026) volume so only the first pod cold-starts a download; (2) Inference -- vLLM (open-source, 'de-facto standard') deployed via the vLLM Production Stack Helm chart, with fp8 quantization, prefix caching, chunked prefill, and Hermes tool-call parsing enabled per-model; GPU autoscaling via KEDA on vLLM's own Prometheus metrics (vllm:num_requests_running / configured batch size at 70% threshold, vllm:gpu_cache_usage_perc at 60%) rather than CPU/memory HPA, anticipating saturation before a queue forms; node provisioning via Karpenter; (3) Access -- Envoy AI Gateway (open-source, built on Envoy Gateway) as the single OpenAI-compatible front door, doing per-model routing, bearer-token auth, and per-token cost tracking via OpenTelemetry Gen AI semantic conventions (gen_ai.* metrics), plus vLLM Semantic Router ('Iris', open-source, a ~100M-param mmBERT-derived CPU classifier) for intent/jailbreak/PII-based dynamic model selection when a client doesn't pick a model explicitly (adds ~250-300ms, acceptable for chat, not for <200ms p95 IDE autocomplete, which uses explicit routing instead). Kubernetes resource generation is abstracted via a custom Crossplane 'InferenceService' composition (written in KCL, unit-tested, OCI-packaged) so swapping models is a 2-line PR. Clients are open-source: OpenWebUI (chat), Continue (VSCode FIM autocomplete), OpenCode (CLI coding agent, an open alternative to Claude Code). Continuous quality evaluation via Promptfoo (deterministic + LLM-as-judge assertions) run as a nightly CronJob, metrics pushed to the same Prometheus/Grafana stack. States vLLM delivers 3-10x throughput over naive serving (HuggingFace Transformers/Ollama defaults) via continuous batching + paged attention, citing Kwon et al. SOSP 2023 (arXiv:2309.06180) for the original 2-4x vs FasterTransformer / 14-24x vs HF Transformers figures. Explicitly states open-weight models only become compelling for orgs with real data-sovereignty requirements (healthcare, defense, finance) or once hardware/financial accessibility improves; for the author's own usage the proprietary Claude ecosystem remains cheaper than replicating equivalent quality self-hosted. `review` (4739 words)
- [[service-accounts-litellm]] — Service Accounts | liteLLM — LiteLLM's service-account (team-scoped virtual key) doc -- an OSS feature (no enterprise tag on this page). A service-account key always has team_id set and user_id null, and applies TEAM-level limits only (not per-member limits), which is the point: 'Prevent key from being deleted when user is deleted' and 'Apply team limits, not team member limits to key.' max_budget, tpm_limit, and rpm_limit can be set directly on the key or inherited from the team. The one enterprise-feature cross-reference on this page: team_member_key_duration (an enterprise feature controlling how long team-member keys live) explicitly does NOT apply to service-account keys. Supports an immutable service_account_id metadata field (once set, any attempt to overwrite or null it returns HTTP 400; omitting metadata on update safely preserves the existing value) for giving shared production/CI keys a stable human-readable identity distinct from the key hash itself. Can enforce that all service-account requests include a user param (service_account_settings.enforced_params) -- useful for per-end-customer attribution even on a shared service-account key. `review` (654 words)
- [[serving-glm-52-for-agentic-workloads-on-llm-d-llm-d]] — Serving GLM-5.2 for Agentic Workloads on llm-d | llm-d — llm-d blog post applying the agentic-serving well-lit path to GLM-5.2-FP8 (744B params, 39B active MoE, 78 layers, Multi-head Latent Attention) on H200 GPUs, using 219 production Claude Code sessions from SemiAnalysis's Weka trace corpus (78,099 model requests). Because MLA cannot be sharded by tensor parallelism (TP=8 would keep 8 full KV-cache copies), llm-d uses Wide-EP (data-parallel attention + expert-parallel MoE) to keep the KV budget viable for long-context agentic serving. Workload characterization: median main-agent request carries 195K input tokens and returns only 317 output tokens; 96% of main-agent turns reuse at least 90% of their input; input tokens make up 98.6% of all tokens served, making prefill capacity (not decode) the binding constraint. Directly quantifies prefix-aware routing's value: it captures 96-97% of the reuse the workload makes available, and in a paused-conversation test, turns with a cached prefix reach first token 2.8x faster than first turns without one. Quantifies CPU offloading's value: in matched 24-GPU runs at concurrency 32, offloading raises output 13%, cuts median TTFT 32%, and cuts median turn completion time 27% versus no offloading; at concurrency 128, offloading reaches 2,776 tok/s versus baseline's ~951 tok/s. Multi-Token Prediction (MTP) lets the same 32 GPUs serve at least 8x more concurrent requests (up to 128) without output dropping below 30 tok/s/user — without MTP the rate holds only to 16 concurrent requests (561 tok/s), with MTP it holds through 128 requests (5,647 tok/s). At a 64-GPU scale with 3 prefill + 1 decode instances, output rose 71% (3,679 to 6,281 tok/s) between concurrency 64 and 128 with only a modest ITL increase (15.5ms to 19.1ms). Reports self-hosted cost of /bin/bash.05-0.08/million input tokens and .92-3.55/million output tokens at .71/GPU-hour H200 rental, versus .40/.40 for Z.ai's managed GLM-5.2 API and /5 for Claude Opus 4.8 (GPU rental only, not full opex). Names six llm-d capabilities required to serve this workload: MLA-compressing KV, wide-EP, prefix-aware routing, CPU offloading, P/D pool separation, and optional MTP. This is the production testbed referenced by the P2P KV cache sharing blog post's saturation benchmarks. `evergreen` (6426 words)
- [[serving-pytorch-llms-at-scale-disaggregated]] — Serving PyTorch LLMs at Scale: Disaggregated — PyTorch Conference deck (Cong Liu, Google; Maroon Ayoub, IBM Research) on llm-d's disaggregated inference. Describes llm-d explicitly as an 'open-source, k8s-native, pluggable inference stack' with an 'LLM aware inference gateway + LLM aware autoscaler'. Details the P/D inference scheduler decision flow: a threshold on '% new tokens to prefill' decides disaggregation vs. no-disaggregation; when disaggregating, D-picking plugins include kv cache utilization and queue depth, P-picking plugins include prefix cache hit and queue depth. Confirms the routing sidecar behavior mirrors the GitHub repo README: in non-disaggregation mode (no x-prefiller-host-port header) it simply forwards to the local decode node; in disaggregation mode it first sends the request to the prefill node with max_tokens=1, then forwards to the local decode node, which pulls the KV cache from prefill via the NIXL connector. Gives two concrete well-lit-path benchmark topologies: 'P/D over RDMA' -- 8xH200 GPUs, InfiniBand OR RoCE RDMA, Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct-FP8, 4x TP=1 prefill workers + 1x TP=4 decode worker; and 'Wide-EP with P/D' -- 32xH200 GPUs, InfiniBand OR RoCE RDMA, DeepSeek-R1-0528, 1x DP=16 prefill worker + 1x DP=16 decode worker. Best-practice guidance: P/D disaggregation pays off for large models (Llama-70B+, not 8B), longer ISL (e.g. 10k ISL | 1k OSL vs 200|200), and sparse MoE with wide-EP opportunities; tune via heterogeneous parallelism (fewer-parallelism/more-replica P workers vs. more-parallelism/fewer-replica D workers) and xPyD ratio. Flags two 'future work' ideas not yet shipped: (1) 'decode-first' PD where the router opportunistically picks a decoder first (since the router lacks visibility into remote KV-cache storage) and only falls back to P/D if the decoder reports low cache hit; (2) 'deferred decoder picking' -- picking D only after prefill completes, using freshest load metrics, instead of picking P and D both at request-arrival time (today's behavior, which can use stale load data since the request lands on D later). `evergreen` (740 words)
- [[setting-team-budgets-litellm]] — Setting Team Budgets | liteLLM — LiteLLM's hands-on config doc for team-level budgets, confirming the mechanism is real and OSS (uses the plain /team/new and /key/generate API, no enterprise tag). Budgets are set via max_budget (dollar ceiling) and budget_duration (reset cadence: 1s/1m/1h/1d/30d), require a connected Postgres database as a prerequisite. On exceeding budget, the default response is HTTP 422 with a budget_exceeded error naming the team_id, current cost, and max budget; this is configurable to HTTP 429 via budget_exceeded_status_code. Enforcement is per-key: a key created with a team_id inherits that team's budget. Exposes a Prometheus gauge litellm_remaining_team_budget_metric labeled by team_alias/team_id for dashboards. Also covers default budgets for JWT-auto-provisioned teams via default_team_settings in config.yaml (team_id_upsert). `review` (559 words)
- [[shadow-mode-forever-nadir]] — Shadow Mode, Forever · Nadir — Vendor blog post (Nadir, an LLM-routing/verification startup) using the SRE-derived shadow-test / canary-rollout pattern for LLM model swaps as context before pitching its own per-request verifier product. Defines shadow mode precisely: production requests mirrored to both incumbent (which answers the user) and candidate (which does not); an automated judge scores the pairs on accuracy/tone/task-completion/format; full shadow mode roughly doubles inference spend for the 1-2 week test window. Canary deployment ramps the candidate through real traffic at 1% -> 5% -> 20% -> 50% -> 100%, holding each stage >=24 hours, with named automated rollback triggers: guardrail trip rate >1.5x baseline in a 15-min window, offline rubric score regressing below noise floor, p99 latency >1.3x baseline, or an error cluster unique to the candidate -- median rollback latency once triggered is ~35 seconds (attributed to Future AGI, 'LLM Eval with Shadow Traffic and Canary Deployment,' 2026). Cites a named failure case: a team that flipped a candidate to 100% of traffic without staged validation saw function-call accuracy drop 9 points and refusal rate climb 7 points, invisible until the flip was live (same Future AGI source). Introduces 'mirror sampling' -- running shadow at 10-25% of full traffic instead of 100% -- as a cost-reduced middle ground (10% mirror on 1M daily requests yields ~100k scored pairs/day), attributed to the same Future AGI source and to TianPan, 'Releasing AI Features Without Breaking Production' (April 2026). Core argument/thesis: shadow and canary validate the swap once, over a bounded pre-promotion window, then the comparison pipeline is torn down at 100% promotion -- so distribution shift after rollout (new customer segment, longer context, new tool schema) goes uncaught; proposes per-request calibrated verification (cheap model drafts, a calibrated verifier scores, escalates to a strong model only on failure) as a permanent alternative to one-time gating. Should be read as vendor content: numeric claims (AUROC 0.961, pass@1 benchmarks) are sourced to the vendor's own RouterBench experiments, not independently verified. `review` (2167 words)
- [[should-you-build-or-buy-your-inference-platform]] — Should You Build or Buy Your Inference Platform? — VENDOR SOURCE: BentoML (sells the 'Bento Inference Platform,' now part of Modular as of this post, authored by BentoML founder Chaoyu Yang, Sept 2025). Direct commercial interest in the 'don't build your own, buy ours' conclusion. Claims a 'basic' production inference layer with autoscaling and distributed inference takes an experienced team 2-3 months to design/stabilize (vendor's platform claims <1 day); vanilla Kubernetes cold-starts an LLM container in ~10 minutes vs the vendor's claimed <30 seconds (a '25x' figure linked to a separate BentoML blog post, not independently verified here); integrating a newly-released frontier model takes DIY teams 1-2 weeks of batching/runtime tuning (vendor claims day-zero access). States senior infrastructure engineers command 30-50% higher salaries than standard DevOps roles (same 30-50% figure independently appears in Azumo's vendor post — possibly drawn from a shared market-data source, uncredited in either). Cites customer-reported case studies as evidence FOR buying: Yext cut dev cycles 70% and compute spend 80% after adopting the platform; Revia cut infra costs 6x after moving off a homegrown system; Neurolabs reached market 9 months faster and saved 70% in costs while avoiding a dedicated infra hire. Claims customers on the Bento platform average GPU utilization of 70%+ — presented as a vendor outcome metric, not an independent industry baseline, and stands in stark contrast to the 5-22% real-world DIY utilization figures reported by Forte Group/Cast AI and Azumo elsewhere in this batch. All customer figures are vendor-reported case studies, not independently audited. `review` (1561 words)
- [[soloio-blog-llm-d-distributed-inference-serving-on-kubernetes-soloio]] — Solo.io Blog | llm-d: Distributed Inference Serving on Kubernetes | Solo.io — DUPLICATE: identical article body (by Christian Posta) to the kgateway.dev cross-post already captured as note llm-d-distributed-inference-serving-on-kubernetes-kgateway; this fetch is >90% blog-sidebar/nav boilerplate (unrelated Solo.io product/blog links) with the same short llm-d content. Deprecated in favor of the cleaner kgateway.dev copy. `deprecated` (5654 words)
- [[source-analysis-cacheroute-planned-prefix-affinity-routing]] — Source Analysis — CacheRoute: Planned Prefix-Affinity Routing — CacheRoute (Meta, Aug 2026) is the strongest adversarial source on cache-affinity routing: hardware-benchmarked evidence that reactive/planned prefix-affinity routing beats flat load balancing 2.3x on a favorable 70B workload, but actively LOSES capacity (0.50-0.67x) on 32B workloads with low recoverable reuse, and that stale routing plans and burstiness measurably degrade performance. Never names llm-d, but evaluates the same mechanism class (Preble/DualMap-style prefix-cache-aware routing) llm-d's scheduler implements, and its shadow-replay-gating prescription and stale-vs-fresh-plan tradeoff data are directly applicable to assessing llm-d's design choices. `evergreen` (2859 words)
- [[source-analysis-mooncake-a-kvcache-centric-disaggregated-architecture-for-llm-se]] — Source Analysis — Mooncake: A KVCache-centric Disaggregated Architecture for LLM Serving — Digest of Moonshot AI's Mooncake tech report: production evidence for Kimi's KVCache-centric disaggregated serving platform (Conductor scheduler, Messenger RDMA cache-transfer, distributed CPU/DRAM/SSD KVCache pool), built atop vLLM but adding distributed caching the OSS version lacked, later open-sourced as kvcache-ai/Mooncake. Captures paired production measurements (23,608-request real trace, 34-55% cache hit rates vs. capacity, P90 TTFT 6.26s vs 92.07s random scheduling, 75% more requests served vs vLLM, up to 525% simulated throughput gain) and the overload/early-rejection mechanics (4,183 to 3,589 rejected requests across policies, HTTP 429, and the oscillation pathology naive rejection introduced). `review` (3067 words)
- [[source-analysis-rfc-multi-tier-kv-offloading-vllm-issue-38260]] — Source Analysis — RFC Multi-tier KV Offloading (vLLM Issue #38260) — Digest of vLLM RFC #38260 (multi-tier KV offloading via TieringManager: CPU primary tier + secondary tiers, canonical TP1 layout) and its comment thread, which is the primary record of how tiered/offloaded KV blocks get exposed via KV events (medium vs. locality) for external routers like llm-d, including a maintainer's explicit warning that stale placement events cause routers to score/route incorrectly. Flags a block of self-reported, unverified 'production' benchmark claims from a single downstream contributor (unmerged fork) that must not be cited as vLLM fact. `evergreen` (2790 words)
- [[source-analysis-sticky-until-saturated-token-aware-routing-in-llm-d]] — Source Analysis — Sticky Until Saturated: Token-Aware Routing in llm-d — Digest of llm-d's engineering blog post specifying its current default router: a prefix-cache-affinity-filter (threshold 0.8) paired with one workload-matched load scorer (token-load-scorer for prefill-bound, active-request-scorer for decode-bound), gated by a saturation threshold tau derived in closed form (tau_sat = R_peak x T_max) from one hardware calibration measurement and an operator TTFT ceiling; replaces a four-signal weighted blend criticized as unpredictable and hotspot-prone. Includes worked Qwen3-32B/H100 calibration (tau=286,720), an 8-path portability table showing tau does not transplant across (model, accelerator, engine), and single-run (no confidence interval) benchmark evidence across three workloads with explicit self-flagged caveats. `evergreen` (2767 words)
- [[source-analysis-thomson-continual-learning-of-frontier-models-for-sovereignai]] — Source Analysis — Thomson: Continual Learning of Frontier Models for SovereignAI — Digest of Thomson Reuters' 66K-word SovereignAI report: builds Thomson-1.0 models on Qwen3.5/3.6 checkpoints via continual learning (NeMo-RL+Megatron-LM+Ray+vLLM for RL, forked ClearML for orchestration, DataHub+SeaweedFS for data infra), serves them via a self-hosted vLLM-on-Kubernetes platform with a bespoke Rust routing/auth plane across 11 clusters/4 geographies (40M+ requests, 370B tokens served since April 2026, 19.7% cost savings vs third-party inference for GLM-5.2). Training-side costs (~$450K/run, ~$40M programme) are clearly training/infra spend, not inference economics; the serving-specific section is comparatively thin (no latency/throughput/batching figures) -- flagged as an audited absence. `review` (3530 words)
- [[source-analysis-llm-d-router-github-repo-readme-commit-history]] — Source Analysis — llm-d Router GitHub repo (README + commit history) — Digest of the llm-d-router GitHub repo (README + commit history through Sep 11 2026, tag v0.10.0): confirms the Inference-Scheduler-to-Router rename and the May 2026 EPP/CRD consolidation from GIE, but finds zero mentions of discovery/EndpointDiscovery/non-k8s standalone operation; the only documented backend-extension seam (ATOM engine metrics, PR #2476) is pod-label-keyed, InferencePool/HTTPRoute/Gateway/RBAC remain hard Kubernetes dependencies, and the freshly-merged 'structured plugin config' PR (#2620, same-day HEAD) is undescribed in this capture and flagged as a priority follow-up fetch. `evergreen` (2843 words)
- [[spend-tracking-litellm]] — Spend Tracking | liteLLM — LiteLLM's cost-tracking/spend-attribution doc -- the most important nuance here is a code-comment-level confirmation of the OSS/Enterprise split for cost attribution: passing a 'user' param to the request (end-customer-level spend tracking) is marked OPTIONAL and available in OSS, while passing 'tags' in request metadata to track spend by tag is explicitly marked '# ENTERPRISE' inline in LiteLLM's own example code -- directly corroborating the separately-fetched Enterprise feature list's 'Tag-based Budgets' entry. LiteLLM auto-tracks spend for all known models via a maintained model_prices_and_context_window.json cost map on GitHub, with provider-specific cost tiers (Vertex AI PayGo/priority pricing, Bedrock service tiers, Azure base-model mapping) applied automatically when tier metadata is present in the response. Every request's cost is written to a LiteLLM_SpendLogs table recording the hashed api_key, the internal user, the team_id, request_tags, the end_user ('customer'), model_group, and the upstream api_base -- i.e. the full attribution join (key/user/team/customer/model/provider) lands in one row per request, which is the OSS foundation the TrueFoundry and Spheron chargeback pipelines in this batch build their own tagging schemes on top of. Ships a troubleshooting workflow (Debugging a cost discrepancy) for reconciling LiteLLM's computed spend against actual provider bills by aligning time ranges and comparing token categories including cache tokens. `review` (3340 words)
- [[splitwise-efficient-generative-llm-inference-using]] — Splitwise: Efficient Generative LLM Inference Using — Splitwise (Patel, Choukse, Zhang, Shah, Goiri, Maleki, Bianchini — Microsoft + Univ. of Washington, ISCA 2024, arXiv 2311.18677) is the earliest widely-cited phase-splitting paper in this corpus and first-person Microsoft/Azure production evidence: its characterization is built on real production traces 'taken from two Azure LLM inference services on November 11th 2023' representing the two dominant real-world request classes — coding and conversation — a 20-minute subset of which is open-sourced at github.com/Azure/AzurePublicDataset (AzureLLMInferenceDataset2023). DistServe, Sarathi-Serve, Mooncake, and the 'Taming the Titans' survey all cite Splitwise as the paper that established the prompt/token-generation phase split as a serving primitive; Mooncake explicitly credits Splitwise's arXiv release as 'at the early stage of the development of Mooncake, which further motivated our progress.' Core technique: rather than disaggregating onto identical hardware, Splitwise explicitly leverages diverging GPU scaling curves (NVIDIA A100 vs H100: 3.43x more FLOPs, only 1.64x more HBM bandwidth, 1.75x more power, 2.16x more cost per machine per hour) to run the compute-bound prompt-computation phase on newer/faster GPUs and the memory-bandwidth-bound token-generation phase on cheaper/older GPUs, transferring KV-cache state between machines over datacenter-grade InfiniBand back-plane interconnects. Designs both homogeneous and heterogeneous cluster configurations optimized respectively for throughput, cost, and power. Evaluated on Llama2-70B and BLOOM-176B using the Azure production traces: Splitwise-based clusters achieve up to 1.4x higher throughput at 20% lower cost than non-split designs, or alternatively 2.35x more throughput under identical power and cost budgets. Also introduces time-between-tokens (TBT) as a formal streaming-latency metric alongside TTFT/E2E-latency/throughput. Explicitly critiques the leading pre-disaggregation batching schemes (request-level batching and continuous batching) for forcing prompt-phase requests to preempt ongoing token-generation batches, which shortens TTFT at the cost of substantially higher tail E2E latency for in-flight requests. `review` (11936 words)
- [[standalone-epp-endpoint-discovery-using-pod-selector-investigation-issue-1779-ku]] — Standalone EPP - Endpoint Discovery Using Pod Selector Investigation · Issue #1779 · kubernetes-sigs/gateway-api-inference-extension — GIE issue #1779 (capri-xiyue, opened Oct 28 2025) tracks investigation of 'Endpoint Discovery with Pod Selector' for standalone EPP, referencing the same 'EPP As a Standalone Request Scheduler' design doc cited in issue #1838. Motivation, stated verbatim: 'The endpoint picker (EPP)... is currently deployed as an ext-proc service tightly coupled to the Kubernetes Gateway API, a setup that adds unnecessary operational overhead for non-serving workloads like Reinforcement Learning (RL) post-training. The proof of concept for a "Standalone EPP" aims to solve this by creating a decoupled deployment mode... packaging the EPP and an Envoy proxy as a single sidecar-based unit, bypassing the Gateway API and instead using a simple command-line pod selector to discover and manage model server endpoints, thereby lowering the barrier for adopting the EPP's advanced scheduling logic for critical RL and batch inference jobs.' A sharp maintainer pushback from nirrozenbaum clarifies an important scope distinction that matters for router customization: 'this issue is not about bypassing Gateway API, it's about bypassing InferencePool CRD which is one of GIE main building blocks... specifying pod labels selector as cmd-line is against k8s principles and is not matching how GIE works today where the selection label can [be] changed dynamically in runtime. How do you handle conflicts if one specifies the cmd-line arg and uses the crd? ... why do we need this? standalone epp without ext proc can work with InferencePool CRD.' Author capri-xiyue's response: the EPP would validate that only one discovery source (cmd-line pod-selector or InferencePool CRD) is active at a time, avoiding conflicts. This exchange is direct evidence of an unresolved tension in the community between (a) fully declarative, CRD-driven discovery (InferencePool, dynamically reconciled) and (b) simpler, static/CLI-configured discovery (pod-selector or file-based) aimed at lowering the barrier for RL/batch users -- the same tension documented independently in the FileDiscovery-plugin work in llm-d-router. `evergreen` (874 words)
- [[standalone-epp-issue-1838-kubernetes-sigsgateway-api-inference-extension]] — Standalone EPP · Issue #1838 · kubernetes-sigs/gateway-api-inference-extension — GIE issue #1838 'Standalone EPP' (opened by ahg-g, a GIE maintainer, Nov 7 2025; closed completed Mar 4 2026) is the design-rationale thread for why standalone (non-InferencePool, non-Gateway-API-provisioned) EPP deployment exists. ahg-g frames it as a maturation ladder: 'The idea is to offer deployment modes that allow the least amount of friction for users as they mature in the journey from simple deployments to full fledged ones that integrate with Gateway API. The end goal is to land users on k8s and the gateway api, but many users are not there yet.' He enumerates four deployment modes: (1) orchestrator-agnostic static endpoint config with ZERO k8s dependency (no reconcilers started at all); (2.1) k8s-native, no Gateway API/CRD dependency, endpoints found via pod-selector+port flags; (2.2) k8s-native, extension CRDs used for richer EPP features but proxy statically configured (typically sidecar); (2.3) full Gateway-API-provisioned mode. Corresponding to this he identifies exactly three discovery mechanisms EPP should support: 1) InferencePool (reconciler-driven), 2) podSelector, 3) static set of endpoints -- each as its own pluggable 'discovery plugin.' Contributor nirrozenbaum initially argued modes (1) and (2.1) were unnecessary and 'awkward to develop... under kubernetes-sigs and say it's going to be with zero dependency on k8s,' but ahg-g countered with concrete demand: 'We hear this from batch inference and RL customers. In fact, Mistral asked for those two deployment modes independently.' ahg-g explicitly rejects requiring any control-plane dependency for the static/orchestrator-agnostic mode -- 'I don't think we need to offer anything beyond a static set of endpoints because otherwise we need to build or create a dependency on a control plane, which goes against the main motivation behind this mode' -- and cites sgl-project/sglang's sgl-router prefill/decode disaggregation static-endpoint config as the model to follow. Contributor elevran proposed generalizing service discovery further to include DNS resolvers and etcd/Consul/cloud-service backends, listed as options for mode (1). This thread is the direct motivating-use-case evidence for why standalone EPP (and thus non-pod backend support) exists: friction reduction for non-k8s-native batch-inference and RL workloads, not a rejection of k8s but a staged on-ramp. `evergreen` (1890 words)
- [[sticky-until-saturated-token-aware-routing-in-llm-d-llm-d]] — Sticky Until Saturated: Token-Aware Routing in llm-d | llm-d — Deep technical analysis of llm-d's default router scheduling change to 'token-aware routing': a single prefix-cache-affinity-filter (threshold 0.8) paired with one load signal matched to the workload's bottleneck — token-load-scorer (uncached prefill tokens in flight) for prefill-bound traffic, or active-request-scorer (active streams) for decode-bound traffic — replacing a previous four-signal weighted blend (prefix match, queue depth, KV utilization, LRU) that was hard to predict/tune. The affinity filter restricts candidates to cache-warm endpoints (affinity >= threshold) unless load there exceeds a calibrated saturation limit tau, at which point it releases stickiness and routes by load alone; tau is derived in closed form from one hardware calibration measurement, tau_sat = R_peak x T_max = (B x T_max)/T(B), where B=max-num-batched-tokens, T(B)=measured wall-clock time to prefill one full chunk, and T_max is an operator-chosen TTFT degradation ceiling. On Qwen3-32B/H100 (B=8192), R_peak=20,480 tok/s and T_max=14s gives tau=286,720 (35 chunks); the shipped default configuration reproduces this via peakPrefillThroughput=15,928 tok/s and maxTTFTPenaltyMs=18,000ms. Benchmarked across three workloads (code-generation, prefill-bound; reasoning, decode-bound; b2b-saas, pathological prefill-bound with a fixed 150-prompt corpus) on 8-10x Qwen3-32B/H100 (TP=2) pods: matched configurations sustain 2-3x the throughput of Kubernetes round-robin on prefill-bound workloads (2.9x at knee for code-gen, 2.0x for b2b-saas) while holding parity or better on decode-bound reasoning workload. R_peak (peakPrefillThroughput) varies widely by (model, accelerator, engine) — from 39,065 tok/s (gpt-oss-120B/H100, sparse MoE) to 15,360 tok/s (Qwen3-32B/H100 on SGLang) — so tau must be recalibrated per deployment and does not transplant. Also evaluates a workload-agnostic latency-predictor pipeline (XGBoost-based TTFT prediction) as an alternative for high-variance/mixed-bottleneck traffic. Ships as default across llm-d's optimized-baseline, agentic-serving, multimodal-serving, and P/D-disaggregation guides; running in production at Google Cloud Vertex AI, Red Hat, and Mistral. `evergreen` (6767 words)
- [[storygraph-scaling-llm-and-ml-models-to-300m-monthly-requests-with-self-hosting]] — StoryGraph: Scaling LLM and ML Models to 300M Monthly Requests with Self-Hosting - ZenML LLMOps Database — ZenML LLMOps-database summary of a StoryGraph (2-person team) NDC Porto talk by engineer 'Rob': self-hosts ~1M LLM req/day (~10 req/s) rather than calling GPT-4, citing a calculated $770,000/month GPT-4 cost at their scale vs. a fraction of that self-hosted, plus data-privacy guarantees. Migrated off Heroku (>$100k/yr) to bare-metal Hetzner servers (~$114/month) managed via Cloud66. ML/LLM inference jobs are distributed with a custom Redis BRPOP queue (any GPU, including a presenter's laptop, can join the worker pool) after direct load-balancing and file-based locking both failed under 30-60s inference latencies. Migrated primary datastore from PostgreSQL to self-hosted YugabyteDB (10 to 60 nodes, 960 cores, 2.5TB RAM, ~$2,000/month) to survive a ~1B-insert traffic spike, using CTE rewrites, extra indexes, and denormalization to fix queries that went from 100ms to 400s. Also self-hosts PostHog (vs Mixpanel, $400/mo vs $100k+/yr) and SigNoz (vs Datadog, ~$100/mo). Primary source is a YouTube talk (https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZFQq5Djd5JY), not a written post. `review` (1872 words)
- [[support-kv-cache-events-in-mp-mode-for-dynamo-kv-aware-routing-issue-3661-lmcach]] — Support KV cache events in MP mode (for Dynamo KV-aware routing) · Issue #3661 · LMCache/LMCache — LMCache/LMCache issue #3661 (opened Jun 12 2026, still open/stale as of Aug 26 2026): as of this issue, LMCache MP mode (standalone server process, not co-located with the inference engine) does NOT emit any vLLM KV events -- vLLM's connector take_events() returns empty because store events happen inside the LMCache server, not the engine, so 'Dynamo's router is completely blind to the cache held by the LMCache server.' Contributor Alorun's Jun 24 design doc proposed bypassing vLLM's event path entirely: the MP server would emit vLLM-wire-format BlockStored/BlockRemoved events over its own ZMQ socket to a Dynamo-side relay KvEventPublisher (needs a Dynamo Endpoint identity, which the standalone LMCache server lacks natively). By Jun 24 2026 the author reported local end-to-end validation (real MP server emits correctly-shaped events, a standalone Dynamo KV indexer ingests them) but 'full multi-GPU router validation still to come' and no PR had landed; the issue went stale Aug 25 2026 with an unanswered Aug 26 ping. This design is Dynamo-specific (uses Dynamo's KvEventPublisher/Endpoint concept), not llm-d-specific. `evergreen` (2812 words)
- [[support-fail-open-fallback-for-standalone-epp-inference-routing-issue-1661-agent]] — Support fail-open fallback for standalone EPP inference routing · Issue #1661 · agentgateway/agentgateway — Open agentgateway feature issue (#1661, opened Apr 24 2026) tracking fail-open fallback for standalone-EPP inference routing. Confirms PR #1658 made agentgateway trust the EPP-selected destination and forward directly to the returned ip:port, but deliberately kept standalone routing FAIL-CLOSED: if the local EPP is unavailable or omits the 'x-gateway-destination-endpoint' header, agentgateway errors instead of falling back to normal service load-balancing. Maintainer howardjohn objects that a fail-open path has no endpoints to route to since users don't configure them in passthrough mode. Contributor steverogersX proposes fail-open only activates if the user explicitly declares a fallback pool (EPP down + no fallback pool = same error as fail-closed; EPP down + fallback pool = route through fallback). As of the last comment (Jun 22 2026) danehans says a real FailOpen design needs either configured/tracked fallback endpoints, a non-passthrough mode with service endpoint discovery, or separate virtual-model failover — and is soliciting standalone-scheduler user feedback before proceeding. Unresolved / unmerged as of fetch. `evergreen` (584 words)
- [[support-k8s-gateway-api-inference-extensions-issue-423-theagentrouteragent-route]] — Support k8s gateway API inference extensions · Issue #423 · theagentrouter/agent-router — GitHub issue #423 (Feb 2025-Jul 2025, now theagentrouter/agent-router, formerly envoyproxy/ai-gateway) is the original design thread for integrating Gateway API Inference Extension into Envoy AI Gateway. Design notes lay out the layered customization seam central to this research: HTTPRoute maps to one or more InferencePools; InferencePool selects pods via labels+port; the InferenceModel (predecessor to InferenceObjective) picks which pool serves a request, normally via the OpenAI-schema 'model' field. Crucially, the spec allows offloading model/endpoint selection to a remote ext_proc service so operators can implement proprietary picking logic, and the generic case needs two ext_proc callouts: (1) select model/pool from body+cost/priority signals, (2) select endpoint within the pool to optimize utilization -- these can collapse to one callout if HTTPRoute maps to a single pool or if one callout service handles both. Reference implementation cited: kubernetes-sigs/gateway-api-inference-extension endpoint-picker-protocol proposal (docs/proposals/004-endpoint-picker-protocol). Work culminated in PR #823 'feat: add support for endpoint picker' merged Jul 22, 2025 (v0.3.0), preceded by design-doc PR #771. `evergreen` (1505 words)
- [[tags-llm-dllm-d-router-github]] — Tags · llm-d/llm-d-router · GitHub — GitHub git-tags list for llm-d/llm-d-router, fetched 11 Sep 2026, confirming exact tag dates: v0.10.0 = 17 Aug 2026 (newest), v0.10.0-rc.1 = 11 Aug 2026, v0.9.0 = 23 Jun 2026, v0.9.0-rc.2/rc.1 = 16 Jun 2026, v0.8.0 = 25 Apr 2026, v0.8.0-rc.2/rc.1 = 25/24 Apr 2026, gaie-1.5-sync = 20 Apr 2026 (GAIE v1.5.0 dependency pin), v0.7.1 = 3 Apr 2026. No tag newer than v0.10.0 exists (no v0.10.1, no v0.11.0-rc.*) as of this fetch -- confirms v0.10.0 GA is the current newest released version and that all discovery-config changes merged after 17 Aug 2026 (PR #2434 on 25 Aug 2026, PR #2501 still open) are unreleased. `evergreen` (822 words)
- [[taming-throughput-latency-tradeoff-in-llm-inference-with-sarathi-serve]] — Taming Throughput-Latency Tradeoff in LLM Inference with Sarathi-Serve — Sarathi-Serve (Agrawal et al., Microsoft Research India / Georgia Tech, OSDI'24, arXiv 2403.02310) — the origin paper for chunked-prefill, a technique now implemented in vLLM's V1 scheduler. Diagnoses the existing throughput-vs-latency tradeoff as a consequence of scheduler design: decode-prioritizing systems (FasterTransformer) protect time-between-tokens (TBT) latency but waste GPU cycles running small decode-only batches; prefill-prioritizing iteration-level batchers (Orca, vLLM) maximize throughput by eagerly scheduling prefills whenever memory frees up, but this causes 'generation stalls' — the paper measures a stall in vLLM lasting several seconds when serving Yi-34B on two A100s against an arxiv-summarization trace (Figure 1a). Introduces two techniques: (1) chunked-prefills, splitting a prefill request into near-equal compute-sized chunks processed over multiple iterations; (2) stall-free scheduling, which coalesces ongoing decodes with one or more prefill chunks from new requests into each batch up to a fixed chunk-size budget, so new request admission never pauses in-flight decodes. The near-uniform per-iteration compute this creates also balances pipeline-parallel micro-batches, substantially reducing pipeline bubbles. Benchmarked against vLLM and Orca across four model/hardware configurations (Mistral-7B on 1 A100; Yi-34B on 2 A100 with TP-2; LLaMA2-70B on 8 A40; Falcon-180B on 8 A100 with TP4-PP2 over commodity 100Gbps ethernet, deployed on Azure NC96ads v4 VMs) using two traces built from real request-length distributions: openchat_sharegpt4 (real multi-round ChatGPT-4 conversations, high prompt-length variance) and arxiv_summarization (long prompts, low output-length variance) — the paper states the latter is 'representative of LLM workloads such as Microsoft M365 Copilot and Google Duet AI.' Headline results: up to 2.6x higher serving capacity for Mistral-7B, up to 3.7x for Yi-34B, and up to 5.6x end-to-end serving-capacity gain for Falcon-180B under pipeline parallelism, all measured under explicit tail-latency SLOs (P99 TBT at 5x or 25x unconstrained decode-iteration time for strict/relaxed settings, following Patel et al.'s Splitwise methodology). Explicitly contrasts itself with the disaggregation approach (Splitwise, DistServe, TetriInfer): disaggregation fully eliminates prefill-decode interference and gives better TTFT, but requires KV-cache migration across replicas (needs high-bandwidth interconnect) and leaves prefill-replica GPU memory underutilized since only decode replicas store KV cache; the paper explicitly defers a quantitative chunked-prefill-vs-disaggregation comparison to future work. Notes that a subsequent system, APIServe, adopted chunked prefills from Sarathi for ahead-of-time prefill recomputation in multi-turn API serving. Source released at github.com/microsoft/sarathi-serve. `review` (12343 words)
- [[taming-the-titans-a-survey-of-efficient-llm-inference-serving]] — Taming the Titans: A Survey of Efficient LLM Inference Serving — Soochow University / Huawei Cloud survey (arXiv 2504.19720, Apr 2025) providing a hierarchical taxonomy of LLM inference serving research: instance-level (model placement/parallelism, request scheduling, decoding-length prediction, KV-cache memory management/reuse/compression, prefill-decode disaggregation), cluster-level (heterogeneous GPU scheduling, load balancing, cloud/edge serving), emerging scenarios (long context, RAG, MoE, LoRA, speculative decoding, agentic/augmented LLMs, test-time reasoning), and miscellaneous areas (hardware, privacy, simulators, fairness, energy). Credits Kwon et al. 2023 (PagedAttention/vLLM) as the origin of OS-inspired paging for near-zero KV-cache fragmentation and of lossless multi-request cache sharing; documents vLLM's reliance on block-level memory management as the ancestor of radix-tree prefix-sharing systems. Catalogs disaggregation systems by name: DistServe (Zhong et al. 2024) optimizes per-phase resource allocation and placement by bandwidth; Splitwise explores homogeneous/heterogeneous device splits; DejaVu addresses pipeline bubbles via microbatch swapping; Mooncake (Qin et al. 2024) is explicitly named as KVCache-centric, leveraging idle CPU/DRAM/SSD for distributed KV-cache storage with early-rejection load shedding; TetriInfer and P/D-Serve round out the disaggregation list. Chunked-prefill (Agrawal et al. 2024c / Sarathi-Serve, cited as OSDI'24) and Dynamic SplitFuse (DeepSpeed) are both named as the techniques that merge prefill fragments with decoding to avoid pausing. Notes LoRA serving work (CaraServe, dLoRA) separately from S-LoRA. Defines the standard evaluation metrics vocabulary (TTFT, TBT, TPOT, throughput, goodput, percentile latencies) used across the field. As a survey it is itself secondary literature, not first-person deployment evidence; useful chiefly as an index into ~150 named systems/papers and for confirming which technique originated with which paper. `review` (13203 words)
- [[technical-performance-the-2026-ai-index-report-stanford-hai]] — Technical Performance | The 2026 AI Index Report | Stanford HAI — PRIMARY SOURCE: Stanford HAI's 2026 AI Index Report, Technical Performance chapter -- an independent academic/nonprofit research effort, no commercial inference-platform interest. Confirms and slightly refines the open-vs-closed capability gap figure cited by Forte Group: as of March 2026, 'the top closed model leads the top open model by 3.3%, up from 0.5% in August 2024' (the gap 'reopened in 2025 after briefly closing in 2024'); six of the top ten Arena Leaderboard models are now closed. Gives exact Arena Elo scores for the top tier: Anthropic 1,503, xAI 1,495, Google 1,494, OpenAI 1,481, Alibaba 1,449, DeepSeek 1,424 -- four companies clustered within 25 Elo points of each other as of March 2026. Separately notes the US-China model-performance gap has 'effectively closed': the top US model leads by only 2.7% as of March 2026, having fluctuated in the single digits since DeepSeek-R1 briefly matched the top US model in February 2025. Flags that benchmarks used to measure AI progress face reliability/gaming concerns, with invalid-question rates ranging from 2% (MMLU Math) to 42% (GSM8K), and that Arena leaderboard standing may partly reflect platform-adaptation rather than general capability -- a caveat relevant to any build-vs-buy capability-gap argument that leans on Arena rankings as ground truth. `review` (747 words)
- [[tensorrt-llmdocssourcefeaturesdisagg-servingmd-at-main-nvidiatensorrt-llm-github]] — TensorRT-LLM/docs/source/features/disagg-serving.md at main · NVIDIA/TensorRT-LLM · GitHub — PRIMARY SOURCE: official NVIDIA/TensorRT-LLM docs (docs/source/features/disagg-serving.md, main branch) describing the disaggregated-serving coordinator/fleet architecture. Confirms TensorRT-LLM's own disaggregated-server design has a first-class notion of a 'KV-cache-aware router' with 'the single ZMQ event-ingest endpoint' owned by the coordinator process: 'Coordinator -- a single process that owns all cluster state: the ctx/gen routers, worker readiness, and (for the KV-cache-aware router) the single ZMQ event-ingest endpoint.' Fleet workers 'compute the routing key locally (e.g. block hashes) and delegate the placement decision to the coordinator over HTTP.' This confirms the ZMQ KV-event ingest path is part of TensorRT-LLM's own documented production architecture (kv_cache_aware and conversation are named stateful router types), not just an external llm-d-side assumption. The doc does not mention llm-d, vLLM, or SGLang anywhere -- it documents TRT-LLM's self-contained disaggregated serving + its own internal KV-cache-aware router, a different consumer than llm-d's router. `evergreen` (3659 words)
- [[text-embeddings-inference-hugging-face]] — Text Embeddings Inference · Hugging Face — HF's official TEI docs-index page: restates the same feature set as the GitHub README (no model-graph compile step, small Docker images/fast boot, token-based dynamic batching, Flash Attention/Candle/cuBLASLt kernels, Safetensors loading, OTel tracing + Prometheus metrics) at thinner detail; no licensing statement or named adopter on this page. `evergreen` (292 words)
- [[the-build-vs-buy-llm-infrastructure-decision-most-teams-get-wrong]] — The Build-vs-Buy LLM Infrastructure Decision Most Teams Get Wrong — Independent engineering blog post (tianpan.co, April 2026) arguing both 'stay on APIs too long' and 'self-host too early' are common failure modes, with staged break-even thresholds. Startup stage (<$10K/mo API spend): self-hosting has 34-month payback for a 70B model, APIs win decisively. Growth stage ($10K-$50K/mo): hybrid routing — bulk/low-complexity traffic to self-hosted 7B-13B models, frontier tasks stay on API; a single H100 running a 13B model at 50% GPU utilization covers ~8,000 conversations/day at $1,500-$5,000/month including staff. Scale stage ($50K+/mo, 100M+ tokens/month): self-hosting bulk traffic is 'almost mandatory for margin' against GPT-5-Mini/Haiku-tier pricing (3-5x cheaper) but self-hosting LOSES against DeepSeek V3-tier pricing ($0.28/M input) for commodity tasks — the piece calls this 'the uncomfortable 2026 reality' that price-competitive providers have reset the math. Cites a (unsourced, peer-reviewed claim) finding that hardware+electricity are only 20-30% of total self-hosting cost across 54 deployment scenarios, with staff the dominant 70-80%. Details CUDA/driver maintenance tax (1-3 engineering days per model upgrade, 8-20 hrs/month steady-state), notes HF's TGI entered maintenance mode Dec 2025 (HF recommends vLLM/SGLang), and ranks serving frameworks by concurrency collapse: Ollama throughput collapses ~20x below vLLM at 10+ concurrent users (head-of-line blocking), llama.cpp ~44x below vLLM at high concurrency. Lists 14 external references (GPU pricing trackers, red hat framework comparisons, TCO blogs) as citation leads. `review` (2112 words)
- [[the-early-bird-catches-the-leak-unveiling-timing]] — The Early Bird Catches the Leak: Unveiling Timing — Primary source for the EarlyBird attack cited by KVGov. First security analysis of LLM serving performance optimizations across multiple users, identifying two exploitable timing side channels: (1) KV-cache sharing across requests with common prefixes, and (2) semantic caching that serves cached responses for similar queries (with cache hits producing a sharp TTFT drop from ~5s to under 1s that far exceeds network jitter). Introduces a token-by-token search algorithm (Predict-and-Sample-Attack, PSA) that uses a next-token predictor plus a TTFT classifier to iteratively recover shared system/victim prompts one token at a time, and a Prompt Nearest-neighbor Attack (PNA) targeting semantic caches by testing privacy-related prompt templates and monitoring TTFT for semantic-match hits. Black-box evaluation against popular online LLM services (via SGLang backend) reports a token-recovery success rate of 89.0% with a false positive rate of 0.04, averaging 5.64 guesses and 112.72 attack queries per recovered token (n=10 trials per position), at an attack cost of approximately /bin/bash.16/token with OpenAI o1, /bin/bash.012 with OpenAI o3-mini, and /bin/bash.001 with DeepSeek-Chat (pricing as of March 5, 2025). Across 200 victim prompts, the attack recovered an average of 11.32 tokens for the top 100 prompts and 17.84 tokens for the top 50, with a maximum of 81 tokens recovered for a single prompt using 519 total guesses.
