Using an untuned generic predictor (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct) instead of a fine-tuned one drops recovery rate to 69% and raises average guesses per token from 5.64 to 163.48 (5,231.30 attack queries), demonstrating the attack degrades but remains viable without a specialized predictor. Also demonstrates cache eviction can be forced without privileged APIs: sending 15 irrelevant ~200-token requests reliably (100% of 10,000 tests) triggers SGLang's default eviction policy within about 5 seconds, letting an attacker reset cache state as part of the attack loop. `evergreen` (13639 words)
- [[the-experimentation-phase-is-over-key-findings-from-1200-production-deployments]] — The Experimentation Phase Is Over: Key Findings from 1,200 Production Deployments - ZenML Blog — ZenML's condensed executive-summary companion (Dec 19, 2025, ~1,545 words) to the longer 'What 1,200 Production Deployments Reveal' post -- same six themes (engineering fundamentals over AI skill, context engineering, infrastructure guardrails, evaluation maturity, MCP stabilisation, not waiting for frontier models) in abbreviated form, same named case studies (Stripe, nib, Ramp, Loblaws, Sentry, Digits, Cox Automotive, Robinhood, Care Access). AUDITED ABSENCE confirmed: like its longer companion, this piece contains no aggregate percentage-of-deployments statistic with stated denominator/methodology for which inference engines (vLLM/SGLang/TensorRT-LLM/etc.), API gateways, or orchestration frameworks organizations actually use -- despite synthesizing patterns across 1,200 catalogued case studies. ZenML's LLMOps-database aggregate content, across both posts fetched in this batch, never quantifies inference-stack-component prevalence; any 'X% of deployments use Y' figure for this research topic will have to come from a different corpus (e.g. a16z/vendor survey, Stack Overflow/JetBrains developer surveys, or direct tally of the database's own tag taxonomy) rather than from ZenML's own narrative posts. `review` (1559 words)
- [[the-llm-is-extraordinary-the-bank-cant-use-it]] — The LLM Is Extraordinary. The Bank Can't Use It. — Mycroft Project (Substack, May 5 2026), companion piece to the infrastructure-decisions post, again built on an EXPLICITLY FICTIONALIZED/COMPOSITE scenario (a tier-one bank tech executive's vendor demo), not a verified named case. Defines the 'trust boundary' as the compounding set of legal/regulatory/contractual obligations -- MNPI information-barrier rules, GDPR/CCPA (legal basis + DPA + cross-border transfer mechanism), bank secrecy/confidentiality obligations, and SR 11-7 model risk management (argues SR 11-7 makes it 'practically impossible to validate and monitor a black-box external LLM used for credit decisioning support') -- that makes routing sensitive data to an external LLM API a compliance event regardless of the vendor's SOC 2/DPA paperwork. Names three architectural patterns financial institutions actually use, in order of operational complexity: (1) on-premise open-weight deployment (names Llama and Mistral explicitly as the open-weight families enabling this) -- no data leaves the building, full compliance control, but the institution owns all serving/versioning/monitoring; viable mainly for large institutions with existing GPU infra and MLOps maturity; (2) private-cloud dedicated tenancy -- physically/logically isolated hardware with contractually committed data residency, cloud-provider-operated but institution-governed, as a middle path; (3) federated inference with RAG -- a smaller on-prem model (e.g., 7B class, explicitly stated as not matching GPT-4 on complex reasoning) retrieves from internal documents/databases so sensitive data never leaves the boundary, trading capability for compliance posture. Argues the two axes vendors conflate are 'security' (SOC 2, DPA) versus 'sovereignty' (legally defensible proof that specific data was processed under specific controls within the institution's compliance perimeter), and that most vendor 'private deployment' offers are a dedicated namespace in multi-tenant cloud rather than genuine isolation. Recommends a portfolio approach: public cloud LLMs for low-sensitivity workloads (internal knowledge management, code assistance) vs on-prem/private-cloud for MNPI/customer-data/regulated decisioning. `review` (1729 words)
- [[the-open-weight-models-that-matter-june-2026]] — The Open Weight Models that Matter: June 2026 — OpenRouter editorial roundup (Chris Clark, June 27 2026, updated June 29) of the four open-weight models it judges most load-bearing as of June 2026, with pricing/throughput from OpenRouter's own routing data and intelligence scores from Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index v4.1. (1) DeepSeek V4 Flash: MIT-licensed ~284B/~13B-active MoE, 1M context, 79.0% SWE-bench Verified (Pro variant 80.6%), first-party API $0.14/$0.28 per M tokens in/out but retains/trains on data routed through China; Western no-train hosts (Fireworks, Together, DeepInfra) charge ~2x. (2) GLM 5.2 (Z AI): #1 open-weight on AA Intelligence Index v4.1 (51), near-frontier agentic coding, released days after a US export-control directive forced Anthropic to disable Claude Fable 5/Mythos 5 for foreign nationals -- framed as a geopolitical tailwind for MIT-licensed alternatives. (3) MiniMax M3: ~428B/~23B-active MoE, native image/video input, 1M context via 'MiniMax Sparse Attention', licensed under MiniMax Community License (commercial use needs attribution; large commercial products need written authorization) rather than MIT. (4) NVIDIA Nemotron 3 Ultra: 550B/55B-active hybrid Mamba-2+Transformer MoE, OpenMDW license, trained with NVFP4, positioned as the leading US-built open-weight entrant, released with full data/recipes/eval tooling/RL infra to drive demand for NVIDIA's inference stack (NIM, CUDA, AI Enterprise). States the open-weight-to-frontier capability gap has held steady at 3-6 months for over 18 months rather than widening. `review` (1954 words)
- [[the-workloadrouterpool-architecture]] — The Workload–Router–Pool Architecture — vLLM Semantic Router project 'vision paper' (arXiv:2603.21354v2, March 2026, authors from MBZUAI/McGill/Mila/UChicago/Tensormesh) proposing the Workload-Router-Pool (WRP) framework as a retrospective organizing scheme for ~20 of the project's own prior papers on semantic/content-based routing (signal-driven routing, hallucination gating, safety classification, semantic caching) and fleet cost optimization (token-budget pool routing claiming 17-39% GPU-instance reduction on production traces; FleetOpt claiming 6-82% GPU cost savings from two-pool fleets vs homogeneous; a '1/W law' claiming tokens-per-watt halves per context-length doubling). TANGENTIAL to the router-backend-discovery question: this paper is about which MODEL to route a request to (semantic/quality-based model selection, safety, cost) and fleet capacity planning, not about the mechanics of how a router discovers or addresses backend workers — it contains no discussion of service discovery, etcd, static URL lists, or Kubernetes-independent worker addressing. Its only connection to llm-d is a single bibliography citation ([42], the llm-d team's 'KV-Cache wins you can see' blog post) referenced in passing. The 'Pool' dimension (homogeneous vs. heterogeneous GPU, disaggregated P/D, KV-cache topology — citing Mooncake and Mélange as external systems) is conceptually adjacent to backend abstraction but is treated purely as a fleet-provisioning/cost variable, not a router-implementation concern. Useful mainly as evidence of a broader industry pattern of decoupling routing POLICY from routing MECHANISM, but does not provide backend-discovery implementation detail. `evergreen` (23023 words)
- [[this-paper-is-included-in-the-proceedings-of-the]] — This paper is included in the Proceedings of the — Orca (Yu, Jeong, Kim, Kim, Chun — Seoul National University + FriendliAI, OSDI'22, usenix.org/system/files/osdi22-yu.pdf) is the foundational origin paper cited by nearly every other paper in this batch (DistServe, Sarathi-Serve, Mooncake, S-LoRA, and the 'Taming the Titans' survey all build directly on its iteration-level scheduling) and predates vLLM by roughly a year. Problem: existing serving systems (e.g., Triton + FasterTransformer) schedule execution at request granularity, dispatching a fixed batch to the engine and only returning results once the ENTIRE batch finishes — a mismatch for autoregressive generation where different requests need different numbers of iterations, meaning early-finishing requests cannot return to the client, and newly-arrived requests must wait for the whole batch to drain. Core contribution: iteration-level scheduling, which invokes the execution engine for only a single model iteration (not a full request) at a time, letting the serving system add newly-arrived requests and return finished ones after every single iteration rather than waiting for batch completion. This creates a new problem — a batch under iteration-level scheduling can contain requests that have each processed a different number of prior tokens, which breaks the standard batched-attention tensor operation (its inputs have variable shapes per request). Orca's fix is 'selective batching': apply batching to all Transformer operations EXCEPT Attention, which is processed per-request individually since Attention has no model parameters and thus gains nothing from batched weight-reuse; the paper reports this costs only a small efficiency hit. Orca also layers on intra-layer and inter-layer model parallelism (borrowed from training systems) for scaling to GPT-3-class models. Evaluated on OpenAI GPT-3 models scaled up to 341B parameters: Orca achieves a 36.9x throughput improvement over NVIDIA FasterTransformer at the same latency — the specific number that establishes iteration-level/continuous batching as a step-change improvement over the request-level batching that preceded it, and the technique every downstream engine in this batch (vLLM's continuous batching, Sarathi-Serve's stall-free scheduling, DistServe's per-phase batching) explicitly builds on or modifies. `review` (13891 words)
- [[thomson-continual-learning-of-frontier-models-for]] — Thomson: Continual Learning of Frontier Models for — LONG PRIMARY SOURCE (66,199 words / 436KB body -- flagged for source-analyst delegation). arXiv:2608.27147 (Aug 27 2026), first-person technical report by Thomson Reuters researchers (with Imperial College London, DatologyAI, Lambda) titled 'Thomson: Continual Learning of Frontier Models for SovereignAI.' Core thesis: 'SovereignAI' (an organization's capability to independently build, deploy, and govern its own AI) is achievable via Continual Learning on open-weight checkpoints rather than pre-training from scratch or shallow fine-tuning, at compute/personnel budgets far below common assumption. Built two open-weight models, Thomson-1.0-Small (35B) and Thomson-1.0-Large, starting from Qwen3.5-397B and Qwen3.6-35B checkpoints, via continued pre-training ('mid-training'), value realignment, and post-training. A team of at most three dozen engineers/scientists, using no more than 368 B200 GPUs at any point, completed development in three months. The final training run (three weeks of GPU time for Thomson-1.0-Large) cost under 50,000; total development cost (staff, compute, domain-expert compensation, vendor partnerships, reusable infra) was approximately 0 million. Data curation partner DatologyAI helped curate a 200-billion-token mid-training set from a pool of more than 19 trillion tokens of permissively-licensed public and proprietary data. Capability drift/preservation was tracked throughout training via 'CapTrack,' a capability-centric meta-benchmark organizing existing community benchmarks into three dimensions -- CAN (latent competence), WILL (default behavioral preferences), HOW (protocol compliance/execution) -- rather than a single new benchmark. Reports that Thomson-1.0-Large, evaluated on an independent agentic benchmark suite, surpasses or matches flagship closed/open frontier releases including Claude Sonnet 5, GLM-5.2, GPT-5.5, DeepSeek V4 Pro, and Gemini 3.1 Pro on a range of professional-work tasks (legal, tax, multilingual, Deep Research, agentic), describing a 'π-shaped' improvement pattern (broad gains across many capabilities, not just the targeted domain) with minimal catastrophic forgetting -- unlike narrow fine-tuning. Explicitly avoids large-scale distillation from a stronger teacher model and uses full-weight updates rather than parameter-efficient methods (e.g. LoRA), on the grounds that distillation cannot surpass its teacher. Frames the whole pipeline as a replicable blueprint for other institutions (not just hyperscalers) to own a competitive, private foundation model stack. `review` (66217 words)
- [[tiered-kv-cache-offloading-in-vllm-vllm-blog]] — Tiered KV Cache Offloading in vLLM | vLLM Blog — vLLM blog (Sept 2026) on the tiered KV cache offloading framework (in vLLM since v0.22), which is the most directly relevant single source for llm-d routing: it explicitly names llm-d and Dynamo as consumers of the KV events this framework emits to make cache-aware routing decisions. Core design: 'host-centric' -- all KV data flows through CPU DRAM as the primary tier, with secondary tiers (filesystem, S3-compatible object storage via NIXL, or peer-to-peer RDMA) cascading from/promoting to that host copy; accelerator memory is never touched directly by secondary tiers. Operates on 'chunks' (default = one accelerator block; configurable via blocks_per_chunk). Host primary tier is a proper LRU/ARC cache, not a staging buffer -- evicted chunks survive in whichever secondary tier already received them. Reload path: scheduler checks host cache first; on miss, queries secondary tiers in configured order, returns RETRY while the tier promotes the chunk back to host. Different chunks of one request can be served by different tiers. Canonical host memory layout (page = one block of one layer, all KV heads across TP ranks gathered contiguously) means nodes with different TP/accelerator/attention-backend configs share KV data with no remapping. P2P tier explicitly states the tier itself does not choose which peer to pull from -- that is the orchestration layer's (e.g., llm-d router) job, driven through the request's kv_transfer_params; used both for classic prefill/decode disaggregation and cross-node load balancing (pulling chunks from an overloaded instance to one with spare capacity). KV events report which chunks were stored/evicted, from which tier, and with what locality (local vs remote) -- llm-d and Dynamo consume these to route requests to the instance most likely to have a cache hit, and llm-d additionally uses them to orchestrate P2P KV transfers between peers. Reports a benchmark (Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, 2x H100 TP=2, multi-turn 12K-prompt/4K-round conversations): up to ~64 concurrent conversations, all caching methods perform well (HBM holds working set); at 64-128, HBM saturates and only CPU offloading sustains throughput; beyond 128, CPU cache also saturates and storage offloading more than doubles throughput versus alternatives by maintaining a high cache-hit ratio despite storage's higher per-access latency. `evergreen` (1908 words)
- [[token-budget-management-and-cost-attribution-for-on-premises-llm-inference]] — Token Budget Management and Cost Attribution for On-Premises LLM Inference — Generic consulting-firm guidance (SysArt Consulting, published/last-reviewed May 7 2026, no named client or real deployment described -- lower-authority 'audited absence' source: it prescribes practices in the abstract rather than reporting what any specific organization actually runs). Lays out a four-stage mechanism for on-prem shared-GPU token budgeting applied 'in that order': metering, attribution, budgeting, enforcement. Metering happens at an API gateway sitting between clients and inference backends, recording input/output token counts (via the serving framework's own tokenizer -- names vLLM, TGI, and Triton as frameworks that expose token counts in response metadata, warning that word-count approximation compounds attribution error), model id, requesting team/service identity, and latency, stored in a time-series DB (Prometheus/InfluxDB/TimescaleDB) with 30-day per-request retention and 12-month hourly/per-team aggregates. Cost attribution models a 'fully loaded cost per token' (GPU lease/depreciation + electricity + cooling + networking + storage + ops time, divided by total tokens) and claims visibility alone, before any enforcement, 'typically reduces aggregate consumption by 15-25%' (no citation given for this figure). Budget design is a named three-tier quota: guaranteed quota (never queued/rejected up to this limit), burst quota (best-effort when cluster has headroom), and hard cap (absolute ceiling). Enforcement flow at the gateway: within-guaranteed proceeds immediately; burst-exhausted-but-headroom proceeds at lower scheduling priority; hard-cap-exceeded returns 429 with Retry-After. Recommends token reservation (pre-estimate total cost of a request and reserve against quota before inference starts) to stop concurrent-request quota overshoot, and cost-aware client libraries that expose remaining quota and pre-submission cost estimates to developers. `review` (1432 words)
- [[tokenlake-a-unified-segment-level-prefix-cache-pool]] — TokenLake: A Unified Segment-level Prefix Cache Pool — TokenLake (Peking University + StepFun) diagnoses three structural failure modes of instance-local cache-aware routing (the prefix-tree-per-instance model used by systems like llm-d's KV-cache-aware router, SGLang, Mooncake): (1) load imbalance -- instances with high prefix hit rates become memory-bandwidth over-utilized while cold instances sit idle, because the router can only route whole requests to whichever instance already holds the matching prefix tree; (2) redundancy -- to rebalance computation, hot shared prefixes (system prompts, shared documents) get replicated across multiple instances, wasting memory; (3) fragmentation -- because a full prefix must reside on one instance to form a contiguous prefix tree, memory slots scattered across instances cannot be pooled together. These problems compound under PD (prefill/decode) disaggregation, since prefill and decode instances have different memory-bandwidth profiles and must each separately hold the same prefix cache to avoid recomputation. TokenLake's fix decouples cache management from scheduling via a declarative interface (exposing query tensors, prefix caches, and cache-aware ops) and manages cache at sub-request segment granularity with a heavy-hitter-aware load-balancing algorithm, letting the scheduler dispatch elastically without needing to track prefix locality. On real-world workloads (CharacterAI/Kimi report 75-95% production hit rates as baseline context), TokenLake improves throughput up to 2.6x and 2.0x and cache hit rate up to 2.0x and 2.1x versus state-of-the-art cache-aware routing and cache-centric PD-disaggregation baselines respectively. `evergreen` (11567 words)
- [[top-7-self-hosted-and-open-source-ai-coding-agents-in-2026-jun-24-2026]] — Top 7 Self-Hosted and Open-Source AI Coding Agents in 2026 | Jun 24, 2026 — Vendor blog post (OpenHands, June 2026, 'Top 7 Self-Hosted and Open-Source AI Coding Agents') -- self-promotional (OpenHands ranks itself #1) but contains a detailed, specific comparison table and per-tool writeups of 7 self-hostable coding agents, each noting license, self-hosting posture, and model flexibility rather than naming a single fixed backing model (all are explicitly model-agnostic/BYOK, routed to whichever provider or local model the operator configures): (1) OpenHands -- MIT license, 'Agent Canvas' local-first visual workspace, VPC/air-gapped deployment, model-agnostic, Enterprise tier adds an 'Agent Control Plane' (sandboxed runtimes, access control, audit, cost visibility); (2) OpenCode -- MIT, terminal TUI, most-starred open-source coding agent at 185,000+ GitHub stars, supports 75+ LLM providers via the Models.dev catalog plus fully-offline local inference through Ollama/LM Studio/llama.cpp, full LSP+AST verification, client-server architecture, but MCP servers can consume 10K+ tokens/turn; (3) Cline -- Apache 2.0 VS Code extension with millions of installs, Plan/Act modes, headless CLI 2.0 for CI/CD, 30+ providers (Anthropic/OpenAI/Gemini/Bedrock/Vertex) plus local Ollama/LM Studio, Enterprise tier adds RBAC/SSO/SCIM/audit logs/VPC/OpenTelemetry; (4) Aider -- Apache 2.0 terminal pair-programmer (Paul Gauthier, 2023), git-native (every AI edit auto-committed, /undo), 'Architect mode' splits planning (strong model) from editing (cheap/fast model), fully offline via Ollama/LM Studio; (5) Goose -- Apache 2.0 agent runtime originally built by Block, transferred to the Agentic AI Foundation at the Linux Foundation on April 7, 2026, written in Rust, 25+ model providers plus local inference, 70+ MCP extensions, subagents spawn up to 10 parallel isolated workers, Agent Client Protocol support lets it reuse existing Claude Code/Codex subscriptions, strictly local-first with no hosted option; (6) Kilo Code -- forked from Roo Code (itself forked from Cline), 500+ models across 60+ providers with mid-task model switching and claimed pass-through pricing, 'Memory Bank' stores architecture decisions in repo Markdown, but reliability reported as uneven and no local-model autocomplete support (per a June 13 2026 GitHub Discussions thread); (7) Tabby -- Apache 2.0, 33,600 GitHub stars, a code-completion server (not an autonomous agent) with newer 'Pochi' agent layered on for issue-to-PR workflows, repo-level RAG context, self-contained deployment (standalone binary/Docker/Homebrew) with CUDA/ROCm/Apple Metal backends; explicitly runs open models StarCoder, Qwen2.5-Coder, CodeLlama, and Codestral behind it, with Tabby's own model registry recommending 7B-13B parameter models on NVIDIA V100/A100/30-40-series GPUs for production, one GPU per instance (scale via multiple instances). Notes market churn as a risk of relying on open-source agent tooling: Continue.dev was acquired by Cursor and Roo Code was archived in May 2026, both mid-2026, stranding teams that had built workflows on them. States explicitly that self-hosting the agent runtime does not guarantee prompts/code/logs stay private -- the model-inference path must be separately validated as local/private. `review` (4110 words)
- [[torch-conference-north-america-vllm-production]] — Torch Conference North America: vLLM Production — Futurum Group analyst insight (AI-generated, unreviewed per its own disclosure; published August 29, 2026) on the PyTorch Conference North America 2026 (San Jose, Oct 20-21) session guide, arguing vLLM has crossed from research project to 'de facto' multi-vendor production inference infrastructure. Enterprise contributor list for vLLM sessions: Red Hat, IBM, NVIDIA, Mistral AI, Amazon, Huawei, Meta, Google. Technical disclosures cited from the session guide: 'KV Push' reduces time-to-first-token, with disaggregated prefill/decode reported to Pareto-dominate co-located serving on Nemotron across concurrency levels; IBM's tiered KV cache offloading framework is now integrated upstream with no external dependencies, routing transfers through CPU memory; LMCache extends prompt caching across vLLM, SGLang, and TensorRT-LLM, connecting to Mooncake, Redis, and AWS S3. Hardware-portability sessions (reported only as software/stack evidence per scope): Google Cloud TPUs via TorchTPU, AWS Trainium via vLLM-Neuron, IBM Spyre via Torch-Spyre, Arm CPUs via vLLM+OpenVINO on AWS Graviton3e (~2x throughput vs baseline), and FlagOS reporting 5-40% inference improvement over vendor-default adaptation across 20+ chips. CAVEAT: this is a secondary analyst write-up of a conference SESSION GUIDE (not a first-person engineering account), authored by an LLM and explicitly unreviewed by Futurum editors per its own disclosure -- treat technical claims as pointers to the underlying PyTorch blog/sessions, not as verified primary fact. `review` (1688 words)
- [[trust-standalone-epp-selected-destinations-by-danehans-pull-request-1658-agentga]] — Trust standalone EPP-selected destinations by danehans · Pull Request #1658 · agentgateway/agentgateway · GitHub — agentgateway PR #1658 (merged Apr 24 2026, danehans/solo.io) adds an explicit inferenceRouting destinationMode config. Default mode stays 'endpoint-validated': agentgateway cross-checks the EPP-selected destination against locally mirrored Kubernetes Endpoints/workload data before routing. The new 'passthrough' mode makes agentgateway use the EPP-selected destination ip:port directly as the proxy backend without requiring any mirrored local endpoint data — i.e. agentgateway does not need to know about the backend via k8s Endpoints/EndpointSlice at all, only the EPP's decision. This is the mechanism that lets a standalone (non-InferencePool) EPP own endpoint discovery for non-pod backends. Fixes issue #620; e2e-tested against kubernetes-sigs/gateway-api-inference-extension PR #2882 ('Add standalone agentgateway proxy support'). A companion issue #1661 'Support fail-open fallback for standalone EPP inference routing' was opened alongside this PR, indicating maintainers explicitly designed passthrough mode with a fail-open path in mind (what happens when EPP is unreachable) as a separate follow-on concern from trusting EPP's picks. `evergreen` (1345 words)
- [[understanding-llminferenceservice-kserve]] — Understanding LLMInferenceService | KServe — KServe's own documentation (v0.20 docs) for LLMInferenceService, the CRD KServe added specifically for generative AI serving. States directly: 'Built on the foundation of llm-d -- a production-ready framework for scalable LLM serving -- LLMInferenceService delivers enterprise-grade capabilities for deploying and managing Large Language Model inference workloads on Kubernetes.' Documents KServe's deliberate dual-track CRD split: the legacy InferenceService CRD remains for 'Predictive AI' (scikit-learn, TensorFlow, PyTorch); LLMInferenceService is 'purpose-built for Generative AI workloads' and is the only path to prefill-decode disaggregation, multi-node orchestration, and intelligent (KV-cache-aware) scheduling -- InferenceService 'can still serve LLMs, but only for basic single-node deployments.' This is a direct, named instance of a broad existing platform absorbing a narrower orchestration-layer project's capabilities via CRD rather than building them independently, exactly matching llm-d's own stated non-goal of not duplicating KServe's platform. Confirms KServe's own governance milestone: a banner states 'KServe has joined CNCF!' (as an Incubating project, Nov 2025) -- places KServe and llm-d in the same CNCF governance structure but at different maturity levels (KServe Incubating vs llm-d Sandbox per other sources). Lists concrete production-oriented features: RBAC/authentication, model storage integration (HuggingFace/S3/PVC), KV cache transfer via RDMA, tensor/data/expert parallelism (the latter explicitly for MoE models like Mixtral and DeepSeek-R1), and a worked YAML example deploying Llama-3.1-8B across 3 replica pods with a managed gateway/route/scheduler. `evergreen` (1106 words)
- [[unlocking-real-time-predictions-with-shopifys-machine-learning-platform-shopify]] — Unlocking Real-time Predictions with Shopify's Machine Learning Platform - Shopify — Shopify Engineering blog (Mar 10, 2023, primary first-person source, 3138 words) on Merlin Online Inference, the real-time-serving extension of Shopify's Ray-based ML platform Merlin. Predates the LLM-specific era but establishes the general-model-serving tech stack later reused for LLM workloads: each use case gets a dedicated Kubernetes (GKE) service/namespace with its own autoscaling; the serving layer is built with one of two open-source options -- MLServer (Seldon's OSS inference server, supports the V2 inference protocol, REST/gRPC, 'no-code'/'low-code' model serving including a prebuilt HuggingFace runtime) or FastAPI (for fully custom APIs) -- chosen per use-case via a Merlin CLI/cookiecutter generator. Services are built into Docker images via Podman and deployed through Shopify's internal Buildkite -> Shipit CI/CD pipeline; a Comet ML-based model registry and a Feast-based online/offline feature store (Pano) are the other open-source components integrated into the platform. Roadmap explicitly names Ray Serve, Seldon Core, and BentoML as open-source options under evaluation for ensemble/multi-model inference graphs. No LLM-specific inference engine (vLLM, TGI, etc.) is mentioned; this is a general ML-serving architecture precedent, useful as context for how Shopify's internal platform conventions (K8s-per-service, OSS serving frameworks, OpenAI-compatible-style model registry pattern) likely carried into their later LLM work. `review` (3138 words)

- [[using-self-hosted-large-language-models-llms-securely-in-government-digital-trad]] — Using self-hosted Large Language Models (LLMs) securely in Government – Digital trade — First-person account by a Data Scientist in the Department for Business and Trade (DBT), a UK central government department, describing DBT's deployment of self-hosted LLMs on an internal AWS SageMaker-based platform called Data Workspace. - [[what-1200-production-deployments-reveal-about-llmops-in-2025-zenml-blog]] — What 1,200 Production Deployments Reveal About LLMOps in 2025 - ZenML Blog — ZenML LLMOps-database trend synthesis (Dec 19, 2025, ~10.4k words) drawn from >1,200 catalogued case studies. Thematically organized around agent patterns rather than inference-engine tech stacks: (1) real engineering replacing POC demos, citing Stripe fraud detection (59%->97% card-testing accuracy), Amazon Rufus (moved from a custom in-house LLM to multi-model orchestration across Amazon Nova + Claude + specialized models, scaling to 80,000 Trainium chips for 250M users), ByteDance (multimodal LLMs on AWS Inferentia2, 50% cost reduction via tensor parallelism/INT8 quantization/static batching -- hardware-layer, out of this run's scope), and Cursor's Tab (400M req/day, 28% acceptance gain from online RL); (2) context engineering (just-in-time injection, tool masking/'choice entropy', compaction vs. summarization, file-system-as-context) as the successor discipline to prompt engineering; (3) MCP's 'quiet stabilisation' at ~1 year old, with Loblaws wrapping 50+ internal APIs and Sentry's MCP server scaling to 60M req/month with a 3-person team, versus Digits explicitly rejecting MCP/A2A for production because 'all our data is internal and major security questions remain unresolved'; (4) evals/guardrails moving from prompts into infrastructure (session-tainting, dual-layer permissions, circuit breakers); (5) a thesis that software-engineering/distributed-systems skill, not AI research skill, is the production bottleneck. Contains NO aggregate percentage-of-deployments-use-X statistics with stated denominator/methodology for inference engines, gateways, or orchestration layers -- that is an absence worth flagging, since the companion post it links to ('The Experimentation Phase Is Over: Key Findings from 1,200 Production Deployments') may carry such figures and was not in this batch. `review` (10402 words)
- [[what-early-open-weight-model-deployments-reveal-about-production-ai]] — What Early Open Weight Model Deployments Reveal About Production AI — Fastino (vendor) blog, Sept 9 2026, surveying NAMED companies' open-weight deployments: Uber, AT&T, Amplitude, Shopify, Pinterest, Harvey, Thomson Reuters. Cites McKinsey May 2026 Enterprise AI FinOps survey: 93% of qualified respondents exceeded AI budgets; spend rises ~4x moving from isolated use cases to enterprise-wide rollout. (2) post-training on proprietary data -- Harvey post-trained Kimi K3 via RL on ~1,750 synthetic/public/expert legal-task environments to build 'Tenet,' reporting it solves ~2x as many held-out legal-agent tasks as the base model; separately post-trained Qwen3.8-27B for firm-knowledge retrieval, reporting 58% fewer tokens/trajectory and 90% lower cost/query. (3) continual learning -- Thomson Reuters' technical report (arXiv:2608.27147) describes building its 'Thomson' model from Qwen checkpoints via value realignment, mid-training and post-training, using a 'CapTrack' framework and curating 200B tokens from a 19T-token pool; final 3-week GPU training run cost under $450K but total development (staff, compute, domain experts, infra) cost ~$40M. UK Business Data Survey 2026: 73% of businesses uncomfortable with data being used to train external AI models. `review` (2291 words)
- [[what-we-learned-from-our-inference-experiment]] — What we learned from our Inference Experiment — Hetzner engineering blog (Aug 17 2026), a DevOps engineer's first-person retrospective on Hetzner's public-facing 'Inference Experiment' (free OpenAI-spec LLM API on Hetzner-owned EU infrastructure), with background on Hetzner's internal inference platform history. States Hetzner built an internal inference platform using 'years of Kubernetes experience' to test server configs and LLM-serving software, eventually settling on a stack of GEX131 servers + vLLM + Open WebUI for internal production-grade applications and model benchmarking -- this predates and underlies the public Experiment. For the public Inference Experiment, models served: Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, Kimi-K2.7-Code, DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731 (Kimi K3 was excluded due to license terms incompatible with free offering). In the week before launch, 'routing for inference requests was greatly improved by introducing llm-d and making changes to some routing components we had developed in-house' -- llm-d explicitly named as adopted OSS, paired with custom in-house routing changes (unspecified mechanism). KV-cache offloading into host RAM is cited as a promised performance improvement, engine unspecified for this feature (likely vLLM, not stated explicitly). Operational findings: demand exceeded expectations within 6 hours; TTFT p99 spiked to ~10 minutes at peak (effectively unusable); they reduced max parallel requests per instance after observing that lower concurrency per instance sometimes raised net throughput; no prompt data is logged, only token metrics. Separately, OpenClaw (a third-party agent/CLI product Hetzner offers as a preconfigured instance) is described as having persistent code-quality problems with frequent upstream bugs -- flagged as a software-quality complaint about a dependency, not Hetzner's own inference stack. `review` (2048 words)
- [[why-i-stopped-self-hosting-ai-models-and-you-probably-should-too-dev-community]] — Why I Stopped Self-Hosting AI Models (And You Probably Should Too) - DEV Community — PERSONAL/HOBBY-SCALE anecdote (dev.to, author 'shadie_ai', a self-described DevOps engineer running SIDE PROJECTS, not an enterprise deployment). Bought a used RTX 3090 ($450) + PSU upgrade ($120) = $570 hardware, ran Ollama with Llama2/Llama3 7B-13B models. Reports a concrete concurrency-collapse data point at n=10 concurrent requests: self-hosted average latency 12s / p99 38s with half of requests timing out, versus GPT-4o-mini API average 0.8s / p99 1.5s with zero failures. A 13B model with CPU-offloaded layers took 45 seconds to summarize a 200-word email. Three-month hidden-cost tally: electricity $40-60/mo + cloud backup for weights $10/mo + ~5 hrs/week self-valued debugging time ($250/mo at freelance rates) = $300-320/month all-in, versus a $1/month API plan for the same low-traffic hobby workload. Model-update treadmill anecdote: swapping Llama 2 to Llama 3 in Ollama took a full evening (re-download weights, reformat Ollama modelfile, fix prompt template, rewrite backend parsing, rerun test suite) versus a 5-minute model-string change via API. Author explicitly carves out exceptions where self-hosting is still right: regulated/PHI data, zero-data-retention requirements, and 'enormous scale' (billions of tokens/day) — but argues these do not apply to 'the other 99%' of indie/startup developers. NOTE: contains an embedded self-promotional plug for a third-party API gateway domain the author claims to use personally; treated as unverified self-promotion, not evidence, and not a recommended source. `review` (1638 words)
- [[wispr-flow-creates-effortless-voice-dictation-with-llama-on-baseten]] — Wispr Flow creates effortless voice dictation with Llama on Baseten — Baseten's own case study on Wispr Flow (voice dictation app 'Flow'), a NEW named organization fitting the managed-middle pattern: Wispr Flow fine-tunes Meta's open-weight Llama models for real-time transcript cleanup/style-matching, then runs that fine-tuned model in PRODUCTION entirely on Baseten's managed dedicated deployments (on AWS workload planes), not self-hosted. Baseten supplies the serving stack: TensorRT-LLM engine builder, the Chains multi-step inference framework, autoscaling GPUs, and SOC 2 Type II / HIPAA-compliant regional infrastructure. Quantified results: full pipeline (speech recognition + Llama-based enhancement) runs end-to-end in under 700ms at p99, with the Llama step generating 100+ tokens in under 250ms. CTO Sahaj Garg is quoted explicitly choosing fine-tuning + managed dedicated hosting over building their own serving infra ('we no longer have to reserve GPUs just in case'). `review` (1033 words)
- [[with-rust-cloudflare-is-trying-to-tackle-the-industrys-inference-bottleneck]] — With Rust, Cloudflare is Trying to Tackle the Industry's Inference Bottleneck — SECONDARY, paywalled account (AIM, Sept 25 2025) of Cloudflare's Infire launch; adds one on-the-record quote from PM Mari Galicer not in Cloudflare's own post: Rust was chosen partly because Cloudflare already has engineers with deep Rust expertise, making it 'a worthwhile investment.' `review` (269 words)
- [[you-decided-to-run-the-llm-inside-now-what]] — You Decided to Run the LLM Inside. Now What? — Mycroft Project (Substack newsletter/practitioner essay, May 5 2026) framed around an EXPLICITLY FICTIONALIZED/COMPOSITE scenario ('a composite of deployment experiences observed across financial institutions,' not a named, verified case) of a regional-bank ML engineer deploying an on-prem LLM for credit analysis. Not empirical data -- an opinionated practitioner framework on five infrastructure decisions: (1) GPU sizing as capacity planning (provision for quantized-precision footprint + >=20% headroom + 2-3x peak concurrent volume + a second deployment unit for zero-downtime updates from day one -- illustrative figures given: 7B at INT8 ~7GB fits an A100 40GB; 70B at FP16 ~140GB needs 4x A100-80GB vs ~35GB at INT4 fitting one A100-80GB); (2) quantization as the lever determining on-prem economic viability, chosen per-workload (e.g., INT4 for summarization vs INT8 for credit-decisioning where regulatory scrutiny is higher); (3) serving framework choice as what separates a model file from a production system -- names vLLM (PagedAttention, default for high-concurrency), TGI (model-sharding across GPUs), and Triton Inference Server (NVIDIA-stack integration) as the three leading options; (4) monitoring architecture must separate infrastructure monitoring (Prometheus/Grafana/NVIDIA DCGM -- GPU utilization, latency percentiles, queue depth) from model behavior monitoring (hallucination rate, refusal rate, output-format violations, task-specific accuracy) -- explicitly ties ongoing model behavior monitoring to SR 11-7 model risk management obligations, arguing a deployment without it is 'an unmonitored model that happens to be running on internal infrastructure'; (5) model currency/update pipeline as a recurring responsibility (versioned, staged, blue-green/canary rollback) because open-weight models improve fast enough that defensibility under MRM scrutiny decays between releases. `review` (1895 words)
- [[bug-pd-multi-tier-supplies-nothing-when-the-producer-already-holds-the-blocks-is]] — [Bug]: PD Multi Tier supplies nothing when the producer already holds the blocks · Issue #52808 · vllm-project/vllm — Closed upstream vLLM GitHub issue #52808 (opened Aug 18 2026 by nilig, same reporter as llm-d-router#2438) diagnosing a root-cause defect in vLLMs PD Multi Tier KV-offload path (remote_decoder/remote_prefiller, secondary tier type=p2p, OffloadingConnector with TieringOffloadingSpec). When a producer already holds the requested prefix blocks (i.e. the prefix cache worked), it creates no store job, so the consumers fetch demand parks in the demanded map and is never matched -- the consumer waits out the hard-coded _LOAD_TIMEOUT_S=30.0s and recomputes locally. Effect is inverted from intent: the better the producers prefix cache, the more often PD Multi Tier fails to deliver. Root cause: P2PTierManager.on_new_request defaults offloading_context to BLOCK_LEVEL policy, which skips prefix-hit chunks from being offloaded (next_stored_chunk_idx stays at num_chunks), and grep confirms REQUEST_LEVEL is never declared by any tier (p2p/fs/obj/example) in v0.27.1 or main, so the cascade to request-level tiers never runs. Reproduced with isolated HTTP calls: warm producer -> consumer took 32.9s vs 0.16s cold; identical in a real P/D pair (30.2s/0 bytes warm vs 0.086s/256MiB cold). At scale (8 producers + 8 consumers, 10,800 requests, shared-prefix workload): fetch-demand match rate fell from 93.8% (cold) to 51.5% (warming) to 36.7% (warm) as producer cache warmed up. 6,083 load timeouts logged against 10,800 requests; PD Multi Tier ended up SLOWER than plain NIXL P/D on identical hardware (1571s vs 1032s wall time to completion; mean TPOT 92ms vs 10.7ms). Maintainer liranschour confirmed the fix direction: the p2p tier should declare REQUEST_LEVEL (bug, not by design). `evergreen` (1136 words)
- [[cibuild-enable-kv-events-in-release-wheels-by-ishandhanani-pull-request-4039-kvc]] — [CI/Build] Enable KV events in release wheels by ishandhanani · Pull Request #4039 · kvcache-ai/Mooncake · GitHub — Mooncake PR #4039 ('[CI/Build] Enable KV events in release wheels,' opened Sep 11 2026) treats ENABLE_KV_EVENTS as an already-working build flag and tries to wire it into official release-wheel CI; closed Sep 14 2026 in favor of direct successor PR #4060 ('Enable KV events in wheels with ZeroMQ dependencies'), which merged. - [[core-add-otel-genai-semantic-convention-metrics-dual-emit-by-shaurya856-pull-req]] — [Core] Add OTel GenAI semantic convention metrics (dual-emit) by Shaurya856 · Pull Request #53858 · vllm-project/vllm — vLLM PR #53858 ('[Core] Add OTel GenAI semantic convention metrics (dual-emit)', by Shaurya856), opened 2026-08-26, implementing the dual-emit approach proposed in RFC issue #51186. - [[experimental-enable-p2p-prefix-cache-sharing-llm-d]] — [Experimental] Enable P2P Prefix Cache Sharing | llm-d — Experimental (dev-version) llm-d well-lit-path doc on P2P prefix cache sharing, which lets a model server pull cached prefix KV blocks CPU-to-CPU over NIXL from a peer that already holds them, instead of recomputing, when prefix-aware routing can't co-locate the request with its owner (hot-prefix saturation, working-set overflow, session rebalancing). Mechanism: the request's originating pod is the KV cache source (computed the prefix, cached a copy in CPU tier); the router names that source on a later request sharing the prefix but scheduled elsewhere; the scheduled pod (consumer) pulls the prefix blocks rather than recomputing, with the source's GPU never touched, so a pull costs the source zero prefill capacity. Builds on the Tiered Prefix Cache path (vLLM OffloadingConnector with a CPU tier plus P2P secondary tier) and the Precise Prefix Cache Routing index; the p2p-source-producer selects a source from CPU-tier holders within one index block of the largest cached prefix, weighted to avoid overloading one source, and only fires when the source leads the scheduled pod by at least minCachedTokenDelta tokens; a failed pull degrades to a normal local miss/recompute. Gives concrete benchmark numbers: on measured H200/RDMA hardware with openai/gpt-oss-120b, the CPU-to-CPU pull was faster than recompute at every measured prefix length from 2,048 to 49,152 tokens, and on a warm 16-pod document Q&A benchmark, load-aware placement plus the pull delivered 1.5x better p99 TTFT and 35% higher throughput than prefix-affinity routing alone. Under P/D disaggregation, the prefill worker is the pull consumer (it computes prompt KV) while a decode worker can be the source for retained session history. `evergreen` (1020 words)

- [[framework-define-a-panic-isolation-posture-for-plugin-extension-points-issue-210]] — [Framework] Define a panic-isolation posture for plugin extension points · Issue #2105 · llm-d/llm-d-router — llm-d-router issue #2105 '[Framework] Define a panic-isolation posture for plugin extension points' (opened Jul 21 2026 by contributor LukeAVanDrie; label needs-triage; still open/unassigned/undecided as of fetch). Asks the maintainers to record a decision on how the EPP plugin framework should handle a panicking plugin, since 'a panicking plugin kills the EPP process from any extension point; nothing recovers plugin invocations anywhere in the framework' (flow control's dispatch goroutine holding queue mutexes is cited as the sharpest case, e.g. priorityqueue.go:76-78, eviction/queue.go:147, processor.go:371-383). THE LOAD-BEARING SENTENCE for router-customization/out-of-tree-plugin research is option 2 of the three proposed: 'Documented at-your-own-risk: in-tree plugins are the supported surface; out-of-tree plugins that panic take the process down, stated loudly in the plugin developer docs.' This is the single clearest maintainer/contributor articulation found of llm-d-router's plugin support boundary: it explicitly names 'out-of-tree plugins' as a real, contemplated category distinct from in-tree ones, and frames in-tree as 'the supported surface' while out-of-tree would be tolerated only on an undocumented-safety, at-your-own-risk basis if this option were adopted -- as of fetch time the issue is unresolved (needs-triage, no comments, no linked PR), so this is a PROPOSED framing under discussion, not a ratified project policy. Explicitly split out of issue #1187 (flow control's own goroutine recover, which the author notes 'incidentally backstops plugin panics on those goroutines regardless of this decision'). `evergreen` (467 words)

- [[mp-add-zmqkveventsink-re-emit-cache-events-in-vllm-kv-event-format-rebel-jinhwan]] — [MP] Add ZmqKVEventSink: re-emit cache events in vLLM KV-event format… · rebel-jinhwan/LMCache@4f5040f · GitHub — Commit on a THIRD-PARTY FORK (rebel-jinhwan/LMCache, referenced from upstream issues #3532 and #4352 on Aug 27 2026, but not itself an upstream LMCache/LMCache PR) implementing ZmqKVEventSink: a second CacheEventSink in lmcache/v1/mp_coordinator/kv_event_sink.py that re-emits the MP coordinator's internal CacheEventBatch stream as standard vLLM KVEventBatch msgpack over a ZMQ PUB socket on topic kv@<emitter_id>@<model> -- the exact wire format/topic llm-d's kv-cache indexer already subscribes to. The design doc's own text explicitly names 'llm-d's EPP' as a target consumer ('so an unmodified vLLM adapter indexes LMCache's tiers next to the engines' GPU tier'), citing LMCache issue #4352 as 'the RFC' -- but #4352 is Dynamo-specific and does not itself mention llm-d, so this llm-d claim is this fork author's own unverified assertion, not confirmed by LMCache maintainers or by the actual RFC. Medium strings are LMCache-tier-specific, not the standard gpu/cpu bucket: medium_for() returns 'lmcache-l1' for all L1 placements (tier-stable across DRAM/CXL backends) and 'lmcache-l2-<backend>' (e.g. 'lmcache-l2-fs') for L2, verified by unit tests (test_shared_l2_store_uses_backend_medium, test_medium_is_stable_between_store_and_delete). Includes BlockRemoved support (delete batches for both L1 eviction and L2 delete), a bounded-ring ROUTER-socket replay protocol matching vLLM's own replay semantics, and non-blocking PUB sends so a slow router can't stall the bus. Deliberately out of scope: engine identity via REGISTER_KV_CACHE, and crediting shared L2 fleet-wide via the coordinator's POST /events. No evidence in this diff that it was ever merged into LMCache/LMCache upstream; a raw-content check of LMCache's dev branch for lmcache/v1/mp_coordinator/kv_event_sink.py returned nothing, and the actual upstream fix that landed as a PR (see companion note on PR #5076) took a different, simpler approach using canonical medium="CPU" rather than these lmcache-l1/lmcache-l2-<backend> strings. `evergreen` (10381 words)
- [[metrics-add-operation-label-to-prometheus-latency-histograms-by-ashraf-bhuiyan-p]] — [Metrics] Add operation label to Prometheus latency histograms by ashraf-bhuiyan · Pull Request #52249 · vllm-project/vllm — vLLM PR #52249 ('[Metrics] Add operation label to Prometheus latency histograms', by ashraf-bhuiyan), opened 2026-08-14, the PR that #53858 was closed in favor of as 'further along'. - [[project-proposal-agent-router-previously-envoy-ai-gateway-issue-18-aaifproject-p]] — [Project Proposal] Agent Router (previously Envoy AI Gateway) · Issue #18 · aaif/project-proposals — AAIF (AI/Agent open-source foundation) project-proposal GitHub issue (opened Apr 24, 2026, status: Approved) for admitting Envoy AI Gateway (since renamed Agent Router) as a foundation project. - [[rfc-lmcache-mp-server-nvidia-dynamo-integration-issue-4352-lmcachelmcache]] — [RFC] LMCache MP server NVIDIA Dynamo integration · Issue #4352 · LMCache/LMCache — LMCache/LMCache issue #4352, '[RFC] LMCache MP server NVIDIA Dynamo integration' (opened Jul 30 2026 by aeon-x). Explicitly and exclusively scoped to NVIDIA Dynamo -- llm-d is never mentioned in the RFC body, despite being cited elsewhere (in the rebel-jinhwan ZmqKVEventSink fork commit) as 'the RFC' behind llm-d EPP support. Motivation: because the LMCache MP server is a standalone process (not an inference worker), naively sending its KV events to Dynamo would index them under the MP server's own identity, not crediting any actual vLLM worker in Dynamo's routing. Two proposals: (1) LMCache MP server fan-out-registers its ZMQ PUB endpoint under each connected engine's (worker_id, dp_rank) so all connected workers get credited when it emits events -- blocked because Dynamo's indexer registry currently rejects a second registration for an existing worker_id/dp_rank; (2) register LMCache as a Dynamo 'shared cache' queried via a worker-agnostic pull path at route time (LMCache coordinator's /directory/lookup_tokens API, returning per-chunk placements with instance_id/tier/backend/size_bytes), folded into scoring as a shared_cache_multiplier with soft-fail to indexer-only scoring -- modeled on how Dynamo's SGLang-HiCache-over-Mooncake shared-cache path already works. Proposal 2 was endorsed by LMCache maintainer DongDongJu (Jul 31 2026) as the preferred direction. Confirms the SGLang tier-transition event vocabulary Dynamo expects: store(GPU), store(CPU_PINNED) after GPU-to-host DMA, remove(GPU) on GPU eviction, remove(CPU_PINNED) on host eviction, store(GPU) on load-back promotion, store(CPU_PINNED) on external-to-host prefetch. `evergreen` (966 words)
- [[rfc-adopt-otel-genai-semantic-conventions-for-metrics-issue-51186-vllm-projectvl]] — [RFC]: Adopt otel genai semantic conventions for metrics · Issue #51186 · vllm-project/vllm — vLLM RFC issue #51186 ('Adopt otel genai semantic conventions for metrics'), opened 2026-08-05. Proposes adding gen_ai.server.* histogram metrics (request.duration, time_per_output_token, time_to_first_token) matching OTel GenAI semantic conventions, requiring new attributes (gen_ai.operation.name, gen_ai.provider.name, error.type). Contributor Shaurya856 proposed a dual-emit migration (keep legacy vllm:* names, add gen_ai.* alongside). Confirmed via GitHub API: issue remains OPEN as of 2026-10-10 (state=open, updated_at=2026-09-14T11:45:11Z, 4 comments, no activity since markmc's objection). The implementing PR (#53858, dual-emit) was closed 2026-09-14 without merging, explicitly citing markmc's objection and lack of maintainer appetite. The alternative PR it deferred to (#52249, operation-label-only) remains open and unmerged as of 2026-10-10, blocked on an unresolved rebase, with no further maintainer engagement. STATUS: CONFIRMS the gap description -- no reversal found; the objection stands and has, if anything, hardened (one implementation path explicitly abandoned). `evergreen` (938 words)
- [[rfc-cache-salting-for-secure-and-flexible-prefix-caching-in-vllm-issue-16016-vll]] — [RFC]: Cache Salting for Secure and Flexible Prefix Caching in vLLM · Issue #16016 · vllm-project/vllm — vLLM GitHub RFC #16016 (opened Apr 3, 2025 by dr75, closed May 6, 2025) proposing cache salting to defend vLLM's prefix cache against the timing side-channel attack described in 'Leaking Secrets from Prefix Caches' (arXiv:2411.18191): an attacker can guess candidate token blocks and infer cache hits (hence prior use by another tenant) from response latency, potentially reconstructing private prompts block by block. Proposes two designs: (A) single-barrier -- one cache_salt string per request, injected into the hash of the first block, restricting reuse to requests sharing that salt; (B) multi-barrier -- a cache_salt_map keyed by message index, allowing hierarchical isolation (e.g., an org-level salt on a shared document plus a user-level salt on the follow-up question, so the document's KV is shared org-wide while the question stays private even from other org members). Salts are base64-encoded 256-bit strings, not passed to the model, used only for block hashing. Maintainer comaniac initially suggested piggybacking on auth tokens (à la OpenAI's org-scoped cache sharing) instead; author dr75 countered that vLLM has only one global API key and that org-scoped-only sharing (OpenAI's model) is less flexible than salt hierarchies (e.g., nested tenant-of-tenants isolation). RFC concludes design (B) is the long-term target but recommends shipping (A) first for simplicity; (A) shipped in PR #17045. This directly explains the cache_salt / extra_hashes mechanism documented in vLLM's Automatic Prefix Caching design doc. A 2026 follow-up comment (user vpfkfl753, PR #52655) reports implementing multi-barrier as token-range salt regions and claims a synthetic timing-side-channel probe showed unsalted cross-tenant cache reuse is fully distinguishable via response usage/timing (reported timing AUC 1.0) while salted isolation reduces distinguishability to near-chance (AUC 0.462-0.551) with a measured throughput cost from lost cross-tenant cache reuse; this is a single commenter's self-reported, non-peer-reviewed benchmark and should be treated as an unverified claim, not confirmed vLLM behavior. `evergreen` (2222 words)
- [[rfc-kv-cache-layout-combining-all-layers-per-block-issue-27742-vllm-projectvllm]] — [RFC]: KV cache layout combining all layers per block · Issue #27742 · vllm-project/vllm — vLLM RFC #27742 (opened Oct 29, 2025 by orozery, closed 'not planned'/stale May 2026 after the uniform-model case shipped) is the original design rationale for the KV cache memory-layout change repeatedly cited elsewhere as the source of order-of-magnitude KV-transfer throughput gains. Motivation: for simple (non-Hybrid-Memory-Allocator) models, vLLM's default KV cache layout breaks each logical block's data into num_layers non-contiguous per-layer segments (further fragmented by k/v and kv_heads dimensions), which is costly for two transfer-heavy consumers -- NixlConnector (P/D disaggregation, GPU-to-host-buffer/other-agent) and OffloadingConnector (GPU-to-CPU). Proposes allocating one contiguous tensor holding all layers' KV data per block instead of one tensor per layer, reducing fragments to copy by a factor of num_layers. Author reports an initial measured 4x transfer-time improvement for both NixlConnector and OffloadingConnector on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, with no noticeable degradation in plain model-execution performance. New layout is opt-in, selected only when (1) the model has a simple uniform (non-HMA) layout and (2) the configured KV connector class explicitly requests it via a new classmethod. Discussion thread with maintainers (ApostaC, heheda12345, ronen tlrmchlsmth) covers extending the approach to Hybrid Memory Allocator models with heterogeneous per-layer page sizes (e.g., DeepSeek V3.2's two KV cache types with different hidden sizes), concluding the uniform-page-size case (is_kv_cache_page_size_uniform) is tractable but arbitrary heterogeneous page sizes per layer (UniformTypeKVCacheSpecs) are not solved by this scheme. The uniform-model implementation shipped in PR #27743 (linked from this RFC), which is the change the Jan 2026 vLLM offloading-connector blog post credits with the observed 0.11.0->0.12.0 throughput jump. `evergreen` (1521 words)
- [[rfc-kv-aware-routing-for-pd-disaggregated-serving-via-sourced-kv-events-issue-99]] — [RFC]: KV-aware routing for PD disaggregated serving via sourced KV events · Issue #9905 · vllm-project/vllm-ascend — Merged-community RFC (GitHub issue #9905, opened June 3 2026, vllm-project/vllm-ascend, ground-truth/code tier) that is directly load-bearing for building a non-k8s-pod-backend router: it proposes an optional 'KV event collector' component that runs NEAR the router (not inside vLLM, not inside the EPP) and republishes a 'sourced' KV event stream, decoupling KV-cache visibility from the k8s-pod identity model entirely. Key primitives reused unchanged from vLLM: KVCacheEvent/BlockStored/BlockRemoved/AllBlocksCleared (vllm/distributed/kv_events.py), KVEventBatch (msgspec batch with timestamp+events+DP rank), ZmqEventPublisher (PUB socket with optional ROUTER replay), KVEventsConfig. Critically, the collector is configured with EXPLICIT upstream sources rather than inferring source identity from ZMQ messages (ZMQ PUB/SUB carries no sender endpoint) -- UpstreamKVEventSource{instance_id: str, endpoint: str, topic: str} where instance_id can be 'a Decode instance ID, PD group ID, or service address,' NOT necessarily a k8s pod. This is the exact abstraction point a non-k8s router should adopt: replace 'pod' with an arbitrary 'source_instance' string. New wire type SourcedKVCacheEvent{event, source_instance} plus SourcedKVEventBatch preserves the existing vLLM event protocol (additive, opt-in decode). Router-side state needed: two indexes block_hash->set[source_instance] and source_instance->set[block_hash], plus an optional token-prefix trie built from BlockStored.token_ids for prefix-aware scoring -- explicitly warns that vLLM block hashes include parent hash + process-local seed, so 'an external router cannot reliably reproduce hashes,' meaning any router MUST consume token_ids/block_hashes as reported rather than recompute them. Notes the RFC explicitly cross-references kvcache-ai/Mooncake#977 (Mooncake-Conductor global scheduler) as a potential integration point for unified KV-cache state maintenance. `evergreen` (1420 words)
- [[rfc-kvblocks-and-metrics-publishing-in-inference-frameworks-issue-16669-vllm-pro]] — [RFC]: KVBlocks and Metrics Publishing In Inference Frameworks · Issue #16669 · vllm-project/vllm — Closed vLLM RFC (#16669, opened Apr 15 2025 by alec-flowers/NVIDIA-Dynamo, closed "completed" Nov 7 2025) is the design-history record for the KVEvents publishing mechanism llm-d now depends on. Original proposal wanted to hook a pluggable KVBlockObserver directly into AsyncLLM's constructor for in-process event injection, paralleling the existing StatsLoggerBase pattern for metrics. Discussion (markmc, ApostaC, ai-dynamo's alec-flowers) rejected the EngineCoreEvent path as too heavyweight/critical-path, debated whether the existing KV Connector API (used for actual KV-tensor transfer, ~100MB/s with retry/ack) was the right hook point, and converged on a separate, decoupled mechanism: alec-flowers proposed introducing "a kv_event_config and kv_event_sink that the Scheduler instantiates next to the KV Connector," explicitly contrasting it with the Connector: "KV-Transfer handles 100MB/s tensor w/ retry and ack... KV Event publishing is low bandwidth publishing, pub/sub w/ fire and forget." This is the design that shipped as ZmqEventPublisher in vllm/distributed/kv_events.py. Thread also surfaces that NVIDIA Dynamo's own kv_router (github.com/ai-dynamo/dynamo, lib/llm/src/kv_router/publisher.rs and indexer.rs) predates and parallels vLLM's native KVEvents, and that vMaroon (IBM Research, later author of the llm-d Red Hat blog) explicitly frames this as complementary to Connector-API-based "external KV locality" awareness rather than a replacement -- i.e., KVEvents give internal-state visibility, Connector API gives external-storage-locality visibility, and IBM's roadmap combines both for fine-grained KV-aware routing. Confirms storage/medium (GPU/CPU/remote like S3 or Redis) was explicitly discussed as a future BlockStored extension point (KuntaiDu's question about Redis-backed KV cache), materializing later as the `medium` field (GPU/CPU/STORAGE) in the shipped BlockStored dataclass. `evergreen` (2080 words)
- [[rfc-mooncake-conductor-design-and-implementation-of-a-global-scheduler-module-fo]] — [RFC]: Mooncake-Conductor: Design and Implementation of a Global Scheduler Module for KV-Cache-Centric Disaggregated Architecture · Issue #977 · kvcache-ai/Mooncake — IMPORTANT PROVENANCE FLAG: this GitHub RFC (opened Oct 28 2025, kvcache-ai/Mooncake issue #977) was CLOSED AS 'NOT PLANNED' -- it is an unmerged, rejected proposal, not shipped Mooncake functionality. Treat every claim below as a design sketch only, not a description of production behavior. That said, it is architecturally the clearest published articulation of a backend-agnostic global scheduler for KV-cache-centric disaggregated serving, directly relevant to a non-k8s-pod llm-d-style router. The RFC proposes 'Mooncake-Conductor,' a global scheduler realizing a three-layer 'sandwich' architecture: Top (Scheduling Layer) = Conductor as an independent process/service acting as cluster entry point, deliberately not performing computation itself; Middle (Compute Layer) = inference engines (vLLM, SGLang) that are 'agnostic to global resource distribution... other nodes or the global location of the KVCache' -- i.e. compute nodes need not be Kubernetes pods, they are addressed as opaque 'instances'; Bottom (Storage/Data Plane) = Mooncake Store (distributed KVCache) + Mooncake Transfer Engine (RDMA Messenger). Defines three scheduler sub-components: a Cache-aware Prefill Scheduler (cost = ttft_estimate - alpha*prefix_match_length, explicitly inspired by 'NVIDIA Dynamo's intelligent router' cost function and SGLang Router's hybrid cache-first/load-first strategy), a Load-balance Decoding Scheduler (load_score = current_batch_size + k*waiting_queue_length, with an early-rejection mechanism when no instance can meet TBT SLO), and a KVCache Balance Scheduler (heat-aware hierarchical storage: hot blocks promoted to GPU HBM, cold blocks demoted to SSD, hotspot replication across storage nodes). Proposes external interfaces as REST/gRPC (request reception, resource discovery/status collection, scheduling-decision dispatch, and a management API) -- a protocol-level, not k8s-API-level, integration surface. Flags the same event-driven-over-polling requirement seen in the vllm-ascend RFC: state sync should use incremental, event-driven index updates (not full polling) to avoid O(n^2) overhead, backed by a global Block-Radix-Tree index recording only KVCache block location per physical storage pool node (deliberately not recording k8s-specific metadata). `evergreen` (8700 words)
- [[rfc-multi-tier-kv-offloading-via-the-vllm-offloading-connector-issue-38260-vllm]] — [RFC]: Multi-tier KV offloading via the vLLM offloading connector · Issue #38260 · vllm-project/vllm — vLLM GitHub RFC #38260 (opened Mar 26, 2026, by dannyharnik) proposing the multi-tier KV offloading architecture that later shipped as the TieringOffloadingSpec (see kv-offloading-usage-guide-vllm and tiered-kv-cache-offloading-in-vllm-vllm-blog for the shipped feature). Defines a single primary tier (CPU DRAM, exclusive GPU access) plus multiple secondary tiers (storage, remote-node P/D, P2P), orchestrated by a new TieringManager. Key design principles as stated in the RFC: (1) always cascade a confirmed block to every secondary tier asynchronously; (2) primary tier is the sole gateway to GPU memory; (3) staged promotion -- lookup() returns None while a secondary-tier hit is being promoted to primary, and the scheduler retries; (4) all SecondaryTierManager methods (lookup/submit_store/submit_load/get_finished/touch) run non-blocking in the scheduler process, with actual data movement on the worker thread only for GPU<->CPU; (5) each secondary tier owns its own eviction policy. CPU memory is mandated to be stored in canonical TP-rank-1 form regardless of the vLLM node's actual TP rank, enabling cross-TP-rank sharing/storage. Comment thread (through Jul 2026) tracks the RFC's evolution into shipped PRs: #40020 (multi-tier data plane), #41968 (object-store tier), #43468 (self-describing KV events for single-tier CPUOffloadingSpec, explicitly NOT yet supported for TieringOffloadingSpec because tier promotions can create primary-tier store events with no live GPU job to describe), #46544 (opt-in hash-only BlockStored events for secondary tiers, with medium naming MEDIUM_CPU/MEDIUM_FS/MEDIUM_OBJ added alongside existing MEDIUM_GPU), and #48281 (tier locality: 'medium says what the storage is; locality says where it sits relative to the publishing instance'). A maintainer (Change72) states explicitly that stale KV placement state is a routing-correctness hazard: 'whoever owns eviction should also provide invalidation. Otherwise, routers may score or route based on stale placement state.' NOTE: several later comments (user coltonottley, Jul 2026) present extremely detailed self-reported 'production' benchmark numbers (exact byte counts, token counts, decode tok/s) for an unmerged downstream fork implementing canonical TP1 CPU-primary storage; these are unverified, self-reported claims by a single GitHub commenter, not peer-reviewed or merged into vLLM, and should not be cited as vetted vLLM performance characteristics. `evergreen` (6940 words)

- [[trtllm-15331feat-improve-kv-event-publish-performance-via-streamingevent-by-tanm]] — [TRTLLM-15331][feat] Improve KV event publish performance via StreamingEvent by tanmayv25 · Pull Request #17023 · NVIDIA/TensorRT-LLM · GitHub — PRIMARY SOURCE: NVIDIA/TensorRT-LLM PR #17023 (TRTLLM-15331, author tanmayv25, merged by allisonlim-nv into NVIDIA:main at commit 05293ce on Sep 10, 2026) -- direct, dated evidence that TensorRT-LLM implements a ZMQ-based KV-cache event publisher compatible in spirit with vLLM/llm-d's event schema, as production (if prototype-flagged) code, not aspirational roadmap.\n\nMotivation quoted verbatim: 'External KV-cache-aware routers (e.g. Dynamo) subscribe to a stream of block stored/removed events to route requests to the engine that already holds a prefix.' Notably credits NVIDIA Dynamo as the named external consumer -- llm-d is not mentioned anywhere in the PR.\n\nPre-existing state: TRT-LLM already had a buffered/pull-based KV event path (LLM.get_kv_cache_events(), built on Python KVCacheEvent objects, all-gathered onto rank 0 under attention DP) before this PR -- so KV-cache eventing already existed; this PR adds a new opt-in STREAMING push path.\n\nNew components added: StreamingKVCacheEventManager (new tensorrt_llm/_torch/pyexecutor/kv_cache_events.py) builds wire-format msgspec structs named BlockStored / BlockRemoved / AllBlocksCleared -- the same three event-type names used by vLLM and SGLang -- reusing 'the low 64 bits of the radix block key as the wire hash (no re-hash)' and coalescing consecutive blocks into one event. ZmqEventPublisher msgpack-encodes each per-iteration batch and publishes over a ZeroMQ PUB socket with 3 frames (topic, seq, payload); each attention-DP rank binds base_port + rank; an optional ROUTER replay socket is supported. New config: KVEventsConfig nested as kv_cache_config.kv_events_config, explicitly marked prototype. Scope guards limit it to KV cache manager V2 (PyTorch backend) only, excluded for draft models and KV-cache-size estimation, and raises under pipeline or context parallelism. Off by default; the older buffered/pull path is unchanged and remains the default.\n\nImplication for llm-d: TensorRT-LLM's event schema (BlockStored/BlockRemoved/AllBlocksCleared over ZMQ, msgpack-encoded) is structurally compatible with what llm-d's kvevents.EngineAdapter interface expects, and this is real merged code as of Sep 10 2026 -- so the llm-d.ai marketing claim that TensorRT-LLM 'publishes every cache change as an event' is technically accurate about TRT-LLM's own capability. However this PR gives zero evidence of an llm-d-side TRTLLMAdapter or wire-format parser; combined with the llm-d-kv-cache architecture.md doc (which lists only VLLMAdapter and SGLangAdapter) and the deployment guide (which ships only vllm/sglang MODEL_SERVER directories), the gap is on llm-d's consumption side, not TensorRT-LLM's production side. `evergreen` (6316 words)
- [[featvllm-publish-mp-connector-kv-events-by-dongdongju-pull-request-5076-lmcachel]] — [feat][vllm] publish MP connector KV events by DongDongJu · Pull Request #5076 · LMCache/LMCache · GitHub — LMCache/LMCache PR #5076 ('[feat][vllm] publish MP connector KV events' by DongDongJu, opened Sep 11 2026, refs issue #3661). It takes a different approach from the rival rebel-jinhwan ZmqKVEventSink fork: rather than a new ZMQ sink with LMCache-specific medium strings, it enhances the existing vLLM connector so completed MP-server stores get converted to vLLM BlockStored events and drained through the connector's normal take_events() path -- the same mechanism the (deprecated) in-process connector already uses -- and 'emits canonical medium="CPU" in both in-process and MP store events,' i.e. it fits directly into llm-d's/Dynamo's existing GPU/CPU weight bucket rather than requiring new tier configuration. Explicitly STORE-ONLY: author states 'It does not publish BlockRemoved or AllBlocksCleared for MP host-cache eviction... I will work on this for next step' -- so even once merged, evicted MP-tier blocks would not be retracted from a router's view, a stale-placement risk. Validation cited: 51 unit tests passing (tests/v1/test_vllm_mp_adapter.py); a two-worker Kubernetes routing test with 'stock vLLM and Dynamo' showing 12/12 correct cache-owner routing decisions with events enabled versus 4/12 without; 24,000 host-cache-hit tokens served; exact ownership for all 3,000 target chunks; zero Dynamo KV-event application errors. Validation was Dynamo-specific, not llm-d-specific, though the mechanism (standard vLLM BlockStored/medium=CPU over the normal KV-events path) is router-agnostic in principle. Discussion thread (Sep 12-14 2026) shows a competing proposal (from aeon-x, author of RFC #4352) to publish events directly from the MP process using a Dynamo-only KvStateAttachmentOwner interface, which DongDongJu's simpler vLLM-connector approach was judged to supersede; PR #4995 (an earlier overlapping attempt by maobaolong) was closed in favor of #5076 on Sep 13 2026. `evergreen` (1030 words)

- [[allowlistgo]] — allowlist.go — Go source (pkg/sidecar/proxy/allowlist.go, llm-d-router@main) implementing AllowlistValidator, the SSRF protection for the P/D disaggregation sidecar. When disabled (default), IsAllowed() unconditionally returns true for 'backward compatibility' -- i.e. any x-prefiller-host-port value is accepted. When enabled via NewAllowlistValidator, it requires a Kubernetes client (clientcmd.NewNonInteractiveDeferredLoadingClientConfig, in-cluster or kubeconfig) and a dynamic.Interface, then watches one named InferencePool CRD (group inference.networking.k8s.io v1, or deprecated inference.networking.x-k8s.io v1alpha2) plus the pods matched by that pool's selector via a Kubernetes SharedInformer, populating allowedTargets with each matched pod's IP and name only. There is no static file, CIDR, or flag-based allowlist source anywhere in this file -- the only inputs are the InferencePool CRD and its selected pods, both Kubernetes API objects. `evergreen` (1301 words)
- [[arxiv250605508v1-csdc-5-jun-2025]] — arXiv:2506.05508v1  [cs.DC]  5 Jun 2025 — NVIDIA paper 'Beyond the Buzz: A Pragmatic Take on Inference Disaggregation' (arXiv:2506.05508v1, 5 Jun 2025, Mitra/Borkar/Bhatia/Matas et al.). First systematic large-scale study of prefill/decode disaggregation design space: simulates hundreds of thousands of design points (model partitioning x traffic pattern x hardware config) using a proprietary datacenter-scale GPU performance simulator on Blackwell/FP4 systems, evaluating DeepSeek-R1 and Llama-3.1-70B. Central finding: disaggregation is most effective for prefill-heavy traffic (ISL >> OSL) and larger models (>10B params); benefit is NOT universal and depends heavily on the target tokens/s/user (interactivity) operating point on the throughput-interactivity Pareto frontier. Assumes KV cache produced at each transformer layer during prefill is transferred to the decode pool immediately as it becomes available, overlapping with subsequent-layer computation (Section 5.1 examines implications of this assumption -- i.e. that real transfer bandwidth/latency constraints matter for realized gains). Identifies rate matching (an integer-solver-based algorithm balancing prefill vs decode instance counts against FTL/TTL SLA constraints and total GPU count) and elastic scaling as critical to realizing gains; without dynamic rate matching, static prefill:decode ratios leave throughput on the table. Also finds Chunked Pipeline Parallelism (CPP) reduces First Token Latency (FTL) under strict SLAs for long-context prefill (e.g. DeepSeek-R1, ISL=256K on 64 GPUs). For co-located (non-disaggregated) serving, finds context-chunked piggybacking effectiveness is highly sensitive to attention mechanism (MLA vs GQA) and most beneficial under relaxed latency targets and generation-heavy traffic. This is the source llm-d's official P/D disaggregation guide cites for its throughput/interactivity tradeoff claim, though llm-d's guide does not adopt the paper's rate-matching/elastic-scaling machinery directly -- worth flagging as a gap between the cited research motivation and llm-d's current (largely static prefill:decode replica ratio) implementation. `evergreen` (6414 words)

Sets router.epp.replicas=1, router.epp.pluginsConfigFile: default-plugins.yaml (the pre-override default), inferencePool.failureMode: FailOpen, envoy sidecar proxy defaults (--concurrency 8, --log-level warn, --drain-time-s 60), and OTel tracing at 10% sample rate (parentbased_traceidratio). Contains no schedulingProfiles/plugins of its own -- those are guide-specific overrides on top of this file. `evergreen` (220 words)

Because IsAllowed() always returns true when SSRF protection is disabled (the required posture for any non-Kubernetes backend, since the validator hard-depends on InferencePool+pod watches), any caller able to reach the sidecar's listening port can set x-prefiller-host-port (and the analogous x-encoder-hosts-ports / x-kv-cache-source-host-port headers) to an arbitrary host:port and the sidecar will reverse-proxy the OpenAI-style request there with no validation at all -- this is the concrete SSRF exposure, not a hypothetical one. `evergreen` (704 words)

Implementations must provide linearizable behavior across every EPP replica sharing the syncer.' CrossReplicaContributor is an OPT-IN interface ('The plugin's Extract method is unchanged; the runtime detects this interface and wires the store transparently') that any endpoint-extractor/data-producer plugin can implement to get its per-endpoint attribute synced across replicas via CrossReplicaSpec{StateKey, AttributeKey, Supply, Aggregate, SyncDisabled}. CRITICAL: this is a distinct, general-purpose plugin surface separate from PeerDiscovery/peer-discovery.md's peer-set membership tracking -- PeerDiscovery only discovers WHICH replicas exist; CrossReplicaSyncer is what actually MOVES per-endpoint state between them, and it is opt-in per plugin (SyncDisabled defaults false = enabled once implemented). The precise-prefix-cache extractor.go (already in vault) implements only fwkdl.EndpointExtractor, NOT CrossReplicaContributor -- confirming precise prefix cache achieves active-active consistency purely via independent per-replica KV-event subscription, not via this cross-replica-syncer path, consistent with the draft's claim.
